CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM

Intended Learning Outcomes {ILOs): 

By the end of this chapter the student should be able to: 
1. Identify different components and function of the nervous system. 
2. Describe main brain fuel pathways to produce energy. 
3. Outline the consequence of brain energy metabolism impairment. 
4, Describe the structure of neuroglia, cerebral cortex, meninges and 

brain barriers. 
5, Define neurotransmitters. 
6. l{lustrate different biochemical pathways for neurotransmitter synthesis and 

degradation. 
7. Relate some clinical disorders to disruption of neurotransmitter metabolism 
8. Describe Signal processing in CNS . 

9. Describe spinal cord, spinal nerves, meninges and their applied anatomy. 
10. Describe layers of the scalp and their clinical significance. 
11. Describe blood and nerve supply of scalp. 
12. Describe muscles of the face. 
13. Describe blood supply, nerve supply and lymphatic drainage of face. 
14. Identify bones, sutures and main features of skull. 
15. Identify the poles, surfaces, borders and main sulci and gyri of cerebral 

hemispheres. 
16. Locate functional and motor cortical areas. 
17. Predict the outcome of lesions in any of these areas. 
18. Clarify amino acid metabolism and production of ammonia 
19. Describe fate of ammonia and urea cycle. 
20. Explain causes and impact of hyperammonemia. 
21. Describe the biochemical basis of different inborn errors of amino acid 

metabolism. 
22. Relate metabolic disorders and vitamin deficiency to brain dysfunction (Role 

of thiamine vitamin in CNS & related dis, PDH deficiency, Phenylketonuria, 
Maple syrup Urine Disease). 

23. Explain one carbon unit metabolism.

INTRODUCTION TO CENTRAL NERVOUS SYSTEM (Reading only} 

CLASSIFICATION OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM: 

Anatomically, it is divided into: 
1. Central nervous system (CNS) 

Includes the brain and spinal cord (Fig.1.1) that are not

The PNS is subdivided into: 

1) Afferent (sensory) branch of the PNS: the branch that carries sensory information towards 
the CNS. When information arises from the skin, skeletal muscles, or joints this is known 
as somatic sensory information; when information arises from the blood vessels or internal 
organs, this is known as visceral sensory information. 
2) Motor (efferent) branch of the PNS: the branch that carries signals 
away from the CNS to the effector organs. When the effector organ is a skeletal muscle, the 
information is called somatic (voluntary) motor; when the éffector organ is cardiac or smooth 
muscle or glands, the information is called visceral (autonomic) motor. (Fig.1.3)

1. Somatic nervous system comprises: 

a. Sensory System. 
b. Motor system 
c. Centers: integrate and processes information, initiates responses and integrates thoughts 
emotions and other mental processes 

2. Autonomic nervous system(visceral): which is involuntary and includes a sympathetic and 
parasympathetic system

CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

DEFINITIONS: 
Grey matter & white matter: 

In the CNS, the cell bodies form the grey matter while the nerve fibers form the white matter. 
- In the spinal cord, the white matter surrounds the grey matter which is H-shaped. 
- In the brain stem, the grey matter collects into nuclei embedded in the white matter. 
- In the cerebral hemispheres and the cerebellum, part of the grey matter collects into deep 

nuclei and another part spreads on the surface forming the cortex. 

Nucleus= a collection of cell bodies that have the same function within the CNS. Outside the CNS, a 
collection of cell bodies is called ganglion. 

Tract (fasciculus)= a bundle of nerve fibers within the CNS having the same origin, same termination 
and same function. Bundles outside the CNS constitute nerves. 

Pathway= a chain of successive tracts having the same function (e.g. carrying pain sensation). 

Lemniscus= collection of ascending fibers in the brainstem. 

Commissure= a band of white or grey matter connecting the right & left sides of the CNS across the 
midline. 

Decussation= a point at which an ascending or descending tract crosses the midline. 

Synapse= site of contact between two neurons; usually between the axon of one neuron and the 
dendrites of the other neuron. 

The functions of the nervous system: 

The nervous system performs regulatory and coordinating activities that are essential for the 
human body to function normally. The nervous system functions include: 

1. Maintenance of homeostasis: by stimulating or inhibiting different body activities to help 
maintain homeostasis. For example, the nervous system increases heart rate and force of 
contraction when body demands are increased as during exercise 

2. Receiving and integrating sensory impulses: sensory receptors monitor many external and 
internal stimuli. Impulses from sensory receptors are received and processed by the nervous 
system either under conscious level {vision, hearing, taste, smell, touch, pain, body position, 
and temperature), or under unconscious level (blood pH, blood gases, and blood pressure). 

3. Integrating information: the brain and spinal cord are the major organs for processing sensory 
input and for initiating responses. The input may produce an immediate response, be stored 
as memory, or be ignored. 

4. Controlling muscles and glands: the nervous system controls the major movements of the 
body by controlling skeletal muscle. Although the nervous system does not initiate contraction 
in cardiac muscle and some smooth muscle because they contract auto rhythmically, it can 
cause the contractions to occur more rapidly or more slowly. In addition, the nervous system 
controls the secretions from many glands, including sweat glands, salivary glands, and glands 
of the digestive system. 

9. Maintaining mental activity: the brain is the center of mental activities, including 
consciousness, thinking, memory, and emotions.

Histological Structure of the Central Nervous System 

Histologically, the CNS is formed of nerve cells (Neurons), supporting cells (Neuroglia); and blood 
capillaries. 

Neurons: Nerve cells are classified into pseudounipolar, bipolar and multipolar. In the CNS, most 
neurons are multipolar. (Previously discussed)

1. Oligodendrocytes (Oligodendroglia) (Fig.1.5) 
Origin: ectodermal. 
Site: white matter of CNS 
Shape: 

® Small in size with rounded, dark 
nuclei and thin cytoplasmic processes. 

® Each process becomes a sheet-like and wraps repeatedly 
around a portion of a nearby axon forming the myelin 
sheath in CNS. 

® One oligodendrocyte can myelinate up to 60 axons. Fig.1.5: Oligodendrocytes 
® The cytoplasm is filled with microtubules but lacks 

intermediate filaments and glycogen. 
Function: 
e Form the myelin sheath of nerve fibers in CNS. Each cell can form myelin sheath of several 

internodal segments on the same axon or different axons. 
e Support the neurons. 
e [Insulation of nerve impulses. 
N.B.: White and gray matter contain a pool of adult progenitor cells, which can differentiate to 
oligodendrocytes that might remyelinate axons in pathologically demyelinated regions. 
Multiple sclerosis (MS): it is a chronic inflammatory disease of the CNS characterized by loss of 
myelin. During periods of activity of the disease, lymphocytes (T cells) initiate an inflammatory 
response accompanied by loss of oligodendrocytes and axon demyelination in the white matter.

2. Astrocytes (Fig.1.6) 
Origin: ectodermal. 
Site: white and gray matter. They are the most numerous glial cells in gray matter. 
Shape: star-like, they have processes which end by expanded parts termed astrocyte feet that 
make contact with blood capillaries, neurons or nerve fibers. These processes are connected by 
gap junctions. 
Nucleus: large, ovoid or rounded. 
The cytoplasm contains: 

* Microtubules 
x * The intermediate filaments (glial filaments), which is formed of Glial fibrillary acid protein 
Mo l Kel & (GFAP) hich is used as a marker for the brain tumor, Astrocytoma. 

* Thread-like mitochondria. 
= Glycogen 

fibrous astrocyte 

Fig.1.6: Types of astrocytes 

5 ypes of astrocytes: 
)C W A. Fibrous astrocytes: found mainlyi n the(white matte). Have long, thin processes. 

B. Protoplasmic astrocytes: found mainlyi n the gray matter) Have short extensive processes. 
N.B.: Astrocytes with specialized morphology: Miiller cells in the retina and pituicytes of the 
posterior pituitary. 
Function of astrocytes: 
1. Regulate metabolic exchange between neurons and blood. 

. Secrete factors regulating neural functions. 

. Regulate the extracellular ionic concentration around the neurons. 

. Regulate the diameter of blood vessels within the CNS. 

. Support neurons. 

. Form a part of the blood brain barrier. 

. Repair processes by proliferation and formation of glial tissue {glial scar). 

. They store glycogen within their cytoplasm which can be broken down into glucose and 
further into lactate, both of which are released to surrounding neurons.

3. Microglia (Fig.1.7) 
Origin: blood monocytes 
Site: gray and white matter of the brain and the spinal cord, in the retina and optic nerve 
Shape: small spindle shaped cells with branching poles. The cell body and processes have minute 
spines on their surfaces. 
Nucleus: dense, elongated nucleus 
Cytoplasm: scanty. 
Function of microglia: 
e Microglial cells are the phagocytes of the CNS. 
e Microglial cells express receptors for neurotransmitters and thus may influence the synaptic 

transmission. 
e During development microglial cells remove apoptotic cells.

4. Ependymal cells {(Ependyma) 

Origin: ectodermal 
Site: lines the ventricles of the brain and the central canal of the spinal cord. 
Shape: Cuboidal or columnar. They do not rest on a basement membrane. Apical surfaces have 
numerous microvilli and cilia. 
Types: 

a. Ependymal cells lining the third and fourth ventricles: 
e Apical surfaces have numerous microvilli 
e Numerous mitochondria and basal infoldings 
e Basal nuclei. 
e Joined by tight junctions and desmosome. 

b. Tanycytes (ependymal astrocytes). These cells have basal processes that facilitate the 

passage of hypothalamic- regulating hormones to pars distalis of pituitary gland. 

c. Choroidal epithelium: 
e Highly modified ependyma. 
¢ lies adjacent to the vascular layer of the choroid plexuses.

Functions of ependyma 
e Play a role in the circulation of CSF (by their cilia). 
e Absorptive function (by their microvilli). 

e Some ependymal cells may be stem cells in adult human brain, but their potential for neural 
regeneration is still under investigation. 

The choroid plexus: 
e [tis found in the roofs of the third and fourth ventricles and the medial walls of the lateral 

ventricles. 

e [tis formed of invaginated leaf like protrusions {villi} into the ventricular cavity. 

e Each villus is formed of a central core of pia mater with fenestrated blood capillaries and 

covered by columnar ependymal cells which line the brain ventricles. 

Function: Secretion of cerebrospinal fluid. 
N.B.: Hydrocephalus: 

¢ [tis a case in which excess quantity of cerebrospinal fluid is present in the brain cavities, that 
increases the intracranial pressure. 

e Causes: 1) A decrease in absorption of cerebrospinal fluid. 
2) Obstruction of SCF outflow from the ventricles. 

e Congenital hydrocephalus results in an enlargement of the head, followed by brain atrophy, 
mental impairment and muscular weakness. 

Brain Barriers 
1. Blood — brain barrier (BBB): {Fig.1.8) 

It is formed of: 
a) The endothelial cells of the continuous blood capillaries which are tightly sealed together by 

\ tight junction (Zonula occludens). 
\v b) The continuous basement membrane of the endothelial cells of the capillaries. 

c) The pericytes of the blood capillaries 
px d) The limiting layer of perivascular astrocytic feet (Foot processes of astrocytes) that completely 
Ra encircles the basal lamina of the capillaries. 

3) Th 2 choroid plexu ] where CSF is produced. 

Function of the blood-brain barrier: 
It protects the brain tissue against toxic drugs and other harmful substances as bacteria that may 
be present in the blood.

Fibers of the cerebral cortex:

a) Afferent fibers of thalamo-cortical radiation. 

b) Efferent fibers of corticospinal, corticoreticular and corticopontine fibers. |

The meninges (Fig. 1.12) 
The brain and the spinal cord are surrounded by three coverings called the meninges: 

The dura mater, the arachnoid, the pia mater. The arachnoid and the pia mater together are called 

the leptomeninges. 

1) The dura mater: 
It is the outermost layer, formed of dense fibro-elastic connective tissue that is continuous with 

the periosteum of the skull. Its inner surface is covered by simple squamous epithelium. It is 

separated from the arachnoid by the subdural space. Around the spinal cord, it is separated from 

the periosteum of the vertebrae by epidural space. 

2) The arachnoid: 
It is a delicate membrane between the dura and the pia mater. It is formed of: 

a) Flat membrane which is adjacent to the inner surface of dura mater. 

b) A system of thin trabeculae which is composed of collagen fibers and fibrobasts. It extends 

from the inner surface of the flat membrane to be continuous with the underlying pia mater. 

These trabeculae surround sponge-like cavities called subarachnoid spaces which are filled 

with cerebrospinal fluid (CSF). Both surfaces of arachnoid and the trabeculae are covered by 

simple squamous epithelium. 

14



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

The arachnoid villi: 

In certain areas, the arachnoid penetrates the dura forming the arachnoid villi to reach the venous 

sinuses located within the dura. These villi help in CSF circulation from subarachnoid spaces into 

the blood sinuses of the dura.

CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

ENERGY SOURCES OF THE BRAIN 
« Brain fuels are food stuffs that supply the brain with energy which is used to constantly 

“recharge the batteries” of neurons, the information processing units of the brain. 

« The signaling mechanisms between neurons necessary for information processing represent 

approximately 90% of the energy used by the brain. 
. The brain is an energy-hungry organ as it usest en times more energy than the rest of the 

organs. 

Types of Brain Fuels

The main brain fuels pathways to produce energy: 
1. Glucose: 

Glucose enters cells through specific glucose transporters (GLUTs) and is phosphorylated by 

hexokinase (HK) to produce glucose-6-phosphate. As in other organs, glucose 6-phosphate can be 

processed via different metabolic pathways 
(a) Glycolysis (leading to lactate production or mitochondrial metabolism), 
(b) The pentose phosphate pathway (PPP). 
(c) Glycogenesis and glycogenolysis (in astrocytes only).

Astrocytic Glycogen Metabolism 
Astrocytes are thee nergetic warehouse of the brain: they are the only cells that contain glycogen. 
Astrocytes can perform glycogenesis and glycogenolysis. Cid 
The Control of the astrocytic glycogen contents: 
(1) Glycogen content is under the dynamic control of neurotransmitters (e.g., noradrenaline, 

serotonin, and vasoactive intestinal peptide [VIP]), neurohumoral agents (e.g., insulin), and local 

energy status (e.g., glucose levels). 
(2) Decreased neuronal activity, as observed during sleep and anesthesia, is associated with 

increased levels of brain glycogen. 
(3) Increased neuronal activity induced by sensory stimulation is associated with a decrease in 

glycogen levels in the activated areas, demonstrating a tight coupling between neuronal activity and 

glycogen mobilization. 
(4) Astrocytic glycogen mobilization (likely associated with the transfer of lactate from astrocytes to 

neurons) is required to sustain neuronal activity. 
(Remembert he astrocyte-neuron. lactate shuttle)

All 31 anterior rami receive grey rami communicants [postganglionic] from sympathetic 

ganglia, they are axons of neurons in the sympathetic ganglia. 

2. Posterior ramus (small): supplies muscles & skin of the back.

Applied anatomy: Nerve compression 

The size of the spinal nerves increases gradually from above 
downwards. The largest spinal nerve is the 1% sacral. 
Meanwhile, the size of the intervertebral foramina decreases 
from above downwards. The 4th" & 5% lumbar nerves are the 
most liable to compression.

form one tube together. Above, it is continuous 
with the cerebral meninges at the foramen magnum. 
Below, it ends opposite S2. 

2. Pia mater (inner layer) is adherent to the cord & continues 
below as the filum terminale 
which pierces the tube of dura & arachnoid to be attached to

one side) to the arachnoid and dura (on the other side). 

¢. Subarachnoid septum: extends from the posterior median septum to the arachnoid mater.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS 
Definition: 

Neurotransmitters (NTs) are substances used by neurons to communicate with one 

another and with their target tissues in the process of synaptic transmission 

(neurotransmission). 
Prior to their release into the synaptic cleft, NTs are stored in secretory vesicles (called 

synaptic vesicles). "ET 
When released, the NT crosses the synaptic gap and binds to specific receptors in the 

membrane of the post-synaptic neuron or cell. 

Thus, NTs are considered as the brain chemicals that communicate information 

throughout our brain and body. 

1. Catecholamine (dopamine, norepinephrine and epinephrine) 

Catecholamines are substances consisting of catechol (an aromatic structure with two 

hydroxyl groups) linked to an amine. 

They are synthesized from the amino acid tyrosine. 

Dopamine and norepinephrine are synthesized in the brain and function as 

neurotransmitters. 

Norepinephrine as epinephrine are also synthesized i| n the adrenal a 

Synthesis of catecholamines (Figure 1.22): 

-Tyrosine is hydroxylated by tyrosine hydroxylase which requires the coenzyme 

tetrah,drobiopterin (BH4) and the product is 3,4-dihydroxyphenylalanine (DOPA). 

-Tyrosine hydroxylase is th rate-limiting step in the catecholamine production. 

-DOPA is decarboxylated to dopamine by DOPA decarboxylase which requires the coenzym PLP, 

-Dopamine is hydroxylated by dopamine -hydroxylase to yield norepinephrine in a reaction that 

requires ascorbic acid and copper. | 
-Norepinephrine is methylated by the enzyme phenyl-ethanolamine N-methyl-transferase to 

form epinephrine. 

-In the newborn the primary end product in the meduila is norepinephrine, but with advancing 

age thereis a dramatic rise in he epinepITing content. This conversion depends “pon cortisol.

Processing of signals in CNS 

Neuronal pools are functional groups of neurons that integrate incoming information and 

forward the processed information to its appropriate destination. Input signals often do not 

affect all pools equally (fractionation of pools), they also do not affect all neurons of certain 

pool equally. 

1-The discharge and facilitated zones of neuronal pools: 

In the central portion of the field in this figure, designated by the circled area, all the 

neurons are stimulated by the incoming fiber. Therefore, this is said to be the discharge zone 

of the incoming fiber, also called the excited zone or liminal zone. To each side, the neurons 

are facilitated but not excited, and these areas are called the facilitated zone, also called the 

subthreshold zone or subliminal zone (Fig. 1.27) 

Gi zone 

i 

Facilitated zone 

Figure (1.27): "Discharge" and "Facilitated" zone of neuronal pool 

(Quoted from Guyton) 

2-Divergence: 

An amplifying type of divergence is shown in( Figure 1.28). This means simply that an 

input signal spreads to an increasing number of neurons as it passes through successive orders 

of neurons in its path. 

Divergence: in same tract Divergence in multiple tracts 
A B 

Figure (1.28): Divergence (Quoted from Guyton)

CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

3-Convergence: 
Convergence means signals from multiple inputs uniting to excite a single neuron. 
Figure 1.29 (a) shows convergence from a single source. Figure 1.29 (b) shows convergence from 
multiple sources which is important for providing enough spatial summation to bring the neuron 
to the threshold required for discharge . 

Source 

Comwerganfrcoem 
multiple sources 

B 

Figure (1.29): Convergence (Quoted from Guyton) 

4-After discharge (prolongation of signals): 

* Asignal entering a pool causes a prolonged output discharge, called after discharge, lasting 
a few milliseconds to as long as many minutes after the incoming signal is over. 

e 2 types: synaptic or interneuronal circuits. 

a-Synaptic after discharge: 

When excitatory synapses discharge on the surfaces of dendrites or soma of a neuron, a 
postsynaptic electrical potential develops in the neuron and lasts for many milliseconds, 
especially when some of the long-acting synaptic transmitter substances are involved. 

b-Interneuronal after discharge: 
1-Parallel: the input signals reach the output neuron in the form of successive impulses via 
a number of interneurons, with different numbers, that run in parallel, and consequently 
different synaptic delay times (Figure 1.30). 

i 4 Fo \ 
Input 

i 
Figure (1.30): Parallel after discharge 

29 



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

2-Reverberatory (Oscillatory) Circuit : 

o One of the most important of all circuits in the entire nervous system is the 

reverberatory, or oscillatory, circuit. Such circuits are caused by positive feedback 

within the neuronal circuit that feeds back to re-excite the input of the same circuit. 

Consequently, once stimulated, the circuit may discharge repetitively for a long time. 

e The cause of this sudden cessation of reverberation is fatigue of synaptic junctions in 

the circuit. (Figure 1.31). 

input 

D ef 
Figure (1.31): Reverbratory circuits {Quoted From Guyton) 

5-Shortening of signals in neuronal pools: 

A.Direct inhibition: 

Post-synaptic inhibition, e.g. some interneurons in spinal cord that inhibit antagonist muscles. 

Neurotransmitter secreted is glycine. 

B.Indirect inhibition: 

Pre-synaptic inhibition. This happens when an inhibitory synaptic knob lie directly on the 

termination of a pre-synaptic excitatory fiber. The inhibitory synaptic knob releases a 

transmitter (GABA) which inhibits the release of excitatory transmitter from the pre-synaptic 

fiber. 

C.Lateral inhibition: 

While the main pathway (A presynaptic cell ) is excited, it sends an inhibitory signal to the 

nearby neighboring nerve fibers, inhibiting undesired responses leading to focusing and 

sharpening of the response. In hearing, lateral inhibition helps to more sharply tune the ability 

of the brain to distinguish sounds of different pitches. In vision, it helps the brain to more 

sharply distinguish borders of light and darkness (as in retina by horizontal cells); and in 

olfaction, it helps the brain to more clearly distinguish closely related odors . (Figure 1.32).

F.Feedforward inhibition: an excitatory neuron stimulates an inhibitory interneurons which

same presynaptic source. The interneuron then outputs its inhibitory signal to the principal cell. 

Thus, the principal cell receives 2 types of input, one excitatory and one inhibitory.

o Recruitment is the gradual increase to a maximum in a reflex when a stimulus of

» There is progressive increase in the "background" activity of the interneurons, leading to 
an-increase in the excitability of more and more motor neurons, until spatial summation 
raises the excitability to the threshold value to discharge.

CEREBRAL HEMISPHERE: SENSORY CORTEX 
(Please refer to the anatomy of the cerebral hemispheres in practical) 

The parietal lobe contains general sensory areas (Fig. 1.38): 

- 2 sensory areas: 1 somatosensory area (Sl) and 2"d somatosensory area (Sl). 

- 2 parietal lobules: the superior contains the sensory association area & the inferior is 

responsible for body orientation + contains the sensory speech area of Wernick. 

First Somatosensory area (Sl): 

e Site: Postcentral gyrus + post part of paracentral lobule (in Brodmann'’s areas 3, 1, and 

2) 

e Body representation: Representation of the body parts in 5-1 is: 

- Crossed: The body and face are mapped in the contralateral cortex. 

- Inverted: The face is represented in the lower half of the postcentral gyrus, while the 

foot, leg and the trunk in the upper half of the postcentral gyrus. 
- Disproportional to body area size but proportionate to number of receptors in body area: 

the lips and some other areas of the body are represented by large areas in the somatic 

cortex, whereas the trunk and lower part of the body are represented by relatively small 

areas. The sizes of these areas are directly proportional to the number of specialized 
sensory receptors in each area of the body. 

s Function: it receives sensory impulses from two nuclei of thalamus (Fast pain, Fine touch, 

Fine degrees of thermal sensations, Vibration, Stereognosis, Proprioception, Deep 

pressure). 
e Lesion: contralateral hemianesthesia = Impaired sensation on opposite side of body. 

Second somatosensory area (Sl): 

e Site: In the superior lip of post. ramus of lateral sulcus behind the central sulcus. 

e Representation: Poor localization is found in somatosensory area Il. Roughly, the face is 

represented anteriorly, the arms centrally, and the legs posteriorly. It receives sensory 
stimuli from both sides of the body. 

e Function: involved in recognizing hand-held objects by texture and size. 

e Lesion: lesions in S-ll produce deficits in learning by object manipulation and in 

recognizing the texture and size of hand-held objects. 

Taste (gustatory) area (Area 43): 

e Site: In the superior lip of post. ramus of lateral sulcus (parietal operculum) extending 
into the anterior part of insula. 

e Function: receives ipsilateral solitario-thalamo-cortical fibers via the thalamus. 

Superior Parietal Lobule & precuneus (Areas 5 & 7) (Somatosensory Association Area): 

e Function: It seems clear that the somatosensory association area collects and combines 

information arriving from multiple areas to understand its meaning. 

e Lesion—> astereognosis (inability to recognize familiar objects by touch) 

34 



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

Inferior Parietal Lobule: 
Site: in the supra marginal gyrus (Area 40) and the angular gyrus (Area 39). 
Function: They are parts of Wernicke’s area (sensory speech area) which is present only 
in the dominant hemisphere. Wernicke’s area includes also the posterior parts of sup. & 
middle temporal gyri i.e. Wernicke's area lies in parietal and temporal lobes. It is 
responsible for comprehension (understanding) of auditory and visual information. It 
selects suitable words & sends them via the arcuate fasciculus to the motor speech area 
of Broca to produce speech. 
Lesion-> Sensory (Receptive) Aphasia. The patient cannot understand spoken or written 
words thus speaks wrong words but fluently; in contrast to motor aphasia, the patient 
doés not feel that he/she has any problem. 
Note: three speech centers are known: 

Anterior (Broca’s) 
Posterior (Wernicke's) 
Superior (in supplementary motor area Mil in medial frontal gyrus).

AMINOACID METABOLISM AND HYPERAMMONEMIA 
(Sources of ammonia and removal of nitrogen from amino acids) 

Metabolism of ammonia 

Overview: 
Amino acid catabolism generates ammonia. Brain tissue is very sensitive to ammonia, 

Ammonia intoxication produces a comatose state. Cells get rid of excess ammonia by the 

reductive amination of a-ketoglutarate to form glutamate by glutamate dehydrogenase and 

the conversion of glutamate into glutamine by glutamine synthetase. Both of these enzymes 

are found in high concentrations in the brain. In the liver, ammonia is detoxified into urea 

through urea cycle. Urea is released into blood stream and cleared by the kidneys. Urea 
accounts for 90% of the nitrogen-containing compounds of urine. 

AMMONIA 
e Blood ammonia level is 5-35 pmol/l 
e Ammonia is toxic to the central nervous system and its accumulation in the body is 

fatal. 

e Once formed in the body, ammonia must be removed from blood. 

e itis removed by the liver that converts it to urea, which is less toxic, water soluble and 
easily excreted in the urine. 

Sources of ammonia: 
(1) Degradation of amino acids: 
a. Deamination of amino acids with formation of a-keto acids and ammonia. 
b. Transamination of most amino acids with a-ketoglutaric acid to form glutamic acid, 

which in turn is deaminated by glutamate dehydrogenase to form a-ketoglutarate and 

ammonia. 
(2) Ammonia is released during catabolism of monoamines (e.g. epinephrine, 

"norepinephrine and dopamine by the action of monoamine oxidase enzyme). 

(3) Ammonia is released during purine and pyrimidine catabolism. 
(4) In the intestine by intestinal bacterial flora through the action of urease on urea. 

AMINO ACIDS DEGRADATION 
Overview: 
Amino acids regardless of their sources, if not incorporated immediately into new proteins, 

are not stored but rapidly degraded. 
Phases of catabolism of amino acids: 
1) First phase: 
e The removal of the a-amino groups forming ammonia and the corresponding a-keto 

acids. 
e The most important route for disposal of ammonia is the synthesis of urea through urea 

cycle in the liver. The remaining portion of free ammonia is excreted in the urine. 
2) Second phase: 
e The carbon skeletons of the a-keto acids are converted to common intermediates of 

energy-producing metabolic pathways. 
e These compounds can be metabolized to glucose, ketone bodies, fatty acids, CO2 and 

water. 

36 



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

REMOVAL OF NITROGEN FROM AMINO ACIDS 
Removing the a-amino group is an obligatory step in the catabolism of any amino acid and 
occurs through the following reactions: Deamination, transamination and transdeamination. 

|. Deamination 
Deamination is the removal of amino group from a-amino acid in the form of ammonia with 
the formation of a-keto acid. 

A. Oxidative deamination: 
It is catalyzed by one of the following enzymes: 
%* Glutamate dehydrogenase 4 
** L-amino acid oxidases 
% D-amino acid oxidases 

Glutamate dehydrogenase (Figure 1.39): 
e |tis present in high concentration in mitochondria of liver, heart, muscles and kidney. 
e It catalyzes the reversible oxidative deamination of glutamate to a-ketoglutarate 

and ammonia thus serving in both amino acid catabolism and synthesis. 
e |t requires NAD* or NADP" as coenzyme. NAD+ is used in in oxidative deamination and 

NADP+ in reductive amination.

In the kidneys, most of ammonia is excreted in the urine as NHg4* to regulate the acid base

In the liver, the ammonia is detoxified to urea. 

Il. Transamination 

-  Transamination is the transfer of an a-amino group from an a-amino acid to an a-keto acid 

forming a new amino acid and a new keto acid. 
-  Transamination reactions are catalyzed by transaminases (or aminotransferases) which 

occur in cytosol and mitochondria of all tissues especially liver. 
- Their coenzyme (PLP) is present at the catalytic site of aminotransferases. 
-  Transamination is reversible and functions both in amino acid synthesis and amino acid 

catabolism. 
Two transaminases are important in clinical diagnosis: 

(1) Serum glutamic oxaloacetic transaminase (SGOT) or aspartate aminotransferase

Fate of amino acids: 
According to the end product of the a-keto acids catabolism, amino acids are classified into: 
1. Glucogenic amino acids: They produce pyruvate, oxaloacetate or any intermediate in the 

citric acid cycle that can be used in gluconeogenesis. 
2. Ketogenic amino acids: They produce acetoacetate, acetyl CoA or acetoacetyl CoA which 

can be used in ketogenesis. These include leucine and lysine. 
3. Glucogenic and Ketogenic (mixed) amino acids: They can give both glucose and ketone 
bodies and include: tyrosine, phenylalanine, tryptophan, threonine and isoleucine. 
Fate of ammonia: 

1. Biosynthesis of urea is the main fate of ammonia. 
2 Biosynthesis of glutamic acid from ammonia and a-ketoglutaric acid by the action of 

glutamate dehydrogenase enzyme. 
Biosynthesis of nonessential amino acids. 
Biosynthesis of glutamine by glutamine synthetase enzyme. 
Small amounts of ammonia are excreted in urine.

CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

UREA CYCLE 
Causes of ammonia toxicity: 
Ammonia toxicity is due to: 
(1) Removal of excess ammonia involves reductive amination of a-ketoglutarate to 

glutamate and conversion of the latter to glutamine according to the following reaction: 

(Hr COOH GH CO-NH- 

(CH2)2 Glutamine swnthetase (CH2)2 

COOH ATP NH; H.O ADP COOH 

Ghitamate Glutamine 

The first reaction depletes cellular a-ketoglutarate required for ATP production. Glutamine 

formation depletes ATP. Therefore, toxic concentrations of NH3 interfere with ATP 
production required to maintain brain functions. 
(2) Depletion of glutamate via glutamine synthetase reaction results into depletion of its 
derivative GABA which is an inhibitory neurotransmitter in the brain. 
(3) Elevated levels of ammonia produce an increased permeability to K* and Ci-ions which 
could interfere with electrical activity in brain. 
4) Glutamine causes osmotic shift of water into cells resulting in cerebral edema. 

5) Excess glutamine is exchanged with Tryptophan a precursor for serotonin (Excitatory 
neurotransmitter). 

Urea Biosynthesis 
Overview: 
Ammonia is highly toxic to the central nervous system. It is converted to urea, which is 
much less toxic, water soluble and easily excreted in urine. 
The liver is the site of urea biosynthesis through urea cycle. 
Summary of urea cycle: 

1. Occurs in five steps: the first 2 steps occur in mitochondria and the last 3 steps occur 
in cytoplasm. 

2. Utilizes 3 ATP and 4 high energy bonds. 
3. Is catalyzed by five enzymes. 
4. Any defecti n one of these enzymes leads to ammonia intoxication. 

Transport of ammonia in the circulation: 
Ammonia is present at very low levelsi n the blood. Amino acid nitrogen is released from 
tissues in the form of glutamine or alanine: 

1) Ammonium ion (NH4*) 
2) Glutamine is transported to the liver where it is cleaved by glutaminase to produce 

glutamate and free ammonia. 
3) Alanine is transported to the liver where it is converted to pyruvate by 

transamination. The pyruvate is used to synthesize glucose which can enter the 
blood and be used by muscles {glucose-alanine cycle). 

40 



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVQUS SYSTEM

Regulation of urea cycle: 
1- Short term regulation: 
e Short-term regulation of urea cycle occurs principally at carbamoyl phosphate 

synthetase-l. 

e N-acetylglutamate is allosteric activator of carbamoyl phosphate synthetase-l. 
e N-acetylglutamate is synthesized from acetyl CoA and glutamate by N- 

acetylglutamate synthase enzyme. 
e Long term regulation: 

Enzymes of the urea cycle are controlled at the gene level with long-term changes in 
the quantity of dietary protein. 

¢ Long-term increase in the quantity of dietary proteins leads to a significant increase 
in the concentration of the enzymes of urea cycle. 

e On return to a balanced diet, urea cycle enzymes return to normal levels. 

Hyperammonemia 
Ammonia is highly toxic to the central nervous system. Accumulation of ammonia in the 
body leads to nervous manifestations and may be fatal. 
Causes of hyperammonemia: 
1-Acquired hyperammonemia: 
Due to liver diseases e.g liver cell failure, viral hepatitis, liver cirrhosis or hepatotoxins. 
2-Congenital hyperammonemia: 
It is due to deficiency of 
- Any enzyme of urea cycle 
- Membrane-associated ornithine transporter 
- N-acetyl glutamate synthase. 
Table 1.1 :Comparison between carbamoyl phosphate synthetase | and ornithine 
transcarbamoylase deficiencies 

CarbamoylilPhosplmate Synthetase Ori thine Transcarbamoylase 

T [NH,*];: hyperammonemia T [NH *);: hyperammonemia

Cerebral edema Cerebral edema 

Lethargy, convulsions, coma, death Lethargy. convulsions, coma, death 

- The two conditions can be distinguished by an increase in orotic acid and uracil, 
which occurs in ornithine transcarbamoylase deficiency, but not in the deficiency 
of carbamoyl phosphate synthetase. 

- Orotic acid and uracil are intermediates in pyrimidine synthesis. This pathway is stimulated 
by the accumulation of carbamoyl phosphate, the substrate for ornithine transcarbamoylase 
in the urea cycle and for aspartate transcarbamoylase in pyrimidine synthesis 

43 



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

Symptoms of ammonia intoxication: 
1. Mental retardation (hereditary type) 
2. Tremors 
3. Slurred speech 
4. Drowsiness 
5. Vomiting 
6. Cerebral edema 
7. Blurorf ivinsigon 
8. Coma 
9. Death 

Treatment: 
1) Restriction of dietary protein 
2) Increase carbohydrate diet to avoid proteolysis of muscle proteins 
3) Decrease nitrogenous load from GIT: 
- Antibiotics (e.g neomycin) to kill intestinal bacteria 

- Enema to eliminate intestinal bacteria 
- Lactulose: 
a. production of lactic acid (gut acidification) leading to increasing urease- deficient bacteria 

thus decreasing ammonia production and increasing the pH gradient which propels NH3 from 

blood to the lumen of the colon, while NH3 of the colon is converted into non-absorbable NH4’ 

ion 
b. A cathartic through increasing osmotic pressure thus reducing colonic bacterial load 

4) Phenylacetate: 
Phenylbutyrate is given orally and is converted to phenylacetate which condenses with ) 

glutamine 
5) L-ornithine-L-aspartate (LOLA, Hepa-Merz): 
Hepa-Merz is a combination of the amino acids ornithine and aspartate 

L-ornithine stimulates the urea cycle, with resulting loss of ammonia. 

Both L-ornithine and L-aspartate are substrates for glutamate transaminase. 

Resulting in increasing glutamate levels. 
Ammonia is subsequently utilized in the conversion of glutamate to glutamine by glutamine 

synthetase.

Phenylketonuria is a metabolic disorder caused by either a deficiency of the enzyme 
phenylalanine hydroxylase or the enzymes that synthesize or regenerate the coenzyme 
tetrahydrobiopterin. | 
Biochemically, it is characterized by accumulation of phenylalanine and deficiency of tyrosine. 
BH4 is also required by tyrosine hydroxylase and tryptophan hydroxylase catalyzing reactions 
leading to synthesis of catecholamines and serotonin respectively. 

Characteristics: 

1- Mental retardation: due to deficiency of brain catecholamines and accumulation of toxic 
products in tissues. These toxic products (phenylpyruvate, phenyllactate and phenylacetataer)e 
derived from abnormal metabolism of phenylalanine.

2- Depigmentation due to decreased melanin synthesis. 
3- Bad (mousey) odor of urine. 
Diagnosis: 
1- NBS by tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) on dried blood spot that was 
withdrawn from the heel of the newborn to determine the concentration of 
phenylalanine and the ratio of phenylalanine to tyrosine (the ratio will be elevated in 
PKU) 
2- Measurement of phenylalanine blood level by ELISA

Treatment: 
The earlier treatment is started, the more completely neurologic damage can be 

prevented. The goal of treatment is to keep plasma phenylalanine levels within the normal 

range through diet. 
a- Phenvylalanine-low diet 
b- Nutritional supplements: formulas containing tyrosine 

c- Enzyme substitute: metabolizes phenylalanine 
d- Tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4): oral supplementation

Maple syrup urine disease (MSUD) results from partial or complete deficiency of the 

branched chain a-ketoacid dehydrogenase complex (BCKD) which catalyzes the 

oxidative decarboxylation step. 

Branched-chain Amino Acids Catabolism

Figure (1.45): Catabolism of branched chain amino acids 

The branched chain amino acids and their corresponding a-keto acids accumulate in blood 

causing a toxic effect that interferes with brain functions. 

Diagnosis: 
1. Newborn screening (NBS): 

a. Newborn screening (NBS) for MSUD is performed by tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS), on 

dried blood spot that was withdrawn from the heel of the newborn, using concentrations of 

leucine and isoleucine and the Fisher ratio (branched chain amino acids/phenylalanine and 

tyrosine) as diagnostic measures. 
b. Itis considered as part of NBS Program especially for babies at high risk and in areas where 

consanguineous marriage is common. 

2. Blood tests: Plasma amino acids testing should be performed to assess for elevated levels of 

branched chain amino acids and to detect L-alloisoleucine (derived from L-isoleucine) 

3, Genetic testing (prenatal diagnosis, confirmation of diagnosis, and genetic counselling of the parents) 

Characteristics: 

1. Neurologic problems that can result in coma (primarily due to the rise in leucine) 

2. Ketoacidosis 

3. Urine has a maple syrup- like odor due torise in isoleucine 

4. If treatment is delayed, intellectual disability results

Treatment: 
1. Life-long daily dietary management by restriction of dietary valine, leucine, and 

isoleucine 
2. Treatment of episodes of acute metabolic decompensation 
3. Some MSUD patients are responsive to thiamine treatment (Thiamine-responsive 

MSUD).

(1) Thiamin (Vitamin B1) 

Sources: 
1) Plant sources: yeast, bran, green vegetables, whole (unrefined) grains, nuts, legumes. 
2) Animal sources: organ meat (liver, heart, kidney), eggs, milk. 

Function of thiamine: 
It forms the coenzyme thiamine pyrophosphate (TPP) by an ATP-dependent enzyme 
present in liver and brain. 
ERR Ta 

Thismise (vitamin B,) Thiamine pyrophosphate (TPP) 
Figure (1.46): Formation of thiamine pyrophosphate from thiamine 

Reactions requiring TPP as a coenzyme: 
a. Oxidative decarboxylation of a-ketoacids, e.g. Pyruvate to Acetyl CoA and a- 

ketoglutarate to Succinyl CoA. 
b. Transketolase reaction, in pentose phosphate pathway (PPP) pathway. 
c. Decarboxylation of pyruvic acid in yeast (Pyruvate to Acetaldehyde). 

Thiamin deficiency: 
A clinical syndrome that arises insidiously as a result of a severe, prolonged deficiency of 
thiamine in the diet. The deficiency decreases activity of pyruvate and ketoglutarate 
dehydrogenases, resulting in decreased production of ATP and, thus, impaired cellular 

function particularly in tissues of the nervous system. 
1) Beriberi: is found in areas where polished rice is the major component of the diet. 
2) Wernicke-Korsakoff syndromes an acute deficiency of vitamin B1 seen mainly 

in alcoholics. 

48 



CHAPTER I: OVERVIEW OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 

Biochemical detection of thiamine deficiency: The diagnosis of beriberi is assisted by a 
dietary history suggestive of a low thiamine intake and clinical manifestations. 
Objective biochemical tests of thiamine status are: 

1) Blood thiamine: Blood contains only about 0.8% of the total body thiamine, and the 
concentrations too low to allow precise extrapolation of the total thiamine status 
2) Measurement of erythrocyte transketolase activity (ETKA) 
3) The thiamine pyrophosphate effect (TPPE) 
4) Urinary thiamine levels 
5) Estimation of blood pyruvate and lactate levels 

Pyruvate dehydrogenase (PDH) deficiency: 

e Pyruvate dehydrogenase complex (PDC) converts pyruvate to acetyl-coenzyme A (acetyl 
CoA), which is one of the two essential substrates needed to produce citrate 

Glycolysis 

Alanine €— Pyruvate ¢———P Lactate

— Acetloacetate 

Oxaloacetate

Citric Acid Cycle 

Figure (1.47): PDH deficiency [The dashed line indicates the blocked pathway]. 

e Because pyruvate does not proceed to acetyl-coenzyme A (CoA), it is shunted to other 
pathways that produce lactic acid and alanine. 

® A deficiency in this enzymatic complex limits the production of citrate. 
e Because citrate is the first substrate in the citric acid cycle, the cycle cannot proceed. 
e Alternate metabolic pathways are stimulated in an attempt to produce acetyl-CoA; 

however, an energy deficit remains, especially in the CNS. 
Clinical picture: 

a. Lactic acidosis. 
b. The neurological form of pyruvate dehydrogenase deficiency contributes to hypotonia, 

poor feeding, lethargy and structural abnormalities in the brain. Patients may develop 
seizures and/or neuropathological spasms. These presentations of the disease usually 
progress to mental retardation, microcephaly, blindness and spasticity. 

Treatment: No proven treatment for PDH complex deficiency, however: 
1) Dietary restriction of carbohydrate may reduce symptoms. 
2} Correction of acidosis.

Importance of niacin: 

¢ Nicotinamide is responsible for the synthesis of two coenzymes: Nicotinamide adenine 
dinucleotide (NAD*) and Nicotinamide Adenine Dinucleotide phosphate (NADP+) 

e NAD*&NADP* functions as coenzymes for numerous dehydrogenases in oxidation- 
reduction reactions. They act as one electron and one hydrogen atom carriers. 

e Nicotinic acid is used therapeutically for lowering plasma cholesterol and TAGs levels 
(niacin strongly inhibits lipolysis in adipose tissue leading to decrease in circulating FFAs). 

Deficiency of Niacin Results in Pellagra 
Pellagra is a disease involving the skin, gastrointestinal tract, and CNS. The symptoms of 
pellagra progress through the three Ds: 

1) Dermatitis: inflamed, red and rough skin. 
2) Diarrhea, with other GIT manifestations. 
3) Dementia, demyelization and CNS symptoms. 

If untreated, Pellagra goes to death. 
Causes: 

1. A diet poor in both tryptophan and niacin e.g. corn (maize). 
2. Defective absorption: Hartnup’s disease in which tryptophan absorption is impaired. 
3. Pyridoxal-phosphate (Vitamin B6) deficiency: as vitamin B6 is needed for niacin synthesis 

from tryptophan. 
4. Carcinoid tumor (60% of tryptophan is converted into serotonin leading to decrease 

production of nicotinic acid. 

Treatment: 

1- Treatment of the cause. 
2- Nicotinic acid supplement. 

(3) FOLIC ACID (FOLACIN, VITAMIN B9) 
There are many reasons for studying folic acid and one carbon unit metabolism and also the 
intimate relation between folic acid and vitamin B12. Both vitamins have fundamental roles 
in CNS function at all ages, especially the methionine-synthase mediated conversion of 
homocysteine to methionine, which is essential for nucleotide synthesis. Also, they have roles 
in the prevention of disorders of CNS development, mood disorders, and dementias. 

Folic acid 

Folic acid or folate consists of the base pteridine attached to one molecule of each p- 
aminobenzoic acid (PABA), and glutamic acid.

Metabolic role; 
The biologically active form is the tetrahydrofolate. It is the carrier of activated one carbon 
units that are essential for the synthesis of choline, serine, glycine, methionine and purines.

* Formyl (oxidized form) 
* Metheny 

Methylene 
* Methyl (Most reduced form)

Methyl Trap: 
Deficiency of Vitamin B12 may lead to a so-called "methyl-trap” of THF, in which THF is 

converted to a reservoir of methyl-THF which thereafter has no way of being 
metabolized, and serves as a sink of THF that causes a subsequent deficiency in folate.

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Intended Learning Outcomes (ILOs): 

By the end of this chapter the student should be able to: 

Describe different types of sensory receptors and correlate to function of 
each receptor. 

Describe mechanisms of sensory adaptation. 
Identify gray matter nuclei and corresponding laminae in different spinal 
cord segments. 
Describe pain and crude touch pathways. 
Recognize different sensations carried by spinothalamic tract. 
Discuss the anterolateral system (beginning, course, lamination and 
termination). 
Describe the pathway of pain from the head. 
Compare between different types of pain. 
Clarify mechanisms of referred pain. 
Describe different mechanisms of pain modulation. 
Classify opioid drugs. 
Clarify mechanisms of action, adverse effects and interactions of opioid. 
Describe pathways of proprioception and fine touch of the body. 
Discuss the beginning, location, course, lamination and termination of the 
gracile and cuneate tracts. 
Discuss the beginning, location, course and termination of the 
spinocerebellar tracts, spino-olivary and cuneo-cerebellar tracts.

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Histological Structure of Receptors 

The nerve endings 

Nerve endings are the structures representing the site of contact between a non -nervous 
tissue and the terminal end of peripheral nerve fiber. They are classified into two main groups. 

. Receptors: These are present at the termination of the afferent (sensory) fibers. 
1 Effectors: These are present at the termination of efferent (motor) fibers. They transmit 
motor impulses to muscles or secretory impulses to glands. 

|. Receptors: (Fig. 2.1). 

Classification: 

Receptors can be classified into three main groups according to their distribution in 

body: exteroceptors, proprioceptors and interoceptors. 

thermo- Meissner's nociceptor pacinian 
receptor corpuscile senses pain corpuscle 
senses heat senses “touch” senses “pressure” 

or cold 

Fig.2.1: Different types of receptors 

1- Exteroceptors: 
Site: on the external body surface which receive stimuli from outside the body. 
They include: 

a) Cutaneous receptors that receive touch, pressure, pain and thermal 
sensations. 

b) Receptors for special sensation as smell, taste, sight and hearing. 
2- Proprioceptors: 

Site: They are present in: 

- Joints capsules, fascia and ligaments. 

- Skeletal muscles as muscle spindle. 

- Tendons as Golgi tendon organ. 

eo They transmit sense of movement and position (kinesthesis). 

e They receive stimuli from the deep parts of the body. 

60



CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

3-Interoceptors or Visceroceptors: 

Site: They are found in the internal viscera as the gastrointestinal tract, respiratory, 
cardiovascular, and urogenital systems. 

-They receive sensory impulses concerned with visceral pain, hunger, thirst, spasm, 
sexual feelings, bladder fullness and sense of malaise or wellbeing

eThe naked nerve fibers penetrate the epidermis and run between the epidermal cells

superficial layers. 

Function: They respond to noxious (painful), crude touch and thermal stimuli.

Function: They are mechanoreceptors. They discharge continuously during sustained 

vertical pressure e.g. holding a pen.

2- Pacinian corpuscles: (Fig.2.6) 

Sites: They are found in: 

¢ The dermis and hypodermis. 

e The pancreas, mesentery, breasts, external genitalia and wall of urinary bladder. 

e The periosteum, tendons and joint capsules 

Microscopic structure: 

e Pacinian corpuscles are large ovoid 

structures, 1-4 mm. X 0.5-1 mm. 

e The corpuscle is supplied by a 

myelinated afferent nerve fiber 

which loses its myelin sheath at the first node of Ranvier just inside the corpuscle. The 

afferent fiber runs axially to terminate by a club- 

like termination in the opposite pole of the Pig2/6:Pacinian sorpustle 

corpuscle. 

e The afferent fiber is surrounded by numerous concentric layers of flattened modified 

Schwann cells (Teloglia) forming the core of the corpuscle. 

The cells are arranged in two cores: 

a) An inner core: The cells are small, thin and closely packed together with very 

narrow intercellular spaces. They form semicircles around the afferent fibers. 

b) An outer core: The cells are large, thick and widely separated. They form complete 

concentric layers around the inner part of the core. 

e The number of the concentric lamellae or layers forming the core of the Pacinian 

corpuscles varies from few layers in small corpuscles to 70 layers in large ones. 

surrounded by connective tissue capsules. 

e Thereis a narrow subcapsular space containing amorphous ground substance, collagen 

fibers, fibroblasts and some macrophages. 

Function: They respond to deep pressure and vibration. They are mechanoreceptors. 

64 



CHAPTER Ii: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

3- Ruffini corpuscles: (Fig.2.7) 
Site: They are present in the dermis and subcutaneous tissues. They are more numerous in 

the planter surfaces of feet and palmer surfaces of hands. 
Microscopic structure: 

e [tis an elongated fusiform structure. Each is surrounded by a thin connective tissue 
capsule and has a core of bundles of collagenous fibers. 

* It receives a myelinated afferent nerve which loses its myelin sheath, and branches 
repeatedly to form diffuse arborizations which end by 
flattened terminals between the collagenous bundles of the 
central core. 

Function: 

They respond to tension in the collagenous fibers of the 
surrounding connective tissue. They are mechanoreceptors. 

4- Krause's end bulbs: (Mucocutaneous corpuscles Fig. 2.7: Ruffini corpuscle 

Site: They are present in the conjunctiva, tongue, mouth and external genitalia, where 
they are known as genital corpuscles. 

Microscopic structure: They are two types: 

1) Small corpuscles: receives a single non-myelinated afferent fiber. 
2) Large corpuscles: receives one or more myelinated afferent fibers which lose their 

myelin sheath and form a network of non-myelinated endings. 

Function: They are mechanoreceptors. 

Proprioceptors 
Types of receptors: 

1) Pacinian corpuscles. 

2) Muscle spindle. (stretch receptor). Discussed later 

3) Golgi tendon organ. (stretch receptor). Discussed later 
4) Unencapsulated receptors (diffused free nerve endings), which are found in joint 

capsules, ligaments and periosteum. 

65 



CHAPTER Il: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Joint receptors: 

a) Golgi tendon organs are found in internal and external ligaments. 

b) Diffuse free nerve endings, Ruffini corpuscles and Pacinian corpuscles are found in 

the capsule. 

¢) Pacinian corpuscles are located in the synovial membranes. 

These together with muscle spindles, play an important role in kinesthesia. 

Effectors (Fig. 2.8) 

These are termination of peripheral motor nerve fibers in the effector organs. They are 

classified into 

1- Somatic effectors that supply the skeletal muscles fibers (Motor end plate). 

2- Visceral effectors that supply cardiac muscle, smooth muscle and glands. They include: 

a) Motor fibers: 

These supply cardiac muscle and smooth muscle fibers 

b) Secretory fibers: 

Plexuses surrounding the acini of the glands or tubules give similar nerve terminals, 

which pass between the cells and end upon the plasma membrane of glandular cells. 

Autonomic nervous system 

Fig. 2.8: Effectors

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

SENSORY RECEPTORS AND SENSORY CODING 
The sensory receptors 

Definition 
They are specialized structures located at the peripheral ends of sensory (afferent) 
neurons, which respond to various stimuli and translate them into nerve. 

Functions of sensory receptors: 
1-Detectors: They detect the stimuli (changes in the environment). 
2-Transducers: They transform these stimuli into action potential, which leads to 
generation of nerve impulses in the sensory nerve. 
3-Generators: Generate action potentials in the afferent nerves and inform the CNS 
about the change in the external and internal environment. 

Properties of receptors: 
1- Specificity: 

oe Muller's Law: each type of receptors respond to detect a particular form of 
energy (adequate stimulus) at a much lower threshold than other receptors (i.e. 
retinal receptors respond to light). Receptors can also respond to other non specific 
stimuli but at much higher threshold (i.e. retinal receptors respond mechanically). 

2- Excitability: 

o The receptors have the ability to 
convert energy into action potential 
in the sensory nerves. 

e When the receptor is stimulated by 
an adequate stimulus, it generates a 
depolarizing potential called receptor 
or generator potential. The 
amplitude of the generator potential 
is related to the intensity of the 
stimulus, the higher the generator 
potential, an action potential is 
produced in the sensory nerve. Fig.2.9a: The receptor (generator) 

potential (Quoted from Fox) 

3- Discharge of impulse: 
o Weber Fechner Law: the frequency of discharge from receptors is directly 

proportional to the logarithm of intensity of applied stimuli (1000 fold increase in 
the intensity increase only 3 fold the frequency (compression function of 
receptors). 

4- Adaptation: 
e This is decline in the frequency of discharge of action potential from receptors 

that occurs on maintained stimulation by a constant strength. 

67

Coding of Sensory Information 

This is the ability of nervous tissue to discriminate the modality (type), locality and 

intensity of various sensations. 
_1-Modality discrimination: 

Each of the principal types of sensation that we can experience—pain, touch, 

sight, sound, and so forth —is called a modality of sensation. 

Muller's Doctrine Law: stimulation of a certain sensory pathway, no matter how or 

where (starting from receptors till the termination at higher centers) produces 

sensation for which receptors are specialized; labeled line principle. i.e. retinal 

stimulation both mechanically and by light resulting in light sensation. 

2-Locality discrimination: 

& Law of Projection: when the sensory pathway is stimulated anywhere along its 

course, the evoked sensation at the higher centers is projected to the location of its 

receptors (i.e. phantom limb). One explanation for phantom limbs is that the nerves 

remaining in the stump can grow into nodules called neuromas, and these may generate 

nerve impulses that are transmitted to the brain and interpreted as arising from the 

missing limb. 
Lateral inhibition: helps the CNS to localize exactly the location of a stimulus.

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

3-Intensity discrimination depends on: 
» Weber Fechner law, frequency of discharge from a receptor in afferent pathway is 

proportional to log intensity of the stimulus. 
es Number of activated receptors. Strong stimulus activates more receptors (i.e. 
recruitment). 
Duration of perception: depends on the ability of the receptor to adapt either rapidly 

or slow. 
Classification of sensory receptors: 

1) Classification by location (Discussed previously). 
2) Classification by type of stimulus detected (functional classification): 

« Mechanoreceptors: respond to physical force such as pressure (touch or blood 
pressure) and stretch. 

» Photoreceptors (electromagnetic): respond to light. 
e Thermoreceptors: respond to temperature changes. 
o Chemoreceptors: respond to dissolved chemicals such as taste and smell and 

respiratory chemoreceptors. 
» Nociceptors (pain receptors): respond to different stimuli associated with tissue 

damage. 
3) Classification according to adaptation of receptors: 

Rapidly adapting (phasic) receptors: rapidly decrease discharge of impulses after 
application of a stimulus, such as touch receptors. 

» Moderately adapting receptors: such as thermoreceptors. 
Slowly adapting (tonic) receptors: slowly or never decrease discharge of impulses 
after application of a stimulus, such as pain receptors.

CHAPTER Ii: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

SPINAL CORD INTERNAL STRUCTURE 
GRAY MATTER 

e The spinal cord has two enlargements; cervical and lumbar, which are associated with the 

large nerve roots innervating the extremities. 

e Spinal nerves (Fig. 2.11): There are 31 pairs of spinal nerves. On this basis, the spinal cord 

is divided into 8 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral and one coccygeal segment. These 

spinal nerves are attached to the spinal cord by two roots: 

1) Dorsal (posterior) root: it is purely sensory (afferent) fiber. 
2) Ventral (anterior) root: it is purely motor (efferent) fiber. 

Arrangement of dorsal fibers entering the spinal cord: 

e The nerve cells in the dorsal root ganglion are pseudounipolar nerve cells of variable size. 

They have two processes: 
a) Peripheral processes (dendrites), which are connected to receptors. 

b) Central processes (axons), which pass to approach the dorsal surface of the spinal cord. 
The nerve fibers form medial and lateral bundles. 

As the root fibers enter the spinal cord, they bifurcate into: 
a} Long ascending fibers that form the long tracts. 

b) Short descending fibers, which form short intersegmental tracts. 

Dorsal horn— Afferent neurone, cell body ——— 

Lateral horn— White matter 

Dorsal root of spinal nerve — Central canal — Dorsal root ganglion

—— Spinal nerve

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

General topography of the spinal cord: 
The spinal cord is formed of two symmetrical halves which are separated posteriorly by the 
posterior median septum. They are separated anteriorly by the anterior median fissure. The 
two halves are connected in the central part by the gray and white commissures. The outer 
surface of the spinal cord shows fissures and sulci (Fig. 2.12).

Fig. 2.12: Topography of the spinal cord 

e Transverse section of the spinal cord shows that it is formed of an inner core of gray matter 
surrounded by an outer covering of white matter. 

The gray matter (Fig. 2.13) 
The gray matter is an H-shaped structure, it consists of: 

1) Two posterior horns extending almost to the surface. 

2) Two anterior horns extending anteriorly but they do not reach the surface. 
3) Two lateral horns, near the base of the anterior horn. They are found in thoracic and 

upper lumbar segments only. 
4) Gray commissure contains the central canal which is lined by ependymal cells. 

Histological structure of the gray matter (Fig. 2.13): 
e The gray matter of the spinal cord is formed of: 

1) The nerve cells and their processes, neuroglia and blood capillaries. The nerve cells 
are multipolar and of various sizes and shapes. 

2) The nerve fibers (portions of axons) in the gray matter are mostly unmyelinated. 
* The nerve cells in the posterior horn and intermediate gray are of two types: 

1) Golgi type I: includes the celis with long axons that form long ascending tracts. 
2) Golgi type lI: these are cells with short axons that terminate in the gray matter near 

their origin. 

e The nerve cells in the gray matter are arranged in groups (Nuclei). 

71 



CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Nuclei of Posterior Horn {Sensory nuclei): 

1) Posteromarginal nucleus: 

This nucleus forms a thin layer of large cells covering the tip of the posterior horn. 

2) Substantia Gelatinosa: 

This nucleus forms a cap-like portion of the apex of the posterior horn. 

3) The main sensory nucleus (Nucleus proprius): 

The nucleus is poorly defined and situated anterior to the substantia gelatinosa (in the 

head of the posterior horn). 
4) Reticular nucleus: 

The nucleus is present {ateral to the nucleus proprius. 
5) Dorsal nucleus of Clarke: 

This column is present in the medial part of the base of the posterior horn. 

6) Intermedio-medial nucleus: 

It is present in the medial part of the intermediate gray, lateral to the central canal. It 

serves as intermediate relay in the transmission of visceral afferent impulses to the 

visceral efferent neurons. 
7) Posterior and anterior commissural nuclei: 

These two nuclei are found in the posterior and anterior gray commissures respectively. 

Their axons form intersegmental tracts in the posterior and anterior white columns. 

N.B.: All sensory nuclei of the posterior horn are found in all levels of spinal cord, except 

Clarke’s nucleus which extends from the 8th cervical segment to the 3rd lumbar segment 

(C8-L3). 
Nerve cell groups in the anterior horn {Motor nuclei): 

e Cells of the anterior horn are somatic efferent neurons, and their axons pass in the 

anterior roots of the spinal nerves to innervate skeletal muscles. 

e They are classified into two types: 

1) Large alpha neurons: they are numerous. Their axons innervate the extrafusal 

skeletal muscle fibers. 

2) Small gamma neurons: Their axons innervate the contractile parts of intrafusal 

muscle fibers of muscle spindles. 

e The nerve cells of the anterior horn are classified into 3 groups, the medial, the central 

and the lateral nuclear groups. 

I- The medial nuclear group: This group is subdivided into: 

a) Antro-medial nucleus, that extends in all levels of the spinal cord. 

b) Postero-medial nucleus, which is smaller. It is more prominent in the cervical and 

lumbar segments. 

These two nuclei of the medial nuclear group innervate the skeletal muscles of the 

neck and back (muscles attached to axial skeleton). 

72 



CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Il- The central nuclear group: It is present in the cervical and lumbar segments. In 
the cervical segments they include: 
- Phrenic nucleus which innervates the diaphragm. 
- Accessory nucleus which forms the spinal part of the accessory cranial nerve. 

Ii- The lateral nuclear group: 
In the cervical and lumbar segments this group is formed of 3 groups of 

a) Antro-lateral nucleus. 

b) Postero-lateral nucleus. 
c) Retropostero-lateral nucleus. 

* In general, the most medial muscles are supplied by the most medial nuclei, while the 
most distal muscles are supplied by the most lateral nuclei. 

* The hand is supplied by retropostero-lateral column of cervical segments and the foot by 
the same column of lumbar segments. 

» Flexor muscles are innervated by nerve cells in the dorsal region of the cell columns and 
extensor muscles by nerve cells in their ventral region. 

* In thoracic segments, this group forms a small single column innervating the intercostals 
and trunk muscles. 

Nerve cell groups in the lateral horn: 

The intermedio-lateral nucleus: 
This nuclear column extends from the first thoracic segment to the second or third lumbar 
segments. The cells give rise to preganglionic sympathetic fibers. 

N.B.: The sacral autonomic nuclei: 
A similar group of cells is located along the lateral surface of the anterior gray horn in the 
second, third and fourth sacral segments. They give rise to preganglionic parasympathetic 
fibers. 

Cytoarchitectural lamination of the spinal cord: (Fig. 2.14) 

Examination of thick spinal cord sections 80-100um stained with cresyl violet revealed the 
cytoarchitectural organization of the neurons of gray matter into laminae. 
A Lamina is a region with similar characteristic properties as regards the size, shape, packing, 
density and cytological features of their neurons. 
Lamina I: It corresponds to the posteromarginal nucleus. 
Lamina Hl: It corresponds to substantia gelatinosa. 
Lamina Il and IV: They correspond to the main sensory nucleus. 
Lamina V: It extends across the neck of the posterior horn. It is divided into medial and lateral 
parts in all segments except the thoracic segments. Its lateral part corresponds to the reticular 
nucleus. 
Lamina VI: A broad lamina extending across the base of the posterior horn. It is divided into 
medial and lateral parts. 

73 



CHAPTER Il: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Lamina Vil: This lamina forms a large area. It includes Clarke’s nucleus, intermedio-medial 

and intermediolateral nuclei. 
Lamina VIII: It includes the medial part of the anterior horn cells. It receives termination of 
extrapyramidal tracts. 
Lamina IX: It corresponds to the nuclear groups of anterior horn. 
Lamina X: It represents the central gray substance surrounding the central canal.

CHAPTER Il: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

ASCENDING TRACTS 

There are two sensory pathways in the body: 
1. The Exteroceptive pathway (anterolateral system): occupies the anterior and lateral 

columns of the spinal cord. Carries superficial (cutaneous) sensations i.e, pain, 
temperature & crude touch. 

2. The Proprioceptive pathway: includes the dorsal column of the spinal cord which carry 
conscious proprioception (deep sensations) & fine touch in addition to other tracts 
carrying unconscious proprioception. 

Generally, the spinothalamic and the dorsal column pathways are formed of three neurons. : 
1. First-order neuron is always a pseudounipolar cell in the Dorsal Root Ganglion. It carries 

sensation by its peripheral process from receptors & conveys this sensation by its central 
processes via the dorsal root to the spinal cord. 

2. Second-order neuron is always a cell in the CNS (spinal cord or medulla oblongata). Its 
axon always decussates to the opposite side and ascends in the brainstem as lemniscus 
to end in the thalamus. 

3. Third-order neuron is always a cell in the Ventral Postero-Lateral Nucleus of Thalamus 
(VPLN). Its axon passes through posterior limb of internal capsule, then through corona 
radiata to reach sensory area of cerebral cortex. 

THE ANTEROLATERAL SYSTEM 

The anterolateral system includes all long ascending tracts in the anterolateral columns of 
the spinal cord These tracts include the: 

1. Lateral spinothalamic tract which carries pain & temperature 
2. Ventral spinothalamic tract which carries crude touch 
3. Spinotectal tract 
4. Spinoreticular tract 

Pathway for Pain & Temperature from the Body (Fig. 2.15) 

First Neuron: Dorsal root ganglion cells (pseudounipolar) 
Their peripheral processes carry pain & temperature sensations from the receptors (free 
nerve endings in skin) & are 2 types: 

1. Ab fibers carry fast pain 
2. C fibers carry slow pain 

Their central processes enter the spinal cord via the dorsal root and divide into ascending 
& descending branches for few segments. These fibers run in the dorsolateral (Lissauer’s) 
tract which lies over the apex of the dorsal horn. They end on neurons in the dorsal horn 
of grey matter of the spinal cord: 
e Fast pain ends mainly on Lamina | (beside other laminae) 
e Slow pain ends on Substantia Gelatinosa of Rolandi (SGR)

SENSATIONS CARRIED BY SPINOTHALAMIC TRACT 

Sensation and perception are two separate processes that are very closely related. 
v' Sensation is information about the environment obtained by sensory receptors. 
v' Perception is the process by which the brain selects, organizes, and interprets these 

sensations.

= Sensory unit is defined as a single sensory axon and all its peripheral branches that 
vary in number. 

* The receptive field of a sensory unit is the spatial distribution (area) from which a 
stimulus produces a response in that unit. It differs from one area to another allover 
the body. 

" Generally, the areas supplied by one unit overlap and interdigitate with the areas 
supplied by others.

» A dermatome is an area of skin that sends information to the brain via a single spinal

Fig. 2.21: 
Dermatomes 

Sensation includes the following types: 
1) Somatic sensations: arising from skin and deeper structures e.g. skeletal muscles, 
tendons, ligaments, joints and periosteum. 

2) Visceral sensations: mediated by autonomic afferent fibers and include sensations from 

the viscera. 
3) Organic sensations e.g.: thirst and hunger. 
4) Special sensations e.g.: vision, hearing, taste and smell. 
5) Emotional sensations: as fear, sadness, pleasure etc.

The Spinothalamic pathway (anterolateral system (ALS): 
The ALS transmits nociceptive (pain), thermal, and nondiscriminatory (crude) touch 
information to higher brain centers by a sequence of three order neurons. 
The neuron sequence: Refer to anatomy lecture. 

Spinothalamic sensations: 
- Crude touch. 
- Thermal sensations. 
- It& tcickhle. 
- Pain 
- Sexual sensations. 

1) Crude touch sensation (light touch) 
Crude touch is a non-discriminatory, not sharply localized type of touch 
Definition: the ability of the body to detect contact with objects. 
Receptors: 
- Name: free nerve endings {F.N.E.) and hair end organs. 
- Type: mechanoreceptors. 
- Mechanism of stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors . 
Afferent neuron type: Ad and C types of fibers. 
Ascending sensory pathway: ventral spinothalamic pathway. 
Higher nervous perception site: thalamus. 

Adaptation: rapid adaptation. 

How to examine: cotton wool test. 

81 



CHAPTER Il: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

2) Thermal sensations 
Definition: the ability of the body to feel different degrees of hot and cold sensations. 
Receptors: 
- Name: F.N.E. for hot (warm) sensations, F.N.E. and Krause end bulb for cold 

sensation. 
- Type: Thermoreceptors. 
- Mechanism of stimulation: 

e Changes in the concentration of metabolites in thermoreceptors that activate or 
inactivate ion channels. 

e The excess change in temperature (above 43°C) activates membrane receptors 
that mediates pain sensation (thermal nociceptors). 

- Range of stimulation of thermoreceptors : 
v' Cold receptors: 10-43 °C, maximal at ~ 25 °C 
v’ Hot receptors: 30-50 °C, maximal at ~ 45 °C 
v’ Less than 15 °C: cold pain 
v' More than 45 °C: hot pain 
v' 30-35 °C: indifferent sensation (detected by both and it is near body 

temperature).

- Distribution of thermoreceptors: 

v' Thermoreceptors are found in spots, not in the form of separate receptors. 
v' Cold spots are more numerous than hot spots by ~ 3-10 times. 
v' Thermoreceptors are more numerous in lips (~20/ Cm?2), than hands (~5/ Cm?) 

than trunk (~2/ Cm?2). 
Afferent neuron type: C for warm sensations, Ab and C for cold sensation. 
Ascending sensory pathway: lateral spinothalamic tract. 
Higher nervous perception site: Thalamus and sensory cortex. 
The thalamus detects wide (crude) changes in temperature, while the cortex detects 
fine temperature changes. 

82 



CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Adaptation: moderate, but hot receptors are relatively rapidly adapting than cold 
receptors. 

How to examine: Two test tube test. 

Paradoxical cold sensation: 

A sensation of cold that occurs at the onset of exposure to hot temperature. e.g. a hot 
shower gives a sensation of coldness in the first second 
v' Explanations: Rise of temperature above 40°C leads to sudden and rapid discharge 

of impulses from cold receptors (may be to its damage). 
3) Itch & tickle 

Definition: 
v' Tickle: local repeated mechanical stimulation of cutaneous receptors by light 

moving objects. 
v" Itch: sensation of irritation caused by chemicals (histamine and kinins), that may 

lead to scratch reflex. 
Receptors: 
- Name: F.N.E. 
Afferent neuron type: C type of afferents. 
Ascending sensory pathway: ventral division of antero-lateral system 
Higher nervous perception site: Thalamus 

4) Pain Sensation 
Definition; 

Protective sensation caused by unpleasant sensation caused by noxious stimulation. It occurs 
whenever any tissues are being damaged, and it causes the individual to react to remove the pain 
stimulus . 
Pain Receptors and Their Stimulation 

Pain Receptors are Free Nerve Endings. The pain receptors in the skin and other tissues are 
all free nerve endings. Three Types of Stimuli Excite Pain Receptors—Mechanical, Thermal, and 
Chemical. Pain can be elicited by multiple types of stimuli. They are classified as mechanical, 
thermal, and chemical pain stimuli. 

Pain producing substances during Tissue Damage: Extracts from damaged tissue cause intense 
pain when injected beneath the normal skin. One chemical that seems to be more painful than 
others is bradykinin. Also, the intensity of the pain felt correlates with the local increase in 
potassium ion concentration or the increase in proteolytic enzymes that directly attack the nerve 
endings and excite pain by making the nerve membranes more permeable to ions. 

Tissue Ischemia as a Cause of Pain: When blood flow to a tissue is blocked, the tissue often 
becomes very painful within a few minutes. The greater the rate of metabolism of the tissue, the 
more rapidly the pain appears. One of the suggested causes of pain during ischemia is accumulation 
of large amounts of lactic acid in the tissues (anaerobic metabolism). It is also probable that other 
chemical agents, such as bradykinin and proteolytic enzymes, are formed in the tissues because of 
cell damage. Therefore, even such simple activities as sitting for a long time on the ischia can cause 
tissue destruction because of lack of blood flow to the skin where it is compressed by the weight of 
the body, and so the person normally shifts weight subconsciously. 

83 



CHAPTER Il: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Muscle Spasm as a Cause of Pain Muscle spasm is also a common cause of pain, this probably 
results partially from the direct effect of muscle spasm in stimulating mechanosensitive pain 
receptors, but it might also result from the indirect effect of muscle spasm to compress the 
blood vessels and cause ischemia. 
Characters of pain: 
e |[t initiates protective withdrawal mechanism (e.g., withdrawal reflexes). 
e Itis widely distributed (especially skin). 
¢ Its adequate stimulus is non specific (mechanical, thermal, chemical; provided causing tissue 
damage). 
e It requires high threshold. 
e Non adapting. 
e [tis perceived at both cortical & thalamic levels.
