Neospinothalamic Tract for Fast Pain (Fig. 2.23): 
v' The fast type AS pain fibers transmit mainly mechanical and acute thermal pain. They 

84 



CHAPTER li: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

terminate mainly in lamina | (lamina marginalis) of the dorsal horns, and there they 
excite second-order neurons of the neospinothalamic tract. 

v' These second-order neurons give rise to long fibers that cross immediately to the 
opposite side of the spinal cord through the anterior commissure and then turn 
upward, passing to the brain in the anterolateral columns. 

v' Afew fibers of the neospinothalamic tract terminate in the reticular areas of the brain 
stem, but most pass all the way to the thalamus. A few fibers also terminate in the 
posterior nuclear group of the thalamus. 

v' From these thalamic areas, the signals are transmitted to other areas of the brain, as 
well as to the somatosensory cortex. 

Paleospinothalamic Pathway for Transmitting Slow Pain (Fig. 2.19): 
The paleospinothalamic pathway transmits pain mainly from the type C pain fibers. 
v" In this pathway, the peripheral fibers terminate in the spinal cord almost in laminae 

It and Ili of the dorsal horns, which together are called the substantia gelatinosa. Most 
of the signals then pass through short fiber neurons within the dorsal horns 
themselves. 

v' Then, the last interneuron give rise to long axons that mostly join the fibers from the 
fast pain pathway, passing to the opposite side of the spinal cord, then upward to the 
brain in the anterolateral pathway. 

v' The paleospinothalamic pathway terminates widely in the brain stem. Only a small 
fraction of fibers passes all the way to the thalamus. Most of neurons terminate in 
one of three areas: 

(1) The reticular nuclei of the brain stem. 
(2) The tectal area of the midbrain. 
(3) The periaqueductal gray region surrounding the aqueduct of Sylvius. 

v" From the brain stem, multiple short-fiber neurons relay the pain signals upward into 
the intralaminar and ventrolateral nuclei of the thalamus and into certain areas of 
the hypothalamus and other brain areas.

it originates from muscles, joints, periosteum. 

It is slow pain.

It may be referred. 
Causes: 

» Trauma to deep structures. 
» Bone fracture. 
» Arthritis. 
>» Severe muscle spasm (tetany). 
» Muscle ischemia. 

e A certain muscle metabolite known as Lewis P factor (probably K+ ions) = pain. i.e. angina 
pectoris, intermittent claudication. 

Muscle Pain 
If a muscle contracts rhythmically in the presence of an adequate blood supply, pain does not | 

usually result. However, if the blood supply to a muscle is occluded, contraction soon causes | 

pain. The pain persists after the contraction until blood flow is reestablished. These | 

observations are interpreted in terms of the release during contraction of a chemical agent 

(Lewis’s “P factor”) that causes pain when its local concentration is high enough. When the 
| blood supply is restored, the material is washed out or metabolized. 
The identity of the P factor is not settled, but it could be K+. Clinically, the substernal pain that | 

develops when the myocardium becomes ischemic during exertion (angina pectoris). Angina is | 
relieved by rest because this decreases the myocardial 02 requirement and permits the blood | 
supply to remove the factor. | 

| Intermittent claudication, the pain produced in the leg muscles of persons with occlusive 

vascular disease, is another example. It characteristically comes on while the patient is walking 
and disappears on stopping. 

Visceral Pain 
e There are few pain receptors in viscera (i.e. cutting intestine with knife rarely causes significant 
pain). 
¢ Inflammed viscus => severe pain {1ry hyperalgesia). 
e Some viscera are pain insensitive: 
» Liver parenchyma. 
» Lung alveoli. 
» Visceral layer of peritoneum. 
» Visceral layer of pleura. 
» Visceral layer of pericardium. 

eo Characteristics: 
» Slow pain. 
» Diffuse (poorly localized), dull aching and may be spasmodic colic. 
» Often referred to skin, causes 2ry hyperalgesia. 
e Associated with sweating, nausea, vomiting accompanied by parasympathetic effects

(bradycardia, hypotension) = sickening pain. 
e Causes somatic reflexes (i.e. contraction of abdominal muscles = guarding rigidity). 

eo Causes: 
» Visceral ischemia (bradykinin release and proteolytic enzymes). 
» Mechanical (overdistension of a hollow viscus can collapse blood vessels causing ischemic 

pain, traction on mesentery, spasm of viscera). 
» Chemical (perforated peptic ulcer, the juice causes widespread digestion of visceral 

peritoneum).

Some Clinical Abnormalities of Pain : 
1-Hyperalgesia: Discussed before. 
2-Herpes Zoster (Shingles) 

Occasionally herpesvirus infects a dorsal root ganglion. This causes severe pain in the 

dermatomal segment subserved by the ganglion, thus eliciting a segmental type of pain that circles 

halfway around the body. The disease is called herpes zoster, or “shingles,” because of a skin 

eruption that often ensues. 
The cause of the pain is presumably infection of the pain neuronal cells in the dorsal root 

ganglion by the virus. In addition to causing pain, the virus causes a rash that vesiculates within a 

few days and then crusts over within another few days, all of this occurring within the dermatomal 

area served by the infected dorsal root. 
3-Tic Douloureux 
Lancinating pain occasionally occurs in some people over one side of the face in the sensory 

distribution area (or part of the area) of the fifth or ninth nerves; this phenomenon is called 

tic douloureux (or trigeminal neuralgia, or glossopharyngeal neuralgia). The pain feels like 

sudden electrical shocks, and it may appear for only a few seconds at a time or may be almost 

continuous. 

88 



CHAPTER il: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Often it is set off by exceedingly sensitive trigger areas on the surface of the face, in the 
mouth, or inside the throat—almost always by a mechanoreceptive stimulus rather than a 
pain stimulus. For instance, when the patient swallows a bolus of food, as the food touches a 
tonsil, it might set off a severe lancinating pain in the mandibular portion of the fifth nerve. 
The pain of tic douloureux can usually be blocked by surgically cutting the peripheral nerve 
from the hypersensitive area. This operation leaves the side of the face anesthetic, which in 
itself may be annoying. 

Fig. 2.25 a: Fig. 2.25 b: 
Shingles or Herpes Zoster Trigeminal neuralgia 

Pain and pain control system 2 
Referred (Radiating) Pain 

Definition 
Often a person feels pain in a part of the body that is fairly remote from the tissue causing the 

pain. This is called referred pain. For instance, pain in one of the visceral organs often is referred to 
an area on the body surface. 

Mechanism of Referred Pain Branches of visceral pain fibers are shown to synapse in the spinal cord 
on the same second-order neurons that receive pain signals from the skin. When the visceral pain 
fibers are stimulated, pain signals from the viscera are conducted through at least some of the same 
neurons that conduct pain signals from the skin, and the person has the feeling that the sensations 
originate in the skin itself. 
4% It is most frequent in visceral pain, and deep somatic pain. 
= [tis referred to an area of the skin that has same embryonic segment. 
+ e.g. 
1-Pain of cholecystitis -> referred to tip of right shoulder. 
2-Cardiac pain - referred to left shoulder and inner side of left arm. 
3-Pain of kidneys -> referred to testicular region. 
4-Gastric pain = referred to umbilical region. 
5-Teeth pain = referred to other teeth. 
6-Headache.

1- Convergence Projection theory 

e The afferent neurons from both somatic structure and viscera that develop from the same

e The brain is used to receive somatic stimuli only, so it is almost unaware of viscera. 

e Thus, upon stimulation of diseased viscus, the brain projects it to the corresponding 

somatic structure i.e., wrongly percieves pain as if it comes from this corresponding somatic 

structure. 

2- Convergence Facilitation theory 
e In substantia gelatinosa of Rolandi (SGR), the neurons receiving pain from skin area are 

facilitated by afferent from the viscous. So pain is felt in the skin due to activation of its pathway 

from SGR to the cortex.

Headache 
Headaches are a type of pain referred to the surface of the head from deep head 

structures. Some headaches result from pain stimuli arising inside the cranium, but others 
result from pain arising outside the cranium, such as from the nasal sinuses.

Migraine Headache. Migraine headache is a special type of headache that is | 

| thought to result from abnormal vascular phenomena, although the exact mechanism is 

Il unknown. Migraine headaches often begin with various prodromal sensations, such as nausea, | 

loss of vision in part of the field of vision, visual aura, and other types of sensory hallucinations. | 

One of the theories of the cause of migraine headaches is that prolonged emotion or tension | 

causes reflex vasospasm of some of the arteries of the head, including arteries that supply the | 

brain. The vasospasm theoretically produces ischemia of portions of the brain, and this is 

responsible for the prodromal symptoms. Then, as a result of the intense ischemia, something 

happens to the vascular walls, perhaps exhaustion of smooth muscle contraction, to allow the 

| blood vessels to become flaccid and incapable of maintaining vascular tone for 24 to 48 hours. 
The blood pressure in the vessels causes them to dilate and pulsate intensely, and it is 

postulated that the excessive stretching of the walls of the arteries—including some 

extracranial arteries, such as the temporal artery—causes the actual pain of migraine 

headaches. 
[EEE 

o CNS modulates pain, it may: 
- Exaggerating it (Pathological in thalamic Syndrome). 
- Inhibit it {physiological in analgesic system).

A-Physiological modulation 
1- The Gate Theory 
e The theory: various relay stations in the nervous pathway of pain act as gates open or close. 

e The most important gates are: 
a) Substantia gelatinosa of Rolandi (SGR, spinal gate). 
b) Thalamus (thalamic gate). 
c) Reticular formation (RF). 

e Inhibition of Pain Transmission by Simultaneous Tactile Sensory Signals 

» Stimulation of large type AB sensory fibers from peripheral tactile receptors can depress 
transmission of pain signals from the same body area. 

» This presumably results from local lateral inhibition in the spinal cord. It explains why such 
simple maneuvers as rubbing the skin near painful areas is often effective in relieving pain. 

It probably, also, explains why liniments are often useful for pain relief. 

e At SGR, pain transmission can be blocked by: 
> Collaterals from thick myelinated AB in dorsal column. 
» Descending fibers (Centrifugal or centripetal) through presynaptic inhibition (GABA or 

enkephalin). 
o AJ (counterirritant or acupuncture); relieves pain by: 

1) Spinal pathway of pain inhibition. 
2) Psychogenic excitation of central analgesia system.

Stress Analgesia: It is well known that soldiers wounded in the heat of battle often feel no 
tpain until the battle is over (stress-induced analgesia). Many people have learned from 
practical experience that touching or shaking an injured area decreases the pain due to the 
injury. Stimulation with an electric vibrator at the site of pain also gives some relief. 
1-Impulses from CC, hypothalamus to pain inhibitory complex in the spinal cord (PIC). 
2-B-endorphin from anterior pituitary gland to blood to opiate receptors. 
Acupuncture: A component of stress induced analgesia appears to be mediated by | 
endogenous opioids. It occurs at a location distant from the site of a pain and appears to act | 
primarily in the same way as touching or shaking (gate-control mechanism). ~~ | 

2- The Analgesia System 
The analgesia system consists of three major components: (1) The periaqueductal gray 

and periventricular areas of the mesencephalon and upper pons surround the aqueduct of 
Sylvius and portions of the third and fourth ventricles. Neurons from these areas send signals 
to (2) the raphe magnus nucleus, a thin midline nucleus located in the lower pons and upper 
medulla, and the nucleus reticularis paragigantocellularis, located laterally in the medulla. 
From these nuclei, second-order signals are transmitted down the dorsolateral columns in the 
spinal cord to (3) a pain inhibitory complex (PIC) located in the dorsal horns of the spinal cord. 
At this point, the analgesia signals can block the pain before it is relayed to the brain. 

Electrical stimulation either in the periaqueductal gray area or in the raphe magnus 
nucleus can suppress many strong pain signals entering by way of the dorsal spinal roots. Also, 
stimulation of areas at still higher levels of the brain that excite the periaqueductal gray area 
can also suppress pain. Some of these areas are (1) the periventricular nuclei in the 
hypothalamus, lying adjacent to the third ventricle, and (2) to a lesser extent, the medial 
forebrain bundle, also in the hypothalamus. Several transmitter substances are involved in 
the analgesia system; especially involved are enkephalin and serotonin. 

Many nerve fibers derived from the periventricular nuclei and from the 
periaqueductal gray area secrete enkephalin at their endings. 

Fibers originating in this area send signals to the dorsal horns of the spinal cord to 

93 

—] 



CHAPTER Hi: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

secrete serotonin at their endings. The serotonin causes local cord neurons to secrete 

enkephalin as well. The enkephalin is believed to cause both presynaptic and postsynaptic 

inhibition ofi ncoming type C and type Ab pain ffii bers where they synapse in the dorsal horns. 

Fig. 2.27 b: The Central — 
analgesic system 

3- The Brain Opiate System 
They are morphine like substances that are naturally present in the body. Morphine is 

an extract of opium which produces potent analgesic effect when it binds with opiate 

receptors. About twelve opiate like substances are found in the different points of the nervous 
system. The most important forms are: 

Enkephalins: 2 types (met ;methionine, leu; leucine), present in the brain stem and spinal cord. 

Endorphins: B-endorphin, more stronger than enkephalin, Present in hypothalamus, anterior 

pituitary gland. 
Dynorphins: More stronger than enkephalin, present in the areas as enkephalin but in lower 

quantities. 
B-Surgical Interruption of Pain Pathways. 

When a person has severe and intractable pain (sometimes resulting from rapidly spreading 

cancer), it is necessary to relieve the pain. 1-Anterolateral cordotomy: cut LSTT. 2-Gyrectomy in 

frontal lobe: to prevent unpleasant emotional effect of pain. 3-Laminectomy: cutting the posterior 

root. 
‘ C-Electrical treatment 

Pain can be relieved within 24 hours by electrical stimulation of Large sensory fibers in spinal cord 

or in skin, Analgesic system (PAG, PVA), Non specific thalamic nuclei. 

D-Medical treatment 
Medications to control pain (Pharmacological), as antacids in peptic ulcer, blood vessels dilator in 
ischemic pain, Anesthetic agents, Analgesic agents.

Four types of sever pain in Which morphine is Contraindicated or Not 
Recommended. 

1. Acute Undiagnosed Abdominal pain 
Because morphine is a strong analgesic and therefore 
it will mask the cause of pain which may lead to 
serious Or even fatal consequences e.g. pain of 
Appendicitis, intestinal obstruction 
2. Normal Labor Pain 
-Because morphine induce uterine relaxation and may 
delay normal Labor 
-Also, morphine can cross the placenta and induce

- Weak Mu agonist (only partially antagonized by naloxone). 

- Advantages: Less constipation, respiratory depression & addiction than morphine. 

- Disadvantages: 1 Risk of convulsions more than morphine. 

Uses (oral, IM, IV) 
- Analgesic in postoperative & chronic moderate pain & in neuropathic pain. 

6. Methadone [Oral] 

Uses 
1. Treatment of opioid addicts (detoxification mainly): 

» Orally-active & long acting, thus, it is used to replace morphine or heroin in addicts. 

Gradual withdrawal of methadone is associated with less severe & smoother 

withdrawal symptoms. 
2. Analgesic in severe chronic pain (efficacy equal to morphine). 

N.B.: Methadone may prolong QT interval = 4 risk of torsades de pointes. 

II. Partial Agonists 

Buprenorphine (partial Mu receptor agonist) 
Advantages over Pure Agonists 

1. Less addiction (less euphoria — less craving). 
2. Less Respiratory depression. 

Uses (parenteral, sublingual) 
1. Analgesic in severe pain. 

2. Alternative to methadone for treatment of opioid addict detoxification (long-acting, 

slowly dissociates from receptors); leading to smooth withdrawal. 
Drug Interactions of Opioids 

1. Opioids + other CNS depressants (sedatives, alcohol, antidepressants & antipsychotics) 

-> additive CNS depression.

DORSAL COLUMN TRACTS (Fig. 2.33) 

e Function: carries proprioception (deep sensations) 
Gracile tract from the lower part of body 
(below T6). 
Cuneate tract from the upper part of body 
(above 76). 

e Beginning: They are formed by the central processes of the pseudounipolar dorsal root 
ganglion cells. They enter the spinal cord via the dorsal root. 

e Position in spinal cord: they occupy the posterior white column. The Gracile tract lies 
medially in all segments & the Cuneate tract lies lateral to it, above T6. 

e Lamination: in the dorsal (posterior) column the coccygeal & sacral fibers lie most medially 
while cervical fibers lie most laterally. 

e Termination: The Gracile & Cuneate tracts ascend in the spinal cord to reach the medulla 
& end on Gracile & Cuneate Nuclei of the medulla. During their ascent, the fibers send 
collateral branches that end on dorsal horn of spinal cord. 

Sensations carried by dorsal column tracts: 
1. Fine touch 

Proprioception 
Vibration 
Deep pressure 
Stereognosis 

Fig. 2.33: gracile & cuneate tracts

CHAPTER ll: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Pathways of Conscious Proprioception & Fine Touch (Fig. 2.34) 

First Neuron: Dorsal Root Ganglion cells which are pseudounipolar. Their peripheral 

processes carry sensations from deep receptors (in muscles, tendons & joints). Their central 

processes pass to the spinal cord via the dorsal root. Fibers from the lower part of the body 

(below T6) ascend medially in the dorsal column forming the gracile tract. Fibers from the 

upper part of the body (above T6) ascend laterally in the dorsal column forming the cuneate 

tract. Lamination: sacral fibers are most medial & cervical fibers are most lateral. 

- During their ascent, the fibers send 
collateral branches that end on the dorsal 
horn of spinal cord. These form the septo- 
marginal tract (from Gracile) & Comma- 
shaped tract (from cuneate). 

- Some cervical fibers end on the accessory 
cuneate nucleus (posterior to the cuneate 
nucleus), its axons (cuneo-cerebellar 
fibers) pass to the cerebellum. 

Second Neuron: Gracile & Cuneate Nuclei of 
the medulla oblongata. Axons of these nuclei 
cross the median plane (forming the internal 
arcuate fibers (sensory decussation). Fibers 
ascend in brain stem as the medial lemniscus 
to reach the thalamus 

Third Neuron: Ventral posterolateral Nucleus 
of thalamus (VPLN). Axons of these cells pass 
through posterior limb of internal capsule, 
then through corona radiata to reach sensory 
area of cerebral cortex. 

Fig. 2.34: Dorsal column Pathway 

104 



CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Tracts of Unconscious Proprioception 

Four tracts carry unconscious proprioception to 
the cerebellum (For coordination of movement) 

1. Posterior spinocerebellar tract: (Fig. 2.35) 
Carries proprioception from lower limb & trunk. 
The central processes of DRG cells enter the spinal 
cord via the dorsal root to end on ipsilateral 
Clarke’s nucleus. 
The tract ascends ipsilaterally in the lateral white 
column, posterior to the anterior spinocerebellar 
tract & enters the ipsilateral cerebellum via the 
inferior cerebellar peduncle (ICP). 

Anterior spinocerebellar tract: (Fig. 2.36) 
Carries proprioception from the lower limb. 
The central processes enter the spinal cord via the 
dorsal root to end on laminae V-ViIl. 
Axons forming the tract mostly decussate, but few 
remain ipsilateral. Fig. 2.36: ant. sp. cerebellar T. 
They enter the cerebellum via the superior 
cerebellar peduncle (SCP) then cross again to reach 
the ipsilateral cerebellum. 

3. Spino-olivary Tract: (Fig. 2.37) 
It carries proprioception from both upper & lower 
limbs. 
Its second order neuron fibers cross & ascend at 
the junction of lateral & ventral white columns to 
end on the contralateral olivary nuclei. 
Olivocerebellar fibers cross & pass via the ICP to 
reach the ipsilateral cerebellum. 

4. Collaterals from cuneate tract: (Fig. 2.38) carry 
proprioception from the upper limb to the accessory 
cuneate nucleus of the medulla. Axons of the 
accessory cuneate nucleus form the external arcuate 
fibers (Cuneocerebellar tract) which reach the 
ipsilateral cerebellum via the ICP. 

Fig. 2.38: cuneo-cerebellar T.

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

SENSATIONS CARRIED BY DORSAL COLUMN TRACTS 
The dorsal column pathway (medial lemniscus) 
The dorsal column pathway carries signals from mechanoreceptors of the skin, joints and 

muscle. It transmits discriminatory (fine) touch, proprioception, vibration, deep pressure and 

stereognosis information to higher brain centers by a sequence of three neurons. 

The neuron sequence: Refer to anatomy lecture. 

Dorsal column sensations: 
1- Fine touch 

‘ 2 Proprioception 
3 Vibration 
4 Deep pressure 
5 Stereognosis. 

1- Fine touch sensation (Discriminatory touch) 

Fine touch is a discriminatory, sharply localized type of touch. It includes 2 types: 
A. Tactile localization. 
B. Tactile (two- point) discrimination. 

A, Tactile localization 
Definition: the ability of the body to detect exactly a point of contact with objects, with 

closed eyes. 
Receptors: 

- Name: Meissner’s corpuscles, Merckle’s discs, Pacinian corpuscles and Ruffini end 

organ. 
- Type: mechanoreceptors. 

- Mechanism of stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors that 
leads to their stimulation. 

Afferent neuron type: AB type of fibers. 
Ascending sensory pathway: dorsal column pathway. 
Higher nervous perception site: somatosensory cortex. 
Adaptation: rapid adaptation 
How to test: Two marker test 

. Tactile discrimination 
Definition: the ability of the body to discriminate between two points of contact, 

touched at the same time, with closed eyes. 
Receptors: 
- Name: Meissner’s corpuscles, Merckle’s discs, Paccinian corpuscles and Ruffini end 

organ. 
- Type: mechanoreceptors. 

- Mechanism of stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors that 

leads to their stimulation. 
Afferent neuron type: AB type of fibers. 
Ascending sensory pathway: dorsal column pathway. 

106 



CHAPTER Ii: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

Higher nervous perception site: somatosensory cortex. 
Adaptation: rapid adaptation 
How to test: Weber compass test 
Threshold distance: 
v' It is the minimal distance between 2 points to be felt as 2 separate points. 
v’ Threshold distance is variable according to body location on body surface. It is about 

2-3 mm on the lips and fingertips, 3-6 mm on the dorsum of hands, and increases to 
over 40-60 mm on the back. 

v' If the distance between 2 points is less than the minimum distance, only 1 point will 
be felt. 

v' Compared to areas with high threshold distance (back), areas of the body with low 
threshold distance (fingertips) are characterized by: 
- Large number of receptors. 
Large number of afferents. 
Less convergence between afferents. 
Small receptive field. 
Large area of representation in the sensory cortex. 
High degree of sensory acuity. 

Fig. 2.39: Compass test showing 
(a): threshold distance between 2 points. 

{b): threshold distance in different areas in the body 

107 



CHAPTER Hl: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

2- Proprioceptive sensations (Sense of position and movement) 

Proprioception includes the sense of position and the sense of movement. These sensations 

are important for maintaining balance and controlling body movements. [tis of 2 types: 

A. Sense of position. 
B. Sense of movement (passive movement by the doctor/ kinesthesia). 

A. Sense of position: 
Definition: It is the ability of the body to detect the position of different body parts, 
relative to each other's and relative to space. 
Receptors: 
- Name: Muscle spindle (in muscles), Golgi tendon organs (in tendons), and Ruffini end 

organ (in joint capsules). 
- Type: mechanoreceptors. 
- Mechanism of stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors that 

leads to their stimulation. 
Afferent neuron type: AB type of fibers. 

Ascending sensory pathway: Dorsal column pathway. 

Higher nervous perception site: somatosensory cortex. 
Adaptation: slow adaptation (tonic receptors) 

B. Sense of movement (kinesthesia) 
Definition: It is the ability of the body to detect the direction and the rate of movement 

of the different body parts, relative to each other’s and relative to space. 
Receptors: 
- Name: Muscle spindle (in muscles), Golgi tendon organs (in tendons), Ruffini end 

organ (in joint capsules), and Pacinian corpuscles (in tendons and ligaments). 

- Type: mechanoreceptors. 
- Mechanism of stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors that 

leads to their stimulation. 
Afferent neuron type: AB type of fibers. 
Ascending sensory pathway: Dorsal column pathway. 
Higher nervous perception site: Somatosensory cortex. 
Adaptation: slow adaptation (tonic receptors) 

v' Pacinian corpuscles are rapidly adapting receptors, but these receptors are 

responsible only for the initial transient signals about the rate of joint movement. 

v' The impulses from a single receptor cover a limited range ofj oint angles. 

v' The impulses from all the receptors from a given joint cover the entire range of limb 

movement. 

N.B.: 
Proprioception is a conscious sensation, transmitted by the dorsal column, and perceived at 

the level of the sensory cortex. However, for maintaining balance, unconscious proprioceptive 

information is transmitted through the spinocerebellar tracts to the cerebellum. 

108 



CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

3- Vibration sensations 

Definition: It is the ability of the body to feel a rhythmic, repetitive pressure sensation when 
a vibrating tuning fork is placed on bony prominences, while eyes are closed. 
Receptors: 

- Name: All tactile (touch and pressure) receptors are involved in detection of vibration, 
although different receptors detect different frequencies of vibration. The main 
receptors that detect vibration are: Pacinian corpuscles (detect vibrations from 30 to 800 
cycles/sec), and Meissner’s corpuscles (detect vibrations from 2 to 80 cycles/sec). 

- Type: mechanoreceptors. 
- Mechanism of stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors that leads 

to their stimulation. 
Afferent neuron type: AB type of fibers. 
Ascending sensory pathway: Dorsal column pathway. 
Higher nervous perception site: somatosensory cortex. 
Adaptation: Vibration sense receptors are rapidly adapting receptors. 

4- Deep pressure sensation 

Definition: It is the ability to detect approximate weight (heaviness) of objects without lifting 
them, while the eyes are closed. 
Receptors: 

- Name: Ruffini end organ and deep Pacinian corpuscles present in deeper layers of skin, 
fascia and muscles. 

- Type: mechanoreceptors. 
- Mechanisomf stimulation: mechanical displacement or deformity of receptors that leads 

to their stimulation. 
Afferent neuron type: AP type of fibers. 
Ascending sensory pathway: dorsal column pathway. 
Higher nervous perception site: somatosensory cortex. 
Adaptation: slow adaptation 
How to test: Test for pressure sense with arm supported. 

5- Stereognosis sensation 

Definition: The ability to recognize the size, shape, and texture of familiar objects by 
palpation, while eyes are closed. 
Requirements for stereognosis: 

- Stimulation of multiple sensory mechanoreceptors as well as muscle and joint receptors. 
- A higher level of cortical processing (cortical association areas). 

Interruption of inputs from receptors and from the thalamus may severely impair 
stereognosis, but the actual physiologic mechanism responsible for this highly educated 
cortical sensation is present in the somatosensory cortex. 
How to test: Test for stereognosis using familiar object. 

109 



CHAPTER Ii: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

SOMATOSENSORY CORTEX 

Somatosensory cortex is the part of the parietal lobe cortex, where sensory information is 

perceived and interpreted to provide a perception of the body and the environment. 

Site: in the parietal lobe, behind the central sulcus and above the lateral sulcus (postcentral). 

Components: it involves: Somatosensory areas | (S-l, primary somatosensory cortex, 

Brodmann’s areas: 3,1,2), Somatosensory areas ll, and sensory association areas (posterior 

parietal areas, Brodmann'’s areas: 5,7). 

Fig. 2.40: Somatosensory areas 

1- Somatosensory areas| {S-| 

Site: Refer to anatomy 
- It is the primary somatosensory cortex because it is the major site of termination of 

axons of the sensory nuclei of the thalamus. 

- Electrical stimulation of SI produces somatosensory perceptions. 

Body representation in S-1 (Fig. 2.37): 
v' Somatosensory area | has a high degree of localization of the different parts of the body. 

v' Representation of the body parts in 5-1 is: 
- Crossed: The body and face are mapped in the contralateral cortex. 

- Inverted: The face is represented in the lower half of the postcentral gyrus, while the 

foot, leg and the trunk in the upper half of the postcentral gyrus. 
- Disproportional to body area size but proportionate to number of receptors in body 

area: the lips and some other areas of the body are represented by large areas in the 

somatic cortex, whereas the trunk and lower part of the body are represented by 

relatively small areas. The sizes of these areas are directly proportional to the number 

of specialized sensory receptors in each area of the body.

\— Intra-abdominal al 

Fig. 2.41; Representation of different body parts in somatosensory area I 

- Neurons of the somatosensory cortex are arranged in vertical columns extending all 
the way through the six layers of the cortex. Each column detects a specific sensory 
modality. The most anterior columns respond to muscle, tendon, and joint stretch 
receptors. Posteriorly columns respond to slowly adapting cutaneous receptors. 
More posterior columns are sensitive to deep pressure. The most posterior columns 
respond only when a stimulus moves across the skin. 

Functions of S-I: 
S-l is responsible for perception of the following sensations: 

- Fast pain 
- Fine touch 
- Fine degrees of thermal sensations 
- Vibration 
- Stereognosis 
- Proprioception 
- Deep pressure 

Lesions of S-I: 
So, lesions in S-I produce severe somatosensory deficits: 

1. Inability to localize the different sensations in the different parts of the body. However, 
the patient can localize these sensations crudely through the thalamus. 

2. Inability to judge critical degrees of pressure against the body. 
Inability to judge the weights of objects. 

4. Pain and temperature are poorly affected, but they are poorly localized, indicating that 
pain and temperature localization depend on the somatosensory area |.

CHAPTER II: THE SENSORY SYSTEM 

2- Somatosensory areas lI (S-11) 

Site: refer to anatomy. 
Body representation in S-ll: 
Poor localization is found in somatosensory area Il. Roughly, the face is represented 

anteriorly, the arms centrally, and the legs posteriorly. It receives sensory stimuli from both 

sides of the body. 
Functions of S-Ii: 
Much less is known about the function of somatosensory area |. 

v' 5-11 receives sensory signals from somatosensory area | and from other sensory areas of 

the brain, even from the visual and auditory areas. 

* S-|| projection to the somatosensory association cortex is involved in higher order 

processing required for recognizing hand-held objects by texture and size. 

* S-|I neurons send their axons to other areas important in tactile learning and memory; 

association cortex, motor cortex, and insula, amygdala and hippocampus. 

So, lesions in S-Il produce deficits in learning by object manipulation and in recognizing the 

texture and size of hand-held objects. 

3- Sensory association areas 

Site: refer to anatomy 

Functions of sensory association areas: Somatosensory association area receives signals from 

(1) Somatosensory areas | and II 
(2) The sensory nuclei and other areas of the thalamus, direct or via 51 and 2 

(3) The visual cortex 
(4) The auditory cortex. 

it seems clear that the somatosensory association area collects and combines information 

arriving from multiple areas to understand its meaning. 

Lesion of somatosensory association area: Inability to judge shapes or forms of objects. This 

condition is called astereognosis. 
Neglect syndrome: 

v' Classically Neglect has been associated with lesions of the right posterior parietal cortex, 

particularly the inferior parietal lobe. 

vA partial neglect (inattention) to tactile, proprioceptive and/or visual stimuli delivered 

from the contralateral side of the lesion site. 
v The patient is described as ignoring the contralateral half of the body and space. The 

perception of a "whole" body is lost, and the body parts affected may be considered to 

belong to someone else. 
The person also forgets to use the other side for motor functions as well. 

Likewise, when feeling objects, the person tends to recognize only one side of the object 

and forgets that the other side even exists. This complex sensory deficit is called 

‘Amorphosynthesis’.

The Thalamus 

Definition: It is a subcortical mass of gray matter at the lateral wall of 3 ventricle. 
Thalamic nuclei: 

v" From functional point of view it is classified into 
v" Non-specific nuclei that receive inputs from reticular formation and project to whole 

cortex. 
v' Specific nuclei that is either sensory or motor nuclei that is connected to certain cortical 

areas. 

Functions of the thalamus: 
1) It is the highest center of crude sensations. 
2) Itis relay station for: 

a) Fine sensation 
b) Hearing and visual pathways. 
c) Motor fibers from cerebellum and basal ganglia. 

3) Asitis connected to the limbic system, it has control on emotions. 
4) As itis connected to RAS, it has a role in consciousness. 
5) As itis connected to pre-frontal area, it has a role in personality and behavior. 
6) Itis responsible for alpha wave in EEG.

CERBRAL HEMISPHERE (MOTOR CORTEX) 

The posterior part of the frontal lobe “Precentral area” is motor in function while its anterior 
part “prefrontal area” is responsible for the higher mental functions (Fig. 3.1). 

I- Precentral area 
includes: 
1. Primary motor area (Area 4) 
2. Premotor area (Area 6) 
3. Frontal eye field (Area 8) 
4. Broca’s area (Areas 44 & 45) 

1. Primary motor area Ml (Area 4): 
® Site: precentral gyrus and ant. part of paracentral lobule. 
* Body representation: it contains a map of the contralateral % of the body 

represented upside down “motor homunculus” i.e., the face is lower down & the 
leg and foot in the paracentral lobule. Representation is proportionate to skill, i.e., 
parts with fine skilled movements (e.g. hands) occupy larger areas. 

* Function: Initiates discrete voluntary movements (which were planned in area 6). 
e Lesion: Contralateral hemiplegia especially skilled, fine movements. 

2. Premotor area (Area 6) 
o Site: Infront of area 4 (in sup, middle & inf. frontal gyri). It extends also on medial 

surface of cerebral hemisphere. 
* Function: Plans the movement and stores the plan. It adjusts the posture to start 

the movement. It inhibits muscle tone and grasp reflex. 
o lesion: Awkwardness of movements “apraxia”, Spasticity of muscles & 

reappearance of the Grasp reflex 

3. Frontal Eye Field | (Area 8): 
eo Site: In front of area 6 (in superior & middle frontal gyri). 
* Function: it is part of frontal eye field responsible for voluntary conjugate eye 

movements. Its stimulation leads to contralateral deviation of both eyes. 
eo lesion: 

2. Ipsilateral deviation of both eyes {towards side of lesion). 
3. Inability to turn eyes to opposite side. 

Note: (Reflex conjugate eye movement is not affected because it is controlled 
by occipital eye field). 

4. Broca’s Area (Areas 44, 45): 
Site: in the pars triangularis (area 45) and the pars opercularis (area 44) of the 
inferior frontal gyrus. It is present only in Dominant Hemisphere (usually the left 
hemisphere) 

e Function: contains the motor speech area (Anterior Speech Area). It receives data 
from the sensory speech “Wernicke’s” area. It programs a sequence of muscle 
contractions necessary to produce intelligible words & project these orders to the 

117 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

nearby area 4. 
e Lesion: Expressive (motor) Aphasia; in which the patient cannot pronounce the 

word easily but selects proper words. 

5. Supplementary motor area (Mii): 
e Site: on the medial surface of hemisphere within the medial frontal gyrus. 

e Body representation: bilateral (its stimulation evokes movements in both 

contralateral and ipsilateral limbs). The head is anterior & the leg is posterior. 

e Functions: It stores programmed motor sequences for stereotyped movements. [t 

also contains a superior speech center. 
e Lesion: leads to akinetic mutism (temporary inability to move & aphasia). 

II- Prefrontal Area: 
eo Site: 

- Remainder of superior, middle, and inferior frontal gyri. 
- Orbital gyri. 
- Most of medial frontal gyrus. 

e Function: It has a role in intelligence, normal expression of emotion, ability to predict 

consequences of an action; also it affects behavior and personality. 

e Lesion: Changes in behavior, personality and mood.

Termination: 
1- In spinal cord: 

The crossed fibers (80%) end on the lateral group of anterior horn cells (AHCs) which 
supply the limbs. 

119 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

e The uncrossed fibers (20%) end on the medial group of AHCs (supplying the trunk). 

o Few fibers from ipsilateral cortex descend directly forming the ipsilateral 

corticospinal tract that joins the lateral corticospinal tract from the opposite side. It 

helps recovery in case upper motor neuron lesion. 

N.B.: Uncrossed fibers of ventral and the ipsilateral corticospinal tract supply mainly 

the abdominal and the respiratory muscles. 

2- In brain stem: 
Some pyramidal tract fibers do not reach the spinal cord but end on motor nuclei of 

cranial nerves that lie in the brain stem. These fibers form the corticonuclear tract 

(corticobulbar tract) which controls muscles of the head. 

Fibers of the corticonuclear tract (Fig. 3.3) descend in the corona radiata, genu of 

internal capsule, to reach the brain stem to terminate mainly on contralateral motor 

nuclei present in brain stem and on ipsilateral nuclei as well. Two exceptions for this 

double control are: 
1. The part of motor nucleus of facial nerve that controls muscles of the lower part of 

the face. 
2. The part of the hypoglossal nucleus that controls genioglossus muscle (the most 

bulky muscle of the tongue). 
These muscles receive contralateral corticonuclear fibers only. 

N.B.: 

e Neurons from the cerebral cortex that descend to end on the AHCs or motor nuclei 

of cranial nerves form the upper motor neuron. 

Fig. 3.3: cortico-nuclear tract

EXTRAPYRAMIDAL TRACTS 
These are descending tracts, apart from the pyramidal tract, that influence the AHCs. These 
tracts are named according to their starting point. They are under control of cortical motor 
areas 6 & 45, the cerebellum and basal nuclei. They are either crossed, uncrossed, or a 
combination. They are responsible for adjusting muscle tone, posture and the semiautomatic 
movements such as swinging the arm during walking. They may be excitatory or inhibitory to 
muscle tone. On the other hand, the pyramidal tract is responsible for skilled voluntary 
movements in the distal parts of the limbs.

THE WHITE MATTER OF THE SPINAL CORD internal structure 

The white matter is peripheral in position in the spinal cord. It surrounds the gray matter. 

It is divided into (Fig. 3.7): 

1- Posterior white column or funiculus: lies between the midline (posteromedial septum) 

and the entry of the posterior nerve roots. 

2- Lateral white column or funiculus: lies between the entrance of the posterior nerve roots 

and the emergence of the anterior nerve roots. | 

3- Anterior white column or funiculus: lies between the point of emergence of the anterior 

nerve roots and the antero-median fissure (midline) 

Lateral 
white 

column 

Fig. 3.7: Parts of white matter of spinal cord 

Structure of the white matter 

It is formed of myelinated nerve fibers, neuroglia and blood vessels (continuous non- 

fenestrated) They are grouped together in bundles known as tracts. 

The tract is a group of myelinated nerve fibers having the same origin, same course, same 

termination and performs certain definite function.

Fig. 3.9: Proprioceptive Pathway 

Unconscious proprioception is carried to the cerebellum as follows: 

1) From 1st — 7th cervical segments, proprioception is carried by the cuneo-cerebellar tract, 

2) From 8th cervical segment to the 3rd lumbar segment (Clarke’s nucleus level), 

proprioception is carried by the dorsal spinocerebellar tract. 

3) Below the 3rd lumbar segment, proprioceptive impulses ascend with gracile tract till the 

Clarke’s nucleus, then it joins the dorsal spinocerebellar tract. 

4) Proprioception from lumbar, sacral and coccygeal segments is also carried by the ventral 

spinocerebellar tract.

Short tracts of spinal cord 

They are present within the spinal cord for its connections. 
They are intersegmental and have associative and integrative 

functions. 
They are subclassified as: 
1-Association---—--connects the adjacent part of the same side 

2-Commissural —----connects opposite halves of spinal cord 

1- Comma shaped tract: 
It Lies between gracile and cuneate tracts. Present only in 

the upper half of the spinal cord. 
It represents the descending fibers from cuneate tract. 

2-Fasciculus proprius tract 
It surrounds the gray matter of the spinal cord. 
It originates and ends in the spinal cord. 
It interconnects the neurons at different spinal levels 

(coordination). 

3- Lissauer’s tract 
It extends from the tip of the dorsal horn to the surface of 

spinal cord. It contains: 
a) Lateral division of the dorsal root of spinal nerves. 

b) Fibers that connect different levels of substantia 

gelatinosa.

The fibers ascend and descend for few segments before termination on spinal cord laminae. 
e For: pain modulation. 
4- Septomarginal tract: 

It lies close to the dorsomedian septum in the lower half of spinal cord between gracile 
tracts. It represents the descending fibers from the gracile tract.

Stretch receptor’s structure 

These receptors are proprioceptors (Muscle spindle, Golgi tendon organ) 

1) Muscle spindles: Neuromuscular spindles. (Fig 3.13). 

Site: They are present in skeletal muscles, mare numerous in the muscles of the 

extremities and muscles of fine control as small muscles of the fingers. 

Microscopic structure: 

e¢ The muscle spindle is encapsulated and fusiform in shape (2-5 mm in length and 
0.2 mm in width at its middle). 

e It is longitudinally arranged parallel to the muscle fibers. 

e Each spindle contains 2-12 intrafusal muscle fibers. The ordinary muscle fibers 
situated outside the spindle are referred to as extrafusal muscle fibers. 

e The intrafusal fibers are characterized by: 
a) Smaller diameters. 

b) Indistinct striations. 
c) Non-contractile central portions. 

2 The intrafusal muscle fibers are surrounded by the tissue fluid occupying the 

expanded middle part of the spindle. They are attached at both ends to the 
epimysium or to the extrafusal muscle fibers.

Innervation of the muscle spindle: 
A) Afferent innervation: 
1) The primary afferents: 

Each nuclear bag fiber and chain fiber receives a terminal branch of a large myelinated 

sensory fiber. This wind in a spiral form around the nuclear areas of both nuclear bag and 
the nuclear chain fibers, forming the annulospiral endings. 

2) The secondary afferents: 
Nuclear chain fibers receive terminal branches of smaller sensory fibers. These 

terminate on each side of the annulospiral ending in clusters known as flower spray endings 
or the secondary endings. 

B) Efferent innervations: 
The contractile parts of the intrafusal fibers of the spindle receive motor fibers which are 

small gamma fibers representing axons of gamma motor neurons. Stimulation of motor 

nerve causes both ends of intrafusal muscle fibers to contract and activate the sensory 

ending. 

The muscle spindles are stimulated by: 

a) Active stretch by contraction of the contractile parts of the intrafusal muscle fibers. 

b) Passive stretch when there is a pull on the tendon as in tendon reflexes e.g. knee jerk. 

This leads to an increase the rate of nerve impulses to the spinal cord and the brain. 

130 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

2) Golgi tendon organs (GTO): Neuro-tendinous organs. (Fig. 3.14) 
Sites: They are found at the junctions of the muscles with their tendons. 
Microscopic structure: 

e These are encapsulated receptors. Each has a thick lamellated capsule. The outermost layer 
continues in the perineural sheath. 

e Primary afferent fibers enter the capsule at its equatorial region, dividing into ascending 

and descending branches to give many unmyelinated branches to surround the collagen 
fibrils. No efferent innervation is present. 

Tendon organ detects changes in muscle tension. They provide CNS with information 

regarding muscle tension. 

Fig 3.14: Golgi tendon organ

CHAPTER Ili: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

MUSCLE STRECH REFEX1&2 
Introduction: 

Two types of movement are performed by the body: 
1- Voluntary movement: It is a purposeful movement correctly ordered in time and space 

e.g. Walking. 
v' Types of voluntary movements: 

a. Fine when performed by distal limb muscles (e.g. fingers & hands muscles). 

b. Gross when performed by axial muscles (e.g. back muscles) and proximal limb 

muscles (e.g. muscles around elbow joint). 

¢. Learned skilled movements: i.e. composed of previous two movements. 

Reflex movement: 

A reflex is a subconscious response due to stimulation of receptors e.g. flexor withdrawal 

reflex 

Reflex arc: is the basic functional unit of the nervous system because it is the smallest, 

simplest unit capable of receiving a stimulus and producing a response. Therefore, we can 

learn much about nervous system functions by examining how reflex arcs receive stimuli 

and how reflex arcs produce responses. 

The reflex arc has five basic components (Fig. 3.15) : 

* 1- A sensory receptor 

2- A sensory neuron 

3- An integrating center 

4- A motor neuron 

5- An effector organ. 

Fig 3.15: components of reflex arc 

132 



CHAPTER III: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Reflexes are homeostatic responses. For example, autonomic reflexes are responsible for 

maintaining relatively constant functions of CVS (Blood pressure), Respiratory system (PCO; & 
P03), .....etc. Somatic reflexes which is either withdrawal reflex i.e. to remove the body away 
from painful stimuli or postural reflexes to maintain body posture against gravity. 

Reflexes vary in complexity. Reflexes are classified into: 
1. Monosynaptic reflexes: has one synapse e.g. muscle stretch reflex (MSR). 

2. Bi-synaptic reflex: has two synapses e.g. Golgi tendon organ (GTO). 
3. Polysynaptic reflexes: has more than two synapses e.g. withdrawal reflex. 

Many reflexes are integrated within the spinal cord, and others are integrated within the 
brain stem or other brain areas. In addition, higher brain centers affect reflexes by either 

suppressing or facilitating them. 

Muscle stretch reflex = Muscle spindle reflex {MSR)= Myotatic reflex 
It is the simplest body somatic reflex. 

Definition: reflex contraction of a muscle in response to a stretching force applied to it. 

Neural components of stretch reflex: 
Stimulus: mild or moderate stretch of a muscle. 
Receptor: Muscle spindle, which is encapsulated mechanoreceptor, consists of 2 types of 
intrafusal fibers. 

Afferent: 2 types of sensory neurons that arise from the center of each of the muscle spindles. 
Type la (Aa) rapidly conducting fibers and type Il fibers. 
Center: spinal cord, anterior horn cells. 

- No interneurons. 

- There is direct synapse between afferent neurons and the efferent neurons. 

- The stretch reflex is the only monosynaptic reflex in the body. 
Efferent: a motor neurons in the spinal (alpha motor neurons) 

Effector: ordinary, contractile skeletal muscle fibers (extrafusal fibers). 
Response: contraction of the muscle. 

Importance: It keeps the normal length of the muscle. Stretch (lengthening) of the muscle 
results in reflex muscle contraction so the muscle will be shortened and returns back to its 
normal length (i.e.) negative feedback reflex. 
Clinical importance: 

1- Examination of static stretch reflex activity (muscle tone). 

2- Examination of dynamic stretch reflex (deep reflex). 

3- Diagnosis of site and cause of neurological diseases. 

N.B.;: Myotatic: means tonic contraction of the muscle in response to stretch.

General characters: 
Encapsulated mechanoreceptor present in the bulk of the muscle. It monitors the rate and 

the degree of muscle length changes. The ends of the capsule are attached to tendons of the 

muscle. Inside the capsule, there are 3-10 small, specialized skeletal muscle fibers called 

intrafusal fibers, which are positioned parallel to the extrafusal ordinary contractile fibers (Fig. 

3.17).

Innervation of the muscle spindle 
Sensory (afferent) neurons arise from the central receptor area of intrafusal fibers, while its 
peripheral contractile area is innervated by motor (efferent) neurons. 
1- Afferent nerves: 

- Sensory neurons innervate the non-contractile centers of the muscle spindle 
cells. 

- They are of two types: Primary afferent endings = type la fibers. 
Secondary afferent endings= type ll fibers.

Fig. 3.18: Nuclear bag, nuclear chain fibers and their afferents 

2- Efferent innervation: 
a) Alpha motor neuron: innervates the extrafusal muscle fibers. 
b) Gamma motor neuron (y): innervate the peripheral contractile parts of the muscle 

spindle cells. 
- They are of two types: 

a- y dynamic fibers: They supply the peripheral contractile part of the nuclear 
bag fibers. 

b- vy static fibers: They supply the peripheral contractile part of the nuclear 
chain fibers. 

Causes of stimulation of the intrafusal fibers: 
Intrafusal fibers are stimulated only by stretch of the central non-contractile part, which 

occurs either by: 
1- Lengthening of the whole skeletal muscle. 
2- Contraction of the peripheral parts of the intrafusal fibers (i.e.) supra spinal gamma 

efferent stimulation. 
Maximal stimulation occurs when both occur at the same time. 
Properties of the stretch reflex: 

1- It is a spinal (its center is the spinal cord), deep reflex (its receptors are deep in the 

muscle) 
2- It is a monosynaptic reflex. 
3- It has shortest reflex time = synaptic delay = 0.3 — 0.5 msec 
4- It has no recruitment or after discharge. 
5- It shows reciprocal innervation (when the agonist contracts, this is accompanied by reflex 

relaxation of the antagonist through inhibitory interneurons). 

6- Its activity is controlled by higher centers changing the activity of y neurons. 
7- It is inhibited by: 

a- Active muscle contraction (silent period of muscle spindle) 
b- Stimulation of the stretch reflex of the antagonist muscle. 

c- Stimulation of Golgi tendon organ reflex (=inverse = negative stretch reflex). 

8- It is a semi quantitative reflex (could be diminished, lost or increased) 

Functional types of stretch reflex: 
Functionally, 2 types of stretch reflex are of physiological and clinical importance: 

1- Dynamic stretch reflex 

2- Static stretch reflex

CHAPTER Hil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD

Functions of gamma (y) efferent fibers = (Effect of y efferent discharge): 

1- It maintains the muscle spindle sensitivity during contraction of the skeletal muscle. 
Stimulation of y efferent prevents unloading. 

- When the extrafusal fibers contract, the muscle becomes shortened, and this 
represents an external compression force on the muscle spindle, and so the 
muscle spindles passively shorten. This causes the muscle spindles to be less 
sensitive to stretch. 

- However, while alpha motor neurons are stimulating the muscle to contract, 
gamma motor neurons are stimulating contraction of the peripheral parts of 
muscle spindle, and this pulls on the central part of the muscle spindles and 
overcomes the external compression on the spindle. 

- This keeps the muscle spindle continue to send signals about the length of the 
muscle even during muscle contraction.

Functions of the stretch reflex 
(Role of the muscle spindle in voluntary motor activity) 
1- Skeletal muscle tone: 

Definition: It is a state of sub-tetanic continuous contraction of skeletal muscle affects 
mainly antigravity muscles. 
Causes: 
It is the static component of the stretch reflex. The muscle is subjected to partial 
maintained stretch all the time, and so its response is sub-tetanic maintained contraction 
(muscle tone). This partial maintained stretch is due to: 
- The distance between the origin and the insertion of the skeletal muscle is longer 

than its original length. 
- Effect of gravity that stretches mainly the antigravity muscles (flexors of upper limbs, 

extensors of lower limbs, extensors of back of neck, extensors of back of trunk and 
elevators of the lower jaw), therefore the tone is higher in the antigravity muscles. 

- Continuous vy static efferent discharge, which leads to continuous stretch of nuclear 
chain fibers. 

Pathway: The pathway of static stretch reflex. 
Functions of muscle tone: 
1- Maintains the body posture against the effect of gravity. 
2- Gives postural background needed to support the body during voluntary 

movements. 
3- Maintains heat production thus regulating body temperature. 
4- Maintains venous return and lymphatic drainage especially in muscles of lower 

limbs. 
5- Keeps the viscera in position by tone of anterior abdominal wall muscles. 
Muscle tone is not accompanied by fatigue because: 
1- it is due to alternate contraction of different muscle fibers. 
2- Contraction is sub-tetanic. 
3- Muscle fibers involved in muscle tone are the red, slow, oxidative muscle fibers, 

which are rich in mitochondria, myoglobin and capillaries. They contract slowly and 
can sustain force in muscle for a long time. 

Servo-assistant function = (force regulation): 
Stretch reflex assists voluntary movements (servo-assist voluntary movements). This is 
explained by a -y co- activation.: 
a -y co- activation: during voluntary contraction, supraspinai facilitatory impulses 
activate both (a) and (y) neurons. The activated (a) neurons produce contraction of 
extrafusal fibers of the involved muscle. At the same time, the activated (y) neurons 
produce contraction of the peripheral parts of intrafusal fibers to prevent unloading, and 
thus keeps the central receptor part of intrafusal fibers sensitive to stretch even during 
shortening (contraction) of extrafusal fibers {Fig. 3.21a). 

139 



CHAPTER li: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

- 

aingl 
Fig. 3.21: Servo-assistance function of muscle stretch reflex. 

If the load, carried by the muscle is increased, it results in stretch of the muscle, both 

extrafusal and intrafusal fibers become stretched (Fig. 3.21b). 
Because the central receptor part of intrafusal fibers sensitive to stretch even during 

muscle contraction, its stretch by the increased load leads to its stimulation and 

increased discharge of impulses in type la afferents. The increased la afferents discharge 

rate leads to more activation of (a) motor neurons, with subsequent increase in the force 

of extrafusal fibers contraction to adjust the muscle force according to the increased 

load (Fig. 3.21c). 
This function of stretch reflex is helpful in the following conditions: 
a) Allow the nervous system to produce strong contraction with minimal nervous 

energy (i.e.) energy saving. 
b) Application of heavy weights to the muscle with stabilization of limb position (load 

reflex), (i.e.) ability of the muscle to maintain a constant position despite application 

of different weights. 
c) Compensate for muscle weakness or muscle fatigue or failure of the muscle to 

contract in adequate force. 
3- Damping action: 

- This means smoothness of voluntary movements or signal averaging function of 

stretch reflex. 
- Signals from the higher centers discharged to the motor neurons of the spinal cord, 

and then to the muscle are often of varying intensities, that may be in excess or less 

140 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

than needed. For example, signals are increasing in intensity for a few milliseconds, 
then decreasing in intensity, then changing to another intensity level, and so forth, 
and therefore would lead to oscillating irregular movements. 

- However, through muscle spindle and alpha-gamma linkage, stretch reflex prevents 
the oscillation or jerkiness of the voluntary movements, and adjust signals to 
produce smooth regular movements 

- When the muscle spindle and its afferent and efferent neurons are not functioning 
well, the muscle contraction is jerky during the course of such a signal (Fig. 3.22)

4- Examination of stretch reflex is of clinical value. 
a) The presence of the normal stretch reflex response indicates: 

1. The muscle spindle, afferent (sensory) and efferent (motor) neurons, 
neuromuscular junctions, and the muscle are working well. 

2. An appropriate balance of excitatory and inhibitory inputs from the higher brain 
levels. 

3. The integrity of the vertebral segments of the spinal cord. 
b) Localization of site and cause of neurological disease: if the biceps jerk (C5 & 6) is 

intact and triceps jerk (C6 & 7) is lost this means lesions is in spinal segment 7. 
c) Deep reflex examination give idea about the tone of the muscle. As hyperreflexia 

accompany hypertonia while hyporeflexia accompany hypotonia. 
Supra spinal regulation of the stretch reflex 

Normally the anterior horn cells particularly y motor neurons receive impulses from many 
supraspinal centers, through descending nervous tracts. 
These higher supraspinal centers send either excitatory impulses or inhibitory impulses to 
affect the level of excitability and function of the spinal cord motor neurons (mainly y 
motor neurons), and subsequently affect the activity of the muscle spindle and the stretch 
refiex. 

The general effects of this supraspinal control on stretch reflex is summarized in table 3.9

OTHER REFLEXES 

Golgi tendon organ reflex (GTO (Fig. 3.24 & 3.25) 

= Inverse stretch reflex = autogenic inhibition reflex 

Definition: It is reflex skeletal muscle relaxation in response to an increase in the muscle 
tension. 
Stimulus: increase in muscle tension by: 

1. Increase active tension by isometrically contracted muscle. 
2. Increase passive tension by excessive stretch of the muscle beyond physiologic limit. 

The GTO is more sensitive to increased active tension (isometric contraction), than to 
increased passive tension {severe stretch). 

Receptors: GTO is a proprioceptive receptor, which is an encapsulated network of nerve 
endings twisted throughout the collagen fibers in the tendon of the muscle. Golgi tendon 
organs are arranged in series 
with the extrafusal muscle fibers. They act as tension detectors. 

Muscle _ 
fibers = 

Capsule Ib Afferent fiber 

It afferent 
EUuron 

GTO = 

Collagen 

Fig. 3.24: Golgi tendon organ Fig. 3.25: Inverse stretch reflex pathway 
Afferent: Group Ib fibers. These afferents have their cell bodies in the dorsal root ganglia, and 
they project into the spinal cord. 
Center: Alpha motor neuron (AHCs) where afferents (lb) synapse with an inhibitory 
interneuron that makes an inhibitory synapse with the alpha motor neuron that innervates 
the same muscle from which the Ib afferent fiber arises. 
Efferent: Alpha motor neuron (AHCs). 
Effector organ: Extrafusal fibers of the skeletal muscle. 
Response: Muscle relaxation. 
Functions of inverse stretch reflex: 

1- Protection of muscles and tendons from damage caused by excessive tension. It act as a 
negative feedback to relax the muscle when tension increased maximally with heavy 
weight (isometric contraction) and prevent its tear. 

143 



CHAPTER lil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

2- Equalize contractile force among the muscle fibers. Those fibers that exert excess tension 

become inhibited by the reflex, whereas those that exert too little tension become more 

excited because of absence of reflex inhibition. 
- If some muscle fibers are performing more work than others, their tension is more 

increased and their Golgi tendon organs will be more stimulated. This stimulation of 

the GTO of some fibers results in partial inhibition of the contraction of those fibers. 

As a result, GTO reflex helps to spread the amount of work evenly across the entire 

muscle, so that all motor units are working efficiently. 
- About 10-15 muscle fibers are usually connected to each Golgi tendon organ thus 

there are multiple Golgi tendon organs in each tendon. Each organ is stimulated when 

its small muscle bundle is tensed by contraction or over stretch. Thus, the GTO reflex 
is involved in motor control under normal conditions.

Flexor withdrawal reflex {Protective Reflex, Fig. 3.26): 

Definition: It is reflex flexion and withdrawal of a limb from a painful stimulus. 
Stimulus: Painful stimuli (noxious stimulus, injurious stimulus). 
Afferents: AS and C afferents (pain afferents). 
Center: Alpha motor neuron (AHCs), it has more than 2 synapses (Polysynaptic reflex). 
Efferent: Anterior horn cell (alpha motor neuron) of the flexor muscles. 
Effector: Extrafusal fibers of flexor muscles. 
Response: flexion and withdrawal of the limb from the painful stimulus. 
Aim: withdrawal of the body parts from painful stimuli for protection of the body. 

144 



CHAPTER Iii: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Properties of flexor withdrawal reflex: 
1- lt is initiated by injurious stimuli 
2- Impulses are transmitted by Ad & C fibers. 
3- Polysynaptic reflex (long central delay of conduction) 
4- Irradiation: a very strong stimulus causes stimulation of much receptors in many adjacent 

receptive fields. This leads to discharge of impulses in many afferents that spread the 
excitatory impulses up and down to many segments in the spinal cord. This leads to 
increased numbers of active spinal segments, numbers of active motor neurons, and 
greater response (e.g. stepping on sharp object by right foot lead to flexion on ankle, knee 
and even hip joints.) 

5- Reverberating circuit between the interneurons results in prolonged after discharge to 
maintain muscle contraction for some time after removal of the stimulus. 

6- Reciprocal innervation: Collateral branches from the afferent neuron carrying the painful 
stimuli synapse with inhibitory interneurons that inhibit the motor neurons of the 
antagonistic muscles. This helps in unopposed action of the agonist muscles.

extensor reflex in left limb. 

Crossed extensor reflex (Supportive Reflex): 

Definition: lt is reflex contraction and extension of the limb, in response to application of a 
painful stimulus to the opposite limb. 
Stimulus: Painful stimuli of the opposite limb {contralateral painful stimulus). 
Afferents: Ad and C afferents (pain afferents). 
Center: Alpha motor neuron (AHCs), where afferents synapse with an intersegmental 
interneuron that transmits impulses to the opposite side of the spinal cord. 
Efferent: Alpha motor neurons (AHCs) of the extensor muscles. 
Effector: Extrafusal fibers of extensor muscles. 
Response: Extension of the limb. 

145 



CHAPTER lll: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Function: When one lower limb is withdrawn from a painful stimulus, the other limb extends 

to prevent falling down. 
Properties of Crossed extensor reflex: 

- This reflex is associated with the flexor withdrawal reflex of the other limb. 
- Long latent period due to large numbers of interneurons involved in the reflex. 
- Long after discharge. 

Superficial reflexes 

Body superficial reflexes are induced by stimulation of receptors in the skin and special sense 

organs. 
General characters of body superficial reflexes: 
Stimulus: Scratch of the skin. 
Receptors: Superficial receptors. 
Afferents: Sensory neurons that enter the spinal cord through dorsal Root. 
Center: Spinal cord segments. 
Efferent: Alpha motor neurons that leave the spinal cord through ventral horn, 
Effector: Extrafusal fibers of a muscle. 
Response: Muscle contraction. 

- In addition to LMN (a neuron that carry motor impulses from the spinal cord, or from the 
nuclei of motor cranial nerves to the muscle). upper motor neuron (UMN, a neuron that 

carry motor impulses from the brain to the spinal cord, or the nuclei of motor cranial 
nerves) is also essential for normal occurrence of superficial reflexes. 

- In contrast, deep reflexes do not require UMN for them to occur. Deep reflexes are 

regulated, not initiated, by the UMN:S. 
1. Abdominal superficial reflexes (Fig. 3.27) 

Stimulus: Scratching the skin of abdominal wall from outward and towards the umbilicus 
by a blunt object. 
Afferent: Sensory neurons that enter the spinal cord through dorsal root. 
Center : Spinal segments T6-T9 for epigastric reflexes, spinal segments T9 - T12 for 

abdominal reflexes. 
Efferent: Alpha motor neurons (AHCs) 
Response: contraction of abdominal wall muscles resulting in shifting of the umbilicus to 
the opposite direction of the scratch. 
-This reflex is under the control of higher centers so it is lost in lesion of upper motor 

neuron. 

Fig. 3.27: Superficial abdominal reflex 

146 



CHAPTER lll: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

2. Plantar superficial reflex (Fig. 3.28) 
Stimulus: Scratching the lateral side of the sole from the heal to the base of toes by blunt 
object e.g. key 
Afferent: Sensory neurons that enter the spinal cord through dorsal horn. 
Center: Spinal segments S1 & S2 
Efferent: Alpha motor neurons (ACs). 
Response: plantar flexion of all toes 

Positive Babinski’ sign: dorsiflexion of big toe and fanning of the lateral toes. It appears 
either in physiological or pathological conditions. 

e Physiological conditions: 
- Deepsleep, general anesthesia and infancy below one year as the pyramidal tract 

neurons (UMN) is not myelinated yet. 
- in all these conditions, the response is termed “Pseudo positive Babinski’s sign”. 

¢ Pathological conditions: 
- Deep Coma 
- Upper motor neuron lesions: fanning of lateral toes and dorsiflexion of big toe. 
- Partial Positive Babinski’ sign: either dorsiflexion of big toe only in lesion in 

motor area 4; Or fanning of lateral toes only in lesion in area 6.

Anterior horn cell and motor function: 
Cells of the anterior horn of the spinal cord are classified into: 

I- Anterior motor neurons. 
lI- interneurons. 
i- Anterior motor neurons: 

1- Alpha motor neurons: it is called (AHCs). They are considered as the final common 
path for skeletal muscle contraction, which means that any type of skeletal muscle 
contraction (voluntary and reflex contraction) is performed through activation of alpha 
motor neurons. 
Arrangement of a motor neurons: 
- Inthe anterior horn, a motor neurons controlling axial muscles lie medial to those 

controlling distal muscles and a motor neurons controlling flexors lie dorsal to 
those controlling extensors. 

- a motor neurons that control proximal and distal muscles are found mainly in the 
cervical and lumbosacral segments of the spinal cord. 

- In the thoracic segments below T1, the AHCs are associated only with axial muscle

v' The motor unit {Fig. 3.30) is a single a motor neuron and all of the muscle fibers it 
innervates. 
- The motor unit for muscles that perform fine movements (eye muscles) consists 

of a single a motor neuron and 3-6 fibers innervated by this neuron (low 
innervation ratio). 

- The motor unit for muscles that perform powerful but gross movement (large leg 
and arm muscles) consists of a single a motor neuron and 200-300 fibers 
innervated by this neuron (high innervation ratio). 

v' The motor neuron pool is the group of AHCs that innervate a single muscle, and this 

pool may extend over several segments of the spinal cord e.g. The motor neuron pool 

of quadriceps muscle is the alpha motor neuronesinl 2, 3 & 4. 
v' Alpha motor neurons respond to and controlled by inputs from three sources: 

1- Nerve endings of sensory neurons in dorsal root ganglia. 
2- Interneurons (cells that do not project out of the area). 
3- Projections from higher centers such as the brain stem.

MOTOR CORTEX FUNCTION 

e Most of voluntary movements initiated by cerebral cortex occur when the cortex activates 

patterns of function (programs of movements) stored in lower brain areas e.g. brainstem, 

basal ganglia and cerebellum. These centers send specific control signals to anterior motor 

neurons of spinal cord that control muscle. 

The motor cortex is the part of the cerebral cortex responsible for motor functions. It is located 

in front of the central sulcus {precentral) in the frontal lobe. It is subdivided into: 

|- Motor area 4 “Primary motor area, M1”. 
{l- Motor area 6 “Premotor area” 
IH- Supplementary Motor Area = Medial Area 6 
IV- Some specialized areas of motor control. 

I Primary 

Fig. 3.32: Motor cortex 

I- Motor area 4 “Primary motor area, M1" 
Site: precentral area in the frontal lobe. 
Body representation: 
a) Somatotopic representation (Topographical Representation): 

v' Crossed and Inverted: 
- Body representation is mainly contralateral; representation of muscles on the 

right side of the body is found in area 4 on the left cerebral hemisphere. 

- However, the exception is that muscles of respiratory and abdominal muscles 
are bilaterally represented. 

- Head muscles is bilaterally represented except muscles of the lower part of 

the face and tongue muscle which is unilaterally represented. 
v' It controls: 

1. Opposite side body muscles. 
2. Respiratory and abdominal muscles of both sides 
3. Upper half of face muscles of both sides. 

150



CHAPTER Iii: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

- It is inverted which means that the body is represented upside-down with the 
exception of the head which is not inverted (eyebrows upward, lips are 
downward). The face and mouth downwards near the Sylvian fissure, the arm 
and hand area in mid-portion of the primary motor cortex; the trunk near the 
apex of the brain; and the legs and feet in the part that dips in the longitudinal 
fissure). 

Fig. 3.33: Representation of different body muscles in primary motor cortex 

b) Motor representation: 
v' Area of representation is proportional to the skilled movement produced, not to 

the actual size of body muscles. e.g. finger and tongue muscles are represented 
by a large cortical area in area 4, while back muscles heave a small area of cortical 
representation. 

Characters of area 4: 
- Contains the large cortical Betz cells, which are highly excitable cells. 
- Betz cells are of 2 types: 

a- Dynamic: discharge at the start of voluntary movements, for initiation and 
direction of movement. 

b- Static: discharge all through the voluntary movements, for maintenance of 
movement. 

Connections of area 4: 
1- To AHCs and cranial nerve nuclei through both corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts. 

It is the main origin of pyramidal tract. 
2- To Area 6. 
3- To neocerebellum of the opposite site. 
4- Basal ganglia. 

15] 



CHAPTER lll: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Effect of lesions of area 6: 
The following manifestations occur on the opposite site of the body: 
1-  Paresis i.e weakness of voluntary movements but not paralysis. 
2- Motor apraxia (inability to do skilled motor act in absence of paralysis). 
3- Increased muscle tone (Spasticity hypertonia). 
4-  Hyperreflexia. 
5- Reappearance of grasp reflex. 
6- Partial +ve Babinski’s sign: Fanning of outer four toes. 
7- Loss of automatic associated movements (e.g. swinging of arms). 

Specialized Areas nearT he Premotor Cortex 
They are four important motor areas that lies very near to premotor cortex. 
1- Area for Hand Skills (Exner’s area): 

v It controls complex skilled hand movements e.g. sharpening of pencil or 
drawing figures. 

v' Its lesion causes Agraphia (hand movement become uncoordinated, and 
purposeful, and there is inability to write or draw but use hands for other 
purposes). 

2- Head Rotation Area: 
v It directs the head towards different visual objects. 

3- “Voluntary” Eye Movement Area (Frontal eye Field Area 8): 
v' it gives origin to cortico-nuclear tract of both sides, which is responsible for 

voluntary conjugate deviation of both eyes to opposite side. 
v' Its lesion causes loss of voluntary ability to produce conjugate eye movement 
v' This is transient because the tectospinal tract compensates and the reflex 

conjugate eye movements return. 
4- Motor speech area (Broca's Area): Words Formation Area (area 44): 

v' It stores the complex sequence of orders for vocalization. 
v' Its lesion causes motor aphasia (inability to speak whole words except simple 

ones as yes or no). 

Ii- SupplementMaotroyr Area 
Site: as mentioned in anatomy. 
Body representation: Horizontal and Bilateral representation of body muscles. 
Functions: 
1- It supplements (helps) area 6 in the control of voluntary movements of the proximal 

parts of the body (especially internal directed movements) as a background for finer 
hand or feet movements. 

2- It is involved in planning and programming of movements, especially complex and 
bilateral movements before they start. 

Effect of lesions of Supplementary Motor Area: 
Its lesions lead to: 
1- Inability to perform complex movements. 
2- Inability to direct and coordinate the hands correctly during bilateral hand 

movements. 

153 



CHAPTER Ili: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Motor Hierarchy 
Generation of voluntary movement 
e Voluntary movements are motions carried out when the nervous system and muscle work 

together. Voluntary movements are either 
a) Fine movement: like painting & drawing. It is usually performed by distal limb muscles 

(e.g. finger muscles). It is the responsibility of motor area 4 so if area 4 is damaged it 

causes permanent loss (paralysis) of fine movement. 
b) Gross movement: like walking & standing. It is usually performed by proximal and axial 

muscles (e.g. back muscles). It is the responsibility of area 4 and 6 so lesion in any of 

them cause weakness or paresis. 
c¢) Skilled movements: which are both fine and gross movements. 

Motor hierarchy (Fig. 3.37) 
It is the Information flow through the motor system. 
The control of movement by the central nervous system is a complicated process that involves 

multiple regions of the brain. Although specific regions of the brain, called the motor cortex, 

is believed to be responsible for the body motor functions, almost all of the neocortex 

becomes engaged during the control of voluntary movement. 

In general, the motor hierarchy is classified into: 
1) Higher motor levels: responsible for generation of the idea of voluntary movement. 

- Association areas in the cortex. 
2) Intermediate motor levels: responsible for planning and programming of voluntary 

movement (convert idea into a plan). 
- The motor cortex, Basal ganglia and Cerebellum. 

3) Lower motor levels: responsible for execution of voluntary movement (sending orders to 

the skeletal muscles involved in the performance of voluntary movement). 

- The brain stem and the spinal cord. 

PLANNING INITIATING EXECUTING
