CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Consider some of the key components of a goal directed movement: 
To carry out goal-directed movements: 

1) The idea is initiated in prefrontal cortex. 
2) The idea is converted into initial decision {or may be ignored) in the limbic association 

areas. 
3) Association areas collect various kinds of information from the various lobes of the 

brain e.g. the frontal lobe, about memories of past strategies and from the temporal 
lobe about motivation. 

4) The posterior parietal cortex (area 5,7) evaluates the body position in space and the 
surrounding environment through somatosensory, proprioceptive, and visual sensory 
inputs (evaluation of the current body position before planning of the movement). 

5) The idea is then converted into a plan or a program as follows: 
a) Premotor cortex (PMC) and supplementary motor areas (SMA) determine how the 

actions will be carried out, and decides which group of muscles to contract, and their 
sequence of contraction to result in the required movement. 

b) The basal ganglia and neocerebelflum share in planning by sequencing and storage of 
learned movements. 

6) The plan must be changed into motor orders i.e. Executed 
This is pure function of the primary motor cortex, which, together with the PMC and SMA, 
contributes most of the axons of the descending corticospinal tract. So, the brain signals 
pass down to the spinal cord, and then to the muscles to convert the plan into an actual 
movement. 

7) Feedback circuits: vision and spino-cerebellum monitor the performance of the 
movement. 

- If an error in performance is detected, they send corrective signal to the 
motor cortex, which then send impulses to the spinal cord for correction of 
movement. 

e Cerebral blood flow (CBF): is an essential determinant of brain function. Cerebral blood 
flow supplies oxygen, glucose and nutrients, and removes CO;, lactic acid & metabolites. 

eo Cerebral arteries: Brain is supplied by 4 arteries; 2 Internal carotid arteries and 2 vertebral 
arteries. These arteries are interconnected in brain to form an arterial circle {circle of Willis, 
COW) below the hypothalamus. From COW, 6 large vessels originate which supply the 
cerebral cortex. Although the circle has anastomosis channels, normally they do not permit 
a very large flow. 

e Cerebral capillaries: are non-fenestrated capillaries (Capillaries in choroid plexus are 
fenestrated) with tight junctions between endothelial cells. They are the basis for blood 
brain barrier (BBB), and are characterized by limited diffusion & vesicular transport. 

e Venous drainage of the brain occurs through cerebral veins, which drain to cerebral venous 
sinuses and then to internal jugular veins 

155 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

e Brain blood supply is 750 mi/min (54 ml/100 gm/min) = ~ 14% of cardiac output. Oxygen 
supply is 46 ml/min (3.3 ml/100gm/min) =~ 18% of total body O02 consumption 

Determinants of cerebral blood flow: 
CBF is relatively constant all the time. However, marked local fluctuations in CBF occur with 
changes in local activity of brain areas. 
CBF is regulated by: 
- Cerebral perfusion pressure 
- Cerebral vascular resistance 
Cerebral Perfusion Pressure (CPP): 

v' is the net pressure gradient causing blood flow to the brain. 
v' CPP = mean arterial pressure (MAP) — mean cerebral venous pressure (MCVP). 
v' The MCVP = intracranial pressure (ICP). So, CPP = MAP — ICP. 
v' Any increase in CPP causes an increase in CBF. 
v’ Any factor affecting MAP (e.g. hemorrhage) or ICP will affect the CBF. 

CBF is maintained normal over a wide range of MAP by ‘autoregulation’ 

Regulation of CBF: 
Autoregulation 
Metabolic mediators and chemo-regulation 
Neural control 

Autoregulation is a property that maintains constant blood flow to the brain despite wide 

fluctuations in MAP. It maintains cerebral blood flow constant in the range of 50 — 150 mm Hg 
MAP. It is explained by: 

- Myogenic property of cerebral vessels: 
Increase ABP produces vasoconstriction of cerebral vessels, to prevent a sudden increase 

in CBF, and vice versa. 
- Metabolic theory: decrease CBF leads to cerebral ischemia and accumulation of 

vasodilator metabolites (hypercapnia, hypoxia, increase H*, increase adenosine) that lead 
to vasodilatation of cerebral vessels to return CBF back to normal. 

Role of ICP in maintaining CBF: 
According to Monro-Kellie doctrine, the volume of blood, spinal fluid, and brain in the 

cranium (closed space in a solid skull) at any time must be relatively constant. So, an increase 
in ICP results in a decrease in CBF, and vice versa. 

Cushing’s reflex: Increase intracranial pressure (brain tumor) leads to a decrease in CBF. This 
leads to ischemia of VMC and sympathetic stimulation in an attempt to increase the ABP to 
increase the CBF back. 

156 



CHAPTER Hl: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

DESCENDING MOTOR TRACT FUNCTIONS 

Performing voluntary contraction: performing learned fine skilled movements need intact: 

1) Upper motor neurons (UMNSs): neurons from the cerebral cortex and terminate either 
in spinal cord (corticospinal tract) or brain stem nuclei ( corticobulbar and cortico- 
nuclear tracts ). 

2) Lower motor neurons (LMNs): neurons from anterior horn cells (AHCs) in the spinal 
cord and terminate at skeletal muscles of body (alpha motor neuron). or start from the 
brain stem to supply muscles of face and eyes (cranial nerves). 

Pyramidal tract 

v' It is well-developed in humans and it goes directly from the motor cortex to the AHCs or 
the nuclei of the cranial nerves. 

v' It is composed of about one million fibers and only 60% of them are myelinated. This 
myelination becomes complete after one year from birth. 

v" ltis so called because some fibers arise from the pyramidal {or Betz) cells present in the 
motor area 4. 

Origin and course: refer to anatomy lecture 

v' All cranial nerves nuclei have double innervation (from the same and opposite sides), 
except the lower part of fascial nucleus {supplying lower quadrant of the face) and the 
hypoglossal nucleus (supplying the tongue muscles) is innervated from the opposite side 
fibers only. 

Cortico-bulbar tract 

® Upper face 

\ Te Lower face

nuclei ¥II 

Fig. 3.35: Corticobalbar tract innervating the fascial nucleus 

Functions of the Pyramidal tract: 
1. Lateral tract controls fine skilled movements especially in the distal joints as | 

those of the fingers, toes and face e.g. writing, typing, piano playing 
2. Ventral tract shares with extrapyramidal tract to control gross movement and 

postural adjustment as a background for skilled movement performance. 
3. Uncrossed to abdominal and respiratory muscles 
4. lt is facilitatory to the superficial and deep reflexes. 
5. It inhibits the primitive withdrawal reflex (Babiniski's sign). 
N.B.: The corticobulbar tract (from frontal eye field area= area 8) controls conjugate 
eye movements.

CHAPTER Iii: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Extrapyramidal Tracts 

The term extrapyramidal motor system has been used to denote all the portions of the brain 

and brain stem that contribute to motor control but are not part of pyramidal tract: 

Pathway: refer to anatomy lecture 

B.: 
- extrapyramidal fibers originate from the cerebral motor cortex and end on basal ganglia, 

thalamus & substantia nigra, red nucleus, tectum (superior & inferior colliculi), 

vestibular nucleus, pontine and medullary reticular formation and inferior olivary 

nuclei 
- the output from the previous areas descend to supply the gamma cells (mainly) as well 

as alpha motor neurons. They are divided into medial and lateral brain stem pathways. 

¢ Functions: 
1) Control gross movements of muscle groups like the axial (trunk) and the proximal limb 

muscles e.g. running, bicycling or swimming but not skilled ones. 

2) Provide postural background (positions of the trunk and proximal joints) that help 

skilled movements and body equilibrium. 
3) With its association with basal ganglia, it is responsible for subconscious associated 

movements e.g. swinging of the arms during walking and facial expressions. 

4) Regulation of muscle tone, some are facilitatory while the others are inhibitory( as 

mentioned in supraspinal control of stretch reflex). 
5) In selective lesion of corticospinal tract, rubrospinal tract can control fine discrete 

movements in wrist joint (but that of fingers are impaired). 
6) Medial TST is concerned with reflex turning of the head to the sound, while lateral TST 

is concerned with eye protective reflexes (e.g. lid closure, raising arms & hands to 

protect the eyes) and it is concerned with reflex turning of the head in response to 

visual stimuli. 
7) Vestibulospinal tracts activate motor neurons of antigravity muscles (eg, proximal limb 

extensors) to control posture and balance. 
8) Control of autonomic functions: many descending tracts that control important 

autonomic functions are considered as a part of this system e.g. 

a. Respiratory system: from the respiratory center to AHCs of respiratory muscles 

b. Vasomotor tract: from VMC to LHCs in thoraco-lumbar region. 

c. sweat secretion (from heat regulating center in the hypothalamus).

v’ It is the fibers that arise from the red nucleus of the midbrain.

The spinal cord is supplied by three sets of arteries: two longitudinal (anterior & posterior 
spinal arteries) and many segmental radicular arteries.

4. Segmental (Radicular) Arteries (Fig. 3.38 
Origin: arise as twigs from the vertebral, ascending cervical, posterior intercostal

Course: They enter the vertebral canal through the intervertebral foramina. They give 
anterior & posterior radicular branches that pass along the ventral & dorsal roots to 
reach the surface of the spinal cord & form an arterial circle of anastomosis with the 
branches of anterior & posterior spinal arteries “arterial vasacorona”. Branches from 
this circle supply the periphery of the spinal cord. 
Some radicular arteries may be large & are called feeder arteries. One of the feeder 
arteries is called artery of Adamkiewicz which arises from 11" intercostal artery & 
may be the main supply to the lower two-thirds of the cord.

Sensory and Motor systems Lesions1 
e Various types of sensory abnormalities can occur when interruption or damage occur 

at any level along the sensory pathways. These sensory abnormalities may result in 
sensory loss, altered sensations or pain. 

e |n addition, motor pathways could also be affected resulting in motor weakness. 

Motor neurone lesions 

| ETS Jose bY 
—r oo. 

Fig. 3.42: Upper and Lower Motor neuron lesion 

1) Lower Motor neuron lesion: 
- Cause: injury of spinal motor neuron or cranial nerve nuclei to effector organ e.g. 

Poliomyelitis 
- Effects: 

1) Effect on voluntary movement: localized ipsilateral flaccid paralysis of a muscle or a 
group of muscles supplied by the affected nerves. 

2) Effect on reflex activity: 
- All reflexes of the affected muscles are decreased or lost due to interruption 

of the reflex arc pathway. 
v' Loss of muscle tone: hypotonia or atonia (flaccidity). 
v' Loss of deep reflexes and tendon jerks: hyporeflexia or areflexia. 
v’ Loss of superficial reflexes of the affected muscles. 

3) Effect on muscle state: 
Marked atrophy (degeneration, wasting) of the affected muscle occur due to: 
- Loss of voluntary and reflex activity of the muscle. 
- Loss of trophic effects on metabolism of the affected muscle due to damage of 

their nerve supply. 
4) The paralyzed muscle may show fasciculation or fibrillations: 

v’ Fasciculations 
Definition: Jerky, visible contractions of groups of muscle fibers. Can be felt and 
seen 
Cause: pathologic discharge of spinal motor neurons. 

v' Fibrillations: 
Definition: Fine, irregular contractions of individual fibers, detectable electro- 
physiologically, but invisible to the eye. Felt only. 
Cause: denervation hypersensitivity of the denervated muscles to the circulating 
acetylcholine

DISORDERS OF MUSCLE TONE 
v Muscle tone is It is a state of sub-tetanic continuous contraction of skeletal muscle affects 

mainly antigravity muscles, caused by static component of stretch reflex. 

v The antigravity muscles (flexors of upper limbs, extensors of lower limbs, extensors of back 

of neck, extensors of back of trunk and elevators of the lower jaw), 
v' Clinically, tone can be defined as the resistance that is encountered when the joint of a 

relaxed patient is moved passively. 
v' Clinically muscle tone may be abnormally increased (Hypertonia) or decreased (Hypotonia) 

Causes of hypotonia (Flaccidity): 

Loss of excitatory supraspinal control of static muscle stretch reflex: 

1) Cerebellar lesions (neocerebellar disease). 
2) Chorea. 
3) Lower motor neuron lesion (e.g. Tabes dorsalis, polyneuritis, myasthenia graves). 
4) Acute stage of upper motor neuron lesion. 
5) Lesion of area 4. 
6) Increased serum Ca*™ level. 

Causes of hypertonia 
e There are two major types of hypertonia 

1) Spasticity. 
2) Rigidity.

2- Spinal cord lesions: Fig. 3.51: Cavitation around 
A) Syringomyelia (central cord lesion) the central canal of spinal cord 

Definition: cyst or glial tissue or fluid 
accumulation that increases over time and leads 
to cavitation around the central canal of the 
spinal cord, commonly of cervical segments. On 
progression of the disease, cavitation extends to 
thoracic segments and medulla oblongata (Fig. 
3.51). 
Cause: Unknown, may be genetic. More common in middle aged females. 
Effects: 
a) In small-limited lesions 

- Sensory effects: damage to spinothalamic fibers 
crossing in front of the central canal, causing bilateral regions of 
sensory loss of pain and temperature in the upper limbs and upper parts of chest and

v’ The first sensation to be lost is vibration. 
v’ The last sensation to be lost is slow pain because the compression is from

Effects:

b- Below the level of the lesion:

exaggerated reflexes).

- Contralateral loss of spinothalamic sensations. 

D) Complete transection of the spinal cord (Fig. 3.56): 
Cause: Gunshot injuries, Dislocation of spine, vascular accident 
Effects: 
- Sensory Effects: 
v complete and permanent loss of all sensations below the level of the lesion. The 

damaged sensory tracts have no neurilemma and can never regenerate, so 

sensations are permanently lost. 
- Voluntary motor effects: 

v' complete and permanent loss of all voluntary movements below the level of 

the lesion. The damaged motor tracts have no neurilemma and can never 

regenerate, so voluntary motor functions are permanently lost, and the patient 

becomes paralyzed.

ii-Transection in cervical region below C5 leads to paralysis of all body muscles in upper and 
lower limbs (Quadriplegia). Respiration is diaphragmatic only, with paralysis of the intercostal 
muscles. 
iii-Transection in mid-thoracic region leads to intact voluntary movements of upper limb, while 
the paralysis involves all lower limb muscles (Paraplegia). Respiration is intact both by 
diaphragmatic and intercostal muscles activity. 

-Reflex motor effects: 
v' Reflexes below the level of the lesion pass in a stage of spinal shock. This stage is 

followed by either a stage of recovery or a stage of failure of reflexes. 
%* Stage of spinal shock: 

-Definition: Complete loss of all reflexes below the level of the lesion. 
-Cause: Sudden withdrawal of the facilitatory corticospinal, reticulospinal, vestibulospinal 
impulses. This result in decrease excitability of spinal motor neurons, and loss of their response 
to stimulation by afferents in reflex pathway. 
-Duration: in human: 2-6 weeks. The duration of spinal shock is proportionate to the degree 
of encephalization of motor function (control of spinal motor functions by the higher brain 
centers). 
-Manifestations: 

v' Loss of stretch reflex, atonia, areflexia and flaccid muscles. 
v' Loss of flexor withdrawal reflex and superficial reflexes. 
v" Loss of autonomic reflexes, resulting in atonia in walls and sphincters of bladder and 

rectum, with retention and overflow dibbling. 
v' loss of vasomotor control on sympathetic lateral horn cells, resulting in loss of 

vasomotor tone and hypotension. 

175 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

v' The blood flow to the paralyzed limb is decreased (due to decrease in blood pressure), 

and the venous return from the paralyzed limb is decreased (due to atonia). The 

paralyzed limbs become bluish in color (cyanosed), cold and dry, with decreased 
nutrition. 

v Bed ulcers (bedsores) may occur especially in the dependent areas (on which the 
patient is lying) of the paralyzed limbs. 

v" Muscle atrophy (wasting). 
v' Bone demineralization, release of bone minerals into blood and hypercalcemia that is 

very dangerous on the heart and kidneys due to degradation of matrix protein.

-Care for the patient during the stage of spinal shock: 
- Physiotherapy to prevent muscle atrophy. 
- Antibiotics to resist infections of bedsores (Fig. 3.57). 
- Frequent mobilization in bed to avoid bed sores. 
- Good nutrition. 
- Urinary catheter and rectal enema. 

* Stage of failure of reflexes: 
- Inappropriate care for the patient during the stage of spinal shock leads to severe 

infections of bedsores and urinary tract. The resulting toxemia depresses the 
functions of the entire CNS, including the vital centers, leading to death. 

* Stage of recovery of reflexes: 
Good care for the patient during the stage of spinal shock leads to recovery of reflex 

activity. 

- Causes of reflexes recovery: The recovery of reflex excitability may be due to: 

1) The development of denervation hypersensitivity by the remaining spinal 
neurons, and so they can now respond to afferent stimulation. 

2) Another possibility is the increase of collaterals from existing neurons, with 

the formation of more excitatory endings on interneuron's and motor 

heurons. ir 
- Manifestation: 

Return of muscle tone at first in flexor muscles (paraplegia in flexion). 
Return of flexor withdrawal reflex. 
Return of knee jerk, then ankle jerk. 
Return of ABP to normal during rest (due to return of muscle tone that 
increases the venous return), but regulation of blood pressure changes is 
inappropriate.

v Return of reflex evacuation of bladder and rectum as in infants, without 
voluntary control (automatic bladder and rectum, filling to about 150 ml and 
then evacuation occurs). 
Sexual reflexes of erection and ejaculation can be induced by local genital 
tactile stimulation that sends impulses to stimulate autonomic LHCs in 
lumbar and sacral segments. However, the cortical sexual desire and act is 
never regained. 
Mass reflex: Mass reflex can be elicited in some cases by scratching of the 
skin over the lower limbs or the anterior abdominal wall. The response will 
be: spasm of flexor muscles of both limbs, evacuation of rectum and 
bladder, profuse sweating below the level of lesion, contraction of 
abdominal muscles, erection, elevation of ABP. 
This reflex is explained by the developed denervation hypersensitivity, 
release phenomenon from inhibitory gamma motor neurons and increased 
collaterals in the spinal cord. The response of these changes is that a large 
portion of the spinal cord is stimulated at the same time with a possibility 
of irradiation and reverberating circuits between these collateral neurons. 

- After some time: 
v Muscle tone increases in extensors (paraplegia in extension) 
v Flexor withdrawal reflex is associated with crossed extensor reflex. 
v Return of infantile reflexes which were suppressed by corticospinal tract e.g. 

grasp reflex and positive Babinski sign. 
v Hyperactivity of the returned reflexes may be present. 

3- Thalamic syndrome: 
It is due to hematoma or thrombosis of thalamo-geniculate artery. 
Manifestation: 
A) Immediate effects (acute stage): 

i. Crossed Hemi-anesthesia = loss of all sensations on the opposite side of the body 
and face. 

ii. Secondary hyperalgesia due to release or rebound from gate inhibitory fibers. 
iif. Sensory ataxia. 

B) Delayed effects: 
1) Recovery of crude sensations but become more poorly localized and with high 

threshold (more crude). 
2) Fine sensation never recover. 
3) Secondary hyperalgesia 
4) Paroxysmal attacks of pain due to release from gate inhibition 
5) Thalamic Hyperpathia = unpleasant sensation but not pain. 
6) Emotional disturbances 
7) Motor ataxia

Basal nuclei are masses of grey matter lying within each cerebral hemisphere near its base.

-  Lentiform Nucleus (formed of putamen medially & globus pallidus laterally) 

e Amygdaloid Nuclei (part of limbic system). 
 Claustrum (grey matter that lies lateral to Lentiform N and separated from it by external 

capsule). It is separated from grey matter of insula by the extreme capsule. It is of unknown 

function.

a- Substantia nigra: 

It is generally considered part of the basal ganglia due to its similar neuronal structure 
and related function of motor control but may also be considered part of midbrain due to 
its location. It contains most of the neurotransmitter dopamine in the brain which play a 
great role in motor coordination. The substantia nigra is divided into a pars compacta and 
a pars reticulata 

b- Subthalamic nucleus: 
It is located lateral to the red nucleus and dorsal to substantia nigra. 

Functions: concerned with muscular activity. They help the motor cortex in learning of motor 
skills (e.g., writing the alphabet) & also prepare the body posture to suit the movement. 

Blood Supply: Central branches of ant. & middle cerebral arteries. Veins drain into 
thalamostriate vein.

Fig. 3.59: Parts of basal ganglia 
Parts of basal ganglia: refer to anatomy lecture 
e The basal ganglia cannot function alone by itself but acts in close association with the 

cerebral cortex (like cerebellum). They do not give direct descending tracts to spinal cord 
motor neurons but control motor functions through their connection to brain stem nuclei 
(i.e. the basal ganglia is a chief part of extra pyramidal system). 

e The corpus striatum receives the afferent input fibers to the basal ganglia, while the globus 
pallidus represents the main efferent pathway from the basal ganglia. 

Connection of basal ganglia can be summarized as follows

2. Connection between nuclei of basal ganglia 

1- Putamen, globus pallidus, subthalamus, thalamus, motor cortex. 
2- Putamen, globus pallidus, substantia nigra, thalamus, motor cortex. 

3. Brain stem connections 
Fibers from globus pallidus project to: 
1- Reticular formation 2- Red nucleus 3- Vestibular nucleus 

Neurotransmitters in basal ganglia: 
1- Fibers from cerebral cortex to corpus striatum release glutamate. 
2- Intrastriatal interneurons (caudate-putamen circuit) release acetyl choline. 
3- Fibers from Substantia nigra to corpus striatum release dopamine. 
4- Fibers from corpus striatum to substantia nigra release GABA. 

5- Fibers from basal ganglia to brain stem release noradrenalin, encephalin and serotonin. 

Caudaten ucleus 

Fig. 3.62: Neurotransmitters in basal ganglia 

Function of the complex neural circuits that link the basal nuclei with each other, with the 

thalamus, and with the cerebral cortex: 
e These feedback loops, some of which are stimulatory and others inhibitory. 

182 



CHAPTER Ili: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

e The stimulatory circuits facilitate muscle activity, especially at the beginning of a 
voluntary movement like rising from a sitting position or beginning to walk. 

eo The inhibitory circuits facilitate the actions of the stimulatory circuits by inhibiting 
unwanted muscle activity. 

e The proper function of the basal ganglia depends on the balance between the 
excitatory neurotransmitter mainly Ach and the inhibitory neurotransmitter dopamine 
inside the cortico ~striatal —cortical feedback circuits 

Direct Pathway (Fig. 3.63) 
o The cortical inputs to the striatum is excitatory (glutamate) 
o Striatal outflow connects directly with the internal globus pallidus (GPi) (GABA, 

inhibitory). 
o It disinhibits the thalamus facilitating the thalamocortical pathway (it removes the 

inhibitory effect of GPi on the thalamus). 
o Thalamic projections excite the motor cortices 
o ltis an excitatory cortical-cortical circuit that likely functions to maintain ongoing motor 

activity. 
Indirect Pathway (Fig. 3.63) 
o The cortical input to the striatum is excitatory (glutamate). 

Outflow first traverses the GPe and subthalamic nucleus (STN) then connects to the GPi 
The net effect is the disinhibition of the STN 
Thus STN excites the GPi pathway which inhibits the thalamocortical pathway 
Therefore, the net effect is cortical inhibition. 
Therefore, the net effect of indirect pathway is cortical inhibition and suppression of 
muscle activity

Fig. 3.63: Direct and Indirect pathway of putamen circuit 

Effects of dopamine and acetylcholine on these pathways: 
1- Dopamine in the nigrostriatal fibers has the following effects: 

DA=Excites the direct pathway through its action on D1 receptors. 
DA=Inhibits the indirect pathway through its action on D; receptors 
So, DA is facilitatory to the movement either by stimulation of the facilitatory direct 
pathway (via D1-R) or by inhibition to the inhibitory indirect pathway (via D2-R) 

2- Acetylcholine in the striatum has the following effects: 
It is present in balance with dopamine to facilitate the movement. 

183 

O OO O O O 



CHAPTER Iii: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

What happen if degeneration in the dopamine releasing neurons occurs? 
The activity in the direct pathway decreases and accordingly there is a decrease in the cerebral 

cortex activity. This appears clearly in the hypokinesia and slow voluntary movements with a 

disease called parkinsonism. Additionally, when the activity of the indirect pathway increases 
(which is inhibitory to cortex) = helping in hypokinesia. 
Functions of basal ganglia 

1- Regulation of muscle tone 
The effect of caudate is stimulatory on muscle tone mean while the lentiform nucleus 

decreases the muscle tone. But in general, the basal ganglion has an inhibitory effect on 
the muscle tone. 
Mechanism of inhibitory action of muscle tone 
a- It mainly decreases the activity of a motor neurons through decreasing the activity of 

the motor cortex. 
b- It decreases the activity of the y motor neurons through stimulation of the inhibitory 

reticular formation. 
2- Role in voluntary movements 

v' Although there are no descending pathways that connect basal ganglia directly to the 
spinal cord, the feedback circuits between it and all areas of cerebral cortex are involved 
in planning and programming of the voluntary movements. 

v’ The basal ganglia may act in conjunction with the supplementary motor cortex, to 
generate highly learned movement modules. 

v' They also seem to play a major role in controlling the proximal (axial) musculature, via 
the "extra-pyramidal" (mainly ventro-medial) pathways. 

The basal ganglia perform the following functions: 
A. Storage of the programs of some learned voluntary movements. The programs of 

some familiar movements which are done automatically and repeated are stored in the 
basal ganglia (putamen circuit) e.g. running when you see a rapid car approaching you, 

writing, eating, driving cars, signature & lighting candle. Lesion to this circuit leads to 

motor apraxia (i.e. as in lesion of area 6) loss of the ability to do familiar movements in 
absence of paralysis. 

B. Planning of motor activity 
The ideas and thoughts to do certain voluntary movements are translated into motor 
activity and controlled in the basal ganglia (caudate circuit) (cognitive control of motor 
activity of muscle). This includes the steps to do a voluntary movement, what muscles 
to be used, the duration, amplitude, speed and the scale of movements. 

C. Provision of postural background for the fine voluntary movements. 

Through adjusting the muscle tone of the proximal parts of the body (mainly via the 

ventro-medial pathways), the basal ganglia provide automatically the necessary 
postural tone to facilitate the more regulated skilled actions of the distal musculature 
i.e. it is a bridge between proximal and distal motor system (through ventro-medial 

pathways ). 
3- Automatic associated movements 

It is concerned (with area 6) with initiation of automatic associated movements carried out 

subconsciously (ex: swinging of arms during walking, fascial expression, gesture 
movements reflecting emotional state). 

184 



CHAPTER lil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

4- in lower animals, BG is highest motor center: so, it functions as primary motor cortex (i.e. 
like area '4'). 

v" The basal ganglia then, appear to be able either to promote and facilitate motor 
activity or to inhibit it - and probably does both: suppressing unwanted actions which 
might interfere with a desired movement, whilst amplifying and facilitating those 
processes which contribute in the genesis of the desired movement. 

v’ This could give some insight into the physiological basis for the observation that 
disorders of the basal ganglia may give rise either to excessive, uncontrolled, 
involuntary movements, or to hypokinesia and difficulty in initiating voluntary actions, 
or to combinations of both. 

Basal ganglia lesions 

The clinical picture depends on the nuclei affected. 

Chorea = dancing 
Causes: 

e Lesion in the caudate nucleus (degeneration of GABA releasing neurons from striatum 
to globus pallidus externa), leading to decrease activity of indirect pathway as shown 
in (Fig. 3.67) due to 

1- As a complication for rheumatic fever (Sydenham’s chorea), self-limited (no 
dementia). 

2- Congenital (Huntington’s chorea) = (progressive dementia). 
3- Prolonged use of L dopa ( Tardive parkinsonism). 

e Dementia means decreased memory and cognitive function. 
Clinical picture: 

1- Hypotonia and pendular knee jerk. 
2- Involuntary movements: sudden semi-purposeful movements 

v' dancing movements of arms, legs, face, head and trunk 
v" increase by emotions and decrease by sleep. 

Athetosis = no fixed position (Mobile spasm) 
Causes: 

e¢ Lesion in lentiform nuclei mainly globus pallidus (degeneration of GABA releasing 
neurons which project to the thalamus). 

Clinical picture 
1- Hypertonia. 
2- Involuntary movements = slow twisting movements. 

v' involving distal parts: hands and fingers and face and neck. 
v' snake-like movement= pronation with flexion followed by supination with 

extension. 
v" increase by emotions, decrease by sleep). 

Hemiballismus 
Cause: 

e Lesion in subthalamic nuclei. When glutamate decreases in subthalamic nucleus, it 
couldn't carry out its normal role in exciting the globus pallidus, the globus pallidus 
becomes inhibited). 

185 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Clinical picture: 
1- Hypertonia. 
2- Involuntary movements: Attacks of rapid violent movements 

v" involving half of the body that may cause loss of balance. 
v' Also, it may be fatal due to spasm of the respiratory muscles 

PARKINSONISM HLMIBALLISMLS CHOREA 

IY Brain Stem, ; Brain Stem, da Brain Stem, 
Spinal Cord Spinal Cord Spinal Cord Spinal Cord 

Fig. 3.64: Disorders of basal ganglia pathways. 

Parkinsonism = Paralysis agitans (old misnomer) 

Definition: 
It is a degenerative disease affecting substantia nigra, leading to damage of nigrostriatal 

fibers with loss of dopamine secretion in the caudate and putamen nuclei, resulting in 

imbalance between acetylcholine and dopamine with actual decrease of dopamine and 

relative increase of acetyl choline. 
Causes of Parkinsonism (most common in the elderly, > 65 yrs.) 
1. Idiopathic (Parkinson's disease): 80-85% 

Due to unknown neurotoxin or oxidation reactions with free radical generation = 

neuronal degeneration (environmental & genetic factors may be responsible). 

2. Viral encephalitis, vascular (post-stroke), trauma, Poison (CO or manganese) 

3. Drug-induced Parkinsonism: 
- Antipsychotics, antiemetics (metoclopramide) -* block D2 receptors. 

- Reserpine, Tetrabenazine -> depletes DA stores. 
- Methyldopa =» inhibits DA synthesis. 

Clinical picture: 
1- Rigidity (pseudo-paralysis): 

v' It is increase muscle tone that lead to stiffness of muscle during movements. 

186 



CHAPTER IIL: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

v" It occurs in both flexors and extensors (but more in flexor muscles causing flexor 
attitude of the patient). 

v' It is mainly alpha rigidity. 
v" It may be in form of; 

a) Cog wheel rigidity i.e. muscular resistance occurs in series during limb flexion. 
b) Lead pipe rigidity i.e. muscular resistance occurs all through limb flexion. This 

occurs due to rigidity of agonist and antagonist muscles. 
v' Mechanism of rigidity 

As the net effect of basal ganglia on muscle tone is inhibitory so it is expected to 
see hypertonia with its lesion due to increased excitability of motor neurons. 
In addition inhibitory effect of basal ganglia to cerebral cortex is lost so 
corticospinal activity is increased and called alpha rigidity 

2- Tremors = (Static tremors) 
v’ {tis characterized by: 

- Involuntary, regular, rapid movements in the form of pill rolling, coin counting, 
alternating pronation and supination in the hands. It may involve lower jaw 
and neck. 

- Disappear during sleep (because it is caused by increased cortical activity). 
- Exaggerated by emotions and fatigue. 
- Diminished by voluntary movements. 

v" Mechanism 
v' It may be due to the associated oscillatory discharge (on and off) in the globus 

pallidus interna and thalamic nucleus (ventralis intermedius) resulting in alternate 
activation and inactivation of the cortical motor areas. These oscillation involve 
multiple neurotransmitters as Ach, NE, GABA and to lesser extent DA and 5-HT 

3- Akinesia= poverty of movements 
Hypokinesia = reduction in speed of voluntary movement. 
v' Manifestation: rigidity affect all muscle of head and body except extraocular 

muscles. 
. Difficulty in initiation of voluntary movement 
Mask face (no blinking.) and staring appearance 

. Monotonous slurred slow speech. 

. Difficulty in buttoning cloths 
Necktie before shirt 
Lack of subconscious movements e.g. swinging of arms. 

7. Shuffling gait {short noisy gait) with trunk bending forward. 
v' Mechanism 

1- The increased inhibition of thalamo-cortical neurons is associated with 
decreased activity in supplementary motor area due to loss of planning and 
programming functions of BG. 

2- The increased muscle tone and rigidity interferes with initiation of movements. 
All lead to avoidance to do any movements so patient looks like statue, even 
simple act like walking, rising up from chair needs mental effort and 
concentration. 

N.B.: Rigidity is accompanied by norm-reflexia and not hyperreflexia as both agonist 
and antagonists are affected.

4- Postural disturbances and tendency to frequent fall. 
5- Non-motor manifestations: Cognitive impairment as Depression 

Neurodegenerative Diseases of basal ganglia 

DEFINITION: Degenerative diseases of the CNS are disorders characterized by the cellular 

degeneration of subsets of neurons that typically are related by function, rather than by 

physical location in the brain. Many of these disorders are associated with the accumulation 
of abnormal proteins. 

e They can be classified in a number of different ways including via the clinical presentation, 

type of protein that accumulates, pathological processes and underlying genetic 

abnormalities. 
e Neurodegenerative diseases that affect the neurons of the basal ganglia result in 

movement disorders. (Parkinson’s disease and Huntington chorea).

DRUG THERAPY OF PARKINSONISM 
The basal ganglia exert a tonic inhibitory effect on muscle tone & are responsible for proper 

initiation & modulation of movement. These effects require a normal balance in the basal 

ganglia between Ach & DA. 
In Parkinson's disease, degeneration of nigrostriatal DA neurons = { striatal DA & dominance 

of cholinergic system (Imbalance between Ach & DA) resulting in: 

1. Bradykinesia or akinesia. 2. Tremors 3. Rigidity. 
Drug-induced Parkinsonism: | 

1. Antipsychotics (AP) (Typical AP e.g. Haloperidol >Atypical AP)—> block D2 receptors. 

2. Antiemetics (Metoclopramide) —> block D2 receptors. 

3. Reserpine, Tetrabenazine (VMATI)—> depletes DA stores. 

4. Methyldopa (Aldomet) = inhibits DA synthesis. 
Antiparkinsonian Drugs aim to restore DA/Ach balance.

1. Levodopa (L-dopa) 
eo L-dopa is a DA precursor that crosses BBB and is decarboxylated to DA in the CNS. 

It is given with the peripheral decarboxylase inhibitor, carbidopa as L-dopa/carbidopa 
(Sinemet). 

eo Carbidopa prevents peripheral decarboxylation of L-dopa to DA = { dose of L-dopa by 
75%. (without carbidopa, 99-97% of L-dopa is converted to DA peripherally which cannot 
cross BBB & T peripheral side effects of L-dopa). 

e |-dopa is the most effective antiparkinsonian drug (Mainstay of Therapy); improves all 
features especially bradykinesia®. 

Pharmacokinetic of Levodopa 
e absorbed rapidly from small intestine. 
e has short half-life (1-2 h). 
¢ Protein interferes with its transport into the GIT & CNS. So, Levodopa should be taken 

on empty stomach, 30 min before a meal.

Management of motor complications in advanced Parkinson's disease 

A. L-dopa-induced nausea & vomiting can be managed by Domperidone (but NOT 

Metoclopramide) as antiemetic because it blocks peripheral Dz receptors in CIZ (outside 

BBB) but not central Dz receptors in basal ganglia (doesn’t cross BBB) => doesn’t interfere 

with anti-parkinsonian effect of L-dopa. 
B. L-dopa motor complications such as dyskinesias & fluctuations (end of dose akinesia & 

on/off effect), can be managed by: 
Long-acting L-dopa/carbidopa formulation (Sinemet CR) 
Add MAO-B inhibitors. 
Add DA agonists {oral or transdermal). 
Add Intermittent subcutaneous apomorphine (=> {'off' time). 

Add COMT inhibitors = J'off' time in advanced cases with motor fluctuations. 

6. Add Amantadine (mainly as Anti-dyskinesia). 

C. Management of psychosis: atypical antipsychotics clozapine or quetiapine are preferred 

(but not typical antipsychotics as Haloperidol) because they are more selective on 

mesolimbic D2 receptors preserving D2 receptors in basal ganglia = doesn’t interfere with 

anti-parkinsonian effect of L-dopa. 
Drug Interactions with levodopa:

2. Typical AP e.g. Haloperidol => 1 Parkinsonism 
3. MAO inhibitors = T CAT = Hypertension 

Nutritional Care in Parkinson's disease “PD”: (especially in relation to L-dopa) 

1. Avoid pyridoxine (Vit. B6): 1 peripheral decarboxylation of L-dopa = | effect. 

2. Diet: low in protein to { fluctuation in L-dopa response (NH2 acids compete with L-dopa 

= labsorption & CNS uptake). 
3. Diet rich in fibers & fluid for constipation (common in PD). 

4. Levodopa should be taken on empty stomach, 30 min before meal.

Il. Anticholinergics 
Benztropine - Trihexphenidyl 

e Block central cholinergic activity > restore DA/Ach balance. 

e Improve tremors (mainly) & rigidity, but less effective on bradykinesia. 

Used in: 

ii. Drug induced Parkinsonism: preferred to dopaminergic drugs, since dopaminergic 

agents cannot be used in antipsychotic-induced Parkinsonism because DA receptors 

are blocked by antipsychotics & dopaminergic agents may aggravate the psychotic 

disorder of the patient. 
iii. Parkinson disease (especially in the young ): adjuvants to dopaminergic drugs in cases 

presenting with tremors & to control sialorrhea. 

Side Effects: 

e Similar to atropine but less severe (dry mouth, constipation, confusion......). 

e lt may delay gastric emptying => ¥ L-dopa absorption. 

Caution in elderly (avoid in patients over 70 yrs) 

e 1 Memory loss & hallucinations, Urine retention in enlarged prostate. 

If anticholinergics are contraindicated: 
- Beta adrenergic blockers e.g., propranolol may be used for tremors. 

- Sialorrhea can be treated Locally by botulinum toxin injections, and sublingual atropine. 

Choice of druin gEarsly Parkinson's Disease'* (NICE guidelines, 2017) 

e First choice agents in Parkinson's disease (PD) & motor symptoms include: 

- L-dopa/carbidopa. 
- Non ergot dopamine agonists (oral/transdermal). 

- MAO-B inhibitors. 
1. Offer levodopa to people in the early stages of PD whose motor symptoms impact on 

their quality of life. (The most effective agent for motor symptoms) 

2. Consider a choice of dopamine agonists, levodopa or monoamine oxidase B (MAO-B) 

inhibitors for people in the early stages of PD whose motor symptoms do not impact on 

their quality of life. 
3. Do not offer ergot-derived dopamine agonists as first-line treatment for PD. 

Amantadine: is used mainly as anti-dyskinesia (If dyskinesia is not adequately managed by 

modifying existing therapy, consider amantadine, NICE 2017). 

Anti-cholinergics: it may be added if tremors are troublesome, especially in young patients. 

14 Therapy with 2 or more agents will eventually be necessary as disease progresses. 

Management of depression by antidepressants. 
Management of dementia: Rivastigmine.

THE CEREBELLUM 

ANATOMY OF CEREBELLUM 
e [tis the largest part of the hindbrain. 
e |tlies inthe posterior cranial fossa with the tentorium cerebelli roofing over and separating 

it from the cerebrum. It lies posterior to the pons and medulla with the cavity of the fourth 
ventricle intervening. 

e [tt has two notches; an anterior notch which lodges the pons and medulla, and a posterior 
notch that lodges the falx cerebelli. 

e Itis formed of two cerebellar hemispheres joined by a median narrow part, the vermis. 
e The surface of the cerebellum shows many fissures. A deep fissure, the horizontal fissure, 

lies along the margin of the cerebellum and separates the superior from the inferior 
surface. 

Surfaces of thec erebellum (Figs. 3.66 & 3.67) 
I. Superior Surface: 

It shows the superior vermis raised more than the cerebellar hemispheres. It also shows a 
deep fissure, Fissura prima, that separates anterior lobe of cerebellum from posterior lobe 
of cerebellum. 

II. Inferior Surface: 
The inferior vermis is present in a deep groove, vallecula that lies between the two 
cerebellar hemispheres. A posterolateral fissure separates the posterior lobe from the 
flocculo-nodular lobe.

Lobes of the Cerebellum 

Anatomically the cerebellum is divided into 3 lobes: 

Anterior lobe in front of the fissura prima, Posterior lobe behind fissura prima, and 

Flocculonodular lobe separated from posterior lobe by the posterolateral fissure. 

Functionally the Cerebellum is also divided into 3 lobes (Fig. 3.68): 

1. Archicerebellum

Histological Structure of the Cerebellum 
The cerebellum is formed of: 

I) Gray matter, that includes: 
a) The outer cortex. 
b) Four pairs of intrinsic cerebellar nuclei. 

II) White matter: formed of afferent and efferent fibers. 
Cerebellar cortex (Fig. 3.74): 
it is composed of narrow laminae or folia. Each folium contains a core of white matter covered 
by a gray matter. 
The cerebellar cortex is formed of the following three layers: 

I) Molecular layer: the outermost layer. It contains two types of cells: 
a) Outer stellate cells: 

These cells are found in the superficial part of the molecular layer. They have small 
cell bodies with short dendrites located in the same layer. Their axons pass parallel 
to the surface of the cerebellum, to synapse with the dendrites of Purkinje cells. 

b) Basket cells: 
These cells are found deep in the molecular layer. Their dendrites are found in the 
same layer. Their axons form basket-like terminal arborizations around Purkinje cell 
bodies, where a single cell makes synapse with many cells up to 10 cells. 
e Fibers of the molecular layer: This layer contains: 

a) Dendritic arborizations and axons of its own cells. 
b) The dendrites of cells in the deeper layers {Purkinje cells and Golgi type I cells). 
c) Climbing fibers, the afferent fibers to the cerebellum. 
d) Axons of granule cells. 
e) Recurrent axons of Purkinje cells.

Fig. 3.74: Layers of the Cerebellar Cortex 

199 



CHAPTER lil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Purkinje cell layer: (Fig.3.75) 

e itis the middle layer of the cerebellar cortex. it is formed 

of one type of cells (Purkinje cell), arranged uniformly in 

a single plane along the upper margin of granular layer. 

e Each cell has a large pyriform, flask-shaped cell body. It 
has a vesicular central nucleus and coarse cytoplasmic 
Nissl’s granules. 

e Their dendrites form an elaborate fan-like flattened 
dendritic tree, at right angles to the long axis of folia. 
These dendrites branch to give rise to primary, secondary 
and tertiary branches. Both primary and secondary 

branches are smooth, while the tertiary branches show 

spines or gemmules. 

e Purkinje cell axons are myelinated passing through the 

granular layer and white matter to synapse with deep 

cerebellar nuclei. They represent the efferent pathway 

from the cortex. 

Fig. 3.75: Purkinje cells 
ll) Granular layer: 

It is formed of closely packed cells separated by non-cellular 
spaces called cerebellar islands (Glomeruli). 
There are two types of cells: 
a) Granule cells: 

These are small cells having large rounded nuclei and thin rim of cytoplasm with few 

Nissl’s bodies. Their dendrites show claw like endings that terminate in the 

glomerulus. Their axons ascend to the molecular layer, where they bifurcate to give 

rise to parallel fibers that run parallel to the surface. They synapse with the 

dendrites of Purkinje cells. 
b) Golgi type Ii cells: 

These are found in the upper parts of the granular layer. Their dendrites extend in all 

layers of the cortex, while their axons end within the cerebellar glomeruli. 
Cerebellar islands (Glomeruli) { Fig. 3.76): 

These are irregularly dispersed pale non cellular areas, composed of a complex 

synaptic structure. Each glomerulus is formed of: 

1) Mossy fiber terminal. 
2) Dendrites of numerous granule cells. 
3) Axons of Golgi type li cells. 

Function: This structure excites granular cells which in turn excite Purkinje cells. Axons of 

granule cells (unmyelinated fibers), ascend vertically to the molecular layer and synapses 

with Purkinje cell dendrites. Granule cells are the only excitatory neurons in the cerebellar 

cortex. 

N.B: 

200 



CHAPTER III: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

e Purkinje cells are the key cells in the cerebellar cortex. They receive 2 types of afferent 
fibers: Climbing fibers directly from the olivary nuclei & Mossy fibers which include all 
other cerebellar afferents which relay first on granule cells. 

oe Purkinje cell axons are inhibitory to cerebellar nuclei which send all cerebellar 
efferents. All purkinje cells discharge to cerebellar nuclei except in the flocculonodular 
lobe where they discharge directly to vestibular nuclei.

The white matter of the cerebellum: 4 

It includes the afferent and the efferent fibers of the cerebellum. 
1) Afferent fibers: are mainly via ICP and MCP. 

a. Climbing fibers: these are the olivocerebellar fibers. These pass from the white 

matter through the granular and Purkinje cell layer to reach dendrites of Purkinje cells. 
These non-myelinated fibers give branches and climb the smooth dendritic branches 
of Purkinje cells. Stimulation of the climbing fibers excites the Purkinje cells. 

b. Mossy fibers: they include all the afferents except the olivocerebellar fibers. 

a) Spinocerebellar tracts. 

b) Cuneocerebellar tract. 
c) Vestibulocerebellar 
d) Pontocerebellar fibers. 
e) Reticulo-cerebellar. 
f) Ventral external arcuate fibers.

These fibers enter the white matter of the cerebellum, then they enter the granular 
layer of the cortex, where they give collaterals and fine lobulated enlargements or 
rosettes. Each rosette forms the center of a cerebellar glomerulus. 

2) Efferent fibers: 
a. Some axons of Purkinje cells project to deep cerebellar nuclei, other axons project to 

vestibular nuclei. 
b. Fibers from deep cerebellar nuclei project to vestibular nuclei.

Physiology of the cerebellum 
The cerebellum (little brain) is a pure motor structure: 
it represents about 11% of brain mass and located dorsal to the pons and medulla. 

- It controls fine movement coordination, balance and equilibrium and muscle tone. 
- its lesions cause a movement incoordination syndrome called motor ataxia. 
- It differs from the cerebrum in: 

1- Cerebellum is not involved in sensory perception. 
2- Cerebellar lesion as in B.G lesion is not associated with paralysis. 
3- Cerebellar representation is of the ipsilateral side of the body. 
4- The body is represented in vermis and intermediate part of cerebellar hemisphere in 

2 maps: In the upper map (ant. Lobe), body is represented ipsilaterally and upside- 

down. In lower map (post. Lobe), body is represented ipsilaterally and upright. Audio- 

visual representation is bilateral and upright. 
Connections of the cerebellum 
Afferent (input) nerve fibers to the cerebellum: The cerebellum receives both sensory and 

motor information through many afferent tracts. 

202 



CHAPTER Ili: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Outputs from the cerebellum: They originate from the cerebellar deep nuclei. Thus, a lesion 
to the cerebellar nuclei has the same effect as a complete lesion of the entire cerebellum. 

Functional division of the cerebellum in relation to deep cerebellar nuclei and their outputs: 
1. Vestibulocerebellum: Most of its output passes directly to brainstem where it relays to the 

vestibular nuclei. 
2. Spinocerebellum: 

» Output from the vermis through fastigial nucleus—> brainstem. 
» Output from intermediate zone of cerebellar hemisphere through interposed nucleus to 

a) VA & VL nuclei of thalamus, then to cerebral cortex, b) several midline structures of 
the thalamus, then to basal ganglia and the reticular formation and red nucleus. 

3. Cerebrocerebellum: 
Projects to dentate nucleus VA & VL nuclei of thalamus, then to cerebral cortex.

» The afferent inputs to the cerebellum are mainly of two types. Climbing fiber type and 

mossy fiber type. 
> Climbing fibers all originate from inferior olives of the medulla. (releasing aspartate) 

» The mossy fibers are all the other fibers that enter the cerebellum from multiple sources- 

the higher brain, brain stem, and spinal cord. (releasing glutamate) 

> The output from the whole cerebellum is from the deep nuclear cells. These cells are 

continuously under both excitatory and inhibitory influences. 

» The excitatory influences arise from direct connections with afferent fibers that enter the 

cerebellum from the brain or the periphery. 
» The inhibitory influence arises entirely from the Purkinje cell in the cortex of the 

cerebellum (releasing GABA) 
» Normally, the balance between these two effects is slightly in favor of excitation so that 

under quiet condition, output from the deep nuclear cell remains relatively constant at a 

moderate level of continuous stimulation. 

Turn-on/turn-off output signals from the cerebellum:

Fig.3.81: showing the general organization of neuronal connections in the cerebellum 

A- In execution of well learned rapid motor movement: 

the initiating signal from the cerebral motor cortex or brain stem at first greatly 

increases deep nuclear cell excitation. Then, another few milliseconds later, feedback 

inhibitory signals from the Purkinje cell circuit arrive. In this way, there is first a rapid 

excitatory signal sent by the deep nuclear cells into the motor output pathway to 

enhance the motor movement, followed within another small fraction of a second by 

an inhibitory signal. This inhibitory signal resembles a delay-line negative feedback 

signal of the type that is effective in providing damping. That is, when the motor system 

is excited, a negative feedback signal occurs after a short delay to stop the muscle 

movement from overshooting its mark. Otherwise, oscillation of the movement would 

Occur. 

204 



CHAPTER Ili: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

B- During training on new voluntary motor act 
v' During training on a new movement, Inferior olivary nucleus functions as an 

"error detector." It receives signals from motor cortex, basal ganglia, reticular formation. 
It compares these signals from brain with those from spinal cord and informs the 
cerebellum via olivocerebellar pathway through climbing fibers. 

v' This direct powerful activation of the target Purkinje cells through the climbing 
fibers and the increase in calcium influx in Purkinje cells (is called conditioning) results 
in powerful activation of Purkinje cells releasing GABA and inhibits the neurons of 
deep cerebellar nuclei hopefully terminating the unwanted component of the action. 

v" when the movement become familiar movement inputs from climbing fibers 
become of low frequency (1spike/sec) and have a minor influence on the overall firing 
rate of the Purkinje cell where the effect of mossy fibers is clearer causing Purkinje cell 
basal firing with high frequency (70 spike /sec). 

N.B.: Cerebellar circuits are characterized by: 
1) No reverberators in these circuits so cerebellar effects are transient. 
2) During activity output signal from cerebellum is initial transient excitation followed by 

sudden transient inhibition.

Functions of the Cerebellum 

1- Controls equilibrium and body posture: 
Input: it receives inputs signals from vestibular receptors in inner ear with movement of 
head and from proprioceptors 
Output: its output is through ({fastigial nucleus) to the vestibulospinal tract and 
reticulospinal tract 
Effects: 

205 



CHAPTER Hil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

A- by affecting the axial muscles (trunk and girdle muscles) mainly during movements that 
involve changes in body directions. 

B- it modulates commands to motor neurons to compensate for shifts in body position or 
changes in load upon muscles. 

C- Control balance between agonist and antagonist muscles during rapid change in body 
position. 

2- Controls eye movements (vestibulo-ocular reflex): 
It receives afferent from superior colliculus and visual cortex and its output Through 

vestibular

Fig.3.82: showing pathway for vestibulo-ocular reflex 

N.B.: In people with vestibulocerebellar dysfunction, equilibrium is far more disturbed during 
performance of movement, especially when these movements involve changes in direction of 
movement and stimulate the semicircular ducts. Lesion in vestibulocerebellum result in truncal 
ataxia and drunken gait. Vertigo and vomiting are common associating symptoms 

In medulloblastoma, tumor common in children affecting vestibulocerebellum no ability to 
stand without support and high incidence of truncal ataxia. 

3- Coordination of voluntary movement: 
Most movements are composed of a number of different muscle groups acting together 
in a temporally coordinated fashion. One major function of the cerebellum is to 
coordinate the timing and force of these different muscle groups to produce fluid limb 
or body movements. 

206 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

A) The spinocerebellum ensures coordination of voluntary movement through 
the following: 
a- Servo-comparator function: 

Fig.3.83: showing servocomparator function of Spinocerebellum 

v" The spinocerebellum receives two types of information when a movement is 
performed: 
(1) Information from the cerebral motor cortex (area 4) and from the midbrain 

red nucleus, through pontine nuclei {in cortico-ponto- cerebellar tract) 
reach to spinocerebellum (efference copy) telling the cerebellum the 
intended sequential plan of movement for the next few fractions of a 
second, and 

(2) Feedback information from the body (tension in tendon, length of muscle, 
change in length) through dorsal spinocerebellar tract telling the 
cerebellum what actual movements result. (afference copy) 

(3) copy of the actual motor control signals that reach anterior motor neurons. 
(reafference copy) carried in ventral spinocerebellar tract. 

v' After the spinocerebellum has compared the intended movement with the 
actual movements, the deep nuclear cells of the fastigial nucleus & interposed 
nucleus send corrective output signals that has braking function to prevent 
overshooting {hypermetria). 

v' This output signals are: 
1) Back to the cerebral motor cortex through relay nuclei in the thalamus. 
2) To the lower portion of red nucleus that gives rise to the rubrospinal tract. 
3) Brainstem areas that give rise to medial brainstem pathways. 

N.B.: Similar comparative signals also go to the inferior olivary complex to correct 
newly learned voluntary movement 

» Control of ballistic movements: 
Ballistic movements are those which occur very rapidly e.g. fingers during typing 
or piano playing and the eyes during reading. They are pre-planned to go to a 
specific distance then stop, but because of their high rapidity, the servo 

207 



CHAPTER Iii: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

comparator mechanism cannot operate. The Mossy circuit is important in this 

condition as it detects the turn on (initiation), duration (according to what is 

planned by the brain) and finally turn off (termination) of movement. The built- 

in timing circuits of the cerebellar cortex are fundamental to this particular ability 
of the cerebellum. 

b- Damping function: 
v' Almost all movements of the body are pendular. For instance, when an arm is 

moved, momentum develops, and the momentum must be overcome before the 

movement can be stopped. Because of momentum, all pendular movements 

have a tendency to overshoot. 
v If overshooting occurs in person whose cerebellum has been destroyed, the 

conscious centers of the cerebrum eventually recognize this occurrence and 

initiate movement in the reverse direction to attempt to bring the arm to the 

intended position. However, the arm, by virtue of its momentum, overshoots 

once more in the opposite direction, and appropriate corrective signals must 
again be instituted. 

v' Thus, the arm oscillates back and forth past its intended point for several cycles 
before it finally fixes on its mark. This effect is called an action tremor or intention 
tremors. 

v If the cerebellum is intact, appropriate learned, subconscious signals stop the 
movement precisely at the intended point by contraction of agonist and 
antagonist, thereby preventing the overshoot and the tremor. 

B) The role of cerebrocerebellum in the control of voluntary movement: 
a- Planning of sequential movements: 
v' The planning of sequential movements requires that the lateral zones of the 

hemispheres communicate with both the premotor and sensory portions of the 
cerebral cortex, and it requires two-way communication between these 

cerebral cortex areas with the corresponding areas of the basal ganglia. 
v It seems that the plan of sequential movements actually begins in the sensory 

and premotor areas of the cerebral cortex, and from there the plan is 
transmitted to the lateral zones of the cerebellar hemispheres. 

v' Then, amid much two- way traffic between the cerebellum and the cerebral 

cortex, appropriate motor signals provide transition from one sequence of 

movement to the next. 
b- Predictive function: 

v" The cerebrocerebellum predicts the next movement at the same time a present 

movement is occurring. This function is necessary for smooth transition from 
one movement to the next (thus joining the sequential movements and 
preventing decomposition). 

¢- Timing function for sequential movements: 
v' An important function of the cerebrocerebellum is to provide appropriate 

timing for each succeeding movement. Without this timing capability, the 
person becomes unable to determine when the next sequential movement 
needs to begin. 

208 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

v' As a result, the succeeding movement may begin too early or, more likely, too 
late. 

d- Extra motor predictive functions of the cerebrocerebellum: 
v" The cerebrocerebelium helps to time events other than movements of the 

body. For instance, the rates of progression of both auditory and visual 
phenomena can be predicted by the brain, but both require cerebellar 
participation. 

v" As an example, a person can predict from the changing visual scene how 
rapidly he is approaching an object (e.g.: the time which you will hit the 
tennis ball when it touches the table). 

C) Control of stretch reflex 
The neocerebellum is excitatory to stretch reflex by increasing activity in pontine 
reticulospinal tract and increasing the gamma neuron discharge to muscle spindles 
increasing muscle tone. While the neocerebellum inhibits the stretch reflex by 
acting through medullary reticulospinal tract. However, the excitatory effect is 
predominant. So, cerebellar lesions is associated with hypotonia and pendular knee 
jerk. 

Cerebellar Lesions 

The manifestations of neocerebellar syndrome (ataxia) occur in the same side of the lesion 

a. Hypotonia: because neocerebellum is facilitatory to muscle tone, its lesion leads to 
pendular knee jerk and lateral flexion of the body to the affected side. 

b. Motor Ataxia: means incoordination of movement due to error in rate, range, force and 
direction of movement. It is manifested by: 

o Dysmetria 
e [ntention tremors (Kinetic tremors) 
e Cerebellar nystagmus 
Decomposition of movements 
Dysdiadochokinesis 
Dysarthria (scanning or Staccato speech) 

Disturbance of posture and gait 
Rebound 

Dysmetria is poor control of distance of movement e.g. when attempting to touch an 
object e.g. nose with a finger, overshooting of the intended point (hypermetria or post 
pointing) or stoppage short before the intended point (hypometria) occurs. Dysmetria 
is due to failure of the damping and timing functions. Dysmetria results in in- 
coordinated movements (ataxia). 
Intention (Kinetic) tremors are involuntary tremors which occur mainly at the distal 
joints. They occur mainly during the movements especially at its end and disappear 
during rest & sleep. Truncal tremors are called titubation. 

209 

AN 



CHAPTER lil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

v' Cerebellar nystagmus is tremors of the eyeballs which occur during a trial to fix an 
object to the side of the lesion. It is due to intention tremors of eye muscles due to 
absence of damping function. 

v' Decomposition of movements is the inability to perform successive components of a 
complex motor act i.e. " complex" movements are decomposed into many "steps". So, 
if the patient is asked to move his right heel upon the left leg (heel-knee test) from 
knee to ankle, he makes it in 3 steps. 

v' Adiadochokinesis or dysdiadochokinesia is the inability to perform rapid successive 
opposite movements e.g. pronation and supination of the forearm or flexion & 
extension of the fingers. This is due to failure of predictive and smooth progression 

function. 
v' Dysarthria (scanning or Staccato speech): Slow interrupted (decomposed) speech due 

to disturbance in coordination of muscles of speech as formation of words needs rapid 
& highly coordinated succession of contractions of the muscles of larynx, oral and 
respiratory muscles. So, words may be long or short, loud or weak. 

v' Rebound is the overshooting of limb when a resistance of movement is removed. This 
is elicited by asking the patient to flex his forearm against resistance. When the 
resistance is suddenly removed, the limb overshoots and strikes the patient face (loss 
of braking mechanism). 

Finish 

Fig. 3.84: Dysmetria and decomposition of movement shown by patient moving his 
arm from a raised position to his nose. Tremor increases on approaching the nose. 

ATAXIA 

This is incoordination of voluntary movements in absence of paralysis. It is either sensory or 
motor. 

1. Sensory ataxia: This occurs as a result of lesions of the proprioceptive sensory 
pathways, commonly in the dorsal column of the spinal cord (the gracile & cuneate 
tracts). 

2. Motor ataxia: This is due to defects in the coordinating system of voluntary 
movements. It commonly occurs in lesions of the neocerebellum or spinocerebellar 
tracts. 

210 



CHAPTER lil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

N.B In MOTOR ATAXIA: lesion of vermis the ataxia is in both sides, but if it affects the 
hemisphere, it will be unilateral with deviation of body towards the diseased side

What are the types of tremors that you know? 
1. The static tremors of the parkisonism. It occurs during rest. 
2. The kinetic tremors of the neocerebellar syndrome. It occurs during movements due to 

loss of the damping function of the cerebellum. 
3. Tremors that occur in outstretched hand in hyperthyroidism. 

What are the abnormal types of "gaits" that you know? 
1. Spastic gait (circumduction) of upper motor neuron lesion i.e. patient walks on a narrow 

base and has difficulty in bending his knee, drags his feet and the foot is raised by tilting 
the pelvis. 

2. Scissoring gait (spastic gait of both lower limbs) in paraplegia 
. Stamping gait in deep sensory loss. 

4. Drunken gait in archicerebellar lesion i.e. patient walk on a wide base, short steps and 
swinging from side to side and looking to the ground. This is due to disturbance in 
equilibrium due to the lesion. 

5. Shuffling gait of Parkinsonism, small steps with inability to step suddenly. 
6. Zigzag like gait of the neocerebeliar syndrome. 

Cerebellar tumors 

Many tumors affect the posterior fossa involving brain stem, cerebellar pontine angle, fourth 
ventricle, and cerebellum. Metastatic lesions to the cerebellum are also common. Only 
medulloblastoma and hemangioblastoma are going to be discussed in this chapter. 

Medulloblastoma, WHO Grade IV 

211 

$5) 



CHAPTER lik: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

v" An embryonal neuroepithelial tumor arising in the cerebellum or dorsal brain stem, 

presenting mainly in childhood. 
v' Medulloblastoma is the most common CNS embryonal tumor. 
v' It is now classified into molecular (i.e. genetic) variants as well as morphological variants, 

all with clinical utility. 
Gross: Most medulloblastomas arise in the region of the cerebellar vermis, as pink or grey 

often friable masses that fill the fourth ventricle. Medulloblastomas located in the 
cerebellar hemispheres tend to be firm and more circumscribed, and generally 
correspond to the desmoplastic/ nodular variant. Small foci of necrosis can be grossly 
evident, but extensive necrosis is rare. 

Microscopic: Several morphological variants of medulloblastoma are recognized: 

e Classic. 
e Desmoplastic/nodular medulloblastoma. 
e Medulloblastoma with extensive nadularity. 
e Large cell / anaplastic medulloblastoma. 

Classic medulloblastomas show densely packed small round cells with mild to moderate 
nuclear pleomorphism and a high mitotic count. Some cases show Homer-Wright rosette 

characterized by primitive tumor cells surrounding central neuropil (Fig. 3.85). 

Genetic analysis 
e Genetic analysis of medulloblastoma has revealed that morphologically similar tumors 

commonly exhibit distinct alterations, and that there is a relationship between these 
genetic alterations and outcome. 

¢ In addition to the histologic variant, it is now important to also list the molecular 
subtype, within the integrated WHO diagnosis for each case. 

e The most common cytogenetic aberration, identified in 40% to 50% of 

medulloblastomas, is a loss of the short arm of chromosome 17 (17p). This is often 
accompanied by duplication of the long arm, resulting in an isochromosome 17q. 

e Overall, most favorable genetic subtype is WNT- activated, which is rarest molecular 
subtype (10%) , and recognized by nuclear B-catenin. 

Fig. 3.85: Medulloblastoma. Left; hypercellular neoplasm, Middle: frequent mitoses, 

Right: Homer-Wright rosettes. 

Hemangioblastoma,W HO grade | 
Hemangioblastoma is a benign slow growing tumor of adults, typically occurring in cerebellum 
(most common), brain stem, and spinal cord. 

212 



CHAPTER lit: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

Hemangioblastomas occur in sporadic forms {accounting for -70% of cases) and in association 
with the inherited von Hippel-Lindau disease (VHL) (accounting for -30% of cases). The VHL 
tumor suppressor gene is inactivated both in VHL-associated cases and in most sporadic cases. 

Gross: 
e Most hemangioblastomas are well-circumscribed, partly cystic, highly vascularized lesions 

(60%); about 40% are completely solid. 
e Occasionally, the tumor is yellow due to rich lipid content. 
e The classic appearance is that of a cyst with a solid vascular nodule that abuts a pial surface 

(Fig. 3.85) 

Microscopic (Fig. 3.86): 

e Hemangioblastomas are characterized by two main components: (1) neoplastic stromal 
cells that are characteristically large and vacuolated and (2) abundant vascular cells. 

e Numerous thin-walled vessels are apparent. 
e [ntratumoural haemorrhage may occur. 
e The exact origin of these stromal cells is still controversial. 

Fig. 3.86: MRI shows a cerebellar hemangioblastoma with an enhancing mural nodules. 
Microscopically, the tumor exhibits stromal cells with clear, vacuolated cytoplasm due to 

accumulation of lipid droplets.

Neurological examination 

History comes first 
. in neurological clinical practice, history taking is the major clinical skill in formulating a 

proper diagnosis 
. “if you were not able to postulate a PROVISIONAL site and nature of the pathology in ) 

nervous system after history taking >>> please revise the history of the patient” 
. From the history we should be able to answer 2 important questions: 

1. Where is the problem ? (brain .... Spinal cord ... peripheral nerve ... etc) 
2. What is the nature of the problem ? (vascular, Congenital, inflammatory, 

neoplastic, degenerative,...etc) 
 Theexamination would test forthe hypothesis generated after history taking.

Sensory examination 

Basic background 

Primary Sensory Modalities 

Reflect Input from sensory receptors, sensory nerves, spinal cord, brainstem, 
through to the level of the Thalamus. 
Pain / Temperature Sensation (Spinothalamic Tract)

Touch (Multiple Pathways) 

Cortical Sensory Modalities 

Reflect Processing by the Somatosensory Cortex (post- central gyrus) 
Stereognosis 
Graphesthesia 
Two-Point Discrimination. 

Sensory pathways 

Pain and Temperature 

+ A-6 and small-unmyelinated fibres provide pain and temperature input which travels 

through the dorsal roots, then up and down a few segments in Lissauer’s tract then 

synapse with neurons in Substantia Gelatinosa)

Gracilis (legs) and Fasiculus Cuneatus (arms)- posterior column- to synapse on the 

ipsilateral Nucleus Gracilis and Cuneatus respectively.

The important message for sensory examination 

e Examine for superficial (pin prick test) and deep (vibration, sense of position and 

movement) sensory modalities 

e Start with reference point ... or compare affected to non affected sites 

e look for specific pattern of sensory loss. 

Approach to Motor localization 

1. Introduction: 

In neurology , history and examination are the way to provisional diagnosis 

Based on 2 simple concepts 

Where is the lesion in the nervous system 

What is the lesion in the nervous system 

2. Anatomy of The motor system: 
ramidal tract {cortico spinal pathway UMN 

eo UMN starts in the Betz cells in the frontal motor cortex , then descends down the 

Corona radiata to the posterior limb of the internal capsule down to the brain stem, 

where the motor decussation takes place in the medulla 

The motor tract then descends in the spinal cord till it ends at the Anterior horn cells 

AHC. 

e LMN : From AHC, motor root exite the spinal cord , then nerve , Neuromuscular 

junction and finally the muscles .

4.Motor examination 

A) Muscle state :is there atrophy , hypertrophy or fasiculations ? 
B) Muscle Tone : is there hypertonia (spasticity/ rigidity) , normotonia or 

hypotonia ? 

C) Muscle power 
Degree of weakness ? 
Distribution of weakness ? 

5.Localization 

a) Weakness due to cortical pyramidal lesions 

e¢ Contralateral monoplegia. 

e Associated cortical manifestations as seizures, dysphasia, apraxia, or cortical sensory 

deficit 
b) Weakness due to subcortical pyramidal lesions: 

e Contralateral weakness (hemiplegia) of pyramidal distribution not equally affecting 

upper and lower limbs (fibers are dispersed to reach different destinations). 

e Contralateral partial hemi-hypoesthesia and quadri-anopia could be present. 

c) Weakness due to capsular pyramidal lesions 

e Contralateral dense hemiplegia (sever and equally affecting upper and lower limbs) 

weakness (closely impact fibers) of pyramidal distribution, associated with 

hypertonia. 

e Contralateral hemi-hypoesthesia and hemianopia could be present 

d) Weakness due to brain stem pyramidal lesions 
» Contralateral (above decussation at the lower medulla) weakness of pyramidal 

distribution, associated with hypertonia. No involuntary movements, and no 

incoordination are evident. 
Ipsilateral cranial nerve palsy (CROSSED deficit is the hallmark). 

+ Weakness could be bilateral especially in intra-medullary lesions resulting in sphincter 

impairment (usually precipitancy ). 
« There could be also, other long tracts deficit, dissociated ophthalmoplegia, conjugate 

eye movement palsy and vertigo. 

e) Weakness due to spinal cord pyramidal tract lesions 

« Below the level (LEVEL is the hallmark) of the lesion there is weakness of pyramidal 
distribution, associated with hypertonia on the same lesion side. No involuntary 

movements, and no incoordination are evident. 

« Weakness is early bilateral (Paraplegia) especially in intra-medullary lesions resulting 
in early sphincter impairment (incontinence at the conus, retention at dorsal levels 

and precipitancy at cervical levels). 
» There could be also, deep sensory level on the lesion side, and superficial sensory 

level on the contralateral side. 

232 



CHAPTER Ill: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

At the level radical manifestation may be evident. 

f) Weakness due to AHC lesion : 

Focal AHC affection will cause segmental weakness, atrophy and fasciculation 

without segmental sensory affection. But spinal cord affection may be evident {long 

tract deficits below level and sphincter impairment). 

Systemic AHC affection (Motor Neuron Disease) causes bilateral and symmetrical, 

weakness, atrophy and fasciculation. This is usually associated with tongue 

fasciculation, hyper-reflexia (tonic atrophy), intact sensations, sphincters and 

abdominal reflexes. 

g) Weakness due to radicular (roots) diseases 

Systemic radiculopathy like in Guillen-Barre syndrome weakness is bilateral and 

symmetrical, more proximal (less root value), associated with hypotonia, atrophy, no 

involuntary movements, but sensory incoordination may be present. 

Associated with dermatomal sensory deficits and radicular pains, but no autonomic 

deficits (autonomic fibers join peripheral nerves directly). 

h) Weakness due to peripheral nerve diseases 

In peripheral neuropathy (hereditary and metabolic) weakness is bilateral and 

symmetrical, more distal (long nerves more vulnerable), associated with hypotonia, 

early atrophy (no nerve growth factor). No involuntary movements, but sensory 

incoordination may be present. 

Associated with impairment of superficial sensation (glove and stocking 

hypoesthesia), deep sensation and autonomic functions (sphincters and postural 

hypotension). 

N.B. on examination there is early hyo-reflexia. 

Weakness due to neuromuscular diseases 

Characterized by diurnal variation (significant fatigability) and specific marsh. 

In primary MG cranial weakness first and in secondary myasthenia skeletal weakness 

first. 

No constant weakness early in the disease, no change of tone and muscle state, no 

involuntary movements, and no incoordination. 

No symptoms of other system affection 

Weakness due to muscular diseases 

Hereditary and metabolic muscular diseases cause bilateral, almost symmetrical 

weakness, more proximal (bulky muscles). 

Associated with hypotonia, late atrophy {bulky), no abnormal involuntary movements 

and no incoordination. 

233 



CHAPTER lil: THE MOTOR SYSTEM, SPINAL CORD 

* No other non-motoric symptoms. 

Summary : 

e Pyramidal tract weakness: D>P, in UL: E>F, in LL: F>E 

e Capsular lesions >>> contralateral hemiplegia 

e Brain stem unilateral lesions >>> crossed hemiparesis 

e In cervical cord lesions >>> pyramidal quadriparesis 

e In dorsal cord lesions >>> pyramidal paraparesis 

e In polyneuropathy >>> distal flaccid weakness 

e In muscle disease >>> mostly P>D
