Intended Learning Outcomes (ILOs): 

By the end of this chapter the student should be able to: 

o Describe ventral and dorsal surfaces of brain stem. 
e Describe boundaries and contents of interpeduncuiar fossa. 
e Describe the shape, walls, boundaries and communications of the fourth 

ventricle. 
e Define reticular formation. 
e Describe histological sections of the medulla oblongata, pons and midbrain at 

different levels. 
e Describe connections of different nuclei in the brain stem 

Describe the origin and pathway of cranial nerves present at different levels 
within the brain stem 

eo List components of medial longitudinal bundle. 

e locate attachment of cranial nerves to brain stem and specify function and 

distribution of each cranial nerve. 

e Discuss the distribution of individual cranial nerve within the different functional 

columns in the brain stem. 

o Identify arteries supplying different parts of the brain stem and their clinical 

significance. 
e Describe detailed anatomy of cranial cavity; dural folds and sinuses, emissary and 

diploic veins and middle meningeal artery with their clinical significance. 

o Describe the intracranial course of cranial nerves and internal carotid artery. 

e Identify the site, type, shape and cells of trigeminal ganglion. 

o Identify boundaries and contents of temporal, infratemporal and pterygopalatine 

fossae. 

e Describe the anatomy of temporomandibular joint. 

e Describe attachment, action and nerve supply of muscles of mastication. 

e Discuss the anatomy of maxillary nerve, mandibular nerve, maxillary artery and 

pterygoid venous plexus. 

eo Discuss site, roots and branches of parasympathetic ganglia of the head and neck.

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

BRAIN STEM EXTERNAL FEATURES 

- The brain stem is formed of midbrain, pons, and medulla oblongata. It connects the cerebrum 
with the spinal cord. It is connected to the cerebellum by 3 peduncles: superior cerebellar 
peduncle (SCP), middle cerebellar peduncle (MCP), and inferior cerebellar peduncle (ICP} which 
connect the midbrain, pons & medulla respectively to the cerebellum. 

- The brain stem contains: 
1. White matter which includes: 

e Longitudinal fibers: 
- Descending tracts: pyramidal, extrapyramidal & corticopontine. 
- Ascending tracts in the form of lemnisci. 
- Association tract called medial longitudinal bundle (MLB) 

e Horizontal fibers: 
-  Decussations (e.g., motor & sensory decussations in medulla) 
-  Arcuate fibers in the medulla 

2. Grey matter which forms scattered nuclei that include. 
e Cranial nerve nuclei. 
e Qther nuclei: as the olivary nuclei in the medulla, the pontine nuclei in the pons and 

the red nucleus in the midbrain 
3. Reticular formation: a core of mixed grey and white matters lying within the brain stem. 

EXTERNAL FEATURES 
VENTRAL surface

1) THE MEDULLA OBLONGATA 
e Extends between spinal cord (below) &

2) THE PONS . 
e A bridge, one inch long, connecting the right & left cerebellar hemispheres by a middle 

cerebellar peduncle (MCP) on each side. 
e |t extends between the medulla oblongata (below) & midbrain (above). 
e In the middle, the basis pontis projects anteriorly & shows: 

- Sulcus for the basilar artery in the midline. 
- Transverse grooves between bundles of transverse pontine fibers. 

¢ Gives attachment to middle 4 cranial nerves: 
- V. (trigeminal) to the junction of MCP & basis pontis, closer to upper border by 2 roots 
(sensory: large, lat. & motor: small, med.). 

- VI. (abducent) to lower border of pons just above pyramid. 
- Vil (facial) to cerebello-pontine angle (bet. MCP & ICP) by 2 roots (motor: large med. & 
sensory nervus intermedius: small lat.). 

- VII (vestibulo-cochlear) to cerebello-pontine angle, lateral to Vii, by 2 roots (vestibular 
medially & cochlear laterally). 

Clinically, cerebellopontine angle tumor causes lesion of Vil => facial paralysis + lesion of Vii 
—> deafness & vertigo. 

3) THE MIDBRAIN 
e Appears as2 diverging cerebral peduncles enclosing the interpeduncular fossa. 
¢ The oculomotor nerve emerges from the medial side of the cerebral peduncle. 
e 4 structures are seen to wind around cerebral peduncle: 

1. Optic tract 2. Trochlear nerve 
3. Posterior cerebral artery 4. Basal vein

RETICULAR FORMATION 

It consists of nerve cells and nerve fibers scattered within the brain stem extending superiorly to 
hypothalamus & thalamus and inferiorly to spinal cord. 
Its neurons are arranged into 3 columns: median, medial & lateral (Fig. 4.6.).

INTERNAL STRUCTURE OF BRAIN STEM 
1. THE MEDULLA OBLONGATA 

The medulla represents a conical, expanded continuation of the upper cervical spinal cord. 

e The medulla oblongata is formed of two parts: 

I. Closed medulla: It is the caudal part, where the central canal is still found. it has two 

levels: 

a) Level of motor decussation, the lowermost part. 

b) Level of sensory decussation, rostral to the motor decussation. 

i. Open medulla: It is the rostral part where the central canal is replaced by the fourth 

ventricle which is found dorsally. 

Internal features of the medulla: 

1) Pyramidal decussation (decussation of corticospinal tract). 

2) Sensory decussation (decussation of internal arcuate fibers to form the medial lemniscus). 

3) The spinal nucleus of trigeminal nerve replaces Substantia Gelatinosa of the spinal cord. 

4) The descending fibers of spinal trigeminal tract replaces Lissaure’s tract of spinal cord. 

5) Ascending and descending tracts 

6) The appearance of the fourth ventricle, representing the rostral continuation of the central 

canal of the spinal cord. 

7) The reticular formation forms the core of the medulla. 

8) Nuclei in medulla (cranial nerve nuclei and cerebellar relay nuclei). 

> The Corticospinal (pyramidal or Motor) Decussation (Fig. 4.7a): 

e Most of the fibers of Gradle nudeus 

corticospinal tract (90%) cross 

as a large bundle anterior to 

central gray. The crossed fibers 

descend in the dorsal part of 

lateral funiculus forming the 

lateral corticospinal tract. 

e About 8% of the fibers descend 

without crossing, forming the 

anterior corticospinal tract. 

These fibers gradually cross 

through their descent in the 

spinal cord. 
Pyramid 

Fig. 4.7a: Level of Motor decussation of closed Medulla 

242 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

eo About 2% of the fibers descend in the anterolateral funiculus forming the uncrossed 

anterolateral corticospinal tract. 

Function of motor decussation: 

It forms the basis for voluntary motor control of one half of the body by the opposite cerebral 
cortex. 

> The Sensory decussation: (Fig. 4.7 b) 

e The gracile and cuneate nuclei appear in the dorsal aspect of the medulla. Their tracts are 

found posterior to the nuclei. 

e At the level of the sensory decussation, the nuclei appear bigger because the gracile and ; 

cuneate tracts end gradually into them. 

e Course: Myelinated fibers from the gracile and cuneate nuclei sweep anteromedially around 

the central gray, forming the internal arcuate fibers. They decussate to the opposite side and 
ascend upwards forming the medial lemniscus. 

¢ The medial lemniscus is a compact bundle parallel and adjacent to the median raphe. 

e Lamination of fibers: The fibers from the gracile nucleus (Sacral, lumber and lower thoracic) 

are anterior in position, while the fibers from cuneate nucleus {upper thoracic and cervical) 

are posterior in position. 

Termination: The fibers project to the ventral posterolateral nucleus (VPLN) of thalamus.

> Spinal nucleus and tract of Trigeminal nerve: 

Site of the nucleus: It is located medial to the spinal tract of trigeminal, extending from 

the trigeminal root at the pons to the second cervical segment. 

Connection: 

Afferent: fibers from spinal trigeminal tract that enter the pons from the trigeminal 

ganglion. These fibers descend in the dorsolateral part in the lower pons and medulla to 

reach the second cervical segment to terminate on the cells of the spinal nucleus of V. 

These fibers carry general somatic sensation (pain and temperature) from the face, 

forehead and mucous membranes of nose and mouth. 

Efferent: Secondary trigeminal fibers are the axons of cells of spinal trigeminal nucleus. 

They cross to the opposite side. They ascend upwards to the thalamus forming the 

trigeminothalamic fibers (Trigeminal lemniscus), which is found close to the medial 

lemniscus. The trigeminothalamic fibers end in the ventral postero-medial nucleus (VPMN) 

of thalamus and then to cerebral cortex. 

» Ascending and Descending Tracts in medulla: 

1) Medial lemniscus. 

2) Spinothalamic tracts. 

3) Dorsal spinocerebellar tract merges into ICP. 

4) Ventral spinocerebellar tract lies in the retro-olivary area. 

5) MLF is anterior to XII nucleus, adjacent to median raphe. 

6) Spinal tract of V lies in the posterolateral part. 

> The Reticular Formation 

Definition: It represents a large collection of neurons of various sizes and shapes in a network of 

fibers extending from the level of the motor decussation of the medulla to the upper part of 

the midbrain. it is the upward continuation of the gray matter of the spinal cord. 

; Connections: 

Afferent: from Efferent: 

e Spinal cord. Ascending fibers: to thalamus in the central tegmental tract. 

e Cerebral cortex. Descending fibers: to spinal cord through medullary and pontine 

| Deep cerebellar nuclei. reticulospinal tracts). 

The reticular formation controls: 

1) The activity of muscles through reticulospinal tracts. 

2) Somatic and visceral sensations. 

3) Autonomic and endocrine nervous systems. 

4) Arousal and the level of consciousness. So, pathological lesion of reticular formation in 

human can result in loss of consciousness and even coma. 

244 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

» Nuclei in the medulla are: {fig 4.8) 

1) Lower four cranial nerve nuclei Xli, XI, X and IX. 

2) The medulla contains many cerebellar relay nuclei: 

a. Accessory cuneate nucleus. 

b. Arcuate nucleus. 

c. Nuclei of reticular formation. 

d. Olivary nuclear complex. 

® Accessory cuneate nucleus: 

Site: lateral to the cuneate nucleus at rostrai levels. 

Connections: it receives fibers from the cervical and thoracic spinal ganglia. It gives 

uncrossed fibers to the cerebellum (cuneo-cerebellar fibers or the dorsal external arcuate 

fibers), that pass in the ICP. 

Function: It is equivalent to the Clarke’s nucleus. It carries unconscious proprioceptive 

sensation from the upper limbs and neck. 

® Arcuate nucleus: 

It lies on the anterior aspect of the pyramid. It represents the caudally displaced pontine 

nuclei. 

Connections: It receives afferent fibers from the cerebral cortex. it gives efferent fibers to 

the cerebellum through ICP (anterior external arcuate fibers). 

The Open Medulla (most rostral part of the medulla) is characterized by (Fig. 4.8): 

e Appearance of the fourth ventricle 

¢ The floor of the ventricle contains: 

1) Hypoglossal nucleus forms the most medial elevation in the floor of fourth ventricle 

(Hypoglossal trigone). 

2) Dorsal motor nucleus of vagus and solitary nucleus form the intermediate elevation 
(Vagal trigone). 

3) Medial and inferior vestibular nuclei form the lateral elevation 

e Appearance of inferior cerebellar peduncle. 

e Appearance of the main inferior olivary nucleus.

Cranial nerves 

They are 12 pairs of cranial nerves. They are distributed in the head and neck except the vagus 

nerves which supply also structures in thorax and abdomen. They are either pure motor or mixed 

nerves. 

Motor nuclei of the cranial nerves are controlled by impulses from the precentral and postcentral 

gyri of the cerebral cortex via corticonuclear (corticobulbar) tracts. Bilateral connection is 

present for all motor nuclei of the cranial nerves except part of the facial nucleus supplying the 

lower part of the face and the part of hypoglossal nucleus that supplies genioglossus muscle. 

Cranial Nerves that originate from medulla oblongata (Fig. 4.9): 

Hypoglossal nerve (XII) 

v' The hypoglossal nucleus lies deep to the hypoglossal trigone in the floor of 4th ventricle. 

It extends down to the closed medulla. 

v ltis a motor nerve. It supplies all muscles of tongue except the palatoglossus muscle. 

Connections of the nucleus: afferent fibers from: 

eo Bilateral corticonuclear fibers, except the part which innervates genioglossus muscles 

which is responsible for protrusion of the tongue (receives supply from the opposite side 

only).

Nuclei of the vagus nerve: 

a) Dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus: 
- {tis the largest parasympathetic nucleus in the brainstem. 

- It gives parasympathetic fibers to cardiac muscle and smooth muscles of the viscera

- It is a motor nucleus, situated deep in the medullary reticular formation, between

- |t extends through the whole length of the medulla.

- |t projects to the sensory thalamus and then to the cerebral cortex. 

d) Spinal nucleus of trigeminal nerve: 
It carries exteroceptive sensation from the cutaneous area at the back of the ear and the 

external auditory meatus.

Glossopharyngeal nerve (IX): It is a mixed nerve. 
Nuclei of the glossopharyngeal nerve: 

a) Inferior salivatory nucleus: 

It gives parasympathetic fibers of glossopharyngeal nerve to relay in the Otic ganglion then 

give secretory fibers to parotid gland. 

b) Nucleus ambiguous: 
Its rostral part gives motor fibers of glossopharyngeal nerve that supply stylopharyngeus 

muscle.

Sensory impulses from the mucous membranes of the posterior 1/3 of tongue, tonsils, pharynx 

(posterior wall) and Eustachian tube. 

d) Spinal nucleus of trigeminal nerve It receives fibers of IX nerve carrying general sensation 

from the oropharynx, posterior 1/3 of tongue, tonsils, auditory tube & middle ear 

Inferior Olivary complex 

It is a convoluted mass of gray matter, formed of: (Fig. 4.8) 

1. Principal inferior olivary nucleus: it appears as a folded bag with a hilum directed 

medially. 

2. Medial accessory nucleus: it lies lateral to the medial lemniscus. 

3. Dorsal accessory nucleus: it lies dorsal to the principal nucleus. 

Connections: 

I) Afferent: 

1. Descending fibers from the cerebral cortex end bilaterally on the principal nucleus. 

2. Rubro-olivary fibers: the fibers from the red nucleus end in the principal nucleus. 

3. 'Brain stem nuclei: The vestibular nuclei (medial and inferior) and Spinal nucleus of V. 

4. Ascending fibers from the spinal cord, mainly to accessory nuclei. 

I) Efferent: 

Olivo- cerebellar fibers to the contralateral cerebellum. The fibers pass in ICP and end as 

climbing fibers in cerebellar cortex. They have powerful excitatory action on Purkinje cells. 

They are responsible for motor learning.

INTERNAL STRCTURE OF THE PONS 

The pons is formed of two parts (Fig. 4.11): 

I- Basis pontis, the ventral portion. 

Il- Tegmentum pontis, the dorsal portion. 

I. Ventral portion (Basis Pontis) 

Structure: It is formed of pontine nuclei as well as Longitudinal and transverse fibers 

1) Longitudinal fibers: these include the following: 

a) Corticospinal fibers: They form the largest bundles which are separated by 

transverse pontine fibers. 

b) Corticonuclear fibers: which end directly on the motor cranial nerve nuclei. 

c) Corticopontine fibers: 

They originate from frontal, parietal, temporal and occipital cortex. Fibers descend 

without crossing. They are called fronto-pontine, parieto-pontine, tempro-pontine and 

occipito-pontine and they terminate in pontine nuclei. 

2) Transverse pontine fibers: 

These are the axons of pontine nuclei which cross the opposite side forming the 

middle cerebellar peduncle (MCP). 

3) Pontine nuclei: these are closely packed cellular aggregates that are found between 

the corticopontine and corticospinal fibers. 

» The basis pontis is considered as a massive relay station, by which impulses from the 

cerebral cortex are transmitted to the contralateral cerebellar hemisphere.

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA

Middle Cerebellar Peduncle 
It is the largest peduncle. lt connects the cerebellum with the pons. It contains cortico-ponto- 

cerebellar fibers (transverse pontine fibers). 

Il. Dorsal portion (Tegmentum Pontis) 
The tegmentum pontis is an upward continuation of the medulla. 

Contents: 

1) Ascending and descending tracts. 

2) Cranial nerve nuclei Vil, VII, Vl and V. 

3) Reticular formation. 

> Tracts present in tegmentum pontis: (Fig. 4.11, 4.13) 

These are as in the medulla with few differences in orientation. 

The Medial Lemniscus lies horizontally dorsal to the basis pontis. 

The Medial Longitudinal Fasciculus (MLF) lies close to middle line. 

The spinothalamic and anterior spinocerebellar tracts. 

The spinal tract and nucleus of V are ventromedial to ICP. 

Central tegmental tract: It is a bundle lateral to the MLF, that contains: 

a) Descending fibers from the red nucleus in midbrain to the inferior olivary complex. 

b) Ascending fibers from the reticular formation to thalamic nuclei.

Cochlear nerve (Auditory pathway) (Fig. 4.12a): 

1) Primary auditory fibers: 

The cochlear nerve originates from bipolar nerve cells in 

the spiral ganglion of the inner ear. Their peripheral Cochlear 

processes end in relation to the hair cells of the organ of a 

Corti. Their central processes form the cochlear nerve, 

which enters the pons at the cerebello-pontine angle to 
Supedon divey 

terminate in the two cochlear nuclei; dorsal and ventral. 

ll} Secondary auditory fibers: 
Fig. 4.12a: The auditory pathway 

They originate from the cells of dorsal and ventral cochlear nuclei (Fig. 4.11). Their axons 

are grouped into three bundles (acoustic striae): (Fig. 4.12a) 

a) Ventral acoustic stria: it is the largest one that arises from the ventral cochlear 

nucleus. 

b) Dorsal acoustic stria: it arises from the dorsal cochlear nucleus 

¢) Intermediate acoustic stria: it arises from dorsal part of ventral cochlear nucleus. 

Course: 

e Fibers derived mainly from the ventral cochlear nucleus run transversally to form the 

trapezoid body between basis pontis and tegmentum of pons and crosses to join the 

lateral lemniscus of the opposite side. 

e Fibers of the dorsal and intermediate striae also cross to join the lateral lemniscus. 

es Some fibers terminate in the superior olive of the same and opposite sides and the nuclei 

of the trapezoid body. The axons of cells of these nuclei join the lateral lemniscus. 

Ill) Tertiary auditory fibers: 

e The lateral lemniscus ascends in the lateral part of the tegmentum till it reaches the 

midbrain. Most of its fibers end in the inferior colliculus, some fibers cross to the 

opposite inferior colliculus. 

e The inferior colliculus gives fibers to the medial geniculate body of thalamus, then these 

fibers project to the auditory cortex.

The lateral lemniscus: It is formed of: 

1) The three acoustic stria; ventral, dorsal and intermediate stria. 

2) Fibers from superior olive of the same and opposite sides. 

3) Fibers from the nuclei of the trapezoid body. 

» NB: Efferent cochlear bundles: These are fibers arise from neurons surrounding the 
superior olivary nuclei. They project bilaterally to the hair cells of organ of Corti to 

suppress the auditory nerve activity. They inhibit impulses concerned with high 

frequency.

Vestibular nerve: 

It arises from bipolar nerve cells in vestibular ganglion of the inner ear. Their peripheral 

processes receive impulses from hair cells of maculae of the utricle and saccules and crista 

ampularis. 

o The central processes form the vestibular nerve which enters the pons at the 

cerebello-pontine angle. The fibers are divided into short ascending and long 

descending fibers to terminate in the four vestibular nuclei (Lateral, medial, superior 

and inferior). 

e The vestibular nuclei give rise to vestibulo-cerebellar fibers (Secondary vestibular 

fibers). 

e Some fibers project directly to the cerebellum (Primary vestibular fibers). 

o Both primary and secondary vestibular fibers enter the cerebellum via the ICP to 

terminate in the ipsilateral flocculo-nodular lobe which is concerned with 

equilibrium. 

Facial nerve (VII): Nuclei: - It is a mixed nerve containing afferent and efferent fibers. 

1) Motor nucleus of facial nerve: 

-It lies in the pontine reticular formation, dorsal to the superior olive. The motor fibers 

project dorsomedially to loop around the abducent nucleus forming the facial colliculus then 

emerge at cerebellopontine angle. 

2) Superior salivatory nucleus: (Parasympathetic nucleus) 

It lies lateral to the motor nucleus of facial nerve. It gives secretory fibers to sublingual and 

submandibular salivary glands, nasal, buccal and palatine glands. 

1) Solitary nucleus: 

Its gustatory part receives taste sensation from the anterior 3% of the tongue. 

2) Spinal nucleus of trigeminal: 

It receives exteroceptive impulses from the external auditory meatus and area of skin back 

fo ear. 

Abducent nerve (Vi) 

The abducent nerve nucleus lies in the floor of the 4th ventricle at the inferior level of pons. it 

is surrounded by the root fibers of facial nerve. 

Course: Axons of cells pass ventrally to emerge from its ventral aspect, at the ponto-medullary 

junction. It supplies lateral rectus muscle of the same side. 

Connections: 

Bilateral corticonuclear fibers, 

Fibers relay around the nucleus of oculomotor ill that control conjugate eye movements.

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

Trigeminal nerve (V) (Fig. 4.13) 
The trigeminal nerve is a mixed nerve, mainly containing sensory fibers. 

The trigeminal nerve nuclei: 

1- Motor nucleus: Motor nucleus of trigeminal present in cranial part of pons (level of 

trigeminal nerve). It supplies muscles of mastication. 

Connections: 

a) Corticonuclear fibers from both sides. 

b) Mesencephalic nucleus: to increase force of bite. 

2- Three sensory nuclei: . 

a. Main sensory nucleus presents in pons lateral to motor nucleus and continuous with 

spinal nucleus. 

b. Spinal nucleus of trigeminal. 

c. Mesencephalic nucleus extends from pons to the upper midbrain level. It receives 

fibers concerned with proprioception. This nucleus is the 1st order neuron in the 

proprioceptive sensation from the face, extra-ocular muscles, muscles of the tongue, 

muscles of mastication and joint capsule of tempro-mandibular joint. 

Connections of Mesencephalic nucleus: 

a) Motor nucleus of trigeminal nerve. 

b) Trigeminal 

lemniscus. 

Fig. 4.13: Pons level of Trigeminal nerve

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

Course of the sensory fibers: (Nice to know) 

Cells of origin are unipolar cells of trigeminal ganglion. 

e The dendrites carry the sensation from the skin of the face by three divisions: 

ophthalmic, maxillary and mandibular. 

The central processes enter the brain stem at the midlevel of pons. They divide into 

descending and ascending fibers: 

1) The descending fibers: 

= These fibers run in the spinal tract of trigeminal in the medulla to terminate in spinal 

nucleus of V. gh 

= The spinal nucleus gives secondary trigeminal fibers which cross to the opposite side 

to ascend as the ventral trigemino-thalamic tract to end around cells of ventral 

postero-medial nucleus of thalamus (VPMN), then project to the sensory cortex. 

2) The ascending fibers: 

= These fibers are distributed to the main sensory nucleus. 

= Axons of the nucleus = secondary trigeminal fibers which ascend in the same and 

opposite sides forming the dorsal trigemino-thalamic tract that form trigeminal 

lemniscus (T). 

» The fibers end around cells of VPMN of thalamus, then they pass to the sensory cortex. 

Medial longitudinal fasiculus or bundle (MLF) 

Definition: It is an association bundle of fibers extending from the brain stem into the spinal 

cord. 

Position: It is a well-defined bundle in the brain stem and spinal cord cervical levels, below which 

it is only formed of separate fibers. 

Components: 

In the brain stem, it is formed of short ascending and descending fibers. In the spinal cord it is 

formed only of descending fibers (crossed and uncrossed) that originate from different nuclei at 

brain stem levels. 

1. Ascending fibers: 

» Vestibular fibers from medial and superior vestibular nuclei {Crossed and uncrossed) to 

the motor nuclei of 3rd, 4th and 6th cranial nerves supplying extraocular muscles. 

» Fibers from 6th nucleus connect with the 3rd nucleus for conjugate eye movements. 

2. Descending fibers: (Nice to know) 

a) Vestibulospinal fibers from the medial vestibular nucleus for neck movement. 

256 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

b) Reticulospinal fibers from the pontine reticular formation descend in MLF to end around 

motor neurons of spinal cord at all levels. 

c) Tectospinal fibers from the superior colliculus of the midbrain, fibers descend to VII 

nucleus to control the eye and neck movements. 

d) Interstitial nucleus of Cajal (interstitio-spinal). 

Functions: 

1} Coordination of reflex eye movements (tectospinal tract) and neck muscles 

in response to visual and auditory stimuli. 

2) Control of conjugate eye movement (vestibulo-ocular}. 

INTERNAL STRCTURE OF MIDBRAIN 
Parts of midbrain (Fig. 4.14) 

1) Tectum: this lies dorsal to the aqueduct of Sylvius which connects the third and fourth 

ventricles. The tectum contains four colliculi, two inferior and two superior colliculi. 

2) Tegmentum: this is the central part; it is the upward continuation of pontine reticular 

formation. It contains cranial nerve nuclei (IV and lll), red nucleus, reticular formation and 

ascending and descending tracts. 

3) Crus cerebri: this is the ventrolateral surface, containing the descending cortical projections. 

It replaces the basis pontis.

Crus cerebri: 

It is occupied by the descending fibers from the cerebral cortex. These fibers are arranged as 

follows: 

1) Fronto-pontine fibers, in the most medial part of the crus. 

2) Corticonuclear and corticospinal fibers, which occupy the intermediate part of the crus. 

Corticonuclear fibers are medial to corticospinal fibers. 

3) Tempro, parieto, occipto- pontine fibers, that occupy the most lateral part of the crus. 

Inferior colliculi: 

Position: these are ovoid masses in the inferior level of Midbrain tectum (Fig. 4.14).

Function: It is one of the relay stations of auditory pathway. 

The Trochlear nerve (IV) (Fig. 4.14) 

Position of nucleus: The nucleus lies ventral to periaqueductal gray at the level of inferior 

colliculus. 

Course: Root fibers curve dorsolaterally near the margin of central gray and decussate 

completely to supply superior oblique muscle. - 

Function: The fibers emerge from the dorsal surface of midbrain (Purely motor). 

Connections: 

a) Bilateral corticonuclear fibers. 

b) Medial longitudinal fasciculus. 

Substantia Nigra 
It is a curved lamina of gray matter. It lies between the crus cerebri and tegmentum. It shows 

maximum size at the level of superior colliculus (Fig. 4.15) 

Parts: 

1. Compact part (pars compacta): It has melanin containing cells with high content of 

dopamine. Degeneration of pars compacta causes decrease of dopamine in basal ganglia 

leading to Parkinsonism disease.

2. Reticular part (pars reticularis): It is formed of large neurons. 

Superior colliculus 

Position: It is flattened laminated eminences in the superior level of the tectum. It is formed of 

alternating layers of white and gray matter (Fig. 4.15):

Red nucleus 
Position and characters (Fig. 4.15): 

It is present in the tegmentum of midbrain at level of superior colliculus. 

-It is pinkish in color due to high vascularity. 

-It is surrounded by fibers of the superior cerebellar peduncle 

-It is traversed by the rootlets of oculomotor nerve. 

Structure: it is formed of two parts: 

1. Caudal part: which is magnocellular (motor function). 

2. Rostral part: which is parvicellular (motor learning).

= Fibers from the emboliform and globose cerebellar nuclei pass to the 

magnocellular part. 

2) Cortico-rubral fibers: from the cerebral cortex to the magnocellular part. These 

convey indirectly impulses from the cortex to the spinal cord.

Tegmental decussations: 
The tegmentum of the midbrain contains the following decussations: 

I. At superior level: 
1) Dorsal tegmental decussation: Formed by the tectospinal fibers. 

2) Ventral tegmental decussation: Formed by rubrospinal fibers. 
Il. Atinferior level: 

1) Decussation of the superior cerebellar peduncle. 

2) Decussation of the trochlear nerve. 

Superior Cerebellar Peduncle (SCP) 
It connects the cerebellum with the midbrain. It is formed mostly of efferent fibers from deep 
cerebellar nuclei to red nucleus and reticular formation. 
Afferent fibers: Anterior Spinocerebellar tract, Tecto-cerebellar tract {from superior 

colliculus).

COLUMNS OF CRANIAL NERVE NUCLEI 

The nuclei for the cranial nerves form columns in the brain stem. Each column gives origin to- 

or receives one type of fibers. They include three motor columns medially and four (actually 

three) sensory columns laterally; they are arranged from medial to lateral and from above 
downwards as follows (Fig. 4.18): 

1. General Somatic Efferent (GSE) column: motor to skeletal muscles that develop from 
myotomes {extraocular muscles and tongue muscles} i.e. it is somatomotor: 
a. Nucleus of Ill (in the midbrain at level of superior colliculus) 
a. Nucleus of IV (in the midbrain at level of inferior colliculus) 
b. Nucleus of VI {in pons) 
c. Nucleus of XII (in medulla oblongata)

Fig. 4.18: columns of cranial nerve nuclei in brain stem 

2. Special Visceral Efferent (SVE) column: They are branchiomotor supplying skeletal muscles 
that develop from the visceral (branchial or pharyngeal) arches. 
a. Motor nucleus of V in pons (supply muscles of the 1% arch; their details are mentioned 

below) 
b. Motor nucleus of Vil in pons (supply muscles that developed from the 2" arch; their 

details are mentioned below) 
¢. Nucleus Ambiguus in medulla oblongata, supply fibers to: 

- IX supplying muscles that develop from 3™ arch: stylopharyngeus 
- X & XI supplying muscles that develop from the 4" & 6!" arches: muscles of palate, 
pharynx and larynx. 

3. General Visceral Efferent (GVE) column: Gives preganglionic parasympathetic fibers. 

a. Edinger Westphal nucleus of (Ill) (in midbrain) supply ciliary muscle and sphincter 
pupillae muscle. 

b. Superior salivatory nucleus (Vil) supply submandibular and sublingual salivary glands. 

¢. Inferior salivatory nucleus (IX) supply parotid gland

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

d. Dorsal vagal nucleus of (X); supply parasympathetic fibers to organs of the CVS, 
bronchial tree and most of the GIT (foregut & midgut). 

4. General & Special Visceral Afferent (GVA & SVA) columns: Nucleus Solitarius, receives 
sensation from viscera (GVA) and taste sensation (SVA) via cranial nerves VII, IX, X. 

5. General Somatic Afferent (GSA) column: 
a. Main sensory nucleus of V: receives crude touch from the head. 
b. Spinal nucleus of V: receives pain & temperature from the head. 
c. Mesencephalic nucleus of V: receives proprioception from the head. 

6. Special Somatic Afferent (SSA) column: 
Cochlear and Vestibular nuclei 

Details of nuclei of origin of the different cranial nerves 
There are twelve cranial nerves. Ail attach to the brain stem and have nuclei of origin within it 
except the first two cranial nerves: the olfactory & optic nerves. 

lll. Oculomotor nerve: 
1. Motor Nucleus (GSE): It lies in the midbrain at the level of the superior colliculus. Fibers 

arising from this nucleus supply all extra-ocular muscles except the superior oblique 
(supplied by IV) and the lateral rectus (supplied by Vi). 

2. Parasympathetic Nucleus (Edinger Westphal Nucleus) (GVE): Fibers arising from this 
nucleus pass with oculomotor nerve =? to its inferior division => to nerve to inferior 
oblique muscle => then leave this nerve to relay in ciliary ganglion = then postganglionic 
parasympathetic fibers run as short ciliary nerves that supply the ciliary muscle & 
sphincter pupillae muscle. {details of this nucleus can be postponed till Special Senses 
module) 

IV. Trochlear nerve: 

It is a purely motor nerve that supplies one muscle in the eye “superior oblique” Its nucleus 
(GSE) lies in the midbrain at level of inferior colliculus. - Fibers arising from the nucleus cross 
and emerge from dorsal surface of midbrain and pass to supply the opposite superior oblique. 

V. Trigeminal nerve: 
It is a mixed nerve. It has 1 motor & 3 sensory nuclei: 

1. Motor Nucleus (SVE): It is present in the cranial part of pons & its fibers supply muscles 
that developed from the 1st pharyngeal arch (4 muscles of mastication & 4 other muscles; 
tensor palate, tensor tympani; anterior belly of digastric & mylohoid) 

2. Three sensory nuclei that belong to (GSA) column: 
a. Main Sensory Nucleus: It is present in pons lateral to the motor nucleus. It receives 

touch fibers from head & face. 
b. Spinal Nucleus of V: It is present in pons & extends downwards to medulla & upper 

cervical levels of spinal cord. It receives pain & temperature from head & face. It 
receives also GSA sensory fibers from VII, IX & X cranial nerves. 

c. Mesencephalic Nucleus: It is present in pons & extends upwards to midbrain. It 
receives proprioceptive fibers from head & face. It contains pseudounipolar cells 
which are considered as 1st-order neuron 

263 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

Vi. Abducent nerve: 
- It is a purely motor nerve that supplies one muscle in the eye “lateral rectus of the same 

side”. 
- its nucleus (GSE) lies in the pons deep to the medial eminence in the floor of fourth ventricle. 

Vil. Facial nerve: 
It is a mixed nerve. It has 2 motor & 2 sensory nuclei: 

3. Motor Nucleus (SVE): It is present in the caudal! part of pons. Fibers of the nucleus make 

a loop around the lower part of abducent nucleus, raising the facial colliculus in the floor 

of 4 ventricle. 
- Its fibers supply muscles that develop from the 2nd pharyngeal arch (muscles of facial 

expression, platysma, posterior belly of digastric, stylohyoid & stapedius). 

- The dorsal part of the nucleus that supplies muscles of the upper part of face receives 

bilateral cortico-nuclear fibers, while the ventral part of this nucleus that supplies 

muscles of the lower part of face receives only contra-lateral cortico-nuclear fibers. 

Thus, in an upper motor neuron lesion UMNL of the facial nerve, muscles of the upper 

face are spared while muscles of the lower face are affected. 

4. Parasympathetic nucleus (Superior salivatory nucleus) (GVE): Its fibers pass with the 

chorda tympani branch of facial nerve => relay in submandibular ganglion = 

postganglionic parasympathetic fibers supply submandibular & sublingual salivary glands. 

(see autonomic ganglia) 

5. Nucleus Solitarius (SVA): Its upper part receives taste fibers carried by chorda tympani 

from anterior 2/3 of tongue & by the greater petrosal nerve from the soft palate. 

6. Spinal Nucleus of Trigeminal (GSA): It receives fibers of general sensation from concha 

of ear. 

Vill. Vestibulocochlear nerve: 
It is a purely sensory nerve 
Vestibular & Cochlear Nuclei (SSA): These nuclei lie at the ponto-medullary junction. 

¢ Cochlear nuclei: dorsal & ventral 
e Vestibular nuclei: medial & lateral, superior & inferior. 

IX. Glossopharyngeal nerve: 
It is a mixed nerve. It has 2 motor & 2 sensory nuclei: 

1. Parasympathetic nucleus (Inferior salivatory nucleus) (GVE): its fibers pass with 

tympanic branch of IX = tympanic plexus => lesser petrosal nerve -=>.relay in otic ganglion 

-> postganglionic parasympathetic fibers reach & supply parotid gland via 

auriculotemporal nerve. (see autonomic ganglia) 

2. Nucleus Ambiguus (SVE): gives motor fibers which pass with glossopharyngeal nerve to 

supply the only muscle that develops from the 3rd pharyngeal arch (stylopharyngeus 

muscle). 

3. Nucleus Solitarius (SVA): Its middle part receives fibers of IX nerve carrying taste from 

posterior 1/3 of tongue & circumvallate papillae. 

4. Spinal Nucleus of Trigeminal (GSA): It receives fibers of IX nerve carrying general 

sensation from the oropharynx, posterior 1/3 of tongue, tonsils, auditory tube & middle 

ear. 

264 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

X. Vagus nerve: 
It is a mixed nerve.it has 2 motor & 2 sensory nuclei: 
e¢ Parasympathetic nucleus (dorsal motor nucleus of vagus (GVE): It lies in the medulla 

beneath the vagal trigone in the floor of the 4%" ventricle. Its fibers are motor to the 
involuntary muscles in thorax (heart & bronchi) and abdomen (GIT) till the end of the 
midgut. 

e Nucleus Ambiguus (SVE): Motor nucleus supply muscles that develop from 4th & 6 
pharyngeal arches which include muscles of palate (except tensor palati), muscles of 
pharynx (except stylopharyngeus) & muscles of larynx. 

e Nucleus Solitarius (SVA &GVA): Its lower part receives fibers of X nerve carrying taste 
from most posterior part of tongue, valleculae & epiglottis. it also receives general 
visceral sensations from the organs supplied by the vagus nerve. 

e Spinal Nucleus of Trigeminal (GSA): It receives fibers of X nerve carrying general sensory 
fibers of auricular branch of X. 

Xl. Accessory nerve: 
It is a motor nerve that consists of two parts: spinal & cranial parts. 
7. Spinal part: Its fibers take origin from the Spinal Nucleus located in the ventral horn of 

upper five cervical segments of the spinal cord. Fibers emerge from the spinal cord 
between the ventral and dorsal roots of spinal nerves. They supply the sternomastoid & 
trapezius. 

8. Cranial part: its fibers take origin from the lower part of the Nucleus Ambiguus (SVE). 
These fibers join vagus nerve in the jugular foramen to be distributed to muscles of palate 
{except tensor palate), muscles of pharynx (except stylopharyngeus) & muscles of larynx 

Xll. Hypoglossal nerve: 
It is a motor nerve 

Hypoglossal nucleus (GSE) 
- lies in the medulla oblongata deep to the hypoglossal trigone in the floor of 4t" ventricle. 
- It extends down to the closed medulla. 
- Fibers arising from the hypoglossal nucleus supply all muscles of tongue except the 

palatoglossus muscle. 
- The part of the hypoglossal nucleus that innervates genioglossus muscle receives 

contralateral cortico-nuclear fibers only. 

Note: 

e 3 cranial nerves are purely sensory (I, Il & VIII), 5 cranial nerves are purely motor (lll, IV, 
VI, XI & Xl), 4 cranial nerves are mixed {V, VII, IX & X); each of the mixed nerves has 4 
nuclei. 

e All motor nuclei of cranial nerves receive bilateral cortico-nuclear tract fibers except the 
part of facial nucleus that innervates muscles of lower part of face & the part of 
hypoglossal nucleus that innervates genioglossus muscle. These two exceptions receive 
contralateral cortico-nuclear fibers only so are liable to an UMNL. 

e Nucleus Ambiguus is shared by 3 cranial nerves IX, X, XI. 
e Nucleus Solitarius is shared by 3 cranial nerves Vil, IX, X. 

e Spinal nucleus of trigeminal is shared by all cranial nerve that carry fibers of general 
sensation, V, Vil, IX, X.

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

BLOOD SUPPLY OF THE BRAIN STEM 

The medulla: is supplied by the four branches of the vertebral artery (Fig. 4.19) 

1. Anterior spinal artery: supplies the part of the medulla medial to hypoglossal nerve 

(containing the XII nucleus, medial lemniscus& pyramid). 

Its occlusion = Medial medullary syndrome which involves the following: 

Pyramid => contralateral hemiplegia of the UMNL type but paralysis is flaccid! 

Medial lemniscus => contralateral loss of proprioception & fine touch 

Xil nucleus => ipsilateral LMNL paralysis of tongue muscles (ask patient to protrude 

tongue, it deviates towards paralyzed side). 
2. Posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA): supplies lateral part of MO. 

> PICA occlusion => Lateral medullary syndrome which involves the following: 
Spino-cerebellar tract & ICP > cerebellar ataxia. 
Spinal N. & tract of V =>loss of pain & temp. from ipsilateral face. 

Spinal lemniscus => loss of pain & temp. from opposite half of body. 

Nucleus ambiguus => ipsilateral paralysis of palate, pharynx, larynx. 

Nucleus solitarius—>loss of taste sensation. 
Descending sympathetic fibers => ipsilateral Horner's syndrome (ptosis, miosis, 

anhydrosis, enophthalmos). 
3. Medullary branches of vertebral artery: supply intermediate area of medulla oblongata 

(i.e., olive). 
4. Posterior spinal artery: supplies posterior part of closed medulla (ie gracile & cuneate 

nuclei) 

The pons is supplied by branches of basilar artery. 
Applied anatomy: pontine hemorrhage => pin-point pupil + hyperpyrexia + quadriplegia 

The midbrain is supplied by branches of the posterior cerebral & superior cerebellar arteries. 

Fig. 4.19: Medullary vascular syndromes

CRANIAL CAVITY 

(Please refer to theb ony structure of cranial cavitiyn practical) 

CRANIAL DURA MATER (Fig. 4.20) 
It is the outermost, thickest and toughest membrane covering brain. It is arranged in 2 layers: 
1. Outer (endosteal) layer: serves as endosteum to the skull bones 
2. Inner (meningeal) layer. 

- The two layers are fused together except in certain regions where the inner layer dips 
to form folds between different parts of the brain, OR when the outer and inner layers 
separate to enclose a venous sinus between them.

DURAL FOLDS (SEPTA): (Fig. 4.21) 
» They are formed by the meningeal (inner) layer of the dura mater reduplicates to form 

partition-like processes, between different parts of the brain. 
» Function: They help to stabilize the brain within the cranial cavity during movements of 

head. 
» These folds are: Falx cerebri, Tentorium cerebelli, Falx cerebelli, Diaphragma sellae

* Nerve supply of dura: 

The dura is supplied mainly by 3 divisions of trigeminal + upper 3 cervical nerves + 
sympathetic nerves. 
a. Ant. cranial fossa by ant. ethmoidal n. mainly + maxillary n. partly. 
b. Middle cranial fossa by maxillary n. in ant.1/2 + mandibular n. in post 1/2. 
c. Posterior cranial fossa by meningeal branches of 10!" & 12" ns. + Recurrent branches 

of C1, 2& 3 ns. 
The recurrent tentorial branch supplies the tentorium cerebelli. 

Applied anatomy: Skull trauma may lead to some injuries such as: 
1. Extradural hemorrhage: is less common, usually of arterial origin due to injury of middle 

meningeal vessels, leads to cerebral compression. 
2. Subdural hemorrhage: is more common, usually of venous origin, also has cerebral 

compression signs, RBCs are commonly present in CSF. 

VENOUS DURAL SINUSES: (Fig. 4.23) 
>» Definition: are venous channels lying between the 2 dural layers. 
» General notes: 

- They are lined by endothelium. 
- They have no muscular wall & are valveless. 
- They drain brain, meninges, bones & CSF. 
- They are connected to extracranial veins via emissary veins drain finally into UV. 

» Classification: They are divided into 6 paired, 4 single & 2 multiple sinuses. 
a) 6 Paired sinuses: are usually peripheral in position & are arranged antero- posteriorly 

as follows: 
1- Sphenoparietal 
2- Cavernous 
3- Superior petrosal 
4- Inferior petrosal 
5- Sigmoid 
6- Transverse 

b) 4 Single sinuses: are present in the median plane. 
1- Superior sagittal 
2- Inferior sagittal Related to falx cerebri 
3- Straight 
4- Occipital: is related to Falx cerebelli. 

¢) 2 Multiple: usually connect dural sinuses on both sides of the body 
1- Intercavernous (anterior, middle & posterior): connect the 2 cavernous sinuses 

together. 
2- Basilar plexus of veins: connect the 2 inferior petrosal sinuses together. 

269 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

Fig. 4.23: Venous dural sinuses 

Details of some dural sinuses: 
1) Superior sagittal sinus: 

> Site: Along the upper attached margin of falx cerebri. 

» Course: 
a. Starts anteriorly at crista galli (here it may communicate with the veins of frontal 

sinus & occasionally with veins of the nose through foramen caecum) 

b. Ends opposite internal occipital protuberance by turning usually to the right side to 

continue as the right transverse sinus. 
¢. The dilated posterior end of the superior sagittal sinus is referred to as the 

confluence of sinuses. 
» Tributaries: 

i. Superior cerebral veins. 
ii. Arachnoid villi and granulations 

2) Cavernous sinus: (Fig. 4.24 & Fig. 4.25) 

>» General notes: It is a large venous space lying in middle cranial fossa, on either side of 

body of sphenoid bone. It is called “cavernous” because of its spongy nature. 

> Extent: Its anterior end reaches the medial end of superior orbital fissure, while its 

posterior end reaches the apex of petrous temporal bone. 

» Dimensions: About 2cm long & 1cm wide. 

» Relations: 
a) External relations 

- Anteriorly: Superior orbital fissure & the apex of orbit, 

- Posteriorly: Apex of petrous temporal bone. 

- Medially: Pituitary gland & sphenoidal air sinus. 
- Laterally: Temporal lobe.

Applied Anatomy: 
a. Thrombosis of cavernous sinus: Is caused by spread of infection from the dangerous area 

of face. This affects cranial nerves lil, IV & VI. 
b. Carotid-cavernous sinus fistula: An arterio-venous communication resulting from head 

injuries; or rupture of aneurysm of ICA into the cavernous sinus, leading to unilateral 

pulsating exophthalmos. 

272 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

Flow of Blood in Venous Sinuses: (Fig. 4.27) 

1. Venous blood passes in superior sagittal sinus from before backward to the confluence of 
sinuses, where it usually continues as the right transverse sinus. 

2. Blood flows in inferior sagittal sinus posteriorly to be joined by the great cerebral vein to 
form the straight sinus. 

. Straight sinus usually continues as the left transverse sinus. 

. Transverse sinus unites with superior petrosal sinus to form the sigmoid sinus. 
5. Sigmoid sinuses on both sides continue, after emerging from jugular foramina, as the 

internal jugular veins on both sides of neck. 
6. Blood flows in cavernous sinuses posteriorly where they are drained by superior petrosal 

sinus into the transverse sinus & by the inferior petrosal sinus into WV.

Fig. 4.27: Flow of Blood in Venous Sinuses 
EMISSARY VEINS: (Fig. 4.28) 

- They are valveless veins connecting dural sinuses & extracranial veins. 
- They are of clinical importance in: 

1. Equalizing venous pressure between outside & inside the skull; acting as safety valves. 
2. Spreading infection from outside to inside the skull. 

- Classification: according to the intracranial dural sinuses: 
1. Emissary veins connected to the superior sagittal sinus: 

a. With scalp veins through the parietal foramen. 
b. With nasal veins through the foramen caecum. 

2. Emissary veins connected to the cavernous sinus: 
a. With pterygoid plexus: through emissary sphenoidal foramen, foramen lacerum 
& ovale. 

b. With internal jugular vein through carotid canal. 
c. With facial vein through superior and inferior ophthalmic veins 

3. Emissary veins connected to the sigmoid sinus: 
a. With internal jugular vein through the hypoglossal canal. 
b. With occipital vein through the mastoid foramen. 
c. With suboccipital plexus through the condylar canal.

Trigeminal Ganglion (Semilunar ganglion): (Fig. 4.29) 

It is the sensory ganglion of the fifth cranial nerve (trigeminal nerve), being similar to the 

dorsal root ganglia of the spinal nerves. 
>» Site: 

o It lies in trigeminal impression, on anterior surface of apex of petrous temporal bone, 

The small motor root of trigeminal nerve is attached to the pons, then it passes beneath the 

ganglion, and then out through foramen ovale, to join the mandibular nerve in the 

infratemporal fossa. 

Fig. 4.29: Trigeminal ganglion 

Cranial Nerves (Intracranial Course): (Fig. 4.30) 
1. First (olfactory) nerve, seen as 15-20 filaments on each side, piercing the cribriform plate 

of ethmoid bone to end in the olfactory bulb. 
2. Second (optic) nerve, passes through optic canal with ophthalmic artery. 

3. Third (oculomotor) nerve pierces the dura mater in front of the point of crossing of the 

free margin and the attached margin of the tentorium cerebelli. 

4. Fourth (trochlear) nerve pierces the dura mater at the point of crossing of the free margin 

and the attached margin of the tentorium cerebelli.

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

5. Fifth (trigeminal) nerve is composed of a large sensory root and a smaller motor root. The 
large sensory root forms the trigeminal ganglion that gives off 3 divisions: 
a. The ophthalmic and maxillary divisions run in the lateral wali of the cavernous sinus. 
b. The mandibular division {with the motor root), pass directly through the foramen 

ovale. 
6. Sixth (abducent) nerve runs forwards inside the cavernous sinus with CA. 
7. Seventh (Facial) nerve 
8. Eighth (Vestibulo-cochlear) nerve 
9. Ninth (Glossopharyngeal) nerve

Middle Meningeal Artery: (Fig. 4.31) 

» Origin: from the first part of maxillary artery within the infratemporal fossa. 
» Course: 

a. Runs upwards, between the roots of the auriculotemporal nerve to enter the middle 
cranial fossa through the foramen spinosum. 

b. In the middle cranial fossa, it has an extradural course, between the two layers of the 
dura. Here, the artery grooves the squamous temporal, and divides into frontal 
(anterior) and parietal (posterior) branches. 

c. lt supplies only small branches to dura mater; however, it is predominantly a periosteal 
artery supplying skull bones. It does not supply brain or the pia and arachnoid. 

» Surface Anatomy: 
a. Artery enters skull opposite a point immediately above the middle of zygomatic arch.

Intracranial part of ICA: (Fig. 4.32) 

» Origin: Begins in the neck as one of the terminal branches of CCA at the level of upper 
border of thyroid cartilage. 

» Course: Divided into 4 parts: 
1) Cervical part: Within the carotid sheath. 
2) Petrous part: enters the skull through the carotid canal, to enter the cranial cavity 

via the foramen lacerum. 
3) Cavernous part: passes forward in the cavernous sinus with the abducent nerve 

inferolateral to it. 
4) Cerebral part: emerges through roof of cavernous sinus medial to anterior clinoid 

process. 
» Terminates opposite anterior perforated substance by dividing into anterior and middle 

cerebral arteries (ACA & MCA). 
» Branches: 

e Cervical part has no branches 
e Petrous part gives the following: 

1- Corticotympanic branches to the middle ear. 
2- Pterygoid branches to pterygoid canal. 

eo Cavernous part gives the following: 
1. Branches to the trigeminal ganglion. 
2. Hypophyseal branches to hypophysis cerebri. 
3. Meningeal branch to dura of anterior cranial fossa. 

e Cerebral part gives the following: 
1. Ophthalmic. 
2. Posterior communicating 
3. Anterior choroidal branches. 
4. Anterior& middle cerebral (terminals)

THE SCALP 

I. DEFINITION: It is the soft tissue that covers the vault of 
the skull. 

Il. EXTENT: (Fig. 4.33) 
e Anteriorly: Supra- orbital margin. 
e Posteriorly: External occipital protuberance and 

highest nuchal line. 
e Laterally: Superior temporal line. 

Fig.4.33: Extent of the scalp 
I1I.LAYERS: (Fig. 4.34) 

- The scalp is formed of 5 layers. The letters of the word "scalp" indicate each of these 

layers: 
S = Skin 
C = Connective tissue 
A = Aponeurosis 
L = Loose areolar tissue 
P = Pericranium. 

The first 3 layers are adherent together. 

Skin Dense connective tissue Aponsurotic layer 

Fig. 4.34: Layers of the scalp 
Pericranium 

1. Skin: Thick, hairy, highly vascular, rich in sweat and sebaceous glands (common site for 

sebaceous cyst due to blockage of the duct draining the sebaceous gland). 

2. Connective Tissue: it is a dense connective tissue, containing arteries, veins, nerves & 

lymphatics. It contains also septa that connect this layer with the layer above & the layer 

below: thus, the first 3 layers are adherent. The septa divide the layer into partitions that 

are filled with fat. 
Since the connective tissue is dense: 

(a) Infection tends to remain localized, however it causes pain. 
(b) Wounds do not gap. 
(c) A small wound causes profuse bleeding because the walls of arteries adhere to the 

connective tissue septa, which prevent them from contraction or retraction. 

3. Aponeurosis called Epicranial Aponeurosis: A strong fibrous sheet adherent to the first 2 

layers and it covers the skull vault like a helmet. 
e Anteriorly: it receives the insertion of frontal bellies of Occipito-frontalis. 

278 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

eo Laterally: it is attached to the superior temporal line. 
eo Posteriorly: it receives the insertion of occipital bellies of Occipito-frontalis (A 

wound reaching this layer gaps because of contraction of the muscle). 
Occipito-frontalis muscle: (Fig. 4.35) 
It is the muscle of the scalp that has 2 frontal and 2 occipital bellies. 

Table 4.1: Comparison between Frontal bellies and occipital bellies

Fig.4.35: Occipitofrontalis muscle 
4. Loose connective tissue: 

o tis continuous anteriorly with the similar layer of the upper eyelid. 
o Allows free mobility of the previous 3 layers on skull bones. 
o This loose areolar tissue allows spread of blood or pus beneath the aponeurosis. -- 

Blood or pus cannot pass to back of neck because of attachment of occipital bellies of 
occipito-frontalis to the occipital bone but can pass anteriorly since frontal bellies are 
not attached to bone, blood can enter the eyelids resulting in “Black eye”. 

o Itis considered the dangerous layer of the scalp as its emissary veins can spread 
infection from the scalp to the cranial cavity. 

5. Pericranium: 
o tis the periosteum of the skull bones; at the sutures it is continuous with the sutural 

ligaments and it is continuous with the inner periosteum at skull foramina. 
o Hemorrhage beneath this layer leads to a collection of blood (hematoma) that takes 

the shape of the underlying skull bone.

IV. BLOOD SUPPLY: 
o The scalp has rich blood supply. 
o The arteries anastomose freely with each other. 
o They are branches of either the external carotid artery (ECA) or internal carotid artery 

(ICA). 
A) Arteries of the scalp: (Fig.4.36) 

Table 4.2: Arteries of the scalp

Fig. 4.36: Arteries of the scalp 
Fig. 4.37 Veins of the scalp 

B) Veins of the scalp: (Fig. 4.37) 
- Veins of the scalp accompany the arteries and have similar names. 

- Veins of the scalp anastomose freely with one another and are connected to diploic 
veins of the skull bone and intracranial venous sinuses by "emissary veins". 

1. Supratrochlear & supraorbital veins unite at the medial angle of eye to 

form the facial vein (anterior facial). 
2. Superficial temporal vein unites with maxillary vein inside parotid gland to 

form retromandibular vein. The latter leaves the lower end of the parotid 
gland dividing into anterior and posterior divisions. 

280 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

3. Facial vein unites with the anterior division of retromandibular to form 
common facial vein that ends in internal jugular vein (JV). 

4. Posterior auricular vein unites with the posterior division of 
retromandibular vein to form the external jugular vein (EJV). 

5. Occipital vein drains the scalp posteriorly and ends into the suboccipital 
venous plexus. 

Emissary Veins (Fig. 4.38) 
- They are valve less veins that pass-through skull foramina to connect veins inside the 

cranial cavity (venous sinuses) with veins outside the cranial cavity. 
- Some are constant, while others may be absent. 
- They help to reduce venous pressure inside the cranial cavity, but they may be the 

routes of spread of infection from outside to the cranial cavity « 
- Example: Parietal emissary vein pass through parietal emissary foramen to connect 

veins of the scalp with superior sagittal sinus inside the skull. 

Fig. 4.38: Emissary Veins | 
V. NERVE SUPPLY: (Fig. 4.39) 

The scalp is supplied by 10 nerves (5 pre-auricular & 5 are retro- auricular: 4 sensory & 1 
motor).

VI. LYMPH DRAINAGE: 
- Pre auricular region: drained by parotid L.N. 

- Post auricular region: drained by mastoid & occipital L.Ns. 

ANATOMY OF THE FACE 

BOUNDARIES (Fig. 4.40) 
e Superiorly: The hair line. 
e Inferiorly: The lower border of mandible. 
eo Sideways: From one auricle to the other. 
(Thus, the forehead is common to both scalp and face). 

LAYERS: 
1. Skin: possesses numerous sweat and sebaceous glands. It Fig. 4.40: Boundaries of the face 

gives attachment to the muscles of facial expression. 
2. Superficial fascia: contains blood vessels, nerves, muscles & small amount of fat. 

3. Deep fascia is absent in most of the face except at the parotid gland and over 
buccinator muscle. 

Arteries of the face: (Fig. 4.41) 
e Two branches of ECA: The facial and the superficial temporal arteries which are 

considered the main supply of the face 
e Two branches of ICA: The Supratrochlear & supra-orbital arteries, supply the forehead. 

o Three small branches of maxillary artery: infra-orbital, mental and buccal. 

282 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

ie 

Fig. 4.41: arteries of the face 

1) Facial artery: (Fig. 4.42) is the chief supply of the face. 
Origin: a branch of ECA, it starts just above the tip of greater cornu of hyoid bone. it 
has a course in the neck and in the face 
Course in the face: 

- Enters the face by winding around the lower border of mandible & pierces 
the deep cervical fascia at antero-inferior angle of masseter m. 

- Runs upward & medially near the angle of mouth, ascend by the side of the 
nose till the medial angle of eye where it ends. 

- tis a tortuous artery to permit free movement of mandible, lips and 
cheeks. 

Termination: At the medial angle of eye, by anastomosing with dorsal nasal a. (a 
branch of ophthalmic a.). 
Branches in the face: 
1. Inferior labial, to lower lip. 2. Superior labial, to upper lip. 
3. Lateral nasal, to ala of nose. 4- Angular artery, to medial angle of eye 
5. Unnamed small muscular branches 
Anastomosis: 
a) The similar branches of both sides anastomose with each other in the midline. 
b) At the medial angle of eye, it anastomose with dorsal nasal of ophthalmic a. This is 

considered as anastomosis between the ICA & ECA. 

2) Superficial temporal artery: 
e Arises in the parotid gland as one of 2 terminal branches of the ECA. 
e Transverse facial artery arises from the superficial temporal artery within the 

parotid gland, runs forward across the cheek just above the parotid duct to supply 
the face.

CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

a —————— — 

Fig. 4.42: Facial artery 

VEINS OF THE FACE: (Fig. 4. 43) 
- Supratrochlear & supraorbital veins unite at the medial angle of eye to form the 

angular vein which continues as the anterior facial vein. 

- The superficial temporal vein unites with maxillary vein to form the 
retromandibular vein which divides into anterior & posterior divisions. 

- The anterior division unites with anterior facial vein to form the common facial 
vein that ends in internal jugular vein (LV). 

- The posterior division unites with the posterior auricular vein to form external 

jugular vein (EJV) that ends in the subclavian vein. 
Deep communications of anterior facial vein: 
It is connected with cavernous sinus via 2 routes: 

a) Facial vein is connected to superior ophthalmic vein which drains in cavernous sinus, 

b) Facial vein is connected to pterygoid venous plexus via the deep facial vein. 

c) Pterygoid venous plexus is connected to cavernous sinus via 3 emissary veins. 

Fig. 4.43: Veins of the face 

284 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

Dangerous area of the face (Fig. 4.44) a bz _. 
The triangular area of facial skin including upper lip, nose and a I 
medial angles of eye is considered dangerous area of face. If this 
area became infected e.g. by squeezing a boil on the skin of nose, 
the infection would spread to the cavernous sinus causing its 
thrombosis. 

Fig. 4.44: Dangerous area of the face 
NERVE SUPPLY: 
A) Sensory Nerve Supply of Face: (Fig.4.45 a) 
Skin of face is supplied mainly by branches of trigeminal nerve (V), except for a small area 
over the angle of mandible and capsule of parotid gland which are supplied by great 
auricular nerve (C2&3) which is a branch of cervical plexus. 

e Branches of Ophthalmic nerve: 
1. Supratrochlear: supplies skin of forehead. 
2. Supraorbital: supplies skin of forehead and skin of scalp up to the vertex. 
3. Palpebral branch of lacrimal: supplies lateral part of upper eye lid. 
4. Infratrochlear: supplies skin of medial part of upper eye lid and adjacent 

part of nose. 
5. External nasal: supplies skin of nose down to its tip. 

e Branches of Maxillary nerve: 
1. Zygomaticotemporal: supplies non-hairy part of temporal region 
2. Zygomaticofacial: supplies skin over zygomatic bone 
3. Infraorbital: It gives 3 branches: 

- Palpebral branch to the lower eye lid. 
- Nasal branch to the side and ala of nose. 
- Labial branch to the upper lip. 

e Branches of Mandibular nerve: 
1. Auriculotemporal: supplies hairy part of temporal region and auricle. 
2. Buccal: supplies skin of cheek over buccinators m. 
3. Mental: supplies skin of chin and lower lip.

Facial nerve 
- It leaves the skull through the stylomastoid foramen. 
- Branches in the neck: 

1. Posterior auricular branch to supply the occipital belly of occipito-frontalis and 

auricular muscles. 
2. Muscular branch to supply posterior belly of digastric and stylohyoid muscles. 

- Then, the facial nerve enters the parotid gland, where it forms a plexus called “Pes 

Anserinus” & then divides into its five terminal branches, which emerge from the 

anterior border of the gland and runs forwards across the face to supply facial 

muscles. 
- The terminal branches are: 

1. Temporal to frontalis and orbicularis oculi, 

2. Zygomatic to orbicularis oculi and other nearby muscles, 

3. Buccal to buccinator and orbicularis oris and muscles of upper lip, 

4. Mandibular to muscles of lower lip 
5. Cervical to platysma. 

N.B.: As the branches of the facial nerve radiate outwards | 

across the face, incisions made in the face should be 

transverse & parallel to these branches in order to reduce 

the likelihood of cutting them. 

Fig. 4.45b: Facial nerve in the face 

LYMPH DRAINAGE (Fig. 4.46) 
1. Area medial to facial artery is drained by submandibular L.N. 

2. Area lateral to facial artery is drained by parotid L.N. 

3. Central part of lower lip & chin is drained by submental L.N.

Muscles of orbit and eye lids 
A) ORBICULARIS OCULI (Fig. 4.48a and b) 

e [tis the sphincter muscle of eyelids encircling the orbit 
e [t has 3 parts: 

1. Orbital part: arises from medial Palpebral ligament and adjacent bones (frontal 
process of maxilla and nasal process of frontal bone). It encircies the orbit 
without bony attachments laterally. 

2. Palpebral part: arises from medial Palpebral ligament and adjacent bones, it is 
inserted laterally in the lateral palpebral raphe. 

3. Lacrimal part: arises from lacrimal crest and lacrimal fascia; it is inserted in lateral 
palpebral raphe. 

ni — 

Fig.4.48: Orbicularis oculi 
e Actions: 

1. Orbital part: closes eye lids tightly for protection. 
2. Palpebral part: closes eye lids gently as in blinking & helps in spread of tears. 
3. Lacrimal part: dilates the lacrimal sac and helps in flow of tears.

Muscles of mouth and lips: 
A) BUCCINATOR: (Fig. 4. 49a and b) 

e Itis the muscle of the cheek it is covered by the buccopharyngeal fascia and buccal 

pad of fat. 
e Origin: 

- Upper fibers: from alveolar margin of maxilla opposite the 3 molars. 
- Lower fibers: from alveolar margin of mandible opposite the 3 molars. 

- Middle fibers: from pterygomandibular raphe which is a fibrous raphe 
connecting pterygoid hamulus and posterior end of mylohyoid line of 

mandible. 
e Insertion: 

- Upper fibers: into upper lip. 
V - Lower fibers: into lower lip. 
! - Middle fibers: decussate at the modiolus so that their upper part goes to the 

lower lip and their lower part goes to upper lip. 

Fig.4.49: Buccinator muscle 
eo Action: compresses the cheeks against the teeth preventing the accumulation of 

food in the vestibule of mouth. It is used in blowing, whistling and suckling. 

N.B.: Modiolus: is a fibromuscular palpable mass present just lateral to angle of mouth 

where muscle fibers converge towards or diverge away from angle of mouth 

B) ORBICULARIS ORIS (Fig.4.50) 
‘ -  Itis the sphincter of lips & is used in speech. aii 

- It consists of 4 quadrants upper, lower, right and left. Each 
8 quadrant consists of 2 parts: 

1. A large pars peripheralis. Fic 
2. A small pars marginalis. 

Thus, the muscle is formed of 8 segments. Each segment Pars | 

resembles a fan with its stem at modiolus radiating to a 

the dermis and submucosa of lips. It is used in speech

Fig. 4.54: Muscles of mastication 

C) Actions: 
1. Temporalis: 

a) Its anterior vertical fibers are strong elevator of the mandible (antigravity muscle). 
b) Its posterior horizontal fibers retract the mandible (only retractor of mandible). 

2. Masseter: 
a) Its deep vertical fibers elevate the mandible (antigravity muscle). 

y b) Its superficial oblique fibers protrude the mandible. 
3. Medial pterygoid: 

a) Elevates the mandible. 
b) Helps lateral pterygoid to protrude the mandible. 
c) Medial and lateral pterygoid of one side acting together push mandible to the 

opposite side & when acting alternatively with muscles of the opposite side, they 
produce side to side chewing movement. 

4. Lateral pterygoid: 
a) Depresses the mandible to open the mouth. 
b) Protrudes the mandible. 
c) Side to side movement (see medial pterygoid).

Temporomandibular Joint (TMJ): 
Type: Synovial - condylar. 
» Articulating bones:{ Fig: 4. 55) 

1- Head of mandible articulates with mandibular fossa & articular tubercle. 
2- Articular surfaces are covered by fibrocartilage. 

» Capsule: It is attached to margins of articular surfaces. 
» Synovial membrane: Lines the fibrous capsule. 
» Articular disc: 

1. It is formed of fibrocartilage 
2. Its upper surface is concavo-convex (to fit the articular eminence & mandibular fossa), 

while its lower surface is concave (to fit the head of mandible).. 
It divides the joint cavity into upper and lower compartments. 

4. Its circumference is attached to: 
a. The fibrous capsule. 
b. Tendon of lateral pterygoid muscle. 

Mandibular Fossa 

Articular 
Tubercle / Articular Disk 

Mandibular head 
Fig. 4.55: Articulating bones ot TMJ 

» Ligaments: (Fig. 4.56) 
1. Lateral (Temporo-mandibular) ligament: 

a. It lies lateral to the fibrous capsule. 
b. Is attached above to the tubercle on root of zygomatic process and below to lateral 

surface of neck of mandible. 
c. It limits posterior movement of mandible; thus, protecting the external auditory 

meatus and preventing posterior dislocation of head of mandible. 

2. Stylo-mandibular ligament: 
a. It lies posterior to fibrous capsule. 
b. Extends from apex of styloid process to angle of mandible. 
c. It is a condensation of the investing layer of deep cervical fascia that separates 

parotid from submandibular salivary gland. 

3. Spheno-mandibular ligament: 
a. It lies medial to joint. 
b. Extends from spine of sphenoid to lingula of mandibular foramen. 
c. lt is an embryological remnant of the first pharyngeal arch.

| Joint Capsule

BB sphenomandibular ligament

Fig. 4.56: Ligaments of TMJ 

>» Movements: 
At rest the 2 jaws are separated by minimal interval, when the jaws are closed both teeth 

come into opposition (occlusal position). 

Y 1- Depression: 
- Lateral pterygoid pulls the head of mandible and the disc forwards causing depression 

of the mandible (by a hinge like movement). 
- Assisted by action of digastric, geniohyoid + mylohyoid & also simply by the effect of 

gravity. 

2- Elevation: 
- In which the reverse of depression occurs 
- Performed by temporalis, masseter and medial pterygoid muscles. 

3- Protrusion: 
- In which the head of mandible and disc move forwards and the lower teeth are thus 

drawn forwards. 
- Done by both lateral & medial pterygoid + superficial fibers of masseter. 

4- Retraction: 
- Done by posterior fibers of temporalis. 

5- Side to side movements {chewing movements): 
. Performed by medial and lateral pterygoids of one side acting alternatively with the 

muscles of the other side. 
- When medial and lateral pterygoids of one side contract, they push the chin to the 

opposite side. 

> Blood supply: Superficial temporal artery & maxillary artery. 
» Nerve supply: Auriculotemporal & masseteric nerves. 

Applied anatomy: Dislocation of the joint occurs mostly in an anterior direction because: 

1. TM lies just in front of the external auditory meatus. 

2. The lateral temporomandibular ligament prevents backward dislocation of the joint, 

thereby protecting the external auditory meatus.

TTRIGEMINAL NERVE 
MANDIBULAR & MAXILLARY NERVES 

Trigeminal Nerve: (Fig. 4.57) 

A) General notes: 
> It is the 5" and largest cranial nerve. 
» It is a mixed nerve carrying sensory and motor fibers. 
» lt is sensory to skin of scalp, face (except skin over angle of mandible). Also, teeth, oral 

and nasal cavities 
» It is motor to the 4 muscles of mastication + mylohyoid, anterior belly of digastric, 

tensor palate & tensor tympani.

Fig. 4.57: Trigeminal nerve 
B) Roots of Trigeminal Nerve: 

» It has 2 roots: A small motor root and a large sensory root, both are attached to the 
ventral surface of pons. 
* Sensory root fibers: are axons (central processes) of pseudounipolar cells of the 

trigeminal ganglion. Peripheral processes of these cells will form the 3 divisions of 
trigeminal nerve: ophthalmic nerve, maxillary nerve & mandibular nerve. 

** Motor root: arises from motor nucleus of trigeminal, passes beneath the trigeminal 
ganglia to be distributed with mandibular nerve. 

C) Mandibular Nerve: (Fig. 4.58) 
Type: mixed sensory & motor. 

* Size: the largest branch of trigeminal nerve. 
* Intracranial course: It starts in the middle cranial fossa, has 2 roots: large sensory & 

small motor roots, both pass through foramen ovale below which they unite to form 
trunk of mandibular nerve 

%* Extracranial course: The nerve trunk enters the infratemporal fossa where it is 
related to: 
v' Medially: tensor palati m.& otic ganglion, 
v' Laterally: lateral pterygoid m. 
v' Posteriorly: middle meningeal a.

Fig. 4.58: Mandibular nerve 
Branches: 
1) From the trunk: (one sensory + one motor) 

a. Sensory meningeal (nervous spinosus): passes with middle meningeal a. via 
foramen spinosum to supply dura of middle cranial fossa. 

b. Nerve to medial pterygoid: supplies medial pterygoid and sends a branch that 

passes to otic ganglia without relay to supply tensor palati and tensor tympani 
muscles. 

c. Anterior & posterior divisions. 
2) Branches of anterior division: (3 motor + one sensory nerves) 

a. 2 deep temporal nerves: appear at upper border of lateral pterygoid m., ascend 

to temporal fossa & enter the deep surface of temporalis m. 

b. Masseteric nerve: appears at upper border of lateral pterygoid m. & passes 

through. 
the mandibular notch to enter the deep surface of masseter m. 

¢. Nerve to lateral pterygoid m.: enters the deep surface of the muscle. 
d. Buccal nerve: is the only sensory nerve of the anterior division, emerges between 

the 2 heads of lateral pterygoid m. then runs on the buccinator & pierces it 

without supplying it. It supplies skin & mucosa of the cheek. 
3) Branches of posterior division: (2 sensory + one mixed). 

. Auriculotemporal nerve. (Sensory) 
Lingual nerve. (Sensory) 
Inferior alveolar nerve. (Mixed) 

. Auriculotemporal Nerve 
= Arises by 2 roots which run backwards encircling the middle meningeal artery 

then reunite to form one trunk that continues backward between spheno- 

mandibular ligament & neck of mandible above maxillary artery. 
» Ascends upwards deep to parotid gland, behind superficial temporal vessels.

= Branches: 
i. Auricular branch: to the skin of upper 2/3 of auricle, external auditory 

meatus & outer surface of the tympanic membrane. 
ii. Temporal branch: to the skin of the temporal region. 
iii. Glandular branches: to parotid gland. 
iv. Articular branch: to temporomandibular joint. 

b. Linqual Nerve 
- It arises deep to lateral pterygoid m. where it is joined by the chorda tympani 
nerve. 

- It appears at lower border of lateral pterygoid, passes downwards between 
mandible & medial pterygoid m. (pterygo-mandibular space), anterior to 
inferior alveolar nerve. | 

- It enters the floor of mouth on the medial side of the root of the 3rd molar | 
tooth where it is in direct contact with the mandible covered only by gum 
mucosa 
(Being superficial, the lingual nerve is liable to injury during extraction of lower 
last molar hence it is considered a dangerous site). 

- In the floor of mouth, it crosses styloglossus, hyoglossus and genioglossus. On 
hyoglossus, the submandibular ganglion hangs from it. Then it hooks around 
the submandibular duct from lateral to medial. 

- Distribution: 
i. Ganglionic branches: to the submandibular ganglion. 

ii. Sensory branches: to anterior 2/3 of tongue, floor of mouth and lingual 
aspect of gingiva. 

iii. Carry taste sensation from anterior 2/3 of tongue via chorda tympani. 
iv. Carry postganglionic parasympathetic to submandibular & sublingual 

salivary glands via chorda tympani. 

¢. Inferior alveolar nerve 
- The largest & the only mixed branch {motor & sensory). 
- [t appears at the lower border of lateral pterygoid m. then passes vertically 
between ramus of mandible & medial pterygoid m. to enter mandibular 
foramen & mandibular canal. 

- It exits from mental foramen as mental nerve. 

- Branches: 
a) Mylohyoid branch: Contains all motor fibers of the posterior division, arises 

just before the nerve enters the mandibular foramen, pierces the 
sphenomandibular ligament & runs in the mylohyoid groove to supply 
mylohyoid & anterior belly of digastric muscles. 

b) Mental nerve: passes through mental foramen to supply skin of chin & 
lower lip. 

c) Incisive nerve: to supply the lower canine and incisors teeth. 
D) Maxillary Nerve: {Figure 4.59) 
» Origin: Trigeminal nerve 
» Type: purely sensory nerve 
» Size: smaller than mandibular 

297 



CHAPTER IV BRAIN STEM, CRANIAL CAVITY, TEMPORAL REGION AND PTERYGOPALATINE FOSSA 

> Intracranial course: -Starts in the middle cranial fossa, runs forward in the lateral wall 

of cavernous sinus below ophthalmic n & leaves skull through foramen rotundum. 

>» Extracranial course: 
1. It enters the pterygopalatine fossa (related to pterygopalatine ganglion & 3 part 

of maxillary a) 
2. Leaves the pterygopalatine fossa via pterygo-maxillary fissure => infra-temporal 

fossa = infraorbital fissure here, it is called infraorbital nerve => infraorbital 

groove, canal & foramen -> face. 
» Branches: 

1. In the cranial cavity: Meningeal nerve to supply dura of middle cranial fossa. 
2. In the pterygopalatine fossa 

a. Two ganglionic branches: carry sensory fibers to the ganglion. 
b. Zygomatic nerve: passes to the orbit via inferior orbital fissure where it divides 

into 2 branches: 
i. Zygomatico-facial n., passes via its foramen to skin over zygomatic bone. 

ii. Zygomatico-temporal n., communicates with lacrimal nerve carrying with it 

the Parasympathetic supply to the lacrimal gland then passes via its foramen 
to supply skin of the non-hairy part of temporal region. 

¢c. Posterior superior alveolar n.: passes via pterygo-maxillary fissure to enter 

multiple foramina in the posterior surface of maxilla to supply mucosa of 
maxillary air sinus & upper 3 molars.

3. Branches of the infra- orbital nerve:
