Pterygopalatine Fossa (Fig. 4.60) 

» It is a small inverted pyramidal space found medial to pterygo-maxillary fissure. 
» Boundaries: 

1. Anterior wall: Posterior surface of maxilla. 
2. Posterior wall: Pterygoid process 
3. Medial wall: Vertical plate of palatine bone. 
4. laterally: it opens into infratemporal fossa through pterygomaxillary fissure. 

» Contents: 
1. 3" Part of maxillary artery and its branches & accompanying veins. 
2. Maxillary nerve. 
3. Pterygopalatine ganglion and its branches

» Communications (Fig. 4.61) 
1. With the infratemporal fossa via pterygomaxillary fissure (transmitting maxillary 

artery). 
2. With the middle cranial fossa via foramen rotundum (transmitting maxillary nerve). 

3. With the nose via sphenopalatine foramen (transmitting nasopalatine nerve and other 
nasal branches of pterygopalatine ganglion). 

4. With the orbit via inferior orbital fissure (transmitting zygomatic nerve and infraorbital 

nerve and vessels). 
5. With the pterygoid canal (transmitting nerve of pterygoid canal). 
6. With the pharynx via palatino-vaginal canal (transmitting pharyngeal branch of 

maxillary artery & pharyngeal branch of pterygopalatine ganglia). 
7. With the palate via greater & lesser palatine foramina (transmitting greater and lesser 

palatine nerves & vessels respectively).

Vessels in the infrate ral fossa: 

A. Maxillary Artery (Fig. 4.62) 
> Origin: 1 of 2 terminal branches of ECA, it begins inside parotid gland at the level of 

neck of mandible 
> Course & relations: it is divided into3 parts by means of lateral pterygoid muscle 

1) The 1st part (Mandibular part): runs horizontally between the neck of mandible & 

sphenomandibular ligament below auriculotemporal nerve, to reach the lower 

border of lateral pterygoid. 

2) The 2™ part (Pterygoid part): runs upward & forward either superficial or deep to 
the lateral surface of lateral pterygoid muscle. 

3)The 3 part (Pterygopalatine part): passes between the 2 heads of lateral pterygoid, 

enters the pterygopalatine fossa via pterygomaxillary fissure.

B. Pterygoid plexus of veins: (Fig. 4.64) 
» It is a venous network that surrounds lateral pterygoid muscle. 
» Tributaries: It receives veins that correspond to the branches of maxillary artery. 
» Drainage: Its posterior end is drained by maxillary vein, which unites with superficial 

temporal vein to form the retro-mandibular vein. 
» Communications: 

1. With the facial vein via the deep facial vein. 
2. With cavernous sinus via emissary veins that pass-through foramen ovale, emissary 

sphenoidal foramen & foramen lacerum. 
3. With the inferior ophthalmic vein via a vein that passes through inferior orbital 

fissure.

Autonomic Ganglia 

There are four ganglia suspended from the 3 divisions of the trigeminal nerve. They are otic, 

submandibular, pterygopalatine and ciliary ganglia.

CHAPTER V 



CHAPTER V ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

CHAPTER V: ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

Intended Learning Outcomes (ILOs): 

By the end of this chapter the student should be able to: 

e Describe the division and attachement of fascia of the neck. 
e List boundaries, floor, subdivisions and contents of anterior and posterior 

triangles of the neck. 
e¢ Describe attachment, action and nerve supply of sternomastoid. | 
oe Describe attachment, action and nerve supply of suprahyoid and infrahyoid 

muscles. 
e List nerves forming and branches of cervical plexus. 
e List the site of sympathetic chain and communications of its ganglia. 
oe Discuss the course and branches of last four cranial nerves and predict the 

effect of their lesion. 

307 



CHAPTER V ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

FASCIA OF THE NECK 

0 Skin; of the neck is supplied by cutaneous branches of the cervical plexus. 
0 Superficial Fascia: (Fig. 5.1) Contains platysma, which arises from the fascia covering the 

chest and ascends to the face. It is supplied by the facial nerve (cervical branch). it is one 

of the muscles of facial expression and can express sadness, horror by drawing the corners 

of mouth inferiorly. 
» Superficial Veins: 
< External jugular vein: Formed by union of posterior auricular vein and posterior 

division of retromandibular vein. It descends superficial to sternocleidomastoid and 

pierces the deep fascia in the lower part of the posterior triangle to drain into the 

subclavian vein. 
eo Tributaries: 

1. Anterior jugular vein. 
2. Transverse cervical vein. 
3. Suprascapular vein. 
4, Posterior external jugular vein. 

Pectoral lnacia Juguias venous serch Stamoc! isidomasmiuoscklde 
yor of 

Fig. 5.1: Superficial dissection of the neck 

Remember: 
% Superficial cervical lymph nodes accompany the oXtorPal jugular vein 
< This vein serves as venous manometer. It is either invisible or observable for only a 

short distance superior to the clavicle if the venous pressure is within the normal range. 

308 



CHAPTER V ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

However, if this pressure rises (as in heart failure or in singers) the vein becomes 
prominent throughout its course along the side of neck. 
* Anterior Jugular Vein: Begins below the chin and descends one on each side of the 

anterior median plane of neck. Just above the suprasternal notch, the right and left 
veins are united by the jugular arch. Each vein then turns laterally deep to the 
sternocleidomastoid muscle to drain into the external jugular vein. 

» Deep Fascia: (Fig. 5.2) Lies beneath platysma muscie and supports muscles, vessels, 
and nerves of neck. It is condensed to form the following four layers: 
1. investing layer. 
2. Pretracheal layer. 
3. Prevertebral layer. | 
4. Carotid sheath. 

infrahyoid muscles: 

4=  rr 

Nuchal Sgament 

Fig. 5.2: T.S in the neck showing the arrangement of the deep fascia 

1. Investing Layerof  D ervical Fascia; 
» The outer layer surrounds the neck like a collar, lies immediately deep to the 

platysma. 
» |t forms the roof of anterior and posterior triangles of neck. 
> It splits to invest sternocleidomastoid and trapezius muscles, in addition to 

investing parotid and submandibular glands and thickens to form the 
stylomandibular ligament between the two glands. 

2. The prevertebral fascia: represents the 2nd layer (lies in front of the floor of 
posterior triangle of the neck).

Sternocleidomastoid muscle 

*Origin: (Fig. 5.4) 
1- Sternal head: superolateral aspect of anterior surface of manubrium. 
2- Clavicular head: medial 1/3 of the upper surface of clavicle. 

*Insertion: 
1- Mastoid process. 
2- Lateral 1/3 of superior nuchal line. 

*Nerve supply: 
1- Motor: spinal accessory nerve. 
2- Sensory: ventral rami of C 2&3 (proprioceptive).

TRIANGLES OF THE NECK 

The anterolateral part of the neck is divided by means of the sternocleidomastoid muscle 

into two triangles: anterior and posterior. 

Posterior Triangle 
*Sjte: A triangular space on the side of the neck situated behind the sternomastoid muscle. 

*Boundaries: (Fig. 5.6) 
- Anterior: Posterior border of sternomastoid. 
- Base (Inferiorly): Middle 1/3 of clavicle. 
- Apex: Lies superiorly, where trapezius and sternomastoid meet. 

- Roof: formed of skin, superficial fascia containing (platysma, external jugular vein and 

cutaneous branches of cervical plexus & deep fascia (investing layer of deep cervical 

fascia). 
- Floor: formed by the prevertebral layer of the deep cervical fascia, covering the following 
muscles (from above downwards): (Fig. 5.6) 

1) Semispinalis capitis (inconstant). 
2) Splenius capitis. 
3) Levator scapulae. 
4) Scalenus medius. 
5) Scalenus anterior (hidden behind sternocleidomastoid).

1) Submental Triangle: (Fig. 5.14) 

*Definition: A median triangle, formed by the meeting of the two anterior triangles 

superiorly, between the chin and the hyoid bone. 
Boundaries:

- Apex: Symphysis menti. Mandible 

- On each side: Anterior belly of digastric. 
N.B.: Half of the Submental triangle is bounded anteriorly 
by median plane, on the side by anterior belly of digastric 
and below by hyoid bone.

2) Submental artery and vein.

4) Mylohyoid nerve and vessels.

*Contents: (Fig. 5.18)

- The carotid body lies posteriorly at the same site embedded in the adventitia of these

b) Ansa cervicalis, lies anterior to the sheath. 
d) Sympathetic chain runs vertically downwards posterior to the carotid sheath. 

D) Carotid sheath, with its vascular and nervous contents. 

E) Lymph Nodes: Deep cervical lymph nodes, situated along the JV.

Infrahyoid Muscles 

» Origin & insertion: (Fig. 5.19 a,b&c) : | 
Muscle | ~ Origin | Insertion 

OE 3) Back of manubrium 

of Sternohy oid | + medial end of the clavicle Hyoid hone 

| A Back of manubrium Oblique line of the thyroid 
Serothyrsia + back of the Ist costal cartilage cartilage.

Omohyoid | Inf.belly: upper border of scapula | in position by a facial sling

» Nerve Supply: All infra-hyoid muscles are supplied by ansa cervicalis, except thyrohyoid 
muscle, which is supplied by Cifibers that accompany the hypoglossal nerve. 

= Actions: All infrahyoid muscles depress the larynx except thyrohyoid, which can elevate 
or depress the larynx. 
1- Sternohyoid: depresses hyoid bone after its elevation during swallowing and vocal 

movements. 
2- Sternothyroid: depresses larynx after its elevation in swallowing and vocal 

movements. 
3- Thyrohyoid: depresses hyoid bone + elevate larynx. 
4- Omohyoid: depresses hyoid in prolonged inspiratory efforts & tenses the lower deep 

cervical fascia.

Fig. 5.20 b: Suprahyoid Mu scles - Lateral view 

** Actions of suprahyoid muscles: 
1) Digastric: 

a. If the hyoid bone is fixed, it depresses the mandible (helping lateral pterygoid m.). 

b. Elevate hyoid bone during swallowing. 
2) Stylohyoid: pulls hyoid bone upward & backward. 
3) Mylohyoid m.: 

a. Elevates the floor of mouth during the early stage of swallowing. 
b. Helps in depression of the mandible (if the hyoid bone is fixed). 
c. Supports the floor of the mouth (called Diaphragma oris). 

4) Geniohyoid m.: Elevates hyoid bone or depresses the mandible (if the hyoid bone is fixed).

Q Construction: - formed by the ventral rami of upper 4 cervical nerves. 
Q Site of formation: between anterior & posterior tubercles of cervical transverse processes. 
O Relations: Lies between Scalenus medius posteriorly and prevertebral fascia anteriorly. 
Q How formed: each nerve is divided into upper & lower divisions which unite together to 

form 3 loops.

Fig. 5.21: Cervical plexus 
Q Branches: - 3 groups: 

a) Communicating branches. 
1. Grey rami from superior cervical ganglion to all roots. 
2. From C1 to hypoglossal (forming superior limb of ansa cervicalis). 
3. From C2 to sternomastoid & C2, 3&4 to trapezius (sensory supply). 

b) Cutaneous branches (see posterior triangle) 
1- Supraclavicular (medial - lateral - intermediate) (C3&4). 
2- Transverse cervical (C2&3). 
3- Great auricular (C2&3). 
4- Lesser occipital (C2) 

¢) Muscular to: 
1- Infrahyoid muscles by ansa cervicalis. 
2- Prevertebral and scalene muscles. 
3- Levator scapulae, trapezius, and sternomastoid. 
4- Diaphragm by phrenic nerve. 

*Ansa cervicalis: (Fig. 5.18¢ & Fig. 5.22) 
* A nervous loop related to infrahyoid muscles & carotid sheath. 
*Site: Front of carotid sheath at the level of lower part of larynx. 
*Structure: formed of 2 limbs:

leave it & descend in front of carotid sheath. 
2- Inferior limb: (descending cervicalis): Formed of fibers from C2&3 that descend in front of 

IJV, and then join the superior limb in front of the sheath. 
*Branches: - supply the infra-hyoid muscles from the convexity of the loop.

Cervical Sympathetic Trunk 
(Fig. 5.23) 

General notes: 
. A ganglionic chain presents on each side of the neck, extends from the neck of the first rib 

to the base of the skull. 
. Each chain lies between prevertebral fascia posteriorly & carotid sheath anteriorly 

opposite the transverse processes of cervical vertebrae. 
« It carries 3 ganglia on each side (superior, middle & inferior). 
. There is no white rami communicant in the neck, so their preganglionic fibers arise from 

upper thoracic segments then ascend in the chain. 
« Their postganglionic fibers divided into 3 groups: 

a- Ist group acts as communicant rami to the nearest nerves. 
b- 2nd group form vascular plexus around the nearest artery. 
c- 3rd group sends visceral branches to the nearest viscera. 

» Some of the nerve fibers connecting the middle and inferior cervical ganglia turn around 
the subclavian artery and are called ansa subclavia. 

324 



CHAPTER V ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

» The source of the pre-ganglionic sympathetic fibers to the head and neck is from the 
upper thoracic ganglia and ascend in the cervical sympathetic trunk.

THE LAST FOUR CRANIAL NERVE 

1- GlossopharyNenrvaee (aIX)l: (Fig. 5.24) 
» General notes: - The nerve of 3rd arch 
» Type: - mixed cranial nerve. 
» Number of ganglia: 2 ganglia, superior & inferior. 
>» Types of fibers: 

1. Branchiomotor to stylopharyngeus (m.). 
2. General visceral efferent Secretomotor to parotid gland. 
3. Special visceral afferent taste from post. 1/3 of tongue. 
4. General somatic afferent from oropharynx, posterior 1/3 of tongue, tonsils, 

auditory tube & middle ear. 
> Exit from the skull: through the middle part of jugular foramen. 
» Extracranial course: 

- Descends between [/V & ICA deep to styloid apparatus, 
- Then turns around stylopharyngeus m, 
- Passes between 1.1.V. & I.C.A. (within carotid sheath). 
- Passes between |.C.A. & E.C.A. (lying on Stylopharyngeus muscle.) 
- It curves forwards and medially to pass between superior and middle constrictors 
of pharynx and deep to hyoglossus muscle to be distributed to tonsil, tongue 

(posterior 1/3 and vallate papillae) and pharynx (mucous membrane). 

326 



CHAPTER V ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

Fig. 5.24.: Glossopharyngeal nerve 

» Branches: (Fig. 5.25) 
1. Tympanic branch: arises from the inferior ganglion to enter the middle ear via 

tympanic canaliculus & share in tympanic plexus {gives branches to greater petrosal 
n., lesser petrosal n. (for parotid gland) + sensory supply to mucosa of middle ear, 
auditory tube & mastoid air cells). 

. Carotid branch: descends on the wall of ICA to supply the carotid sinus & carotid 
body 

. Pharyngeal: share in pharyngeal plexus supply pharynx by sensory fibers. 

. Muscular: branch to stylopharyngeus m. 

. Tonsillar: share in tonsillar plexus to supply palatine tonsil & soft palate. 

. Lingual (terminal): to mucosa of posterior 1/3 of tongue, carry general &special 
taste sensations. 

Applied anatomy: 
1. Tested clinically by gentle tickling of posterior wall of pharynx to produce contraction of 

throat muscles, lost if paralyzed. 
2. Usually affected in combination with 10%.

4. Special visceral afferent (Gustatory to epiglottis). 
5. General somatic afferent (Sensory from external ear). 

>» Exit from the skull: through the middle part of jugular foramen. 

» Extracranial course: 
- Below the inferior ganglion, the vagus nerve is joined by cranial part of accessory 
nerve, which is distributed through its pharyngeal and recurrent laryngeal branches. 

- The vagus runs vertically down the neck within the carotid sheath, lying at first 
between [.J.V. & |.C.A., then between |.J.V. & C.C.A. 

- It crosses first part of subclavian artery to enter thorax (on Rt. Side) but passes 
between Lt. subclavian artery and Lt. C.C.A. to enter thorax (on left. side). 

» Branches: (Fig. 5.27 & 5.28) divided as following:-(2 branches from superior ganglion + 
2 from inferior ganglion +3 from vagus in the neck +2 branches in the thorax). 
A) From the superior vagal ganglion: 

1- Meningeal nerve - supplies the dura of the posterior cranial fossa. 
2- Auricular nerve: get outside via tympano- mastoid fissure to supply {concha & 

root of auricle, + post. 1/2 of external auditory meatus & tympanic membrane). 
B) From inferior vaqal ganglion: 

1- Pharyngeal nerve: (Most of its fibers are from cranial accessory n.) reaches 
middle constrictor to share in pharyngeal plexus. 

2- Superior laryngeal nerve: divides into 2 branches: 
*External laryngeal nerve: passes with superior thyroid artery then pierces 
inferior constrictor supplying it and cricothyroid (m.) 
*Internal laryngeal nerve: Passes downward, pierces thyrohyoid membrane to 
supply laryngeal mucosa above vocal cord. 

C) Branches int he neck: 
1. Carotid nerve supplying carotid body. 
2. Cardiac nerves (superior and inferior) from each vagus, the left inferior passes to 

superficial cardiac plexus while the other 3 share in deep cardiac plexus. 
3. Rt recurrent laryngeal nerve arises from RT vagus in front of RT subclavian a. 

then turns around it &ascends in tracheo-esophageal groove to pass with 
inferior thyroid a. to supply larynx (muscle& mucosa below vocal cord). 

D) Branches of in the Thorax: 
1- Left recurrent laryngeal nerve (winds around arch of aorta). 
2- Pulmonary and cardiac branches.

Applied anatomy: 
1- Tested clinically by examining the palatal arches on both sides, on paralysis there is no 

arching and uvula is deviated to the normal side. 
2- Vagal paralysis leads to (nasal regurgitation, hoarseness of voice, flattened palatal arches, 

cadaveric position of vocal cords & dysphagia). 
3- Irritation of auricular branch of vagus in external ear by wax or ear wash causes reflex 

cough. 

3: Accessory Nerve (XI): (Fig. 5.29) 

> Type: mixed n. formed of 2 separate parts spinal &cranial {join 10). 

= Cranial Part of Accessory: 
- Arises from nucleus ambiguus of medulla oblongata. 
- Exit from brain: (Medulla) groove between olive and inferior cerebellar peduncle 
below vagus nerve. 

- It runs to jugular foramen where it unites with spinal part. 
- Exit from skull: Through jugular foramen with vagus and glossopharyngeal 
nerves. 

- After its exit from jugular foramen, it separates from spinal part and unites with 
vagus. 

331 



CHAPTER V ANATOMY OF THE NECK 

- It is distributed to pharynx, palate and larynx through pharyngeal and recurrent 
laryngeal branches of vagus. 

= Spinal Part of Accessory: 
- Axons of nerve cells in spinal accessory nucleus (found in upper 5 cervical 
segments). 

- Ascend and enter cranial cavity through foramen magnum. 
- Joins cranial root as they pass to jugular foramen. 
- Separates from cranial root and supplies sternomastoid and trapezius. (Fig. 5.29)

Applied anatomy:

LJ In unilateral paralysis, the tongue is deviated to the affected side & when retracted to inside 
the paralyzed side raises up higher. 
In bilateral paralysis, the tongue lies motionless, while sensation is perfect; articulation is 
slow with difficult swallowing.

CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 
SLEEP AND EPILEPSY 

Intended Learning Outcomes (ILOs): 

By the end of this chapter the student should be able to: 

e¢ Describe different parts of diencephalon, main nuclei, connections, relations and 
blood supply. 

¢ Describe boundaries communications, relations and recesses of the third 
ventricle. 
Describe the content and function of reticular formation. 
Identify the role of reticular activating system and factors affecting it. 
Outline the types of brain potentials. 
Describe the electroencephalogram waves and mention the value of EEG. 
Differentiate between sleep and coma and between different types of sleep. 
Explain function and mechanism of sleep. 
Classify different types of anxiolytic, hypnotics and anesthetics. 
Predict therapeutic uses and adverse effects of each drug. 
Apply the pharmacological bases for proper selection of the anxiolytic and 
hypnotic drugs in the management of anxiety disorders and sleep disorders. 
Realize the advantages of sedative hypnotics and sedative anxiolytic over 
benzodiazepine. 
Explain the action and advantages of Ramelteon. 
Classify different types of general anesthetics. 
Realize the concept of balanced anesthesia and how to achieve. 
ldentify the molecular mechanism of action and adverse effects of general 
anesthetics. 
Recognize the advantage of new general anesthetics. 

oe |dentify the molecular mechanism of action of local anesthetics, uses, toxicity 
and side effects. 

e Explain the importance of vasoconstrictors, tissue pH and drug pKa on activity of 
local anesthetics. 

e (Classify antiepileptic drugs and identify their mechanism of action. 
Choose the proper antiepileptic drugs for each type of epilepsy and according to 
age, gender and daily activity. 
Apply pharmacokinetic bases for proper combinations of antiepileptic drugs. 
Link drug interactions with specific adverse effect of antiepileptic drugs. 
Diagnose convulsive status epilepticus and its serious complications. 
Construct a summarized treatment algorithm for status epilepticus based on the 
most recent guidelines. 
Provide a pharmacotherapeutic plan for termination of acute migraine attack. 

e Decide the appropriate drugs that can be used for migraine prophylaxis. 

338 



CHAPTER VI; DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 
sleep & epilepsy 

DIENCEPHALON (Fig. 6.1) 

The diencephalon includes everything with the name thalamus i.e. Thalamus, hypothalamus, 
epithalamus & subthalamus. 
Its cavity is the 3 ventricle. 
It is divided by the hypothalamic sulcus (which extends from the interventricular foramen to 
the mouth of the cerebral aqueduct) into 

Fig. 6.1: medial surface of cerebral hemisphere 
Dorsal part: 

1. Thalamus. 
2. Metathalamus (MGB & LGB). 
3. Epithalamus {pineal body, habenular nuclei & posterior commissure). 

Ventral part: 
1. Hypothalamus. 
2. Subthalamus. 

THALA 

e tis an oval mass of grey matter which acts as a gateway for the cerebral cortex. 
it relays all sensations except smell. It also relays motor and limbic impulses going to the 
cerebral cortex. 

e It has 2 ends: 
- A narrow anterior end which lies posterior to interventricular foramen of Monro. 
- Its posterior end is expanded to form the pulvinar. 

o |t has 4 surfaces: (Figs. 6.1 & 6.2) 
- Superior surface -» is free, related to the choroid plexus and forms part of the floor of 

the body of lateral ventricle. 
- Inferior surface —-> is separated by hypothalamic sulcus from subthalamus & 

hypothalamus. The posterior part of this surface shows the medial & lateral geniculate 
bodies. 

- Medial surface => Forms the lateral wall of 3 ventricle. Both medial surfaces are 
connected together by the interthalamic adhesion (connexus). 

- Lateral surface —- separated from the lentiform nucleus by the internal capsule. 

339 



CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 

sleep & epilepsy

Thalamic nuclei: (Fig. 6.3) The grey matter of thalamus is divided by a Y-shaped sheet of white

{. Anterior part = Between the fork of the lamina. 
ll. Medial part = Medial to the lamina.

I. Anterior nuclei: 
- Site: between the 2 limbs of the Y-shaped internal medullary lamina. 
- Afferents: receives the mammillo-thalamic tract from the mammillary bodies. 
- Efferents: sends the anterior thalamic radiation to the cingulate gyrus. 
- Function: forms part of the limbic system (Papez circuit) which is concerned with 

emotions and recent memory. 

Il. Medial nuclei (Dorso-medial nucleus): 
- Afferents: from the hypothalamus, amygdala & olfactory cortex 
- Efferents: via the medial forebrain bundle to the prefrontal cortex. 
- Function: forms part of the limbic system involved in thinking & mood.

lll. Lateral part of thalamus: Divided into: 
e Dorsal tier: 

- Includes 3 association nuclei: Lateral-dorsal nucleus, Lateral-posterior nucleus & 
Pulvinar. 

- Afferents: they receive input from the other thalamic nuclei and integrate them;

- Afferents: from the basal ganglia (globus pallidus).

Other thalamic nuclei: 
eo Medial & lateral geniculate bodies: (both are called metathalamus) 

1. The medial geniculate body (MGB) 
- Afferents: receives auditory input from the inferior colliculus of midbrain. 
- Efferents: projects auditory radiation to the auditory area of cortex. 

2. The lateral geniculate body (LGB) 
- Receives visual input from the optic tract. 
- Projects optic radiation to the visual area of cortex. 

¢ 3 Non-specific nuclei which occupy strategic positions in the thalamus: 
1. Intralaminar nuclei (within the internal medullary lamina). 
2. Midline nuclei (on the medial surface of thalamus beneath the ependyma of 3" 

ventricle): 
- Afferents: from the reticular formation of the brain stem. 
- Efferents: to the whole cortex non-specifically; increases its activity. 
- Functions: part of RAS responsible for alertness. 

3. Reticular nucleus: 
- Site: on the lateral surface of the thalamus. 
- Afferents: from whole cerebral cortex. 
- Efferents: do not leave the thalamus but end on the thalamic nuclei. 
- Functions: inhibits the thalamic nuclei during sleep.

FUNCTIONS OF RETICULAR FORMATION and ELECTRICAL ACTIVITY 
OF BRAIN 

The reticular formation (RF) somatosensory 
cingulate prop cortex 

- It is a widespread network of certex | > — 

interconnected neurons located in X@ WoT ’ N 
the brain stem. {OS |) thalamus 

- It extends upwards to the thalamus 1 | : 
and hypothalamus and its lower end 
is continuous with the interneurons 
of the spinal cord. RIL - 1 

TE locus 
coeruleus 

Types of cells in RF: 

e Sensory neurons fsticuigs 

¢ Motor neurons pone 

Fig. 6.11: Reticular formation 
a) Sensory neurons 

- These neurons make interconnections with each other and allow convergence, 
divergence and after discharge. These neurons receive afferent impulses from: 

v' Long ascending sensory tracts (spinothalamic tracts and dorsal column) 
v' Trigeminal, visual, auditory and olfactory nervous pathway 
v' Basal ganglia 
v' Vestibular apparatus 
v' Hypothalamus 
v' Cerebellum 
v' Cerebral cortex 

b) Motor neurons 
- The reticular nuclei are divided into 2 groups which function antagonistically to each 
other: 

[. Pontine reticular nuclei. 
Il. Medullary reticular nuclei. 
I. Pontine reticular nuclei (Reticular Excitatory Area) 

- This area is also known by the name bulboreticular facilitatory area. 
It forms most of the reticular formation. 
It is located in the pons and midbrain. 
It has an intrinsic activity. 
It has ascending and descending fibers: 
1) Ascending fibers discharge excitatory signals upward to all areas of cerebral 

cortex. These fibers compose the ascending reticular activating system 
(RAS). Most of these go first to the thalamus (midline and intralaminar 
nuclei) which, in turn, project diffusely and nonspecifically to almost all parts 
of the cerebral cortex. 
RAS is responsible for arousal (awaking) state of the individual. 

346 



CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 

sleep & epilepsy 

2} Descending fibers transmits facilitatory signals downward to the spinal 
motor neurons that control antigravity muscles through ventral 
reticulospinal tract increasing the antigravity muscles tone. 
Stimulation has a generalized activating effect on both flexor and extensor 
muscles, with its predominant effect on extensors. 
The activity of the reticular excitatory area is increased by impulses from 
area 4, neocerebellum, vestibular nucleus, basal ganglia ((caudate) 

- The activity of the reticular excitatory area is inhibited by impulses from 
reticular inhibitory area, inhibitory areas of cerebral cortex, paleocerebellum, 
basal ganglia and red nucleus. 

Il. Medullary reticular nuclei {Reticular Inhibitory Area) 
- It is located in the medulla oblongata. It has no intrinsic activity. 
- This area can inhibit the reticular facilitatory area and thereby decrease activity 

in the brain. 
- ft sends inhibitory impulses to the spinal motor neurons that control antigravity 
muscles through lateral reticulospinal tract decreasing antigravity muscles 
tone. Stimulation has a generalized inhibitory effect on both flexor and extensor 
muscles, with the predominant effect on extensors. 

- Its activity is stimulated by impulses from the inhibitory areas of cerebral cortex, 
paleocerebellum, basal ganglia and red nucleus. 

Ascending reticular activating system (ARAS or RAS) 
- This 1s a muttineuronal polysynaptic system of nerve fibers that originates at the excitatory 

reticular formation. 
- Its fibers extend upwards then some project directly to the cerebral cortex while the 
majority relay first at the nonspecific thalamic nuclei from which fibers arise and diffuse to 
almost all parts of the cerebral cortex. This pathway is called reticulo-thalamo-cortical 
pathway. 

Functions of RAS: 
1. Arousal and consciousness: 

The RAS discharges to the thalamus and cortex to potentiate thalamic and cortical 

activity. 

2. Attention: 

The RAS mediates transitions from relaxed wakefulness to periods of high attention 

and alertness. 

Causes of stimulation and inhibition of the RAS 
I. Factors stimulating RAS 

" Sensory signals that enter the brain from the periphery in particular pain signals. 
= Signals from the cerebral cortex (positive feedback) (as during emotions, brain 

thought processes or motor processes). 
= Analeptic and Sympathomimetic drugs 
m Stress 
s (Caffeine 
t= Thyroid hormone 

347 



CHAPTER Vi: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 
sleep & epilepsy 

Il. Factors inhibiting RAS 
= Lack of sensory input signals or cerebral cortex signals 
» Signals from sleep centers 
= General anesthesia 
= Damage as by tumors and vascular lesions. 

= Alcohol 
= Sleep inducing drugs 
®* Tranquilizers 

Lesion in RAS 
Causes impaired consciousness and severe damage can cause coma 

Functions of reticular formation: 
1. It is a vital link between higher centers in the brain and lower centers in the spinal cord, 

through it these centers are integrated with each other. 
2. Maintenance of posture and equilibrium and muscle tone. 

During standing there are continuous facilitatory impulses passing from the reticular 
formation and vestibular nucleus to motor neurons of the spinal cord to increase muscle 
tone in extensors thus help to support the body. 

3. Arousal and consciousness: 
- RAS controls the overall degree of the cerebral cortex alertness causing wakefulness 

(normal alert conscious state). RAS is responsible for arousal and attention. 
- Decreased activity of the RAS leads to decreased activity of cerebral cortex, leading 

to sleep or loss of consciousness. 
4. RF contains many of the areas concerned with regulation of heart rate, blood pressure, 

respiration and many digestive activities. 
5. Pain modulation: 

- lt is the site of perception of slow pain (It is one mean by which pain signals 
from lower body reach cerebral cortex). 

- Also, it is the origin of descending analgesic system. Projections from raphe 
nuclei down to dorsal horn of the spinal cord inhibit pain sensations (block 
transmission of pain signals to the brain). 

6. The mechanism that triggers REM sleep is located in the pontine reticular formation. 

ELECTRICAL ACTIVITY OF BRAIN 

There is continuous electrical activity recorded in the brain. 
Types of potentials recorded from brain: 
e Evoked potentials. 
e Spontaneous potential = electroencephalogram. 
1. Evoked cortical potentials 

e These are the electrical events that occur in the cerebral cortex after stimulation of 
receptor. 

e They are recorded by placing the exploring electrode over the excited cortical area. 
eo Evoked cortical potentials are 2 types: 

a- Primary evoked potential 
- It is in the form of + ve wave followed by a — ve wave. 
- It is recorded from specific area of cerebral cortex (localized) 

348 



CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 
sleep & epilepsy 

b- Secondary evoked potential (Diffuse secondary response) 
- It follows the primary evoked potential and consists of large prolonged + ve 
wave. 

- [tis not localized. 
- It is due to activation of cortical neurons by impulses arriving from the non- 

specific thalamic nuclei. 
Evoked cortical potentials are used for: 

- Mapping of brain areas. 
- Diagnosis of diseases. 

2. Spontaneous potential = electroencephalogram (EEG) or brain waves 
e [tis the record of spontaneous electrical activity of the brain in conscious subjects by 

applying electrodes to the scalp of the patient. 
eo The EEG recorded from the scalp is not the result of action potentials but it is a 

measure of the summation of postsynaptic potentials of neurons in cerebral cortex. 
eo The electrocorticogram is the record obtained with electrodes applied directly on 

surface of the cortex. 
e EEG recording should be performed in calm room with comfortable temperature and 

the person should be in complete physical and mental rest. 
Electroencephalogram waves 

a (Alpha) wave 
e [tis the wave of adult awake relaxed person with eye closed. 

Site of recording: occipital, parietal and frontal regions. 
Amplitude: 50 uv. 
Frequency: 8-13 cycles/sec. 
It is produced by activity of non-specific thalamic nuclei. 

B (Beta) wave 
e [tis the wave of awake person with eyes opened (adult during brain activity). 
e Site of recording: parietal and frontal regions. 
eo Amplitude: 25 pv 
e Frequency: 13-30 cycles / sec. 

0 (Theta) wave 
o |tis the wave of children and adults during light sleep and emotional stress. 
e Site of recording: temporal and partial region. 
eo Amplitude: 100 pv. 
e Frequency: 4-7 cycles/sec. 

o (Delta) wave 
e [tis wave of deep sleep, infants and in cases of brain damage and surgical anesthesia. 

Site: all cortical areas (they originate from cortical neurons). 
Amplitude: 100 pv. 
Frequency: less than 3.5 cycles/sec. 

vy (Gamma} wave 
e [tis wave of: Hyperaroused, higher mental activity and processing complex tasks 
e Site: all cortical areas

sleep & epilepsy 

eo Amplitude: 0.5-2 pv. 
e Frequency: 30-80 Hz 

Value of EEG 
It is non invasive clinical test for investigating the function of brain. 
o Localization of brain tumors. 
o Diagnosis of epilepsy. 
o Monitor of sleep stages and diagnosis of sleep disorders. 
o Indicates brain death (flat EEG). 

Alpha block 
e When a subject attention is focused on something, the regular alpha rhythm is 

replaced by the fast irregular low-voltage beta rhythm. 
e This phenomenon is called alpha block. Another term for this phenomenon is the 

arousal or alerting response, because it is correlated with the aroused, alert state. It . 
has also been called desynchronization. 
It is due to stimulation of ascending reticular activating system (ARAS). 

Sleep & Arousal 

Consciousness: is the awareness of both: 
1- Self. 
2- One’s surroundings, thoughts and feeling. 

Sleep: It is a state of unconsciousness from which person can be aroused by sensory or motor 
stimuli. 

Coma: it is a state of unconsciousness from which the person cannot be aroused 

Function of sleep 
1. Provides the brain with time to restore biochemical or physiological processes that have 

progressively decreased during wakefulness. 
a. Cognition 
b. Maintain metabolic-caloric balance. . 
¢. Thermal equilibrium. 
d. Immune competence. 

2. Facilitation of learning and memory. 
3. Neural maturation. 

Types of sleep 
Sleep is classified into two types that follow each other in a cyclic manner during sleep. 

1- Slow wave sleeps (non- rapid eye movement sleep= nonREM=NREM). 
2- Rapid eye movements sleep (REM). 

Slow Wave Sleep 
Timing: Usually sleep starts by this type, it occupies most of the sleeping duration (75%) of 
total sleeping hours in adults. It is deeper and longer early in night. 

Stages: 
As one goes to sleep, the alpha waves characteristics of drowsiness appear. This is followed 
by four stages characterized by progressively low frequency and high amplitude: 

350 



CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 

sleep & epilepsy 

v' Stage 1: Sleep is very light (theta waves) (5%—10% of total sleep cycle). 
v' Stage 2: Sleep is light (sleep spindles (bursts of large alpha waves 12-14 Hz) and K 

complexes (large and slow potential) are seen within theta waves). (40%—50% of total 

sleep cycle) 
v' Stage 3: Sleep is moderate (delta waves (3-3.5 Hz). 
v' Stage 4: Sleep is deepest (delta waves with least frequency (0.5-1.5 Hz). 
v' Stages 3 & 4 represent (10%—20% of total sleep cycle) 

Importance 
Physical rest (increases when person is tired) 

Characteristics 
1. No rapid eye movements. Eye deviates up and miosed. 

. Sleep talking and walking are present. 

. No dreams (dreams that occur are not remembered). 

. Decreased pulse, blood pressure respiration and basal metabolic rate (by about 10-30 %) 
due to increase parasympathetic activity. 

. Increased growth hormone. 

. Skeletal muscle tone is slightly inhibited. 

. Easy arousal: threshold of arousal is low. 

. Penile erection and teeth grinding are absent. 
Rapid Eye Movement Sleep (REM) 
Timing: It follows non REM sleep. In a normal night of sleep, bouts of REM sleep lasting 5 to 
30 minutes usually appear on the average every 90 minutes. It is repeated 4-6 times/ night. 
It occupies 25% of sleep hours in adults. REM sleep occupies 80% of total sleep time in 
premature infants and 50% in fullterm neonates. It decreases by age (20% of sleep hours), 
fatigue and increases by rest. 

Importance 
Mental rest. 

Characteristic 
1. Rapid eye movement. 
2. Dreams that are remembered. 
3. Marked decrease in muscle tone indicating strong inhibition of the spinal muscle control 

areas. 
4, Large body movements do not occur; however, movements of smaller muscles can occur 

(fingers, toes, legs, wrists). 
5. Irregular or increased pulse, blood pressure and respiration (increased sympathetic 

activity). 
. Penile erection and teeth grinding are present. 
. Decreased growth hormone. 
. Sleep talking and walking are absent. 
. Person is difficult to be aroused: threshold of arousal is high, yet people spontaneously 
awake in the morning during an episode of REM sleep.

- Children have more total sleep time (8=10 h) compared to most adults (about 6 h). 
Physiology of consciousness (wakefulness) 

- Certain areas of the brainstem; midbrain reticular formation (the RAS) and hypothalamus 
promote wakefulness by sending excitation signals to the cerebral cortex. Thus, the 
cortex is activated, and we stay awake. Consciousness is maintained by +ve feedback 
mechanism through re-excitation of RAS by signals discharged from the activated cortex 
via corticofugal fibers. 

- After wakefulness for many hours occurs, sleep tends to occur due to: 
a. Fatigue of neurons in RAS so the +ve feedback cycle between RAS and cerebral cortex 

fades. 
b. Spontaneous activation of sleep centers which inhibit arousal promoting neurons. 

- Then during the sleeping hours arousal promoting neurons regain their excitability and 
the sleep centers become less excitable. 

Mechanism of sleep 
1. Passive theory of sleep 

o The reticular activating system is simply fatigued during the waking day and became 
inactive as a result. 

o However, this view was changed recently. 
2. Active theory of sleep 

o Sleep is caused by stimulation of several specific areas of the brain (sleep centers). 
I. Centers that lead to non- REM sleep: 

- Anterior hypothalamus and adjacent basal forebrain area 
Il. Centers that lead to REM sleep: 

- Reticular formation in upper part of brain stem contains acetylcholine 
secreting neurons = REM sleep by discharging cholinergic signals directly to 
cerebral cortex. 

352 

—-  ee — reoy  — 



CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 

sleep & epilepsy 

* The suprachiasmatic nucleus of hypothalamus synchronizes the sleep / wakefulness 
rhythm with dark light cycle. 

Neurochemical Mechanisms Promoting Sleep and Arousal 
v" Transitions between sleep and wakefulness manifest a circadian rhythm consisting of an 

average of 6-8 h of sleep and 16-18 h of wakefulness. 
v' Nuclei in both the brain stem and hypothalamus are critical for the transitions between 

these states of consciousness. 
v' The RAS and posterior hypothalamus produce the alerting response and arousal. 
v' The anterior hypothalamus and adjacent basal forebrain region induce sleep. 
v' The reticular formation is composed of several groups of neurons: locus coeruleus 

neurons release norepinephrine, raphe nuclei neurons release serotonin and pontine 
reticular formation neurons release acetylcholine. 

vIn the case of the forebrain neurons involved in control of the sleep-wake cycles, 
preoptic neurons in the hypothalamus release GABA and posterior hypothalamic 
neurons (tuberomamilary neurons) release histamine. 

v' Also, orexin is produced in lateral hypothalamic neurons and appear to be important in 
switching between sleep and wakefulness. 

v' One theory regarding the basis for transitions from sleep to wakefulness involves 
alternating reciprocal activity of different groups of RAS neurons. 

v It assumes that wakefulness and REM sleep are at opposite extremes. 
v" When the activity of norepinephrine- and serotonin-containing neurons {locus coeruleus 

and raphe nuclei) is dominant, there is a reduced level of activity in acetylcholine- 
containing neurons in the pontine reticular formation. This pattern of activity contributes 
to the appearance of the awake state. 

v’ The reverse of this pattern leads to REM sleep. 
v' When there is a more even balance in the activity of the aminergic and cholinergic 

neurons, NREM sleep occurs. 
v' The orexin released from hypothalamic neurons may regulate the changes in activity in 

these brainstem neurons. 
vIn addition, an increased release of GABA and reduced release of histamine increase the 

likelihood of NREM sleep via deactivation of the thalamus and cortex. 
v' Wakefulness occurs when GABA release is reduced and histamine release is increased 

353 



CHAPTER VI: DIENCEPHALON, RETICULAR FORMATION, 

sleep & epilepsy 

Fig. 6.13: Neurochemical mechanisms promoting sleep and arousal 

3. Metabolic theory of sleep: 
o Itis possible that prolonged wakefulness causes progressive accumulation of a sleep 

factor or factors in the brain stem or in the cerebrospinal fluid that lead to sleep. 
o A lipid produced by brain is said to have sleep-inducing properties. Several 

investigators have argued that a peptide produced in the brain is responsible for 
sleep. Muramyl peptide, a low molecular weight substance was found to accumulate 
in the cerebrospinal fluid and urine of animals kept awake for several days.

GABA, Receptor Cl- ion Channel Complex 

eo The GABAx receptor is a pentamer formed of 3 different types of subunits

e The GABA binding site is at the interface between a and 8 subunits.

e a; subunit is associated with sedation/hypnosis. 
e a; subunit has greater anxiolytic and muscle relaxation actions. 

Mechanism of action of drugs acting at GABA-A receptor 
¢ Binding of GABA to its binding site between a and B subunits of GABA- A receptor opens 

a CI channel with influx of CI" resulting in hyperpolarization —inhibit neuronal firing. 
¢ Binding of drugs, Barbiturates BZDs or Zolpidem, to their sites on the GABA- A receptor 

complex =» T affinity of GABA to bind to its receptors. This increases opening of CI 
channel facilitating the inhibitory effects of GABA. 

Barbiturates 

e More powerful CNS depressants than BZDs because barbiturates have direct GABA- 
mimetic effects and inhibitory effects on glutamate receptors (AMPA-R) in high 
concentrations?. Formerly used as sedative-hypnotics but largely replaced by BZDs due to: 
1. Narrow therapeutic index (BZDs are safer) 

Barbiturates induce a dose-dependent CNS depression: sedation (at low doses), 
hypnosis (at higher doses) & anesthesia, coma, cardiovascular & respiratory 
depression = death (toxic doses). 

2. sNpo ecific antidote is available for barbiturates whereas, flumazenil is a specific BZD 
antagonist. 

3. Drug dependence is greater with barbiturates than with BZDs. 
4. Enzyme induction occurs with barbiturates but not with BZDs =» drug interactions. 
5. Acute porphyria® may be precipitated by barbiturates in susceptible patients. 

Members of Barbiturates 
Long-acting: phenobarbital (antiepileptic). 
Short-acting: pentobarbital, secobarbital (sedative-hypnotic). Replaced by BZD.

GENERAL ANESTHETCS 

General anesthetics are drugs used in surgery to induce reversible loss of consciousness, 
analgesia, amnesia, skeletal muscle relaxation & inhibition of visceral reflexes. 

Balanced Anesthesia 
Balanced anesthesia is used to achieve all requirements for general anesthesia & to 
minimize adverse effects of individual drugs. It includes: 

1. Preanesthetic medication: preoperative administration of sedatives, opioid analgesics, 
anti-muscarinics, anti-emetics and H1& H2 antagonists aiming to: 
a. Induce amnesia, 4 anxiety & facilitating induction of anesthesia. 
b. Suppress autonomic reflexes, ¢ salivary & bronchial secretion, {gastric acidity. 
c. Potentiate weak anesthetics. 

2. IV anesthetics (for rapid induction) plus succinylcholine (for intubation). 
3. Inhalation anesthetics (for maintenance) & NMBs (if needed) in operation. 

Classification of General Anesthetics 

: 3 
I. Inhalation Anesthetics il. IV Anesthetics 
{Mainly for Maintenance) (Mainly for Induction)

¢ Opioids- Benzodiazepines. 

Mechanism of Action of General Anesthetic 

General anesthetics inhibit neuronal activity in many brain regions, specially the midbrain, 
reticular activating system & thalamus.

B. Nitrous Oxide (N.O) 
Rapid onset & recovery. 
Low potency: used for maintenance anesthesia, not alone, but in combination with 
other inhalation agents to \ their side effects & 1* their analgesic effects. 
Effective analgesic: can be used alone in subanesthetic doses in brief procedures; e.g. 
dental outpatient clinic - obstetric analgesia. 
Safest inhalation agent: less cardiac or respiratory depression - less uterine relaxation - 
no hepatitis - no malignant hyperthermia. 

Adverse Effects of N.O*° 
1. Expands closed gas spaces = pressure in middle ear — Cl: in bowel obstruction & 

pneumothorax. 
2. Hypoxia on prolonged use (mixed with O; = 50 - 70 % nitrous oxide). 
3. Abuse: euphoria (laughing gas). 
4. Megaloblastic anemia & abortion (chronic exposure of operating room staff). 

Il. INTRAVENOUS ANESTHETICS 

A. Ultra-Short-Acting Barbiturates: 
Thiopental 
Kinetics 
- Rapid onset due to rapid crossing of BBB (highly lipid soluble). 
- Short acting; redistributes from brain to fat & skeletal muscle. 
- Slowly metabolized & liable to accumulate in body fat, thus may cause prolonged 

effect if given repeatedly =» toxicity. 
Clinical Uses 
1. Most widely used IV anesthetic for induction followed by maintenance with 

inhalation agents for major operations (the principal use). 
2. Given alone in anesthesia for short procedures. 
Adverse Effects (narrow safety margin) 
1. Respiratory & cardiovascular depression (toxic doses). 
2. Severe vasospasm if accidentally injected intra-arterially = gangrene. 
3. Can precipitate porphyria. 

B. Nonbarbiturate IV Anesthetics 
1. Propofol

e Used in conscious?! & deep?? sedation (in intensive care). 

Advantage: antiemetic (¥ risk of postoperative vomiting). 
e Disadvantage: respiratory & CVS depression — pain at injection site.

LOCAL ANESTHETICS 

Definition 
e A local anesthetic (LA) is a drug that induces a reversible loss of sensation in a localized 

area of the body without inducing loss of consciousness or sleep.

Mechanism of Action of LA 

e LA bind to receptors on inner surface of Na channel in nerve membranes resulting in their 
blockade =» inhibition of generation & propagation of action potential. 

Factors Affecting Sensitivity of Nerves to LA 
1. Rate of firing: =» more effective in rapid-firing nerves (sensory). 
2. Nerve size: small nerve fibers conducting pain, temperature & autonomic activity are 

more sensitive to LA than large fibers conducting pressure & motor impulses (with higher 
concentrations motor nerves become affected). 

pH and Local Anesthetic Activity 
e LAs are weak bases (pKa between 7.7 and 9). In order to increase their solubility and 

stability they are prepared as acid salts (e.g. lidocaine HCI). 
e The local anesthetic exists in 2 forms in equilibrium according to its pKa & to the 

extracellular pH: 
1. Ionized form 
2. Unionized form. 

e¢ Only the unionized form can cross the nerve membrane to block Na* channel’. 

26 Other mechanism of action: LAs dissolve in neuronal membrane and induce swelling of membrane 
&physical inactivation of Na channel. 

*" blockade of Na" channels by LA is use-dependent, i.e. they block Na* channels that are in activated 
(open) & deactivated states more than those in resting state =» more effective in rapid-firing nerves 
(sensory).

Factors affecting activity of local anesthetics 
1. Plasma protein binding: affects duration of action 

Bupivacaine is 95% bound to plasma proteins thus longer acting than lidocaine (65%). 
2. Lipid solubility: affects potency 

The more lipid soluble the drug is the greater its potency. Bupivacaine is 7 times more 
lipid soluble than lidocaine therefore more potent. 

3. PKa of drug: affects speed of onset. 
The lower the PKa the greater the speed of onset since more drug will be non-ionized 
and can cross nerve membrane. Lidocaine (PKa 7.9; 25% non-ionized) acts more rapidly 
than bupivacaine ( PKa 8.1; 15% non-ionized). 

4. Extracellular pH®® 
Infections or repeated injections of lidocaine Hel = 1 extracellular acidity = 71 
extracellular ionized form of LA =>{ LA crossing =>4 activity. 
Adding NaHCO; to LA solution = { extracellular acidity = 1 extracellular unionized 
form=> 1 LA crossing => 1 activity (rapid onset). 

5. Addition of vasoconstrictors: prolongs duration of action. 
e LAs (except cocaine) induce VD => 1 absorption => termination of effect & 1 risk of 
systemic toxicity. 

e Vasoconstrictors are added to LA (except cocaine) to ¢ absorption = % duration & 
J systemic toxicity. 

Precautions 

e Vasoconstrictors are Cli n anesthesia of fingers, toes & nose =» gangrene. 
e Felypressin is preferred in cardiac patients (no effect on HR & BP). 

28 Intracellular pH 
Carbonated solutions of LA have a rapid onset since they are saturated with CO, which readily enters 
nerve cells =>1 intracellular acidity =% intracellular ionized (active) form available to block Na” 
channel. 

PLocal anesthesia is also not preferred in infections to avoid spread of infection.

DRUG THERAPY OF EPILEPSY AND MIGRAINE 

Definition of Epilepsy*3: 
Chronic disease characterized by repeated attacks (2 or more seizures with at least 24 
hours apart) of abnormal electrical discharge of cerebral neurons resulting in EEG, motor, 
sensory, autonomic or psychological changes. 

Etiology 
A. Primary epilepsy (unknown cause, i.e. idiopathic). 
B. Secondary epilepsy caused by: 

1. Trauma, meningitis, brain tumors, fevers, hypoglycemia. 
2. Drugs: insulin (hypoglycemia) - TCAs — cocaine- antipsychotics — Li*. 

Classification of Epilepsy 
Epilepsy is caused by a group of hyper excitable neurons with excessive electrical discharge 
which might be localized (partial) or spread (generalized). 
The major types of epilepsy: 

|. Generalized epilepsy: Tonic-Clonic, Absence, Myoclonic ...... 
Il. Partial epilepsy: Simple partial, Complex partial. 

Antiepileptic Drugs 
Members 

I. Classic agents (major or older agents) 
Phenytoin (prototype) — carbamazepine — valproate (valproic acid) - ethosuximide. 
Phenobarbital - benzodiazepines. 

Il. Newer agents®® 
e Lamotrigine — levetiracetam — oxcarbazepine. 
eo Topiramate — gabapentin- pregabalin. 

Mechanism of Action 
Anti-epileptics block initiation or spread of seizures by | hyper-excitability of cerebral 
neurons by acting on neurotransmitters or by blocking ion channels. 
Most antiepileptic drugs act by more than one mechanism: 

1. Blockade of Na* Channels **2{ repetitive firing of neurons 
e Phenytoin — carbamazepine — valproate. 
e Topiramate - lamotrigine. 

2. Blockade of T- type Ca** Channels in thalamus => 4 brain rhythmic activity {effective 
in absence seizures) 

e Ethosuximide. 
eo Valproate. 

3. Blockade of presynaptic voltage gated Ca™ Channels — J glutamate release

tract. It improves GIT tolerance of Valproic acid. 
Newer agents: lacosamide - zonisamide- rufinamide- felbamate, vigabatrin- tiagabine, preampenel. 
*¢ Other Na channel blockers: lacosamide - zonisamide- rufinamide.

e Lamotrigine.

Specific adverse effects of newer antiepileptics*® 
They are better tolerated with fewer drug interactions than the older agents. 
e Topiramate: renal stones- myopia (glaucoma )- weight loss- hypohydrosis®, 
e Levetiracetam: mood & behavioral changes. 
¢ Gabapentin /Pregabalin: sedation, ataxia, weight gain- peripheral edema. 
e Lamotrigine: rash > fatal dermatitis {stevens johnson syndrome), hypersensitivity. 
Lamotrigine Drug interaction: 
1. Vaproate inhibits its metabolism (need to J its dose to 50%) 
2. Oral contraceptive pills and carbamazepine increase its metabolism (need to 4 its 

dose to control seizures) 

General guidelines for antiepileptic drug therapy 
1. Proper diagnosis of type of epilepsy & prescribing the appropriate drug (wrong choice 
may worsen the condition). 

2. Monotherapy is preferred since anti-epileptics are either enzyme inducers (phenytoin, 
carbamazepine & phenobarbital) or enzyme inhibitors (valproate). Combinations could 
result in changes in serum level of drugs. 

3. Start therapy with a small dose & a single drug and gradually 1 dose. If no effect is noted, 
gradually substitute or add another drug. 

4. Long duration of treatment: if epileptic fits are absent for 2 years, consider terminating 
therapy; but if fits recur, repeat treatment for another 2 years. 

5. Gradual drug withdrawal: over 6 months to avoid status epilepticus or relapse. 
6. Folic acid supplements: in childbearing period to |, teratogenic risk. 
7. Pregnancy & childbearing period: Be aware of potential effect of valproate in pregnancy. 

Pregnant females should receive the lowest effective dose of the drug (do not stop drug 
as seizures may be harmful to fetus). Pregnancy T metabolism of anti-epileptics =» adjust 
dose. Enzyme inducer anti-epileptics may reduce efficacy of contraceptives. 

8. Periodic monitoring of serum level of drugs, since a drop below therapeutic level loss 
of seizure control while an increase in level =>toxicity (CNS depression =» stupor - coma - 
respiratory depression). 

9. Sedation & tolerance limit the use of phenobarbital & clonazepam. 

% Zonisamide: rash -renal stones- weight loss- hypohydrosis; -Oxcarbazepine: hyponatremia, rash 
Levetiracetam, Gabapentin & Pregabalin are renally excreted & need dose adjustment in renal patient. 
Vigabatrin —nystagmus, blurred vision, vision impairment up to Vision loss and induces CYP2C9 
Phenytoin and Valproate are highly bound to plasma proteins (Aspirin, NSAIDs, Omeprazole may displace 
them from plasma protein —1ffree drug level — may {risk of their toxicity). 
Topiramate and Felbamate: induce CYP3A4 and inhibit CYP2CI19 
 hypohidrosis (sweat & may— hyperthermia; but rare & reversible) 
- Levetiracetam, Carbamazepine, Lamotrigine& gabapentin are prefered in elderly 
. Pharmacokinetic changes in the elderly that may affect and need dose adjustment: 

1. | clearance e.g. Carbamazepine 
ii. Lprotein binding if hypoalbuminemic e.g. Phenytoin, Valproic acid. 

The use of phenytoin is limited due to its troublesome kinetics resulting in unpredictable serum level due to: 
a. Irregular bioavailability (-»use one formula from a single manufacturer). 
b. Saturation kinetics (serum conc. may? to toxic levels = monitor serum level). 
c. Drug interactions if combined with other antiepileptics.

Migraine and Antimigraine Drugs] 

e An attack of migraine starts by a prodroma (irritability, fatigue, muscle aches), followed 
by an aura {visual disturbances; scotoma) and in 30 minutes by throbbing headache; 
starting unilaterally with nausea, vomiting & photophobia. 

e Migraine is triggered by stress, hormonal changes (menstruation), disturbed sleep 
pattern, weather changes & alcohol. 

Pathophysiology of migraine (not fully understood) 
e Migraine may be due to release of pro-inflammatory vasodilatory neuropeptides (e.g., 

CGRP) from nerve endings of trigeminal nerve into the perivascular space = dilation of 
dural vessels = stretch of pain nerve endings. 

Drugs used to terminate acute migraine attack 

I. Mild analgesics: NSAIDs & acetaminophen (for mild infrequent attacks). 
Il. Triptans: "specific" antimigraine therapy: therapy for outpatients with moderate to 

severe migraine is begun with a triptan. 
Mechanism of action (selective SHT1s/10 agonists) 
1. Activate SHT1g/10 receptors on presynaptic trigeminal nerve endings to inhibit release 

of vasodilatory neuropeptides. 
2. Vasoconstriction of dural vessels —» prevent stretching of pain nerve endings. 

7" Prolonged or repeated tonic-clonic seizures: Active part of a tonic-clonic seizure > 5 minutes or Repeated 
seizures within < 30 minutes and A second seizure without recovering consciousness from the first one. 

8 Preferred to diazepam as its antiepileptic effect is longer & it is non irritant to veins.

Preparations of triptans 
Sumatriptan: prototype *° (oral, SC, nasal spray) 
Adverse effects: 

1. Injection site reaction (with SC) or unpleasant taste (with intranasal). 
2. Chest pressure (resolves spontaneously in 30 minutes). 
3. Paresthesias, warmth, drowsiness, dizziness, weakness, malaise. 

Contraindications & Precautions

diabetics, hypertensives, men over 40 & in postmenopausals. 
2. Pregnancy. 
3. With serotoninergic drugs: SSRIs or within 24 hrs of ergots = SHT syndrome. 

lll.Ergots: SHT 1s/1p agonists similar to triptans

frequent headaches. 
2. Dihydroergotamine (IV, IM, SC & intranasal).

Chest pressure.

e Cl. Pregnancy.

V. Benzodiazepines & opioids in resistant cases (may lead to habituation & rebound 
headache). 

Drugs for migraine prophylaxis >° 
e Anti-migraine therapy should not exceed 10 days /month to avoid medication 

overuse headache “MOH”. Prophylactic drugs should then be administered: 
1. Beta blockers: propranolol and timolol: are of choice 

2. Calcium channel blockers: Flunarizine, Verapamil. 

3. Antidepressants: amitriptyline (TCA) 
4. Antiepileptics: valproate & topiramate. ({, Excess firing of trigeminal nerve).

mirtazapine, ACE inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers & botulinum toxin.

CEREBRAL DOMINANCE AND CORTICAL ASSOCIATION AREA 

Cerebral Dominance [Complementary Specialization of Hemispheres) 

* There is functional asymmetry between the 2 halves of the brain. 

« Language functions depend on one cerebral hemisphere than on the 
other. This hemisphere has been called the dominant hemisphere. 
However, the other hemisphere is not less developed or non dominant. 
Each hemisphere is specialized for certain function. Consequently, the 
concept of cerebral dominance and a dominant and nondominant 
hemisphere has been replaced by the concept of complementary 

¢ specialization of the hemispheres or cerebral lateralization. 

« The hemisphere responsible for language and logical, analytic, 
sequential, and verbal tasks is called categorical hemisphere. The other 
hemisphere which is concerned with the visuospatial relations as 
recognition of faces, perception of body orientation in space, identification 
of objects by their forms, recognition of musical themes and artistic and 
musical talents, and emotions is called the representational hemisphere. 

The left hemisphere tends to process information in a fine-detail, 

fragmentary way, while the right hemisphere views the world in a big- 
picture, holistic way. 

Left cerebral hemisphere dominance tends to be associated with 

thinkers, whereas right hemispheric skills dominate in creators. 

« lesions in categorical hemisphere produce language disorders. Lesions 
in representational hemisphere produce agnosia (inability to recognize 
objects by a particular sensory modality) and contralateral neglect, they 
may impair the ability to tell a story or make a joke. They may also impair a 
subject's ability to get the point of a joke and, more broadly, to comprehend 
the meaning of differences in inflection and the “color” of speech. 

Hemispheric specialization is related to handedness. In 96% of right- 
handed individuals and 70 % of left-handed individuals, the feft hemisphere 
is the dominant or categorical hemisphere. 

Cortical association areas (figure 7.1) 

* They occupy most of human cerebral cortex. 

* They are involved in processing and integration of information received 
from many areas of the brain {motor and sensory areas as well as from 
subcortical structures).

e The most important association areas are: 
1.The parieto-occipito-temporal association area 

2.The prefrontal association area 

3.The limbic (temporal) association area 

1. The parieto-occipito-temporal association area 
This association area lies in the large parietal and occipital lobes bounded by the 
somatosensory cortex anteriorly, the visual cortex posteriorly, and the auditory 
cortex laterally. 
Functions 
3 Analysis of the spatial coordinates of the body and its surrounding. 

Its lesion = Amorphosynthesis = failure of recognition of contralateral side of body 
leading to unilateral inattention and neglect = the patient ignores stimuli from 
opposite side of his body and the space around it and forgets its existence, dress 
half their bodies, shave half faces and read half page. 
J Area for language comprehension. The major area for language 
comprehension called Wernicke's area lies behind the primary auditory cortex 
in the posterior part of the superior gyrus of the temporal lobe. 
3. Area for interpretation of visual language (Reading): angular gyrus area 
lies posterior to Wernicke’s area is needed to make meaning out of the visually 
perceived words. In its absence, a person can still have excellent language 
comprehension through hearing but not through reading. 

4, Area for naming objects located between the anterior part of occipital 
lobe and posterior part of temporal lobe. 

2. The prefrontal association area 
It lies in frontal lobe anterior to premotor area. 
It is in charge of executive functions (=skills that one use to manage everyday tasks 
as making plans, thinking, solving problems and adapting to new situations). The 3 
main skills are working memory, cognitive flexibility and inhibition control.

SPEECH, LANGUAGE AND APHASIA 
Speech 

- {tis the means of communication between persons 
- [tis the ability to express words (spoken or written) in response to visual 
and auditory stimuli. 
- It is the highest cortical function. It is the function of the categorical 
hemisphere. 
- Speech has 2 aspects: 

. Sensory (language input), involving the ears and eyes 

. Motor {language output), involving vocalization and writing 
Aspects of Communication

(|. Motor centers

Sensory Aspects of Communication 
For comprehension 

l. Visual association area 
Site: The occipital lobe. 
Function: Understanding of the meaning of written words. 

Il. Auditory association area 
Site: The temporal lobe. 

Function: Understanding of the meaning of spoken words. 
Ill. Wernicke’s area 

Sensory center of speech 
Site: Posterior part of the superior temporal gyrus in the categorical 
hemisphere. 
Function: 

1- Comprehension of the thoughts of visual and 
auditory information. 

2- Determination of thoughts to be expressed. 3- 
Choice of proper words to be used. 

Motor Aspects of Communication 
For expression 

I. Broca’s area = Word formation area 
Motor center of speech 
Site: Frontal lobe in the lower part of the premotor area (area 6). 
Function: 

- Formation of words (speaking ability) 
- Stores programs of the spoken words = sequence of coordinated 

contractions of lips, tongue and laryngeal muscles. 
It sends this pattern via a speech articulation area in the insula to the motor 
cortex (face and laryngeal regions) — corticobulbar tract — which initiates the 
appropriate movements of the lips, tongue, and larynx to produce spoken 
words. 

b. Exner's area = Hand skills area 
Site: It is present in frontal lobe in the upper part of premotor area (area 
6). Functions: 

- Stores program of the written words = sequence of coordinated 
contraction of the hand muscles. 

It sends this pattern to hand region in the motor cortex — corticospinal 

tract — written words. 
Mechanism of speech 

1. Spoken speech 
* Perception of spoken words occurs in primary auditory area which discharges 

to auditory association area where interpretation of individual words occurs. 

378 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

e In Wernicke’s area, comprehension of auditory information and 
determination of the thoughts and the words to be spoken occur. 

Transmission of signals from Wernicke’s area to Broca’s area occurs by way 

of the arcuate fasciculus. 

 |n Broca’s area: skilled motor programs for control of word formation are 
activated. 

* Transmission of signals to the motor cortex to stimulate the speech muscles 
resulting in act of articulation. 

2. Written speech (reading) 
» Perception of written words occurs in primary visual area which discharges 

to visual association area where interpretation of individual words occurs. 
* Then, the information passes to the angular gyrus which is located behind 

Wernicke’s area and appears to process information from words that are read 
in such a way that they can be converted into the auditory forms of the words 
in Wernicke’s area. 

* Finally reaches its full level of comprehension in Wernicke’s area. 
* From here, the sequence is the same as for spoken speech. 

Broca’'s ares 

Figure (7.2): Mechanisms of speech a) for spoken words b) for written words 

Speech disorders 
- Aphasia 
- Dysarthria 

Abnormality of speech caused by lesions affecting speech centers and not 
due to defects of vision or hearing or motor paralysis. 

379 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

They are caused by lesions in categorical hemisphere. 
The most common is embolism or thrombosis of a cerebral blood vessel. 
1. Sensory aphasia = receptive aphasia = fluent aphasia 
2. Motor aphasia = expressive aphasia = non fluent aphasia 
3. Global (general) aphasia 

a. Sensory aphasia 

i. Visual aphasia= word blindness 
Lesion: Visual association area. 
Patient can see the written words but cannot understand their meaning. 

ii.Auditory aphasia = word deafness 
Lesion: Auditory association area. 
Patients can hear the spoken words but cannot understand their meanings. 

iii. Wernicke’s aphasia 
Lesion: Wernicke’s area. 
Patient fails to comprehend the idea and thoughts conveyed by the spoken or 
written words {patient is unable to grasp the meaning of the words). Patient 
talks excessively but what they say make no sense. So, he cannot form thoughts 
or choose appropriate words to convey their thoughts. 

Conductive aphasia: 
A type of fluent aphasia in which patient can speak well and has good 

auditory comprehension but cannot put parts of words together or conjure up 
words and words cannot be repeated correctly. 
It was believed to be due to lesion in the arcuate fasciculus but now it appears 

to be due to lesions near the auditory cortex. 
Anomic aphasia: 

It is due to damage in the angular gyrus in the categorical hemisphere without 
affecting Wernicke’s or Broca’s areas. 
Patient finds difficulty in naming seen objects only. No difficulty in speech or 
understanding auditory information but patients is unable to understand 
written words and pictures because visual information is not processed and 
transmitted to Wernicke’s area. 

b. Motor aphasia 
i. Broca’s aphasia Lesion: Broca’s area. 
Patient understands the meaning of the spoken and written words but he 

cannot express himself by spoken words although he knows what he wants to 
say! (speech is slow, hard to come and limited to two or three words only). 

ii. Agraphia 
Lesion: Exner's area hand skills area. 

Patient understands the meaning of the spoken and written words but he 
cannot express himself by written words, although he knows what to write! 

380 

oo 



“TE — 

CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

c. Global (general) aphasia 
It occurs in extensive brain lesions involving both receptive and expressive 

functions. Speech is scant and nonfluent. 
2. Dysarthria 

It is abnormality of speech due to defect in act of articulation. 
Types and Causes: 

a. Spastic dysarthria due to UMNL giving slow labored stiff speech 
b. Flaccid dysarthria due to LMNL giving weak breathy nasal 
speech. 
ec. Ataxic dysarthria due to Neocerebellar lesion (lack of 
coordination) giving staccato speech. 
d. Hypokinetic dysarthria due to Parkinsonism giving slow 
monotonous low volume speech. 
e. Hyperkinetic dysarthria due to damage of basal ganglia causing 
quick involuntary movement (bursting speech) 

f Mixed dysarthria

A system concerned with emotions (e.g fear and anger), instinct(e.g., feeding, drinking,

includes a group of curved structures seen on the medial aspect of the cerebral hemisphere 
around the thalamus; these include (Fig. 7.3):

Limbic Lobe 
C-shaped rim of cortex on the medial surface of cerebral hemisphere surrounding the 
diencephalon. It consists of: 

1. Subcallosal area (Paraolfactory Gyrus) 
2. Cingulate Gyrus. 
3. Isthmus. 
4. Parahippocampal Gyrus. 
5. Uncus. 

Papez circuit: Papez (1937) described a closed circuit involved in the expression of emotions 
and the consolidation of recent memory. it is formed as follows: 

1. The cingulate gyrus receives input from 3 association areas (sensory, visual & auditory). 
It is connected with the parahippocampal gyrus & uncus via the cingulum. 

2. The parahippocampal gyrus (Entorhinal cortex — Area 28) is connected with the 
hippocampus. 

. The hippocampus is connected to the mammillary bodies of hypothalamus via the fornix. 
4. The mammillary body is connected to the anterior nucleus of thalamus via the 

mammillo-thalamic tract. 
5. The anterior nucleus of thalamus is connected to the anterior part of the cingulate gyrus 

via the anterior thalamic radiation thus closing the circuit. 
Papez proposed that emotional expression is experienced in the cingulate gyrus, 
organized in the hippocampus and expressed via the mammillary bodies then through 
descending autonomic outflow, peripheral expression of emotion occurs.

The hippocampus is an elongated elevation of gray matter bulging in the floor of the inferior 
horn of the lateral ventricle. Its anterior end is expanded to form the pes hippocampus. It is 
named hippocampus because it resembles a sea horse in coronal section. The convex 
ventricular surface is covered with ependyma, beneath which lies a thin layer of white matter 
called the alveus which consists of efferent fibers arising from the hippocampus. The alveus 
converge to form a bundle called the fimbria which becomes continuous with the crus of the 
fornix. 

The fornix: (Fig. 7.4) is the white matter that connects the hippocampus to hypothalamus 
(mammillary bodies). It is formed of two posterior columns (crura) that begin from the fimbria 
of hippocampus. They arch around the thalamus,

PHYSIOLOGY OF LIMBIC SYSTEM 

Papez circuit: 

One of most important excitatory neuronal circuit involved in emotions, 
characterized by prolonged after-discharge due to presence of reverbrating 
circuits. 

This may explain the prolonged nature of emotions. 
It involves the following: 
Hippocamus = fornix = hypothalamus (mammillary bodies) = thalamus 
(anterior thalamic nuclei) = cingulate gyrus = hippocampus. 

Function of limbic system 

1-Olfaction 

Limbic system (Piriform cortex) is responsible for perception and discrimination of olfactory 
sensation. The piriform cortex, the amygdala and entorhinal cortex are regions of the 
olfactory cortex Conscious discrimination of odor depends on the neocortex {orbitofrontal 
and frontal cortices). Amygdala is involved with the emotional responses to olfactory stimuli 
Entorhinal cortex is concerned with olfactory memories. 
This function may explain close relation between smell, memories, emotions and sex. 

2- Control of feeding 

Limbic system is involved in the neuronal regulation of appetite. 
Lesions of amygdaloid nuclei © moderate hyperphagia (i.e. increase eating) and 
amniophagia (i.e. indiscriminative ingestion of all kinds of food). 
3- Control of autonomic response 
Limbic stimulation produces autonomic effects which are parts of the response to 
emotions. 

4- Maternal and sexual behavior 
Limbic system is important for normal maternal behavior and is concerned with 
emotional expression during the sexual act. 
Sexual behavior and function are controlled by limbic system and hypothalamus and 
modified by prefrontal cortex as well as social and psychic factors. 
5-Control of emotions 
Both limbic system and hypothalamus are concerned with genesis of emotions 
(feelings and moods) and their expression that include somatic motor, autonomic 
and endocrine responses. Emotions: feelings associated with autonomic and 
endocrine response as fear, rage and placidity. Stimulation of certain areas of the 
amygdala as well as stimulation of the lateral nucleus of hypothalamus produces 
rage and aggression. 
Stimulation of certain areas of the amygdala as well as stimulation of the 
periventricular nucleus of hypothalamus produces fear 
Stimulation of the ventromedial nucleus of hypothalamus produces placidity 

384 

ee ———— ——— —— Taa R r  ———— EE



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 
functions 

6-Relation to memory 
Hippocampus is the site of encoding of short term memory and its consolidation. 
Bilateral lesion of hippacampus leads to anterograde amnesia. 
7-Motivation 
it is the force that activates or inhibits certain behavior to achieve certain goal. 
Limbic system has two centers that control motivation: 

Reward (approach) system: 
Main center is present in the lateral and ventromedial nuclei of the 
hypothalamus. 
Less potent reward centers, which are perhaps secondary to the major ones in 
the hypothalamus, are found in the septum, the amygdala, certain areas of the 
thalamus and basal ganglia. Its stimulation leads to pleasure, satisfaction so 

y person continues to do the act. The main neurotransmitter is dopamine. Many 
substances that produce addiction increase the dopaminergic activity of that 
system. 
Other neurotransmitters include norepinephrine and serotonin. 
Punishment (avoidance= aversion) system: 

Main center is present in the periventricular nucleus of the hypothalamus and its 
extension downwards in periaquductal gray area. Less potent centers are in the 
amygdala and hippocampus. 

Its stimulation leads to displeasure, fear so stop doing the act. The main 
neurotransmitter is acetylcholine. 

Hypothalamus 
Hypothalamus 
The hypothalamus is situated at the base of the brain, in front of brain stem. It 
lies between the optic chiasma anteriorly and mammillary body posteriorly. It is 
divided into a variety of nuclei and nuclear areas.
