Functions of hypothalamus 
The hypothalamus controls most of the vegetative (relating to involuntary bodily 
functions) and endocrine functions of the body, as well as many aspects of 
emotional behavior. It is concerned mainly with control of internal homeostasis. 
The major functions of the hypothalamus are: 
1- Control of endocrine glands: It occurs through: 

a- Nervous control 
The hypothalamus controls two endocrine glands 

- Posterior pituitary gland: where it controls secretion of oxytocin and antidiuretic 
hormones - Adrenal medulla: Posterior hypothalamus stimulates the secretion of 
catecolamines from adrenal medulla by sending signals through sympathetic nerve to 
adrenal medulla 
b- Hormonal control 
The hypothalamus controls anterior pituitary hormones through the 

release of hypophysiotropic (releasing and inhibiting) hormones. 
e Arcuate nucleus synthesizes dopamine, growth hormone-releasing 
hormone(GHRH) 
¢ Preoptic nucleus synthesizes gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) 
e Paraventricular nucleus synthesizes thyrotropin-releasing hormone (TRH), 
corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH), somatostatin (SST) 
2- Control of autonomic nervous system 

a- Stimulation of posterolateral part produces sympathetic effects, 
(alarm response). 
b- -Stimulation of the anterior nuclei produces parasympathetic effects. 

3- Control of food intake and appetite: 
Hypothalamus contains 2 centers (called appestat) which act reciprocally: 

a- Feeding center: It is present in lateral hypothalamus. 
b- b- Satiety center:it is present in ventromedial nucleus of the 
hypothalamus. 

Feeding center is always active but it can be inhibited by satiety center, 
which contain glucoreceptors (glucostat). When blood glucose increases after 
meals this stimulate satiety center glucoreceptors, thus inhibiting feeding and 
producing satiety. 
N.B: Entrance of glucose to satiety center is insulin dependent this explains 
excessive hunger noticed in diabetic patients. 

« Lesion in Feeding center = Anorexia 

* Lesion in Satiety center - Hyperphagia 

4 Control of water balance and ECF volume: 
The hypothalamus controls water intake by stimulation of thirst center in 
lateral nuclei and water output by the release of ADH from posterior pituitary 
which controls urinary water loss. 
5- Control of body temperature 
Hypothalamus contains centers that regulate body temperature (thermostat), 
which maintains body temperature at the set point of the hypothalamic 
thermostat. 

386 



CHAPTER VI: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

a- Heat gain center in posterior hypothalamus causes cutaneous V.C. and 
shivering. 
b- Heat loss center in anterior hypothalamus causes cutaneous V.D. and 
sweating. 

ie Control of circadian rhythm 
Hypothalamic suprachiasmatic nucleus is considered as an internal biological 
clock responsible for periodic changes (diurnal rhythm) of many functional 
parameters in body (waking sleep cycle, body temperature, heart rate 
regulation, blood pressure, digestive activities, hormonal secretion) 
7- Control of motivation and learning 
Motivation is the feeling which activates certain behavior to achieve a certain 
goal. Both hypothalamus and limbic system control motivation through the 
rewarding and punishment centers. 
8- Control of emotional and sexual behavior, together with limbic 
system. 
Sexual drive can be stimulated from several areas of the hypothalamus especially 
the most anterior and most posterior parts. 

Lesion in anterior hypothalamus —= abolish sex interest 
9- Control of sleep 
Stimulation of the posterior hypothalamus produces arousal and awake state. 
Stimulation of the anterior hypothalamus induces sleep. 
Lateral hypothalamic neurons produces orexin hormone which appears to be 
important in switching between sleep and wakefulness. 

Memory, Learning & Amnesia 
A characteristic of human is the ability to alter behavior on the basis of past 
experiences. 

Memory 

It is the retention and storage of information for later recall. 
Learning 

It is acquisition of knowledge or skills as a consequence of experience, instruction 
or both. 

Classifications of memory: 
1.Physiologic classification 

From a physiologic point of view, according to the type of information stored, 
memory is divided into: 
a. Explicit or declarative memory 
b. Implicit or nondeclarative memory 

2. General (common) classification According to stage, memory is divided into: 
a.Short-term memory 

b. Long-term memory 

387 

er E———TT 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 
functions 

Physiologic classification 
1. Explicit or declarative memory 
Explicit memory is the memory for factual knowledge about people, places, and 
things. 
It involves conscious recall or awareness. 
It is dependent on the hippocampus and medial temporal lobes of the brain, 
neocotex and prefrontal cortex for its retention. 
It is divided into: 

-Semantic memory: general memories = facts (words, rules, and 
language) 
-Episodic memory: personal memories (events and experiences). 

Explicit memories that are initially required for activities such as riding a bicycle can 
become implicit once the task is thoroughly learned. 

2. Implicit or nondeclarative memory 
-Implicit memory is the memory for training reflexive motor or perceptual skills. 
-It does not involve awareness. 
-Its retention does not usually involve processing in the hippocampus. 

-It is subdivided into four types: 
-Priming: is the facilitation of the recognition of words or objects by prior exposure to 
them. 
It is dependent on the neocortex. 
-Procedural memory: includes skills and habits, which, once acquired, become 
unconscious and automatic. 

This type of memory is processed in the striatum and cerebellum. 

-Associative learning: includes classical and operant conditioning in which one learns about 
the relationship between one stimulus and another. 

This type of memory is dependent on the amygdala for its emotional responses and the 
cerebellum for the motor responses. 

-Nonassociative learning includes habituation and sensitization It is dependent on 
various reflex pathways.

General classification: 
Explicit memory and many forms of implicit memory involve 

1. Short-term memory, which lasts seconds to hours. 
2. Long-term memory, which stores memories for days, years and sometimes 
for life.

N.B. 
The amygdala is closely associated with the hippocampus and is concerned with 
encoding and recalling emotionally charged memories. 
In normal humans, events associated with strong emotions are remembered better 
than events without an emotional charge, but in patients with bilateral lesions of 
the amygdala, this difference is absent. 

Working memory 
It is a form of short-term memory that keeps information available, usually for 
very short periods, while the individual plans action based on it. 
The prefrontal association area is the site of operation of working memory. 

Role of synaptic plasticity in memory and learning 

Synaptic transmission between neurons can be strengthened or weakened for 
short or long duration on the basis of past experience. 

‘ These changes in synaptic function are of great interest because they represent 
forms of learning and memory. 
They can be presynaptic or postsynaptic in location. 

Forms of plastic change 

e Posttetanic potentiation 

* Habituation 
Sensitization 

* Long-term potentiation {LTP) 
* Long-term depression (LTD) 

Posttetanic potentiation 

It is the production of enhanced postsynaptic neuron response following 
stimulation of presynaptic neuron by brief tetanizing train of stimuli. 
This enhancement lasts up to 60 s. 

Mechanism: 
- The tetanizing stimulation causes Ca®* to accumulate in the presynaptic neuron 

and increase neurotransmitter release. 
Value: 

It is the basis of short-term memory 

Habituation 

It is a simple form of learning and a classic example of nonassociative learning. 
It is a decreased responsiveness to repetitive presentations of a neutral 
stimulus. 
Mechanism: 
Gradual inactivation of Ca?* channels at presynaptic neuron by repeated 
stimulation causes decreased intracellular Ca?* and decreased release of 
neuratransmitter from the presynaptic terminal. 

Value: 
It is the basis of short-term or long-term memory._ 

391 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

Sensitization 

It is the opposite of habituation. 
Sensitization is the prolonged occurrence of augmented postsynaptic responses 
after a stimulus to which one has become habituated is paired once or several 
times with a noxious stimulus. 
Sensitization is due to presynaptic facilitation. 
Mechanism: 

1. The noxious stimulus causes serotonin release from facilitating interneuron 
that synapses on the presynaptic (sensory) terminal. 
2. Serotonin acts on serotonin receptors in the sensory terminal membrane, 
causing activation of adenyl cyclase inside the membrane that causes increased 
formation of cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP). 
3. The cyclic AMP results in blocking of the potassium channels in the 
presynaptic terminal resulting in decrease K* efflux. 
4. Lack of potassium conductance causes prolonged action potential in the 
synaptic terminal which causes prolonged opening of the calcium channels, 
allowing greater Ca%* influx which greatly increase transmitter release which 
produces a larger postsynaptic potential resulting in a more vigorous response. 

Value: 
It is the basis of short-term or long-term memory 

Long-term potentiation (LTP): 

LTP is a rapidly developing persistent enhancement of the postsynaptic potential 

response to presynaptic stimulation after a brief period of rapidly repeated 

stimulation of the presynaptic neuron, 

It resembles posttetanic potentiation but is much more prolonged and can last for 

days or even weeks—long enough for the short-term memory to be consolidated into 

more permanent longterm memory. 

LTP involves protein synthesis and growth of the presynaptic and postsynaptic 

neurons and their connections. 

LTP occurs in many parts of the nervous system and is especially prevalent in the 

hippocampus, a site critical for converting short-term memories into long-term 

memories. 

Mechanism: 
There are multiple mechanisms by which LTP can occur, some are dependent on 
changes in the N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor and some are independent 
of the NMDA receptor. LTP is initiated by an increase in intracellular Ca%* in either 
the presynaptic or postsynaptic neuron. 
Value: 
It is the basis of long-term memory. 

392 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

Long-term depression (LTD) 

LTD is the opposite of LTP. it is characterized by a decrease in synaptic strength. 
Mechanism: 
It is produced by slower (prolonged periods of low frequency) stimulation of 
presynaptic neurons and is associated with a smaller rise in intracellular Ca?*within 
the postsynaptic neuron than that which occurs in LTP. 
Value: 
It may be involved in the mechanism by which learning occurs in the cerebellum. 

Consolidation of memory 
Definition: Conversion of short term memory into long term memory. 
It occurs when structural changes in the synapse are initiated. 
Duration: it requires minimally 5-10 minutes up to 1 hour or more for strong consolidation 
Factors affecting consolidation of memory 

I. Factors increasing consolidation: 
1- Attractive, interesting information. 
2-Quiet sleep. 
3-Mental rest. 

Il. Factors interrupting consolidation: 
1- Deep anesthesia. 

2- Brain concussion. 
3- Electroshock therapy. 

Memory disorders 

Amnesia 
Loss of memory either partially or totally. 
It may be: 
1. Anterograde: Inability to form new long-term memories as in 
atherosclerosis, old age and lesions of hippocampus or medial temporal lobe. 
2. Retrograde: Inability to recall memories from the past especially 
recent events that occurred shortly before onset of injury as in brain 
concussion, anesthesia and electroshock therapy. 
3. Global: Forgotness of both recent and remote events as in extensive 
brain lesions. 

Alzheimer’s disease: 
It is the most common an age-related neurodegenerative disease. 
it is slowly progressive and irreversible. 
It is the most common cause of senile dementia. 
It results in loss of synapses and neurons and neurotransmitters as 
acetylcholine. 
it is characterized by progressive loss of memory; first manifests as loss of 
episodic memory which impedes recollection of recent event. 
Loss of short-term memory is followed by general loss of cognitive and other 
brain functions, agitation, depression, the need for constant care and eventually 
death. 

393 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

Learning 

It is acquisition of knowledge or skills as a consequence of experience, instruction 
or both. 
It is two types: 

1-Simple {non associative) learning 
2-Associative (Conditioning) learning 

1-Simple (non associative) learning 
-It is concerned with modification of behavioral response to a repeated 
stimulus. - [tis two types: 
a. Habituation 
b. Sensitization 

2-Associative {Conditioning) learning 
-This involves establishment of correlation between one stimulus and the 
other or stimuli and responses. 
-It is two types: 
a. Classical conditioning: 

A conditioned reflex is a reflex response to neutral stimuli that normally do not 
produce response. A conditioned response is acquired by repeated pairing of 
stimulus that normally does not produce the response (conditioned stimulus) 
with another stimulus that naturally produces the response = (unconditioned 
stimulus), e.g. Pavlov's experiments Mechanism of conditioning (Similar to 
sensitization) 

b. Operant (Instrumental) conditioning: 
It is the use of consequences to modify the occurrence and form of behavior. 
It involves applying reinforcement (reward) or punishment after a behavior. 
The person or animal are taught to perform a specific task to obtain reward or 
avoid punishment e.g. response is learned by reinforcement or punishment 
(positive or negative), e.g. car driver slows down and stops the car on seeing red 
traffic light and drive on when seeing the green light. 

Reinforcement increase Punishment (decrease 
behavior})) behavior) 

Positive (something something added increases something added decreases 
added) behavior behavior 

Negative (something something removed something removed 
removed) increases behavior decreases behavior 

Mechanism: 
Stimulation of reward and punishment center

ANTIDEPRESSANTS 
Depression is a mood disorder. lt is classified into: 
1. Unipolar depression or Major depressive Disorder (17%): a genetically determined 
biochemical disorder manifested by inability to deal with normal stress of life. 
2. Bipolar manic depressive disorder (~ 2%) 

e Episodes of depression alternating with episodes of mania. 
eo Manic attacks are characterized by decrease need of sleep, flights of ideas, grandiosity, 

euphoria, talkativeness, lack of judgment, psychomotor agitation & may be associated 
with psychotic symptoms (hallucinations, delusions). 

Symptoms of Depression 
e Sadness — inability to experience pleasure (anhedonia) - fatigue- loss of libido - 

disturbance in appetite & sleep (usuallyd) - guilt — worthlessness - inability to 
concentrate or indecisiveness- psychomotor agitation or retardation- recurrent suicidal 
ideation.Psychotic symptoms (hallucinations & delusions) occur in severe depression. 

Monoamine Theory of Depression! 
e Brain monoamines, norepinephrine (NE) & serotonin (5-HT) are neurotransmitters 

responsible for mood. 4 Activity of these transmitters results in depression, whereas 1 
activity leads to mood elevation. So, antidepressants act via increasing monoamines.

Mechanism of Action of Antidepressants 

1. Increase in brain biogenic amines (immediately within 12 hours). 

2. Downregulation of brain 3 & 5-HT; receptors (delayed for 2-4 weeks). 

3. Affect neurotrophic factors essential for survival & function of neurons.

Il. Selective Serotonin Reuptake Inhibitor (SSRI) 
(Fluoxetine, Paroxetine, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Sertraline) 

The most widely used initial therapy (15 line for treatment of depression). 
No Autonomic side effects. Less cardiotoxicity (But Citalopra4m Q-T>) - safer in overdose.

V. Monoamine Oxidase Inhibitors (MAOIs) 
e MAOIs were among the earliest antidepressants introduced for therapy of depression but 

due to their toxicity & serious food & drug interactions, their use is limited & several 
precautions have to be taken during their administration.

Precautions with MAOIs 
1. Patients on nonselective MAOIs should be warned against serious drug interactions & 

should be given a list of the foods they should avoid. 
2. Patients on MAOIs should not receive TCAs or SSRIs except after 2 weeks from 

stopping MAOIs (effect persists for 2 weeks, washout period). 
3. Avoid in the elderly = postural hypotension - atropine-like actions. (Autonomic) 

Moclobemide®’ 
e Short-acting, reversible inhibitor of MAO-A (RIMA). 
e Has the advantage of a reduced risk of cheese reaction due to: 
-Selective MAO-A inhibitor = allows MAO-B to metabolize tyramine. 
-Short-reversible MAQO-A inhibitor => easily displaced by tyramine allowing tyramine to 
be metabolized by MAO- A. 

55 Cheese Reaction: Tyramine in food is metabolized in GIT by MAO-A & MAO-B. MAOIs allow tyramine in 
tyramine-rich food (old cheese, chicken liver, chocolate) to escape metabolism & release T 1 T amounts of 
catecholamines from neurons =» hypertensive crisis. 

> Inhibition of pethidine metabolism by MAOIs =>formation of neuro toxic metabolite, norpethidine. 

Other Antidepressants :Agomelatine 

Melatonin M1 and M2 receptor agonist and exhibits 5-HT2C antagonism {‘T* DA &NA). 

Agomelatine is not hypnotic like Ramelteon because it increases release of NE via SHT2C antagonism=-1> 

Arousal. It is used in major depressive disorder in non-responders and intolerants to SSRIs. 

It corrects disturbances in sleep circadian rhythm associated with depression. Hepatotoxic 

°! Reversible Inhibitors of MAO-A (RIMA) 

Antidepressant activity of MAOIs is related to inhibition of MAO-A enzyme (responsible for NE & SHT 

metabolism). Thus to reduce risk of cheese reaction, selective inhibitors of MAO-A were developed.

Therapeutic Uses of Antidepressants: 
A. Psychiatric Disorders 

1. Major depression disorder (main use) 
2. Anxiety disorders: generalized anxiety disorder (GAD) -panic disorder -phobias — 

obsessive compulsive disorders (QCD). 
3. Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD). 
4. Eating disorders : Anorexia Nervosa & Bulimia Nervosa ® 

B. Non-psychiatric Diseases: 
1. Pain disorders: e.g., chronic neuropathic pain (Amitriptyline & Duloxetine) 8. 

Stress Urinary incontinence>® (Duloxetine, due to central action). 
Nocturnal enuresis (Imipramine; anticholinergic = contracts urethral sphincter). 
Smoking cessation (Bupropion). 

. Insomnia: (Mirtazapine & Amitriptyline). 
6. Migraine prophylaxis: Amitriptyline. 

: Guidelines for the use of antidepressants 
e Antidepressant effect is delayed for 2 - 4 weeks; treatment is continued for 4 weeks 

before considered unsuccessful. If partial improvement, continue for another 4 weeks. 
¢ Following remission, continue treatment for 6 months. Long term maintenance therapy 

for 2 years may be needed if patient experiences recurrent episodes. 
e Gradual withdrawal over 4 weeks (to avoid Withdrawal syndrome). 
e [n cases resistant to initial therapy switch to a different member of same class or to 

another class. Then do combination therapy (e.g. SSRI + SNRI) 
e Atypical antipsychotic, lithium, or T3 may be added in resistant cases. 

“8 pain ( in the form of pins & needles or electrical shock), due to affection of sensory nerves( of pain, touch, 
heat & cold) e.g. trigeminal neuralgia, diabetic neuropathy or herpes zoster. Corticospinal monoaminergic 
pathway is important for pain transmission; thus antidepressants that f*m onoamines especially NE possess 
analgesic activity independent of effects on mood. 

59 Stress urinary incontinence (SU) is the involuntary, sudden loss of urine secondary to increased 
intraabdominal pressure that is bothersome or affecting the patient's quality of life. Physical activities 
precipitating SUI include laughing, sneezing, straining, coughing, or exercising.

CEREBRAL MENINGES 
Similar to the spinal cord, the brain is also protected by three meninges: dura, arachnoid and 
pia. (Figs. 7.18 & 7.19) 
Dura Mater: 

It is the outer layer & is tough and fibrous. 
It is formed of two layers around the brain, the outer endosteal layer which is adherent 
to the skull and the inner meningeal layer which forms 4 dural folds. 
The two layers lie close together but separate to include a venous sinus or where the inner 
layer forms a fold. 
The inner layer is continuous with the spinal dura “one layer only”. 

Arachnoid Mater: 
It is a delicate membrane that lies 
between dura and pia. 
It covers gyri & bridges over sulci of sdura) space 
the brain. Reanne a 
The space between dura & arachnoid ” 
is called the subdural space. It 2 
contains a thin film of fluid. 2A : 
The space between arachnoid and pia Fig. 7.18 : meninges 
is called subarachnoid space. The VHIIOUS BUTE SINUS 
later contains CSF and the arteries Arachnold granulation 
supplying the brain. 
The outer surface of the arachnoid 
forms at certain sites the arachnoid 

jit Cerebral vein 
villi and granulations through which 
CSF passes to the venous sinuses. 3 = ~ Subarachnoid 

The inner surface of the arachnoid is 7 space 
Pia 

connected to the pia by fine threads 
that transverse the subarachnoid space. Fig. 7.19 : arachnoid granulations 

Pia Mater: 

It is a delicate vascular membrane that is adherent to the surface of brain 
It covers gyri and dips into its sulci. 

@ The pia mater + blood vessels + ependyma form the choroid plexuses of the ventricles. 
The pia mater around the spinal cord forms the filum terminale at the level of lower 
border of 1% Lumbar vertebra where the spinal cord ends. On each side of the spinal cord, 
it forms the denticulate ligaments which pass through arachnoid to attach to dura. These 
denticulate ligaments help to suspend the cord within the vertebral canal. 

Subarachnoid cisterns: are wide parts of the subarachnoid space (Fig. 7.20). 
1. Cerebello medullary cistern = cisterna magna 

- Lies below the cerebellum & behind the closed medulla. 
- ltis continuous with the subarachnoid space around the spinal cord 
- It receives CSF from the fourth ventricles via three foramina: 1 median foramen of 

Magendi and 2 lateral foramina of Luschka. 

404 



CHAPTER VII: CEREBRUM.MENINGIES & higher brain 

functions 

2. Cistern of great Cerebral vein (cisterna ambiens) 
- Lies above the cerebellum & below the splenium of corpus callosum. 
- It contains the great cerebral vein. 

3. Cisterna pontis: 
- Lies on the ventral surface of pons. 
- Contains basilar artery. 

4. Interpenduncular Cistern: 
- Lies ventral to the interpeduncular fossa. 
- Contains the circle of Willis. 

5. Cistern of lateral fossa: 
- Lies over the lateral sulcus. 
- Contains the middle cerebral artery.

Hydrocephalus:

in circulation of CSF. This excess fluid compresses the brain.

anastomose at the circle of Willis.

Circle of Willis “Circulus arteriosus” (Fig. 7.24) 
Definition: a large arterial anastomotic circle between the carotid and vertebrobasilar 
systems. It provides alternate channels if one vessel is occluded. 
Site: at the base of the brain, in the interpeduncular cistern. 
It is formed by: 
1. Right and left anterior cerebral arteries (ACA), with the anterior communicating artery 

joining them. 
2. Right and left internal carotid arteries (ICA). 
3. Right and left posterior cerebral arteries (PCA). 
4. Posterior communicating artery joining ICA + PCA. 

Central branches arise from the circulus arteriosus or very close to it to supply nearby 
structures. These central branches are divided into groups (sets): 
1. The antero-medial group from ACA. 
2. The antero-lateral group from MCA. 
3. The postero-medial group of PCA. 
4. The postero-lateral group of PCA,

over corpus callosum. It ends at parieto-occipital sulcus (P.0.S.) by anastomosing with the 
posterior cerebral artery.

Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA): (Fig. 7.25) 
- Origin: It is the larger terminal branch of internal carotid artery. 
- Course: it runs in the lateral sulcus & insula to reach the superolateral surface. 
- Branches: 

v Lateral surface (a large area except | Antero-lateral set (AL

Applied anatomy: the MCA supplies the motor & sensory areas of the contralateral upper 
limb + speech & auditory areas + frontal eye field. Its thrombosis is very serious & if occurs 
on the dominant side aphasia occurs. 

Posterior Cerebral Artery (PCA): (Fig. 7.25) 
- Origin: The terminal branch of basilar artery. 
- Course: It receives the posterior communicating artery and turns around the cerebral

Intended Learning Outcomes (ILOs): 

By the end of this chapter the student should be able to: 
~

identify characteristics of common pathogens affecting muscle tone and nervous 
system. 
Describe mode of transmission, clinical presentations, laboratory diagnosis, 
prevention and treatment of tetanus and botulism 

e Describe life cycle of parasites causing CNS infections. 
e Identify mode of transmission of pathogens causing meningitis and encephalitis. 

Correlate the pathogenesis of the microbial infection to the clinical presentation 
and laboratory diagnosis of meningitis and encephalitis. 
Outline preventive measures for each infection. 
Describe pathological manifestations in each type of CNS infection. 
List treatment of choice in different infections. 
Classify antibiotics according to penetration of CNS. 
List antibiotics that can be used in prophylaxis and treatment of meningitis. 
Realize the role of dexamethasone in treatment of meningitis 
Explain precautions with selection of antibiotics in meningitis. 
Solve a case study problem about CNS infection.

Tetanus 

Clostridium tetani 
e Gram positive anaerobic, motile, non-capsulated spore forming bacilli. 
e Spores are terminal and bulging (drumstick appearance). 
e Clostridium tetani (C. tetani) presents naturally in intestine of animals and it reaches the 

soil from their excreta, so manured lands as gardens are heavily contaminated with its 
spores 

e C. tetani is non-invasive and its virulence depends on the production of a polypeptide 
exotoxin (tetanospasmin). 

e Vegetation of spores is favored by necrotic tissue and poor blood supply in the wound. 

Pathogenesis: 
Infection can occur through the following routes: 

e Spores of C. tetani are widespread in soil. 
e The portal of entry is usually a wound site e.g., when a nail penetrates the foot. 
e Vegetation of spores is favored by necrotic tissue and poor blood supply in the wound. 
e Another form is the neonatal tetanus where the organism enters through a 

contaminated umbilicus or circumcision wound (tetanus neonatorum/neonatal 
tetanus. 

414 



CHAPTER VIN: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

Toxin production: 
e After vegetation of spores at the wound site tetanospasmin is produced. 
e toxin reaches the anterior horn cells of the spinal cord by retrograde axonal transport 

(intra-axonal) to the central nervous system 

e [t binds to ganglioside receptors and blocks release of inhibitory mediators (e.g., 
glycine and y-aminobutyric acid [GABA]) at spinal synapses leading to sustained 
excitation causing the characteristic muscular spasms of tetanus. 

e Tetanus toxin has one antigenic type. 

Clinical features: 
e Strong muscle spasms (spastic paralysis, tetany), manifested by lockjaw (trismus) 
e Due to rigid contraction of the jaw muscles, a characteristic grimace known as risus 

sardonicus; and exaggerated reflexes. 
e Respiratory failure occurs as a consequence (high mortality rate). 

Laboratory Diagnosis: 
Tetanus diagnosis is strictly clinical. Lab diagnosis for confirmation for epidemiological 
purposes 
Specimen: wound exudate. 
e¢ Microscopic examination of Gram stained film: Gram positive drumstick bacilli 
e (Cultivation on anaerobic enrichment medium, Robertson cooked meat medium, followed 

by subculture on blood agar incubated anaerobically for 2-3 days at 37°C. 
e B-hemolytic colonies (due to production of tetanolysin) can be further identified by: 

o Gram stained film 
o Biochemical reactions: no sugar fermentation or gelatin liquefaction. 
o Toxin detection: 

= Subculture of isolated colonies on another blood agar plate containing tetanus 
antitoxin, after anaerobic incubation the hemolysis produced by C. tetani is 
inhibited by the antitoxin 

Treatment: 
General measures: Maintain an adequate airway and place patient in adark quiet area to 
avoid excitation. 
Antitoxin (Tetanus immune globulin): given at once to neutralize the unbound tetanus toxin 
Antibiotic: 
Metronidazole or Penicillin G to eliminate the organism. 
Prophylaxis: 
Tetanus toxoid is usually given to children in combination with diphtheria toxoid and the 
acellular pertussis vaccine (DTaP). Another form of the vaccine (Tdap) is given to adolescents 
and adults as a booster. 
When trauma occurs: 

a. The wound should be cleaned and debrided, and tetanus toxoid booster should be given. 
b. If the wound is grossly contaminated, tetanus immunoglobulin and toxoid booster as 

well, should be given and penicillin administered. 
c. The administration of both immune globulins and tetanus toxoid (at different sites in the 

body) is an example of passive=active immunity. 

415 



CHAPTER Vili: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

Botulism 
Clostridium botulinum 
¢ Anerobic Gram positive motile, non-capsulated, spore-forming bacilli. 
o It is essentially a saprophyte in soil that is frequently present in the intestinal tract of 

domestic animals. 
e Spores are oval and subterminal 

Mode of Transmission: 
e The spores are widespread in soil can contaminate vegetables and meats. When these 

foods are canned or vacuum-packed with inadequate sterilization, spores survive and 
germinate in the anaerobic environment. 

e Toxin is produced within the canned food and ingested preformed 
e¢ The highest-risk foods are alkaline vegetables such as green beans, peppers, and 

mushrooms and smoked fish. 
¢ The toxin is relatively heat-labile; inactivated by boiling for several minutes. Thus, disease 

can be prevented by sufficient cooking. 

Pathogenesis: 
e Botulinum toxin is absorbed from the gut and carried via the blood to peripheral nerve 

synapses, where it blocks release of acetylcholine. 
e [tis a protease that cleaves the proteins involved in acetylcholine release. 
e The toxin is a polypeptide encoded by a lysogenic phage. It is among the most toxic 

substances known. 
e There are eight immunologic types of toxin; types A, B, and E are the most common 

in human illness. 

Clinical Findings: 
¢ Descending weakness and paralysis, including diplopia, dysphagias, and respiratory 

muscle failure, are seen. | 

eo No fever. 
e Two special clinical forms occur: 

1. Wound botulism: in which spores contaminate a wound, germinate, and produce 
toxin at the site 

2. Infant botulism: in which the organisms grow in the gut and produce the toxin 
there. Ingestion of honey containing the organism is implicated in transmission of 
infant botulism. Affected infants develop weakness or paralysis and may need 
respiratory support. 

Laboratory Diagnosis: 
Initial diagnosis is based on clinical symptoms. Treatment should not wait for laboratory 
confirmation. Laboratory diagnosis is commonly required to supplement and confirms clinical 
diagnosis. 
Specimen: 
1- Tests for toxin detection in food remnants or patient blood 

a. PCR can identify the organism and the toxin gene. 
b. Animal pathogenicity and in vivo neutralization test (Food extract is injected in mice; 

the animal will die with flaccid paralysis. The antigenic type of the toxin is identified 

416



CHAPTER Vili: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

by neutralization with specific antitoxin in mice, i.e. the animal will not die if protected 
by a specific antitoxin. 

c. ELISA assays have been developed to detect the botulinum toxin, 

2- Isolation& identification: 
Specimen: Food remnants, gastric contents obtained by gastric lavage. 
e (Cultivation on Robertson cooked meat media and subculture on blood agar plate 

incubated anaerobically at 37°C, for 48h. 
e Hemolytic colonies on blood agar are further identified by: 

o Microscopic film: Gram stained film shows Gram positive spore forming bacilli 
o Biochemical reactions: C. botulinum ferments glucose and maltose. proteolytic 

strains (A, B and E) causes blackening of Robertson cooked meat medium, gelatin 
liquefaction and positive H2S production test. 

o Animal pathogenicity toxin detection by intraperitoneal injection of the 
supernatant from the culture extract into mice. The mouse dies rapidly with 
generalized flaccid paralysis. The type of toxin can be determined by neutralization 
with specific antitoxin in mice. 

Treatment: 
Trivalent antitoxin (types A, B, and E) and respiratory support. 

Prevention: 
e Proper canning techniques and adequate heating of home-canned food to inactivate any 

toxin present. 
e Swollen cans must be discarded (clostridial proteolytic enzymes form gas, which swells 

cans). 

Other organisms causing flaccid paralysis 
v' Poliovirus 
v' Enterovirus 68, 
v' Enterovirus 70, 
v' Coxsackieviruses A and B 

Portals of infection of the CNS: 
CNS is a sterile body site; Pathogens may access the CNS through: 

e Hematogenous spread by way of the arterial blood supply is the most common means 
of entry. There can also be retrograde venous spread, through the anastomoses between 
veins of the face and the venous sinuses of the skull. 
Direct implantation of microorganisms is almost invariably due to traumatic 
introduction of foreign material. In rare cases it can be iatrogenic, as when microbes are 
introduced with a lumbar puncture needle. 

o Local extension can occur with infections of the skull or spine. 
e Peripheral nerves also may serve as paths of entry for a few pathogens—in particular, 

viruses such as the rabies and herpes zoster viruses. 

417 



CHAPTER Vil: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

Meningitis 
Meningitis is an inflammatory process involving the meninges, it can be due to infection or 
non- infection causes. This part is concerned with infection causes. 
Depending on speed of the initial presentation and the rate of progression of the illness; 
Meningitis can be categorized as: 
- Acute meningitis: is caused by either pyogenic bacteria, such as Streptococcus 
pneumoniae and Neisseria meningitidis, or viruses, such as Coxsackie virus and herpes 
simplex virus type 2. 

- Subacute meningitis is caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis and fungi, such as 
Cryptococcus. . 

- Chronic meningitis: complication of acute or subacute meningitis 

Pathophysiology of meningitis: 
e¢ Hematogenous spread (i.e., bacteremia) after invasion of the upper respiratory mucosa 

is the most common route by which organisms reach the meninges. 
e Following invasion of the meninges, meningeal irritation, inflammatory response and 

edema cause many of the clinical manifestations. 

Signs and symptoms: 
e Fever. 

e Headache, vomiting. 
e Photophobia. 

Loss of various brain functions leading to drowsiness and altered mental state. 
Neck stiffness. 
Signs and symptoms of meningitis may rapidly develop; for example, meningococcal 
meningitis can kill within six hours of the initial symptoms, allowing little time for 
treatment. 

e Petechiae (small, dark purplish hemorrhages of blood vessels in the skin) are sometimes 
present. 

Laboratory diagnosis: CSF examination 
e CSF is obtained by performing a lumber puncture at the L3-L4 intervertebral space. 
e A lumbar puncture reveals the normally clear CSF. 
¢ In meningitis, the CSF pressure is measured, and fluid obtained for analysis of cells 

(both number and cell type, i.e., neutrophils or lymphocytes), protein, and glucose 
(Table 8.1) and microbiological diagnosis. 

¢ Further microbiologic examination is performed according to the results of CSF analysis 
and this includes: 
Oo microscopic examination of Gram stained films prepared from the CSF deposit after 

centrifugation. 
o Ag detection by specific antisera from the CSF supernatant. 
o Culture on suitable media for isolation of microbial cause, identification and 

antimicrobial susceptibility. 
o PCR test particularly for suspected viral causes and non-culturable or difficult to 

culture microbes. 

418 



CHAPTER VII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

o Although CSF analysis is a very important step in the diagnosis of many CNS infections, a 
lumbar puncture should not be performed if there is (are): 
o Signs of increased intracranial tension, such as papilledema or focal neurologic signs, 

to avoid herniation of the brain stem that may lead to death. 
o A mass lesion on CT scan (brain abscess or tumor).

Bacterial meningitis 
It is mostly caused by: 

1. Neisseria meningitides 
2. Haemophilus influenzae 
3. Streptococcus pneumoniae 
4. Other pyogenic bacteria as Staph. aureus, Pseudomonas aeruginosa cause meningitis 

when introduced by lumbar puncture or head trauma. 
5. M. tuberculosis: tuberculous meningitis. 

N.B.: Most common causes of neonatal meningitis are E. coli K1, Streptococcus agalactiae, 
and Listeria monocytogens. 

Neisseria meningitides 
e Meningococci are non- motile, Gram negative kidney-shaped capsulated diplococci 

(always appear in pairs). 
e [t is the most common cause of purulent meningitis called epidemic cerebrospinal 

meningitis. 
e Meningococci are divided into at least 13 capsular polysaccharide types(serotypes), of 

them five serotypes cause most cases of meningitis: A, B, C, Y, and W-135. 
e Serotype A is the leading cause of epidemic meningitis worldwide. 
Virulence factors: 
1. Polysaccharide Capsule: It is the most important virulence factor as it isanti-phagocytic 

and anti-complementary. It is also antigenic; against which protective antibodies are 
produced. 

2. Lipopolysaccharides: It is the endotoxin which can cause fulminant meningococcemia and 
septic shock. 

. Pili: mediate attachment to mucosa 
4. \gA proteases: helps the bacteria attach to the membranes of the upper respiratory tract 

by destroys secretory IgA. 
5. Factor H binding protein; Inhibits c3b complement component and helps the organism 

evade the immune response. (This protein is an immunogen used in development of 
vaccine against group B meningococci). 

Mode of transmission: 
e Respiratory droplets of cases or carriers 
e They colonize the mucous membranes of the nasopharynx and become part of the 

transient flora of the upper respiratory tract in 5-10% of normal population. 

419

Lad 



CHAPTER Vil: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

¢ The carriage rate can be as high as 35% in people who live in close quarters (e.g., military 
recruits). During epidemics the carrier rates may reach 20-90%. 

e (Carriers are usually asymptomatic. 

Pathogenesis and clinical presentations 
¢ From the nasopharynx, the organism can enter the bloodstream and spread to the 

subarachnoid space mainly through blood as a result of bacteremia (hematogenous 
spread) or in some instances meningococci reach CNS through the sheath of the olfactory 
nerve (direct spread). 

e The disease is characterized by fever, sore throat, severe headache, photophobia, 
projectile vomiting, nuchal rigidity, coma and death. 

e Fulminant meningococcemia (septicemia) with intravascular coagulation, circulatory 
collapse and potentially fatal shock may occur without meningitis 

¢ Asymptomatic carrier: About 5% of people become chronic convalescent carriers and 
serve as a source of infection for others. 

Laboratory Diagnosis: 
Diagnosis of a case: 
e Specimens: CSF, Blood for blood culture, and Nasopharyngeal swab 

1- CSF examination 
CSF is turbid and under tension, after centrifugation: 
Supernatant examination shows 
o Elevated Leucocytic cell count, mostly neutrophils, decreased glucose levels and 

increased protein levels as indicators of bacterial meningitis (Table 8.1). 
o Detection of capsular meningococcal antigen by latex agglutination or ring 

precipitation test. 
Deposit is used for bacteriological examination: 
a. Direct Gram stained film: typical morphology within pus cells. 
b. Culture on Thayer- Martin medium or chocolate blood agar medium (5-10% CQ»). 

Colonies are small transparent or grey colonies that are furtherly identified as 
e Gram negative diplococci. 
e¢ (Oxidase positive. 
e Glucose and maltose fermentation with production of acid only. 

c. A presumptive diagnosis of meningococcal meningitis can be made if gram 
negative diplococci are visualized microscopically in direct smears and oxidase- 
positive colonies of gram-negative diplococci are isolated. 

d. Confirmatory test: 
Latex agglutination test can assist in the rapid diagnosis of meningococcal 
meningitis, which detects capsular polysaccharide in the spinal fluid and used for 
Serogrouping. 

2- Blood culture: done as usual then subculture is done onto chocolate agar (further 
isolation and identification as CSF samples). 

3- PCR (if available) is sensitive and specific. 
Diagnosis of asymptomatic carrier: 
e A nasopharyngeal swab is inoculated onto enriched selective medium as Thayer-Martin 

medium and incubated for 24 hours at 37°C with 5% CO. If delay is unavoidable, swabs 
should be plunged into a transport medium until cultured. 

420



CHAPTER VII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

e Suspected colonies are identified as before and should be differentiated from commensal 
Neisseriae. 

e Unlike pathogenic Neisseria, the Commensal Neisseriae: 
1- Can grow on ordinary media. 
2- Do not require CO2 enriched atmosphere. 
3- Can grow at room temperature. 
4- Sugar utilization is variable. 
5- Cannot agglutinate with group-specific antiserum. 

Prevention 
Chemoprophylaxis (for contacts of cases during epidemics) 
e Rifampicin is preferred because it is efficiently secreted into saliva (alsoeradicates 

carriers); or 
e Ciprofloxacin: widely used as a prophylactic drug for adolescents as a single oral dose. 
Vaccination: 
Effective in preventing meningitis epidemics and in reducing carrier rates 
Against groups A, C, Y, W-135: 

oe Types: 
o The conjugate vaccines: contains the four polysaccharides (A, C, Y, W135), conjugated 

to a carrier protein (either tetanus toxoid or genetically modified diphtheria toxin). 
o The unconjugated vaccine that contains only the four polysaccharides (A, C, Y, W135) 

(Meningococcal polysaccharide vaccine; MPSV4). 
eo The conjugate vaccines induce higher titers of antibodies in children than does the 

unconjugated vaccine. The vaccines induce similar antibody titers in adults. 
e Conjugate vaccines are preferred because the combination with carrier proteins make 

them immunogenic even in the youngest infants. They also effectively generate 
immunological memory and reduce the nasopharyngeal carriage. 

Against group B: 
Contains factor H binding protein as an immunogen 

Streptococcus pneumoniae 
(Pneumococci) 
e¢ Pneumococci are catalase-negative Gram-positive cocci, non-motile non-spore, forming, 

typically lanceolate in shape. 
e Arranged in pairs and possess polysaccharide capsule that permits typing with specific 

antisera. 
Pathogenesis : 
Pneumococcal meningitis occurs mostly as a result of local spread from the mastoid to 
meninges (in case of otitis media) or hematogenous spread as a result of bacteremia 
associating lobar pneumonia. 

Virulence factors: 
1. Capsule: [tis the main virulence factor. lt is anti-phagocytic. Pneumococci can be classified 

into 90 serotypes according to the capsular polysaccharide. Capsule swelling test is used 
for typing of Pneumococci (Quellung reaction). 

2. IgA1 protease: cleaves human IgA the principal immunoglobulin in the upper respiratory 
tract. 

3. Pneumolysin: a membrane damaging toxin. 

421 



— = 

CHAPTER VII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

4. Autolysin: (t breaks the peptide cross-linking of peptidoglycan leading to lysis of 
pneumococci. This releases large amounts of cell wall fragments. The massive 
inflammatory response to these fragments is an important component of the 
pathogenesis of pneumonia and meningitis caused by Pneumococci. 

Laboratory Diagnosis 
Specimen: CSF, Blood for blood culture. 
1- Isolation and identification 

Strept pneumonia forms a-Hemolytic colonies on blood agar (needs enrichedmedia and 
aerobic incubation in 5-10% CO; at 37°C for 24 hours). 
e Colonies have "draughtsman" appearance. 
e Positive Quellung reaction (capsule swelling test): rapid slide precipitationtest. 

2- Latex agglutination test: rapid detection of capsular polysaccharide antigen in CSF. 

Prevention 
= 13-valent pneumococcal conjugate vaccine: capsular polysaccharides conjugated to a 

carrier protein (protects against 13 serotypes). 
Indications: 

- Children <5 years. 
- Immunocompromising conditions (congenial or acquired). 
- Anatomical or functional asplenia (e.g. sickle cell disease). 

= 23-valent pneumococcal vaccine: un-conjugated polyvalent capsular polysaccharide 
antigens (23 serotypes). 
Indications: 

- immunocompetent elderly (>65yrs) 
- Adults with chronic medical conditions (e.g. chronic heart and lung diseases). 

Haemophilus influenzae 
oe Pleomorphic Gram-negative coccobacilli. 

The most virulent strains of H. influenzae have a polysaccharide capsule (typeable strains) 
The capsulated strains are of six antigenic types (a-f). 
Type (b) is the most virulent 
Meningitis caused by Haemophilus influenzae mostly affects children from 6 months to 
6years and caused by type b (the most virulent strain) {Hib)transmitted from person to 
person through respiratory droplets. 

e |ts growth on laboratory media requires the addition of twocomponents, heme (factor X) 
and NAD (factor V). 

Virulence factors: 
eo Polysaccharide capsulewith a polyribosyiribitol phosphate (PRP) componont: 

antiphagocytic (the major virulence factor). 
e Lipopolysaccharide endotoxin : responsible for the inflammatory process. 
e |gA protease: destroys secretory IgA aiding the mucosal colonization. 
e Pilli: adhesion. 

422



CHAPTER Vill: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

Laboratory Diagnosis 
Specimen: CSF, Blood for blood culture. 

1- Gram-stained smear (plenty of pus cells and short Gram-negative pleomorphicbacilli). 
2- Culture on chocolate agar as it requires both factors X and V and 5-10% CO; for 1-2 

days. 
3- Satellitism: H. influenzae grows well around colonies of Staphylococcus aureus. 
4- Rapid Capsular antigen detection: in serum by latex agglutination, IF or ELISA. 
5- PCR. 

Prevention 
The Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib) conjugate vaccine: 
e polyribosylribitol phosphate (PRP) polysaccharide from the Hib capsule conjugated to a 

protein carrier, prevents invasive Hib disease and reduces naso-pharyngeal carriage. 
e for infants between 2 and 15 months. 

Streptococcus agalactiae 

Classification: 
e [tis one of the group-B beta-hemolytic Streptococci (GBS) 
e (Catalase negative Gram-positive cocci 
Pathogenesis and clinical diseases: 
¢ [tis an intermittent commensal in the vagina of some females. 
e |f acquired by neonates from the birth canal during delivery, it can cause neonatal sepsis 

and neonatal meningitis. 
e The main predisposing factor is prolonged (longer than 18 hours) rupture of the 

membranes in women who are colonized with the organism. 
Virulence factors: A polysaccharide capsules 
Laboratory Diagnosis 
Specimen: vaginal and rectal swab 
Culture on blood agar medium, Streptococcus agalactiae is identified as: 
eo Beta hemolytic colonies on blood agar 
e Positive Hippurate hydrolysis test. 
e Positive CAMP test (Group B Streptococci produce a factor that increases § hemolysis of 

an S.aureus indicator strain) 
Prevention: To reduce the incidence of neonatal sepsis and meningitis: 

1. Screening for all pregnant women at 35 to 37 weeks’ gestation by doing vaginal and 
rectal cultures. If cultures are positive, then penicillin G (or ampicillin) should be 
administered intravenously at the time of delivery. 

2. Penicillin G (or ampicillin) administered intravenously at the time of delivery 
(intrapartum) without screening results in the following conditions: 

Prolonged (longer than 18 hours) rupture of membranes. 
Preterm labor (before 37 weeks of pregnancy). 
Fever at the time of labor (238°C). 
History of previous infant with invasive GBS disease. 
GBS bacteriuria during any trimester of the current pregnancy.

Listeria monocytogenes 
Listeria is non spore forming Gram positive bacilli that spread widely in nature; in GIT of 
animals, plants, water and soil and transmitted to humans by contact with animals or their 
feces. 
Human listeriosis is caused by Listeria monocytogenes as a zoonotic disease (sporadic 
cases or small epidemics). 
Listeria monocytogenes is a Gram positive, non- spore forming short bacilli characterized 
by tumbling motility at 22 °C but not at 37 °C. 
The organism can grow at 4°C (replication continues in refrigerated food) 
L.monocytogenes is a facultative intracellular parasite that infects and multiplies in 
epithelial cells, macrophages and other parenchymal cells. 

Mode of transmission: 
Animals are the reservoir of Lysteria monocytogenes 

Listeriosis is primarily a food-borne disease. It is transmitted to humans by ingestion of 
unpasteurized milk products, undercooked meat, and raw vegetables coleslaw 
(cabbage). 
It is transmitted to the fetus or a newborn as a result of transmission across the placenta 
or during delivery from infected mothers 
Contact with domestic farm animals and their feces is also an important source. 

Pathogenesis: 
Following ingestion, the bacteria appear in the colon and then can colonize the female 
genital tract. | 
From this location, they can infect the fetus if membranes rupture or infect the neonate 
during passage through the birth canal. 
Newborns infected at the time of delivery can have acute meningitis 1 to 4 weeks later. 
The bacteria reach the meninges via the bloodstream (bacteremia). 
The pathogenesis of Listeria depends on the organism’s ability to invade and survive 
within cells. 
Upon entering the cell, the organism produces listeriolysin, which allows it to escape from 
the phagosome into the cytoplasm, thereby escaping destruction in the phagosome. 
o Attachment of the organism to the cytoskeleton proteins of infected cells facilitates 

transfer of bacteria to adjacent cells. 
o Due to this mode of cell-cell transmission, the organisms are never extracellular and 

are not exposed to humoral antibacterial agents (e.g., complement, antibodies) or 
polymorphic nuclear leucocytes. 

o Immunity to L. monocytogenes is primarily cell mediated. 
The most significant virulence factor is listeriolysin O; it disrupts the phagocytic vacuole and 
is vital in cell-cell transmission of the organism 
Clinical presentations 
Neonatal Disease 
e Early onset disease: 

o ltis acquired trans-placental. 
o In utero acquired infection (granulomatosus infantiseptica): abscesses and 

granulomas in multiple organs and very frequently results in abortion, intra uterine 
fetal death or death after delivery. 

424



CHAPTER VII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

e Late onset disease: 
o Itis acquired at birth or soon after birth (hospital or attending personnel). 
o Exposure on vaginal delivery may result in meningitis or meningo-encephalitis with 

sepsis within 2 to 3 weeks. 

eo Immunosuppressed individuals and elderly: 
o Meningitis, endocarditis and sepsis. 
o [tis one of the leading causes of bacterial meningitis in patients with cancer and in 

renal transplant recipients. 
Laboratory Diagnosis 
Specimens: CSF or blood for blood culture (cold enrichment can be used) 
e Microscopic examination of a Gram stained film (CSF): shows intracellular Gram-positive 

bacilli occurring singly or in pairs. 
e [solation & Identification: 

o Culture on blood agar: beta hemolytic colonies. 
o The colonies can be identified by: Gram stained film, the characteristic tumbling 

motility and catalase test (catalase positive). 

Fungal meningitis 
Cryptococcous neoformans 
(cryptococcal meningitis) 

Antigenic Structure: 
lt is capsulated Oval, budding yeast that contains capsular polysaccharide according towhich 
it is divided into 5 serological types: A, B, C, D, and AD. 

Pathogenesis and clinical picture: 
e The organism is transmitted by inhalation of dust contaminated with bird droppings 

especially pigeons (No human-to-human transmission) 
e Birds are not infected 
e In most cases, phagocytes in the lungs limit spread of the organism resulting in 

asymptomatic infection, in some cases patient develops flu like symptoms or pneumonia 
e Unlike other forms of fungal meningitis, it affects healthy people as well as those who are 

sick or immunocompromised 
¢ In patients with reduced cell-mediated immunity (AIDS patients and transplant recipients) 

it spreads causing chronic meningitis or meningoencephalitis 

e The pathogenesis of Cryptococcus is also enhanced by the ability of the yeast to produce 
melanin, which appears to inhibit phagocytic killing mechanisms 

Laboratory Diagnosis: 
Specimen: CSF. 

e Direct film stained with India ink stain: budding capsulated yeast cells are seen 
e Culture: On Sabouraud’s dextrose agar (37° for 3 days), it gives whitish, highly mucoid 

colonies that furtherly identified by biochemical reactions 
eo Capsular antigen can be detected by the latex particle agglutination test in CSF or in blood 

in AIDS patient, this test is called the cryptococcal antigen test, often abbreviated as 
“crag.” (highly sensitive and specific).

SYSTEM 

Other fungi causing CNS infections 
Aspergillus species 

Aspergillus spp. is an opportunistic fungus. The most commonly isolated species is Aspergillus 
fumigatus. 

Morphology and characters 
e Aspergillus is saprophytic opportunistic fungi found in soil, plants and grows as a mold on 

decaying vegetable matter. 
e [t has branching septate hyphae and produces numerous spores on the tips of long 

conidiophores. 

Disease and risk factors 
eo Exposure to Aspergillus in the environment may cause disseminated disease in highly 

immunosuppressed individuals. 
e Aspergillus invasion of the CNS may result in brain abscess, cerebritis, and meningitis. 
e Aspergillus hyphae can invade directly into the vessel wall, which becomes weakened due 

to necrosis and polymorphonuclear infiltration, resulting in mycotic aneurysm formation. 
These patients may present with typical subarachnoid hemorrhage syndrome. 

e¢ The most important risk factors for brain infection are neutropenia and corticosteroid use. 
In addition, immunocompromised, organ transplant recipients, oncological, 
haematological, and AIDS patients also are at higher risk. 

Pathogenesis 
e The primary portal of entry for Aspergillosis organisms is the respiratory tract. 
e Infection reaches the brain by hematogenous dissemination from invasive lung infection, 

or by direct extension from the ear, paranasal sinuses, or mastoids in patients with 
localized invasive aspergillosis. 

e A very important virulence factor of Aspergillus spp. is its ability to produce mycotoxins 
which can damage and kill microglia, astrocytes, and neurons. These mycotoxins inhibit 
phagocytosis and increase conidial resistance to opsonization. 

Clinical manifestations 
e (Clinical presentation of Aspergillus infection of the CNS is non-specific and varies 

depending on the extension of the infection. 
e Patients may present with a variety of signs and symptoms, such as fevers, headaches, 

d lethargy, altered mental status, seizures, abnormal gait, dizziness, or focal neurological 
findings (e.g. hemiparesis, dysarthria). 

Laboratory Diagnosis 
Specimen: biopsy from CNS lesion. 
A. Microscopic examination: 

- With KOH: shows branching septate hyphae. 
- Fluorescence microscopy: Calcofluor stain for tissue biopsy. 

B. Isolation: Aspergillus species grow on most media. They're incubated for a few days at 
room temperature. Colonies are identified according to morphology of conidia. A. 
fumigatus produces a fluffy to granular, white to blue-green colony. 

C. Antigen testing (galactomannan) and molecular testing on cerebrospinal fluid. 

426



CHAPTER VIL: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

Treatment: Surgical resection of cerebral lesions in combination with antifungal therapy with 
voriconazole. 

Mucor and Rhizopus species 

These are saprophytic organisms found on bread, soil, air, and decaying vegetation, especially 
in tropical countries. 

Disease and risk factors 
e The major clinical form is rhinocerebral mucormycosis. Other forms of the disease are 

less frequent: cutaneous, pulmonary, disseminated, and gastrointestinal. 
e Rhinocerebral mucor mycosis is an acute invasive infection of the nasal cavity, paranasal 

sinuses, and orbit that involves the facial structures and extends into the CNS, involving 
the meninges and brain. 

e Most of these infections occur in patients with metabolic acidosis, particularly diabetic 
ketoacidosis, and those with hematologic malignancies. 

Pathogenesis 
e Infection is caused by the inhalation of spores present in the air. 
e The disease is characterized by fungal hyphal invasion of blood vessels resulting in 

thrombosis and infarction of the nasal, paranasal sinus, orbital, and cerebral tissues. 

Clinical manifestations 
The most common symptoms include headache, especially in the face, periorbital oedema, 
often with loss of vision, fever, diplopia, rhinitis, and decreased mental function. 

Laboratory Diagnosis 
A. Direct examination of biopsy specimens, and surgical resection specimens mounted in 

KOH with calcofluor white reveals the broad aseptate hyphae. 
B. Isolation: These fungi grow rapidly on laboratory media, producing abundant cottony 

colonies. Identification is based on the sporangial structures. 

Treatment 

Amphotericin B remains the first-line therapy, often supplemented by surgical debridement 
and immune reconstitution.

Aseptic meningitis andp oliomyelitis 
Aseptic meningitis is also known as viral meningitis, to indicate that no bacteria are detected 
in CSF. {t is the most common form of meningitis except in children younger than 10 years of 
age, where bacterial meningitis is more common. 
Signs and symptoms: 

- Usually a milder disease than either bacterial or fungal meningitis. 
- Although signs and symptoms may be the same, death from viral meningitis is rare. 
- Some meningitis viruses also cause skin rashes, sore throats, and colds.

Control 
e No available antiviral drugs

Poliomyelitis 
Polioviruses 
Properties and classification: 
o Three serologic {antigenic) types based on different antigenic determinants on the outer 

capsid proteins. 
¢ Reaction to physical and chemical agents inactivated by heating at 55°C for 

30 minutes, proper pasteurization, chlorination (0.1 ppm for drinking water) 

429 



CHAPTER Vill: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

Transmission and epidemiology: 

e Fecal-oral transmission 

e Natural infection occurs only in man. 
e Because of the success of the vaccine, poliomyelitis caused by naturally occurring “wild- 

type” virus has been eradicated from the Western hemisphere. 
Pathogenesis and immunity: 

¢ Source of infection: Apparent and subclinical patients. 
e Primary multiplication takes place in oropharynx and intestinal mucosa (alimentary 

phase). The virus then multiplies in the tonsils and Peyer's patches (lymphatic phase). 
Most infections stop at this level (asymptomatic or subclinical). 

e Only in about 1% of infections, viremia occurs {viremic phase), the immune system may 
stop the disease here by forming neutralizing antibodies resulting in abortive 
poliomyelitis. 

e Nervous system may be invaded through the blood stream (neurologic phase) the virus 
may reach the meninges (aseptic meningitis or non- paralytic polio) or poliovirus spread 
along axons of peripheral nerves to the central nervous system (anterior horn cells of 
the spinal cord) and it multiplies in the anterior horn cells resulting in paralytic 
poliomyelitis. 

e Neural spread may occur if a child with an unapparent infection had 
tonsillectomy, the virus in the oropharynx gains access to cut nerve fibers. 

e The immune response consists of both intestinal IgA and humoral IgG to the specific 
serotype. Infection provides lifelong type-specific immunity. 

e [Immunity to one serotype does not confer significant immunity to the other serotypes. 
The virus replicates in the oropharyngeal and intestinal mucosa 

No further spread of virus Virus spreads to spinal cord > Paralysis 
Asymptomatic or mild and bran 
Febrile undifferentiated illness 

Fig. 8.3: Pathogenesis of poliovirus infection

SYSTEM 

Clinical findings: 
The range of clinical findings of poliovirus infection includes: 

1. Asymptomatic or subclinical infection. 
2. Abortive poliomyelitis: 

e [ncubation period: 7-14 days 
e Fever, malaise, drowsiness, headache, nausea, vomiting, constipation and sore 

throat 
e The most common presentation that recovers in few days 

3. Non-paralytic poliomyelitis (aseptic meningitis): 
e [n 5% of cases presenting with fever, headache and neck stiffness 
e Lasts 2-10 days usually followed by complete recovery 

4. Paralytic poliomyelitis develops in 1% of cases in the form of: 
e Flaccid paralysis 
e Fatal respiratory paralysis in brainstem involvement 

- 

e Flaccid paralysis is the predominating complaint with maximal recovery within 6 
months leaving minor residual paralysis that may lead to post poliomyelitis muscle 
atrophy. 

Laboratory diagnosis: 
1) isolation of the virus: 

e Virus can be isolated from throat swabs (early in disease course), rectal swabs and 
stools (longer period). 

e Specimens are inoculated onto primary monkey kidney tissue culture (MKTC) or 
human cells. 

e¢ CPE occurs within 3-6 days. 
e The isolated virus is serotyped by Nt test using specific antisera. 

2) Serology: 
e Paired serum specimens are taken to show a rise in specific antibody titer detected 

by ELISA or neutralization test 
Treatment: 

oe No specific antiviral therapy is available. 
eo Symptomatic relief and respiratory support if needed 
Prevention and control: 
1. Preventing fecal contamination of drinking water and food plays an important role 
2. Avoid operations in children with fever. 
3. Gamma globulin can protect against paralytic disease if given before infection or before 

nervous lesions. 
Vaccines: There are two vaccines available. 
e Oral polio vaccine (OPV live, attenuated, Sabin, 1957) 
e |nactivated poliovirus vaccine (IPV, Salk, 1954) 
Sabin vaccine (OPV) 
e Live attenuated magnesium chloride stabilized containing the three poliovirus strains 

grown in human or monkey diploid cell cultures. 
e |tcan be kept at 4°C for a year. 
Advantages of OPV: 

1. Administered easily by oral drops in three doses at 2, 4, 6 months of age and a booster 
dose at 4-6 years of age. 

431 



CHAPTER VIII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

. Effectiveness (The normal response rate to OPV is close to 100%). 

. No need for repeated booster doses 

. Lifelong immunity 

., OPV multiplies in the intestine, producing not only IgM and IgG antibodies in the blood 
but also secretory IgA in the intestine. Thus, protecting GIT from reinfection and the CNS 
from subsequent invasion by the wild virus 

6. The attenuated virus is shed in the stools of immunized children. Persons being exposed 
to fecal material of vaccinated children may be exposed and infected with vaccine virus 
leading to development of herd immunity. 

Disadvantages of OPV 
1. Can mutate to more virulent strain 
2. Unsafe administration for immunodeficient patients 
3. Not given to feverish children to avoid the interference by another enterovirus virus 

infecting the gut at the same time, thus blocking both the vaccine virus replication and 
immunity. 

4. Must be kept refrigerated to avoid heat inactivation of the live virus 
Salk vaccine (IPV) 
e itis formalin killed vaccine containing the 3 poliovirus strains, grown in human or monkey 

cell cultures. 
e Itis administered IM in 3 doses at 2, 4, 6 months 
e No local intestinal immunity (no IgA) 

Advantages of IPV 
1. Cannot undergo genetic virulence 
2. Safe administration in immunodeficient patients 

Disadvantages of IPV 
1. Requires parenteral administration 
2. Need for booster vaccine for lifelong immunity. Confers immunity only after 4 boosters 
3. Lack of induction of local intestinal immunity (no secretory IgA) 
4. Expensive

Drug Therapy of Meningitis 

Meningitis is inflammation of meninges. it can be classified according to causative agent 
into bacterial “Purulent” and non—bacterial “Aseptic” meningitis.

Drug-induced Aseptic Meningitis “DIAM” 
- DAMS is non-infectious meningitis. 

-1t is a rare but challenging diagnosis, most commonly reported with nonsteroidal anti- 
inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), antibiotics such as Trimethoprim /sulfamethoxazole (TMP- 

SMX), and also with biologic drugs such as intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG). 

- DIAM is poorly understood and may be related to hypersensitivity reactions, usually 

associated with autoimmune diseases e.g., SLE, Rheumatoid A .... 

- Treated by Immediate cessation of offending medications with excellent prognosis. 

Pathogenesis of Bacterial Meningitis and Site of Drug Action (figure 8.6....) 
Bacteria enters the CNS through the cribriform plate or blood stream — Bacteria colonize in 
the subarachnoid space = intense inflammation of the meninges = release of inflammatory 
cytokines and chemokines leading to f intracranial pressure (ICP) by: 

e¢ Endothelial damage = TM permeability of BBB—> 1° ICP (Vasogenic edema). 
eo tNeutrophils entry to subarachnoid space and produce inflammatory exudate —= 

obstruction of CSF flow, > 1° ICP (Interstitial edema) 
e swelling of cellular elements of the brain through the release of toxic factors from the 

bacteria and neutrophils => 1 ICP (Cytotoxic edema). 

-Also, inflammatory cytokines lead to vasculitis disrupting blood flow = ischemic damage.

Accordingly, drug therapy of bacterial meningitis consists of: 

Antibiotic therapy (Empirical and then Pathogen-specific) 
Anti-inflammatory therapy (Corticosteroids as Dexamethasone) 
ICP lowering agent (Mannitol; Osmotic diuretic) 
Anticonvulsant drug (e.g., Diazepam if patient has seizure) 

Practical guidelines for Management of Bacterial Meningitis (IDSA%, 2017) 
Once Suspected Must begin treatment and take CSF samples and do other investigations to 
confirm diagnosis. Steps of managements: 

, Initial Management (Immediate Dexamethasone + Empiric Antibiotic) 
Il. Pathogen-Specific Antibiotic Therapy for Bacterial Meningitis 
IH. Chemoprophylaxis for Bacterial Meningitis.

I. Initial Management of Suspected Bacterial Meningitis: “IDSA Rule” 
immediate DexamethaSone + Antibiotic (ABCDEF) 

Antibiotic, Broad, Parenteral, Cidal, Combination, 2-3 Days, Empirical, Full dose 

Dexamethasone 
- Dexamethasone {0.15 mg/kg/6h, 1V) is given 10 - 20 min before or concomitantly with 
antibiotic (All patients > 6 weeks) for first 2-4 days. 
- Mech. & Value: |, inflammatory response & J, cytokine-mediated neurotoxic 2 |, Brain 
edema & |, Neuronal damage -» Improve outcomes = J, Neurological sequelae, |, hearing 
loss and |, mortality in S. pneumoniae. (Cochrane Review, 2015). 
Empirical Antibiotic “AB” 
AB used must be broad spectrum, bactericidal and based on the patient's age, risk factors and 
clinical features. 
AB should be administrated in full dose intravenously “IV”. 
Intrathecal injection may be used in Nosocomial meningitis or if patients do not respond to 
IV especially with AB poorly cross BBB e.g. Gentamycin, Vancomycin BUT NOT with penicillins 
or cephalosporines = neurotoxicity & seizures. 
NEVER postpone AB even 1h (It takes 12 - 24 h to change CSF) and continue Empirical AB for 
the first 2-3 days till CSF results. 
If CSF results revealed aseptic meningitis => antibiotics can be discontinued. 
If CSF results confirm bacterial meningitis and determine pathogen — Pathogen-specific 
Antibiotic therapy should be administered for 7-21 days.

VIRAL ENCEPHALITIS 

Encephalitis is an infection of the brain parenchyma predominantly caused by viruses. 
Sometimes both the brain and the meninges are involved, a condition called 
meningoencephalitis. 
Life threatening disease with cerebral dysfunction 

Etiology and pathogenesis 
1. Herpes simplex viruses: 

eo HSVs are the most common cause of severe sporadic meningoencephalitis. 
e They cause disease following primary infection of infants (usually HSV-2) or due to 

reactivation of HSV-1 following immunosuppression in adults. 
¢ The infection is localized to the temporal andfrontal lobes so computed tomography 

(CT) scans and electroencephalograms (EEGs) are diagnostic aids. 

Varicella zoster virus. 

Rabies virus. 

Arboviruses:West Nile Virus (WNV) is the most common arboviral cause of 
encephalitis in the United States. St. Louis encephalitis virus, the LaCrosse strain of 
California encephalitis virus, and Eastern and Western equine encephalitis viruses (EEE 
and WEE, respectively) can also cause encephalitis. 

Cytomegalovirus (CMV) causes encephalitis primarily in immunocompromised 
individuals such as AIDS patients and those receiving immunosuppressive drugs. 

Epstein-Barr virus (EBV): as a rare complication of infectious mononucleosis. 

HIV: 
e HIV encephalitis refers to cognitive impairment resulting from cerebral infection by the 

human immunodeficiency virus. 
e HIV virus gain entrance to the nervous system within monocytes which easily traverse 

the blood brain barrier 
e Infected monocytes then fuse with resident microglial cells, resulting in activation with 

release of cytokines and other molecules which result in secondary brain injury. 
e |n addition, HIV associated glycoproteins may interfere with synaptic activity. 
e HIV also infects astrocytes and this might be linked to development of HIV associated 

dementia. 
All viruses reach brain via blood except HSV type 1 and rabies reach brain via axons 

8. Post-infectious encephalitis: 
e A milder form of encephalitis which follows immunization or infection by several 

weeks. 
e Immunopathological mechanisms perhaps involving autoimmune reactions to CNS 

antigens are thought to be responsible as in cases of subacute sclerosing 
panencephalitis. 

e Influenza B, VZV, and adenovirus have been implicated in conjunction with salicylates 
in Reye’s syndrome. 

43S 



CHAPTER Vil: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

Clinical manifestations: 
e The main clinical manifestations of encephalitis include fever, headache, and altered 

mental status, as well as seizures and focal neurologic deficits. 
e The clinical distinction between meningitis and encephalitis is based upon the state of 

brain function. 
e Seizures can be seen with meningitis alone and should not be considered as definitive 

evidence of encephalitis. 
e Personality change, hallucinations, and aphasia suggests temporal lobe localization 

in HSV encephalitis. 
e Manifestations of Rabies encephalitis (furious rabies and dumb rabies), with coma and 

death as the final common pathway in both forms 
Laboratory diagnosis: 
1. CSF analysis: variable findings 

oe A mild elevation in CSF lymphocytes can be seen along with an elevation of protein 
and a normal glucose. 

e A normal CSF pattern can also be seen in encephalitis. 
2. PCR-based testing of CSF: 

e Commonly used to determine a specific etiology, such as with HSV and VZV. 
e The single most useful test in diagnosis of HSV encephalitis. 

3. Detection of antibodies in the spinal fluid or blood. 
4, Radiographic findings can be useful as well. In particular, in HSV encephalitis, 

temporal lobe abnormalities are frequently seen. 
5. Diagnosis of Rabies 
Treatment: 
e [Intravenous acyclovir is the treatment of choice for HSV-1, HSV-2, and VZV 

encephalitis. 
eo Ganciclovir is used for CMV encephalitis 
e¢ There is no antiviral therapy for arboviral or rabies encephalitis. 
Prevention: 
eo There is no vaccine for HSV-1, HSV-2, and WNV. 
e¢ To reduce the transmission of HSV-2 to the neonate, pregnant women with active lesions 

late in pregnancy should receive acyclovir and should be considered for Cesarean section. 
e¢ Pre- and post-exposure prophylaxis of Rabies 
Outcomes: 
e¢ Outcomes are variable depending on etiology. 
e WNV is associated with significant morbidity and mortality. 
e Mortality of HSV encephalitis before acyclovir was 60% to 70%, and with treatment 

approximately 30% 
e Cognitive disability, seizures, and motor deficits are common sequelae seen 

among survivors

Arboviruses that cause Encephalitis 

West Nile Virus 
Epidemiology: 
e [tis endemic in Africa but has caused encephalitis in areas of Europe and Asia as well 

Transmission: 
Reservoir: Wild birds are the main reservoir of this virus 
Vector: transmitted by mosquitoes, especially Culex species 
Humans are dead-end hosts. 
Transmission of the virus via solid organ transplants or blood transfusion has also 
occurred. 

Clinical presentation: 
» 80% of infections are asymptomatic. 
» fever and headache without encephalitis occur in about 20% of infections. 
» The most important clinical picture is encephalitis with or without signs of meningitis 

in only 1% of infections. 
Laboratory diagnosis: 
1. WNV encephalitis is often diagnosed by finding WNV specific IgM in the spinal fluid. 
2. Isolation of the virus from brain tissue, blood, or spinal fluid or by detection of antibodies 

in spinal fluid or blood. 
3. PCR-based assays are also available. 
Prevention 
1. No vaccine is available. 
2. To prevent blood-borne transmission, screening of donated blood for the presence of 

WNYV using nucleic acid probes specific for the virus. 
3. General measures for vector control and personal protection. 
Treatment: 
1. No available antiviral therapy. 
2. Hospitalization and intravenous fluids, respiratory support, prevention of secondary 

infections, and good nursing care. 

RABIES ENCEPHALITIS 

Rabies virus 
e¢ The virus has a broad host range. 
e It can infect all mammais causing lethal form of acute encephalitis. 

Transmission: 
1. The bite of rabid wild animal as dogs, cats and bats 

Rabid animal manifests aggressive biting behavior induced by viral encephalitis. 
2. Non bite exposure: 

a- Exposure to aerosols of bat secretions containing rabies virus 
b- Corneal transplantation taken from an infected cadaver.

Fig. 8.6: Pathogenesis of rabies 

Pathogenesis: 
1. The virus multiplies at the site of inoculation, infects the sensory neurons and moves by 

axonal transport to the central nervous system. 
2. During its transport within the nerve the virus is sheltered from the immune system and 

little if any immune response occurred. 
3. Within the CNS encephalitis develops with neuronal death. Infected neurons contain 

Negri bodies (intracytoplasmic eosinophilic IB) which are pathognomonic of rabies. 
4. The virus multiplies in the CNS then migrates through peripheral nerves to salivary glands 

and other organs. 
S. From salivary gland, it enters saliva to be transmitted by the bite. 
6. Viral replication is restricted almost exclusively to neuronal tissue and there is no viremia.
