Clinical Picture 
e [.P ranges from 2-16 weeks. 
e |.P is shorter with bites in children and in head and neck and langer with bites in the 

limbs. 
e The patient exhibits a prodrome of non- specific symptoms as fever, anorexia headache 

and changes of sensation at the site of the bite. After the prodromal 
e phase rabies manifests as either of two forms 

1. Furious rabies: occurs in 80% of cases. The brain is involved. There is excitement, 
delirium, seizures and hydrophobia that is aversion to swallowing water 
because of painful spasms of pharyngeal muscles. 

2. Paralytic rabies: The spinal cord is primarily involved and an ascending paralysis 
occurs. 
Death invariably occurs following both forms. 

Laboratory Diagnosis: 
>» There are no laboratory tests to diagnose rabies infection in humans after exposure to 

bite and before the onset of symptoms. 
» Several tests are necessary to diagnose rabies ante-mortem (before death) in humans; 

no single test is sufficient 

1. Direct Virus Demonstration: 
Specimen: Saliva, serum, spinal fluid, and skin biopsies of hair follicles at the nape of the 
neck 

a. DIF for direct detection of antigen. 
b. RT-PCR for detection of viral RNA. 

2. Viral Isolation: 
e Suckling mice or on human diploid cell culture. 
e |nfection of mice results in encephalitis and death. 
e Brain tissue of the dead animal is examined for detection of Negri bodies and virus 

antigen. 
3. Serology: 

Serum and CSF antibodies can be detected late during disease progression by using 
immunofluorescence or neutralization tests. 

4. Postmortem diagnosis: 
L/M examination for detection of Negri IB in the brain or spinal cord. 

Management of Rabies: 
1. Immediate washing of bite wounds and scratches with generous amounts of soap and 

water. 
2. Wound should not be sutured immediately. Sutures should be placed after local 

infiltration of antiserum. 
3. Tetanus immunization should also be considered. 
4. Initiation of rabies post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) according to vaccination status: 

A. Unvaccinated Persons or Vaccination was administered more than 5 years 
previously or incomplete: 
e A combination of RIG and vaccine is recommended for both bite and non- bite 

exposures, regardless of the time interval between exposure and initiation of PEP. 

443



CHAPTER VI: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

e Discontinue PEP if the animal that caused the exposure was captured and proved 
to be non-rabid by laboratory diagnostic testing (i.e., the direct fluorescent 
antibody test) 
Although no human-to-human non-transplant transmission has been 
documented clearly, anyone coming into contact with CSF, saliva, or mucus 
membranes of a person suspected of having rabies should receive complete 
prophylaxis. 

Rabies immunoglobulin: 
Human or equine antirabies serum is given once iM on day 0. 
Much as possible of the HRIG should be infiltrated into and around the wound, 
and the remaining is given IM frequently in the gluteals. 
If HRIG was not administered on day 0, it can be administered up to and including 
day 7. 

Rabies Vaccine: 
Four doses (1-ml), given IM, usually in deltoid on days (0, 3, 7, 14,) 

B. Completed the schedule of rabies vaccine within last 5 years: 
Two doses (1- ml) of the vaccine are given IM on days (0, 3).

Fig. 8.8: Management of Rabies 
Rabies Vaccines: 
¢ Alf vaccines for human use contain inactivated rabies virus grown in cells derived 

from human, monkeys or duck embryo. 
é Human diploid cell vaccine (HDCV) is regarded as the gold standard for rabies 

vaccination. 
Pre-exposure vaccination: 

eo Three doses of the vaccine given on day 0,7,21 or 28. 
e Few of the people should consider rabies pre-exposure vaccinations: 
1. High-risk individuals (veterinary doctors, animal handlers and laboratory workers) 
2. Travelers to a country where rabies is widespread must consider pre-exposure 

prophylaxis if: 
o They will visit remote areas where medical care is difficult to obtain (for example, 

hiking through remote villages where dogs are common). 
o Their stay is longer than 1 month.

Prevention and Control 
1. Eliminate stray animals as much as possible and vaccinate domestic dogs and cats. 
2. Avoid wild and unknown domestic animals. 
3. Follow pre-exposure and post-exposure recommendations. 
Slow CNS infections 
1. Measles virus: 

e Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE). 
e Develops 1 to 10 years after the initial infection. 

2. JC virus: 
 Arare syndrome known as progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML). 
e (Occurs in immunocompromised patients, notably AIDS patients and patients on 

immunosuppressive therapy. 
e |nfection with JC virus occurs early in life and remains latent until the immune 

system is compromised. 
Diagnosis: Detection of viral DNA in the CSF by PCR. 

e There is no antiviral drug therapy and no vaccine. 
3. Prions: 

e Kuru and variant Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (vCID): 
e Progressive brain disorders involving typical spongiform histological changes in 

affected areas of the brain. 
Mode of Transmission: 

They are transmitted by inoculation or ingestion of prion-containing beef. 
e Corneal transplant and transplant of dura mater of an infected cadaver can also transmit 

the infection. 
Pathogenesis: 
e The disease is caused by a misfolded form of a normal prion protein, thereby altering 

the function of the protein, and leading to death of neurons. 
eo The disease is confined to the CNS where death of neurons causes vacuolation and 

spongiform changes in the brain. 
Clinical presentation: 
e Gradual onset, slowly progressive dementia, and myoclonus. 
e Severe disease resulting in coma and death. 
Laboratory Diagnosis: 
¢ Brain biopsy shows spongiform changes. 
e No serologic tests are available, and Prions cannot be grown in culture. 
Prevention and Treatment: 
e Infected animals should be slaughtered, and their carcasses destroyed. 
e No available treatment or vaccine is present.

PARASTES AFFECTING CENTRAL NERVOUS SYSTEM 

PATHOGENIC FREE- LIVING AMOEBA 
INTRODUCTION 
e They are normal inhabitants of soil and water (fresh or stagnant lakes) where they feed 

on bacteria. 
e Few members have the ability to become facultative parasites when an opportunity to 

enter a vertebrate exists. 
There is no direct human to human infection. 
They have no relationship with poor sanitation. 
They have no definitive host, reservoir host or insect vector. 
The most important potentially pathogenic free-living amoebae are: Naegleria fowleri 
and Acanthamoeba species. 

1. NAEGLERIA FOWLERI 
(PRIMARY AMOEBIC MENINGOENCEPHALITIS (PAM)) 

MORPHOLOGY: 
Naegleria fowleri has two stages (three forms) in the life cycle: 

1. Trophozoite stage: it occurs in two forms (biphasic): 
a. Amoeboid form (Fig. 8.9a): 

It is the dividing form. 
It measures 7-20 u. 
Has a broad end and a narrow tapering end. 
Moves by the help of broad pseudopodium (lobopodia). 
Cytoplasm is differentiated into a clear ectoplasm and a granular endoplasm that 
contains contractile vacuoles. 

eo The presence of large central nuclear karyosome surrounded by a halo is the 
characteristic feature of the trophozoite. 

It is the only form found in man in C.S.F. or in the tissues. 

Fig. 8.9: Trophozoite Forms of Naegleria fowleri 
a) Amoeboid form, b) Flagellated form 

446



CHAPTER VII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

b. Flagellated form (Fig. 8.9b): 
e [tis a temporary non dividing, non-feeding form of the parasite formed when the 

amoeboid stage is exposed to change in water ionization. 
e [tis pear shaped having at least two flagella with the same nuclear character. 
e Changes to amoeboid form in unfavorable conditions before encystation (this 

change occurs in water at 27-37°C). 

2. Cyst stage (Fig. 8.10): 
e |tis the resistant stage of the parasite. 
e¢ |[t measures 7-15 um. 

e Spherical with single nucleus and a thick double wall. 
Cyst is not seen in clinical specimens because the course of the 
disease is so rapid and fatal before the trophozoite can encyst. 

LIFE CYCLE (Fig. 8.11): Fig. 8.10: Cyst stage 
1. Naegleria fowleri is a free-living thermo-tolerant amoebo-flagellate parasite. 
2. The amoeboid trophozoite is the feeding and multiplying form. 
3. When food is scarce, the amoeboid trophozoite transforms to the biflagellate 

trophozoite. 
4. The biflagellate is a transient non-multiplying, rapidly motile form; it moves rapidly 

searching for food. 
5. Interconversion between the two forms of the trophozoite occurs frequently in 

response to change in the environmental conditions (mainly, presence of food). When 
the conditions are unfavourable, e.g. in cold environment, the biflagellate form 
transforms into the amoeboid form before changing into cyst.

HABITAT: 
Outside the human body they are free living in warm aquatic environment in fresh or brackish 
water (lakes, rivers, and ponds). On infection of man, they inhabit the brain. 
MODE OF INFECTION AND INFECTIVE STAGE: 
Man acquires the infection by swimming or diving in warm fresh water lakes contaminated 
with Naegleria fowleri amoeboid trophozoites (infective stage) (Fig. 8.9) 
EPIDEMIOLOGY: Worldwide distribution. 
PATHOGENESIS AND CLINICAL PICTURE: 
e [t is an acute suppurative rapidly fatal and fulminant infection of the brain meninges 

resulting from invasion of the brain tissue by the pathogenic free-living amoeba, Naegleria 
fowler. 

eo Termed as Primary as CNS is primarily involved to be differentiated from secondary 
amoebic encephalitis caused by Entamoeba histolytica. 

e The organisms penetrate the nasal mucosa, migrate via the olfactory nerve =» cribriform 
plate = to enter the meninges, where they multiply in the peri-vascular spaces causing 
suppurative meningoencephalitis (PAM). 

e Naegleria fowleri injures nerve cells by two mechanisms: 
1. Trogocytosis, which is ingestion of the cytoplasm through a feeding cup. 
2. They produce contact dependent lysis: acute necrotising meningoencephalitis. 

Clinically The condition is commonly seen in children and young adults (immuno-competent) 
with a history of swimming, diving, or jumping into warm fresh water, stagnant ponds, or 
lakes, or inadequately maintained public heated swimming pools. 
e Incubation period: 2-5 days. 
e Onset: Acute onset. 
e Symptoms and signs: 

1. There is alteration of taste and smell. 
2. High fever with sensory and motor changes. 
3. Increase of the intracranial tension = severe headache, neck rigidity. 
4. Coma and death within few days. 

PROGNOSIS: is very poor, most cases are fatal. 
DIAGNOSIS: 
a. Clinical diagnosis: 

History of contact with water and clinical picture. 
The physical signs are similar to those of bacterial meningitis, but the change of taste and 
smell may help for differentiation. 

b. Laboratory diagnosis: 
1. Complete blood picture: shows polymorph nuclear (PMN) leucocytosis (differentiates 

it from viral encephalitis). 
2. CSF examination: Cerebrospinal fluid obtained by lumbar puncture: 

i. Naked eye appearance: cloudy, purulent, or bloody and under tension. 
ii. Increased protein content and decreased or normal glucose content. 

iii. Microscopic examination: 
¢ Bacteriologically free. 
e Increased PMNs and lymphocytes. 
e Detection of the motile amoeboid trophozoites by examination of Wet mount 
and stained smear. 

448

Ih 



CHAPTER VIII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

iv. Culture: Non-nutrient agar seeded with gram negative bacilli (Escherichia coli). 
Diagnostic stage: Amoeboid trophozoite (Fig. 8.9a). 

3. Detection of the parasite's DNA by PCR. 

TREATMENT: 
1. Treatment must be given early enough to save the patient's life. 
2. AMPHOTERICIN B is given by slow intravenous infusion in a dose of 1 mg /kg /day for 9 

days &1mg intrathecally on alternate days until CSF is clear. 

PREVENTION and CONTROL. 

1. Hold your nose shut or use a nose clip if you jump or dive into the fresh water lake or 
pond. 

2. Always keep your head above water in hot springs and other naturally hot water. 

2. ACANTHAMOEBA SPECIES 
(GRANULOMATOUS AMOEBIC ENCEPHALITIS (GAE)) 

MORPHOLOGY: 
Two forms exist: 
1. Amoeboid trophozoite (Fig. 8.12): 

e Size: 22-30 um. 

e Shape: irregular with spine-like pseudopodia. 
e Content: single nucleus with large central karyosome 

and a single contractile vacule. 

2. Cyst (Figure 8.13): 
e Rounded. 

e About 18 pin diameter. 
e With double wall with a slightly wrinkled outer cyst wall 

and a polyhedral inner cyst wall. 
e Cysts are dispersed by wind to contaminate the 

environment. 

Fig. 8.13: Cyst 
LIFE CYCLE: 
The life cycle of Acanthamoeba consists of two stages: 
1. An actively feeding and dividing trophozoite. 
2. Adormant cyst. 
Cyst formation occurs under unfavourable environmental conditions such as: food 
deprivation, desiccation, and changes in temperature and pH. 
HABITAT: 
It is present in all types of environment throughout the world: stagnant water, dust and soil. 
It may contaminate contact lenses, and dialysis machines. In humans, the parasite might be 
encountered in the CNS, skin, eye, lung and genitourinary tract. 

449 



CHAPTER VIII: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

MODE OF INFECTION AND INFECTIVE STAGE: 
Both cysts and trophozoites ( infective stages ) can enter the body through various means. 

eo The eye causing severe keratitis in otherwise healthy individuals particularly contact 
lens users 

e The nasal passages to the lower respiratory tract 
e Ulcerated or broken skin 

When it enters the respiratory system or through the skin, it can invade the central 
nervous system by hematogenous dissemination causing granulomatous amebic 
encephalitis (GAE) or disseminated disease in individual with compromised immune 
system 

PATHOGENESIS AND CLINICAL PICTURE: 

¢ Acanthamoeba is an opportunistic pathogen, occurs in immunosuppressed chronically ill 
patients with AIDS, tuberculosis, radiotherapy and chemotherapy or to immuno- 
privileged sites (away from the immune system e.g. cornea). 

e Tissue invasion is slow leading to subacute or chronic iliness and marked granulomatous 
tissue reaction. 

A. GRANULOMATOUS AMOEBIC ENCEPHALITIS (GAE): 
e [tis a rare subacute or chronic granulomatous infection of the CNS that occurs in 

immunosupressed patients where the parasites reach the brain via blood. 
eo The amoebae produce patchy lesions of granulomatous reaction formed of areas 

of necrosis, haemorrhages and focal oedema. 

e The amoebae produce injuries by: 
a. Contact mediated lysis of the cells. 
b. Ingesting cytoplasm of the cells. 

Clinically: 
e¢ The onset is gradual with I.P weeks or months (chronic course). 
oe Symptoms and signs of increased intracranial tension: 

1. Severe frontal headache. 
2. High fever, nausea, and vomiting. 
3. Neck rigidity. 
4. Sensory and motor changes and mental confusion. 

e Skin lesions may associate CNS manifestations and may include ulcers, nodules, 
or subcutaneous abscesses. 

B. AMOEBIC KERATITIS AND CORNEAL ULCER (Fig. 8.14): 
e |t occurs in healthy individuals. 
e Patients who use contact lenses are at special risk. 
e The condition starts following trauma to the cornea 

with the use of lenses and subsequent exposure to 
water contaminated with trophozoites or cysts. 

Clinically: 
1. It is a chronic progressive ulcerative disease of the eye. 
2. Painful ulcer of the cornea, congested conjunctiva, 

complete corneal opacity and blindness, Fig. 8.14: Amoebic keratitis 
3. Disease is increasing due to frequent use of contact lenses. 

A450 



CHAPTER VIN: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

C. DISSEMINATED GRANULOMATOUS AMOEBIC DISEASE: 
1. It begins as a nodule followed by ulceration. 
2. It may be accompanied by granulomatous lesions in the eye, ear, genitourinary 

tract, and internal organs with or without associating GAE. 
DIAGNOSIS: 
A. Clinical diagnosis: History and clinical picture. 
B. Laboratory diagnosis: 

1. GAE: 
a. Brain biopsy is the most reliable diagnostic approach in GAE. Trophozoites and cysts 

can be detected in stained smears of biopsy specimens. 
b. Culture: Acanthamoeba trophozoites and cysts can be cultured from clinical samples 

(CSF, biopsy tissue) on non-nutrient agar with a Page’s saline and Escherichia coli 
overlay. 

c. CSF examination: 
i. Microscopic examination: 
e Bacteriologically free. 
e [ncreased lymphocytes. 
e Examination of wet mount and stained smear for trophozoites and cysts which 

are rarely seen in CSF. 
il. Increased protein content and decreased or normal glucose content. 

2. Acanthamoeba keratitis: Examination of corneal scrapings for trophozoites and 
cysts by wet mount, stains, histopathologic examination and culture. Contact 
lenses and their solutions can also be cultured. 

3. Disseminated disease: Skin biopsy. 
TREATMENT: 
GAE: Antimicrobial combinations are used: Sulfadiazine (50 mg/kg IV) + Ketoconazole or 
Fluconazole + Pentamidine + Flucytosine. 
Amoebic keratitis: Topical chlorhexidine (0.02%) and polyhexamethylene biguanide (0.02%), 
either alone or in combination. If the epithelium alone is involved, debridement may be 
sufficient for cure. Penetrating keratoplasty may be necessary in cases that do not respond to 
medical treatment. 
PREVENTION AND CONTROL OF AMOEBIC KERATITIS : 

1. Proper cleaning of contact lenses. 
2. No wearing of lenses while swimming.

LARVAE OF CESTODES 

A- SPIROMETRA MANSONI 

Spirometra mansoni is an adult cestode that inhabits the small intestine of dogs and cats. 
Its larval stage is called Sparagnum. 
The sparganum has a solid, elongated, glistening white body, few mms up to 30 cm in length 
with invaginated scolex (Fig. 8.15). 
Intermediate hosts of Spirometra mansoni: 
1st intermediate host: Cyclops (water flea). 
2" intermediate host: Frogs, snakes, birds, mammals and occasionally man, containing the 

larva known as Sparganum mansoni. Human acts as a blind intermediate host. 

SPARGANOSIS 
ETIOLOGY: 
It is a disease resulting from Infection of human tissues by the larval stage of the 
pseudophyllidian tapeworm Spirometra mansoni calied Sparganum mansoni. 
Man acts as a blind end intermediate host. 
EPIDEMIOLOGY: 
The disease is prevalent in South East Asia, North and South America, Australia, and East 
Africa. 
MODE OF INFECTION AND INFECTIVE STAGE: 
1. Drinking water containing Cyclops infected with procercoid larva. 
2. Eating raw or undercooked frogs or birds containing the 
Sparganum which migrates from the frogs or birds’ tissues 
through the intestinal mucosa to various organs. 

3. Applying the infected flesh of frogs as poultices to inflamed 
tissues as skin or eye. The Sparganum larva will migrate to 
the hot inflamed tissues. The use of frog tissues as pouitices Fig. 8.15: Sparangum of 
is common in some countries as China. Spirometra mansoni 

PATHOGENESIS AND CLINICAL PICTURE (Fig. 8.16): 
Wherever it settles, the Sparganum larva induces chronic inflammatory reaction ending in a 
swelling formation. Symptoms depend on the tissue invaded and the number of larvae. The 
common sites are subcutaneous tissues, eye, lymph glands and CNS. 

1. Skin: migratory subcutaneous swelling which may become painful and tender to form 
abscesses from which the Sparganum is discharged. 

2. Eye: conjunctivitis and peri-orbital oedema. 

452



CHAPTER Vil: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

Fig. 8.16: Skin and eye manifestations of Sparganosis 

3. Patient may suffer from urticaria, oedema, fever, pain and eosinophilia. 
4. Cerebral sparganosis: symptoms include seizures, fatigue, confusion, headaches, 

memory loss, coma, fever, paresthesias, hemiparesis, motor weakness, and other central 
nervous system symptoms. The infection generally involves the cerebral hemispheres, 
especially the frontoparietal lobes, and in some cases extends to the cerebellum. The 
disease may appear as a massive cerebral hemorrhage. 

DIAGNOSIS: 
a. History of the use of frogs or drinking infected water. 
b. CT and MRI are helpful in confirming the site of the lesion. 
c. Definitive diagnosis is achieved by biopsy and removal of the swelling which cabne 

identified as a Sparganum larva from its morphology. 

TREATMENT: 
Surgical removal of the nodule containing the Sparganum. 

PREVENTION AND CONTROL: 
1. Destruction of Cyclops by boiling or filtering the water before drinking. 
2. Stopping the use of raw frogs as poultices. 
3. Proper cooking of frogs and birds ‘meat. 

B- CYSTICERCOSIS 

ETIOLOGY: 
It is a disease resulting from invasion of human tissues by the larval stage of Taenia solium 
(Cysticercus cellulosae). Man acts as a blind intermediate host. 

EPIDEMIOLOGY: 

e The parasite is common in Central and South America, most of sub-Saharan Africa, 
South Asia and China. 

e Araund 50000 people die annually from cysticercosis, mostly due to 
neurocysticercosis (NCC). The presence of cysticerci in the brain represents the most 

453 



CHAPTER Viil: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 

SYSTEM 

frequent parasitic infection of the human nervous system and one of the most 
common causes of adult-onset epilepsy throughout the world. 

MODE OF INFECTION AND INFECTIVE STAGE: 
Ingestion of Taenia solium eggs (infective stage) (Fig. 8.17) by one 
of the following ways: 
1. Heteroinfection: ingestion of eggs of Taenia solium in foods or 

drinks contaminated by stools of persons infected by the adult 
worm. 

2. Autoinfection of a person having the adult worms in his 
intestine. Fig. 8.17: Taenia solium eggs 
a) External autoinfection: hand to mouth infection. 
b) internal autoinfection or retrofection (due to reverse peristalsis): some detached 

segments may ascend by antiperistaitic movements to the stomach then descend 
again to the intestine where onchospheres hatch and penetrate intestinal mucosa 
then to the circulation to various tissues where they change into Cysticercus 
cellulosae. 

PATHOGENESIS AND CLINICAL PICTURE: 
While the most common sites for infection are the skeletal muscles and brain,L ysiicerd may 
be found in almost any tissue of the body, including the | ls sookx 
eye, lungs and subcutaneous tissues. Developing cysts Owl 4 
elicit a host inflammatory response resulting in fibrous y 
encapsulation. The cysticercus {Fig. 8.18) is an ovoid, milky 
white bladder with the head invaginated into the bladder. 
The bladder worms measure 5 mm {ong by 8 to 10 mm 
wide, except in brain or submeningeal spaces where they Cysticercus cellulose 
can have a volume of about 60 ml. Fig. 8.18: Cysticercus cellulosae 

The effect produced depends on the location of cysticerci in the body. 
1. Muscular cysticercosis: cysticerci located in skeletal muscle result in nodule formation, 

but they are usually asymptomatic. 
2. Ocular cysticercosis: subconjunctival swelling (Fig. 8.19) or may cause damage to the 

retina, iris or choroids resulting in visual disturbances. 
3. Neurocysticercosis (Fig. 8.20a):Neurocysticercosis may be parenchymal (occurring in 

the brain substance, the most common location) or extraparenchymal {occurring in the 
meninges, the ventricles, the basilar cisterns, or the subarachnoid space of the brain or 
spinal cord). 

Clinical manifestations of neurocysticercosis vary with the locations of the lesions, 
the number of parasites, and the host's immune response. When the larvae are found in 
the brain, symptoms can result from actual larval invasion of the brain tissue and/or 
death of the organism, which stimulates tissue reactions around the larvae. Symptoms 
vary from mild manifestations as headache, vomiting, vertigo to convulsions and 
epilepsy, with epilepsy being the most common manifestation. Other less common 
manifestations include abnormal behaviour, transient paresis, intermittent obstructive 
hydrocephalus, disequilibrium, meningoencephalitis and cranial nerve abnormalities. 

454 



CHAPTER VIM: INFECTIONS AFFECTING MUSCLE TONE & NERVOUS 
SYSTEM 

Fig. 8.1 9: Ocular cysticercosis Fig. 8.20 a) Neurocysticercosis, b) Hole-with-dot sign. 

DIAGNOSIS: 
e Diagnosis often requires both CNS imaging and serological testing. 
e Cysticercosis should be suspected in any patient living in an endemic area who develops 

nervous manifestations, subcutaneous nodules and myositis especially if the patient 
harbors adult T. solium in the intestine. 

e Antibodies detection tests in serum and antigen detection tests in CSF. 
e [maging techniques: CT scan is superior to MRI for demonstrating small calcifications. The 

presence of cystic lesions demonstrating the scolex is considered pathognomonic of 
neurocysticercosis. The scolex is visualized by both CT and MRI as a bright nodule within 
the cyst producing the “hole-with-dot” appearance (Fig.8.20 b). 

e Stool Examination: For eggs of T. solium as taeniasis and neurocysticercosis coexist in 10- 
15% of patients with neurocysticercosis. 

e CSF analysis: Abnormal findings are found in about 50% of cases if cysticerci are found in 
the ventricles or basal cisterns. These findings include high eosinophils, elevated protein 
and normal or low glucose levels. 

e Definitive diagnosis can be reached by surgical removal of the cysts to be examined 
histologically for the presence of suckers and hooks on the scolex of cysticerci. 

TREATMENT: 
1. Albendazole is recommended in a dose of 15 mg/kg/day in divided doses with meals, for 

8 days is very effective especially for active brain cysts. OR 
2. Praziquantel 50mg/kg/day in three divided doses for 15 days. 
3. Simultaneous administration of steroids is essential to relieve the intense local 

inflammatory reactions of the antigenic material of dead larvae. 
4. Antiepileptic drugs for patients with seizures. 
5. Surgical treatment, if possible, usedi n removal of orbital, cisternal and ventricular cysts. 
PREVENTION AND CONTROL: 
1. Early detection and treatment of patients harboring adult T. solium. 
2. Environmental sanitation 
3. Personal hygiene to avoid autoinfection. 
4. Protection of food or drink against possible contamination by human faeces containing 

the eggs.

SYSTEM 

OTHER IMPORTANT PARASITIC INFECTIONS OF THE CNS: 

1) Toxoplasma gondii 
a. In immunocompromised hosts { toxoplasma encephalitis }: 

Toxoplasma gondii (T. gondii) can cause cerebral infection in immunocompromised 
patients especially AIDS patients (opportunistic infection). 
Toxoplasma encephalitis occurs through one of the following route: 

1. Reactivation of latent cysts that had occurred earlier in life. 
2. Eating undercooked meat of animals harboring tissue cysts . 
3. Consuming food or water contaminated with cat feces harboring oocysts 
4. Blood transfusion or organ transplantation 

Symptoms: Fever, headache, vomiting, confusion, speech difficulty, behavioural 
changes, and seizures. 
Signs: Focal neurological deficits, ataxia, cranial nerve deficits, visual field defects, 
altered mental status and seizures. 
Investigations: 
a. CD4+ count: variable; typically, <0.1 x 10°/L {<100 cells/microlitre). 
b. CT and MRI: Single or multiple ring-enhancing lesions. 
c. Toxoplasma serology (IgG); Positive. However, antibody detection may be 

unreliable in immunodeficient individuals. 
d. CSF analysis: Mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, elevated protein and normal or low 

glucose. 
e. Detection of T. gondii DNA in CSF by PCR. 
f. Brain biopsy: Indicated in patients who fail to respand ta empiric treatment. 

Treatment: 
Pyrimethamine + sulfadiazine + folinic acid for life or until immune reconstitution. 

b. Congenital toxoplasmosis: 
Toxoplasmosis can be transmitted from mother to fetus if the pregnant woman is 
infected for the first-time during pregnancy. The severity of fetal damage is higher 
when infection occurs early in pregnancy. When infection occurs in the third trimester 
of pregnancy, the majority of the newborns are asymptomatic. However, they may 
develop CNS manifestations as learning disabilities, mental retardation or seizures as 
well as chorioretinitis later in life. When congenital infection is severe, newborns may 
have a typical triad of microcephaly or hydrocephalus, intracerebral calcifications and 
chorioretinitis. 

Detection of IgG in the newborn is not a marker of infection since maternal IgG crosses 
the placenta from mother to fetus. On the other hand, specific IgM and IgA are 
manufactured by the fetus in response to active infection and are therefore important 
markers of infection in the newborn. 

4) Hydatid disease of the CNS caused by Echinococcus granulosus. 

3) Cerebral malaria caused by Plasmodium falciparum. 

4) Sleeping sickness caused by African Trypanosomes. 

5) Amoebic brain abscess caused by Entamoeba histolytica.

PATHOLOGICAL ASPECTS OF CNS INFECTIONS 

As previously discussed, infectious agents may reach the nervous system through blood, 
trauma, local extension from osteomyelitis, infected sinuses or teeth or through peripheral 
nerves 

Epidural and subdural infections: 
>» These spaces can be involved with bacterial or fungal infections, usually as a consequence 

of direct local spread. 
>» Epidural abscesses arise from an adjacent focus of infection, such as sinusitis or 

osteomyelitis. 
» Infections of the skull or air sinuses may also spread to the subdural space, producing 

subdural empyema. Large subdural empyema may produce a mass effect. In addition, 
thrombophlebitis may develop in the bridging veins that cross the subdural space, 
resulting in venous occlusion and infarction of the brain. 

Meningitis 

Meningitis is an inflammatory process involvitnhge leptomeninges, if the infection spreads 
into the underlying brain it is termed meningoencephalitis. 
Causes

General clinical features of meningitis: 
Fever. 

Headache, irritability. 
Photophobia. 
Cranial nerve deficit. 
Altered mental status. 
Neck stiffness. 

A- Acute pyogenic meningitis (bacterial meningitis): 
Etiologic agents and mode of infection: refer to the Microbiology section. 
Gross: 
e A suppurative exudate is evident within the leptomeninges over the surface of the 

brain (Fig. 8.21a). 
e The meningeal vessels are engorged and prominent. 
eo CSF is mixed with pus. 
Microscopic (Figure 8.21b): 
e Meningeal vessels are congested and dilated. 
e The subarachnoid space is filled with fibrin, neutrophils, pus cells and histiocytes. 
CSF changes: Table 8.8.

with damage of cranial nerves; mainly lil, IV, Vi leading to diplopia, squint and ptosis. 

e Encephalitis.

B- Viral (Aseptic) Meningitis: 
- common viral etiology of meningitis: refer to microbiology section 
- The clinical course is less fulminant than in pyogenic meningitis.

may be discrete white granules scattered over the leptomeninges. 

- Chronic tuberculous meningitis is a cause of arachnoid fibrosis, which may produce 
hydrocephalus. 

» Fungal Meningitis: 
- Usually found in immunodeficient patients. 
- Please refer to microbiology section.

CNS Parenchymal Infections 

A- Brain Abscess 

Brain abscesses are mostly caused by bacterial infections. 

Brain abscesses can arise by: 
1. Direct implantation (traumatic). 
2. Local extension: e.g. suppurative mastoiditis or acute suppurative otitis media. 
3. Hematogenous (systemic pyemia): 

a- Acute bacterial endocarditis. 
b- Suppurative lung diseases via vertebral system of veins. 

Morphology: 
e Acute abscess appears as an irregular area of liquefactive necrosis surrounded by a zone 

of congestion. 
e Chronic abscess has a smooth fibrous tissue capsule surrounded by a zone of reactive 

gliosis. 
¢ Ring-enhancing lesions on CT/MRI. 

Clinical: 
e Progressive focal deficits. 
e Increased ICT and progressive herniation which may be fatal. 
e Abscess rupture can lead to ventriculitis, meningitis and venous sinus thrombosis. 

Fig. 8.22: Brain abscess: gross appearance and radiograph with ring pattern of 
enhancement 

B- Viral Encephalitis 

e [t is infection of the brain that is invariably associated with meningeal inflammation 
(meningoencephalitis). 

e The most characteristic histologic features are perivascular and parenchymal mononuclear 
cell infiltrates, microglial nodules and neuronophagia. 

e Certain viruses may form inclusion bodies e.g. Rabies has characteristic Negri bodies. 
eo Numerous viruses can affect the CNS (refer to microbiology and virology section). Only 

those occasionally encountered in surgical neuropathology practice are going to be 
shortly discussed:

SYSTEM 

(a) Human immunodeficiency viru1s  (HIV-): 
» Up to 60% of patients with AIDS develop neurologic symptoms and 

neuropathologic changes have been observed in 80 - 90% of cases. 
» HIV can have direct effects on the nervous system as well as setting the stage for 

opportunistic infections or tumors (primary CNS lymphoma) that can involve the 
nervous system. 

» Patterns of direct injury to the brain include: 
o HIV -1 aseptic meningitis. 
o HIV-1 meningoencephalitis (AIDS dementia). 
o Vacuolar myelopathy. 

(b) Progressive Multifocal Leukoencephalopathy (PML): 

» Caused by JC virus, a papovavirus. 
» Virus infects oligodendrocytes and astracytes, resulting in demyelination. 
» Occurs in immunosuppressed patients e.g. Transplant, HIV. 
» Irregular, ill-defined destruction of white matter. 
» Poor prognosis. 

(c) Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) Encephalitis: 

Herpes Simplex Virus type 1: 
» More common in children and young adults. 
» inferior and medial regions of the temporal lobes and orbital gyri of the frontal 

lobes, causing alterations in mood, memory, and behavior. 
» Cowdry type A intranuclear viral inclusion bodies can be found in both neurons and 

glia. 
Herpes Simplex Virus type 2: 
» Presents as meningitis in adults. 
» Presents as diffuse encephalitis in neonates born by vaginal delivery to mothers 

with active HSV genital infections.

C- Fungal Encephalitis 

e Candida albicans, mucormycosis, aspergillus fumigatus, and Cryptococcus neoformans are 
the most common fungi that can cause encephalitis. 

e Although most fungi invade the brain by hematogenous dissemination, direct extension 
from sinuses may occur, particularly with mucormycosis, in diabetics. 

e Parenchymal invasion, in the form of granulomas or abscesses. 
e Aspergillus causes a widespread septic hemorrhagic infarction due to invasion of blood 

vessel walls with subsequent thrombosis.

e Unlike in other infectious diseases, the agent in prion diseases is an abnormal form of a 
cellular protein.

NEURODEGENERATIVE DISEASES 

DEFINITION: Degenerative diseases of the CNS are disorders characterized by the cellular 
degeneration of subsets of neurons that typically are related by function, rather than by 
physical location in the brain. Many of these disorders are associated with the accumulation 
of abnormal proteins. 

They can be classified in a number of different ways including via the clinical presentation, 
type of protein that accumulates, pathological processes and underlying genetic 
abnormalities. 

Types of Neurodegenerative diseases based on their clinical presentation and area of the 

nervous system affected (Fig. 9.1): 
1- Diseases that affect the cerebral cortical neurons result in loss of memory, language, 

insight, and planning, all components of dementia (Alzheimer’s disease and 
frontotemporal dementia). 

2- Those that affect the neurons of the basal ganglia result in movement disorders. 
{Parkinson's disease and Huntington chorea). 

3- Those that affect the cerebellum result in ataxia (Spinocerebellar ataxias). 
4- Those that affect motor neurons result in weakness (Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis).

CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE& DEMYLANATING DISEASES 
TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

Dementia is defined as the development of memory impairment and other cognitive deficits 
severe enough to decrease the affected person’s capacity to function at the previous level 
despite a normal level of consciousness. 

Etiology of dementia 
1) Primary i.e. neurodegenerative disease (Idiopathic; genetic defect): commonest cause 

e.g. Alzheimer’s disease. 
2) Secondary: e.g. 

a. Vascular: Multiple cerebral infarcts. 
b. Nutritional, metabolic, Toxins or Drugs e.g. Alcoholism. 
c. Benign tumors, Inflammatory/infective disorders. 

In the management of patients, it is particularly important that reversible causes such 
as obstructive hydrocephalus, vitamin deficiency, hypothyroidism, neurosyphilis and 
HIV encephalitis are excluded. 

|. Alzheimer’s Disease (AD) 
INCIDENCE: 
- AD is the most common cause of dementia in the elderly population. 
- Age is animportant risk factor for AD; the incidence is about 3% in persons 65 to 74 years 

old, 19% in those 75 to 84 years old, and 47% in those older than 84 years. 
- Most cases of AD are sporadic, but at least 5% to 10% are familial with early onset. 
PATHOGENESIS: The crucial event is the accumulation of beta amyloid peptide, or AB, in 
the brain over time, initiating a chain of events that result in AD. 

(1) AB is created when the transmembrane protein amyloid precursor protein (APP) is 
sequentially cleaved by the enzymes B-amyloid converting enzyme (BACE) (Beta 
secretase) and y-secretase. When APP is cleaved by a-secretase and y-secretase, it 
liberates a different peptide that is non- pathogenic. Mutations in APP on chromosome 
21 or in enzymes responsible for its degradation lead to familial AD by increasing the 
rate at which AB is generated. 

(2) These deposits are toxic to the neurons as they affect neurotransmission and finally lead 
to neuronal death. 

(3) The presence of AB also leads to hyper-phosphorylation of the neuronal microtubule 
binding protein tau. This causes tau to redistribute from axons into dendrites and cell 
bodies, where it aggregates into tangles, which also contribute to neuronal dysfunction 
and cell death. 
Amyloid deposits—> glutamate — activates NMDA receptors — ca**influx into 
neurons— cell apoptosis; exocitotoxity. 
Progressive loss of cholinergic nicotinic neurons involved in cognition & memory (will be 
explained in pharma lecture). 

(4) The other major genetic risk factor is a variant of apolipoprotein E called £4 (ApoE4). 
Each ApoE4 allele that is present increases the risk of AD by approximately 4-fold and 
also appears to lower the age of onset. How ApoE4 influences AB accumulation is still 
unknown.

A. This schematic illustrates a hypothetical mechanism for the formation of senile plaques 
(SPs) from soluble AB peptides produced inside celis and secreted into the extracellular space. 
Amyloidogenic Al may encounter fibril-inducing cofactors and go on to form A fibrils to 
deposit in SPs. SPs are surrounded by reactive astrocytes and microglial cells, which secrete 
cytokines that may contribute to the toxicity of the SPs. 
B. This schematic illustrates a hypothetical mechanism leading to the conversion of normal 
tau overlying microtubules into paired helical filaments that are generated in neuronal 
perikarya and their processes leading to formation of neurofibrillary tangles. The death of 
affected neurons would release tau and increase the levels of tau in the CSF of patients with 
AD.

Demvelinating disorders 

Any disease of the nervous system in which the myelin sheath of neurons is damaged. 
This damage impairs the conduction of signals in the affected nerves. 

Classified into: 
1- Myelinoclastic diseases: Normal and healthy myelin is destroyed by a toxic, chemical 

or autoimmune substance, e.g. multiple sclerosis. 
2- Leukodystrophic diseases (Dysmyelinating diseases): Myelin is abnormal and 

degenerates, e.g. Krabbe disease. 

Clinical picture: 
Ocular paralysis 
Impaired muscle coordination 
Weakness (muscle) 
Loss of sensation 
Impaired vision 
Unsteady gait 
Spastic paraparesis 

Incontinence 

Hearing problems 
Speech problems 

PRIMARY DISEASES OF MYELINE 

Within the CNS, axons are tightly ensheathed by myelin, an electrical insulator that allows 
rapid propagation of neural impulses. 
Myelin consists of multiple layers of highly specialized, closely apposed plasma membranes 
that are assembled by oligodendrocytes. 
Although myelinated axons are present in all areas of the brain, they are the dominant 
component in the white matter; therefore, most diseases of myelin are primarily white 
matter disorders. 
Most diseases of CNS myelin do not involve the peripheral nerves to any significant extent, 
and vice versa. 

In general, diseases involving myelin are separated into two broad groups:

Multiple Sclerosis 

MS is an autoimmune demyelinating disorder characterized
 by distinct episodes of 

neurologic deficits, separated in time, attributable to white m
atter lesions that are separated 

in space. 

it is the most common of the demyelinating disorders, having
 a prevalence of 

approximately 1 per 1000 persons in most of the United Stat
es and Europe. 

- The disease may become clinically apparent at any age, alt
hough onset in childhood or 

after age 50 is relatively rare. 

- Women are affected twice as often as men. 

- In most patients with MS, the illness shows relapsing and remitting episodes of 

neurologic impairment. The frequency of relapses tends t
o decrease during the course 

of the illness, but a steady neurologic deterioration
 is characteristic in a subset of 

patients. 

PATHOGENESIS (Fig. 9.8) 
- Autoimmune disease caused by a combination of enviro

nmental and genetic factors that 

result in a loss of tolerance to self-proteins (in this case, mye
lin antigens) 

_ The nature of the initiating agent suggested to be an infec
tious agent, remains uncertain. 

_ Genetic factors related to HLA-DR and genes encoding recep
tors for the cytokines IL-2 and 

IL-7 which are known to control the activation and regulation
 of T cell- mediated immune 

responses. The evidence of genetic factor relation to MS: The
 disease risk is 15-fold higher 

when the disease is present in a first-degree relative, and the concordance rate for 

monozygotic twins is approximately 25%, with a much lower
 rate for dizygotic twins. 

- A central role for CD4+ T cells has been suggested. There
 is also evidence for important 

contributions from CD8+ T cells and B cells. 

470 



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE & 
DEMYLINATING DISEASES, 

TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

- While MS is characterized by the presence of demyelination out of proportion to axonal 
loss, some injury to axons does occur. Toxic effects of lymphocytes, macrophages, and 
their secreted molecules have been implicated in initiating the process of axonal injury, 
sometimes even leading to neuronal death. 

(T helper cells) and CD8+ 
{cytotoxic T cells), recognize myelin as foreign and attack it, initiating a damaging, 

inflammatory response. 
MORPHOLOGY 
Gross: is primarily a white matter disease with affected areas showing multiple, well- 
circumscribed, slightly depressed, glassy-appearing, gray-tan, irregularly shaped lesions 
termed plaques. These commonly arise near the ventricles. They also are frequent in the optic 
nerves and chiasm, brain stem, ascending and descending fiber tracts, cerebellum, and spinal 
cord. 

Microscopic: 
- The lesions have sharply defined borders at the microscopic level. 
- In an active plaque there is evidence of ongoing myelin breakdown with abundant 

macrophages containing myelin debris. Lymphocytes and macrophages are present, 
mostly as perivascular cuffs. 

- Small active lesions often are centered on small veins. 
- Axons are relatively preserved but may be reduced in number.

CLINICAL FEATURES 
- The course of MS is variable, but commonly 

there are multiple relapses followed by 
episodes of remission; typically, recovery 
during remissions is not complete. 

- As a consequence, over time there is usually a 
gradual, often stepwise, accumulation of 
neurologic deficits. 

- Imaging studies with MRI brain are mandatory. 
- The CSF in patients with MS shows a mildly 

elevated protein level with an increased 
proportion of immunoglobulin. When the 
immunoglobulin is examined further, 
oligoclonal bands usually are identified.

DRUG THERAPY OF NEURODEGENERATIVE AND MYELINE DISEASE 
ALZHEIMER'’S DISEASE (AD) 
e [tis the most common cause of dementia in the elderly. 
e It is a neurodegenerative disorder characterized by impairment of memory & cognitive 

function together with behavioral changes (psychosis, depression). 
e [t may lead to a completely vegetative state and early death. 
e Dementia is defined impairment of cognitive functions including memory in a fully 

conscious patient, severe enough to interfere with daily activity. 
Pathophysiology of AD 
1) Accumulation of senile plaques (-amyloid accumulations), and formation of numerous 

neurofibrillary tangles. 
2) “cholinergic hypothesis” loss of cortical neurans, particularly cholinergic neurons (J{,Ach). 
3) Sustained activation of glutamate I> NMDA-receptors = 1° excessive Ca influx = 

Excitotoxicity = Neuronal Dysfunction and cell death or apoptosis. 
Aim of therapy in AD 
4) improve cholinergic transmission within the CNS by using cholinomimetics (to 1 Ach) 
5) Prevent excitotoxic actions resulting from overstimulation of NMDA-glutamate receptors 

by using NMDA receptor antagonist in selected areas of the brain (Block NMDA-R). 

N.B.: Pharmacologic intervention for Alzheimer’s disease is only palliative and provides 
modest short-term benefit. None of the available therapeutic agents alter the underlying 
neurodegenerative process.

Drugs therapy of Multiple Sclerosis 

Multiple sclerosis is an autoimmune inflammatory demyelinating disease of the CNS. 
The course of MS is variable. It is usually chronic, relapsing, or progressive disease that may 
span 10 to 20 years.

Management of MS 

l Treatment of Acute Episode (Exacebations): Corticosteroids 
Il. Disease Modifying Drugs (DMDs) 
Il. Symptomatic Treatment 

Many drugs are used to manage symptoms of MS such as spasticity, constipation, bladder 
dysfunction, and depression. 

Fampridine, an oral potassium channel blocker, improves walking speeds in patients with 
MS. It is FDA approved for this use.

The major target of these medications is to modify the immune response through 
inhibition of white blood cell-mediated inflammatory processes that eventually lead to 
myelin sheath damage and decreased axonal communication between cells. 
1. Interferon Bla and interferon B1b: “Parenteral” 

~- Intramuscular or subcutaneous injection. (Weekly or every other day) 
- Itis a first line therapy. 
- The immunomodulatory effects of interferon help to diminish the inflammatory 
responses that lead to demyelination of the axon sheaths. 

Adverse effects: 
a. Flu-like symptoms. 
b. Depression. 
¢. Local injection site reactions. 
d. Hepatic enzyme increases (ALT, AST). 

2. Fingolimod: “Oral daily” 
- It is the first oral DMDs approved from FDA. 
- It binds and blocks S1P; receptors and alters lymphocyte migration, resulting in 
fewer lymphocytes in the CNS and “T-cell Sequestration in the lymph nodes” (Fig. 
9.15) 

Adverse effects: 
a. First-dose bradycardia (used with caution in cardiac patients). 
b. Increased risk of infection. 
¢. Macular edema {used with caution in DM).

It is a monoclonal antibody against “a4B1-integrin” — J, BBB invasion by T-cells. 
It is indicated for Progressive MS in patients who have failed first line therapies.

Drugs Used In Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis 

- Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS) is characterized by progressive degeneration of motor 
neurons, resulting in the inability to initiate or control muscle movement. 

- ALS, also known aslou Gehrig's disease, is a specific disease that causes the 
death of neurons and is considered the most common "motor neuron disease”. 

The clinical picture of ALS: 
Muscle fasciculation, hypotonia, muscle wasting, paradoxical reflexes stiff muscles, muscle 
twitching, and gradually worsening weakness due to muscles decreasing in size. This results 
in difficulty in speaking, swallowing, and eventually breathing. 
The cause of ALS: Giotto fy Gluamats Regulation

Cerebral Edema 

Definition: Cerebral edema is the accumulation of excess fluid within the brain parenchyma. 

Pathogenesis: There are two underlying mechanisms for the development of cerebral edema 

that often occur together particularly when there is generalized injury. 

Vasogenic edema Cytotoxic edema 

« Occurs when the integrity of An increase in intracellular 
the normal blood-brain barrier fluid secondary to neuronal 
is disrupted with subsequent and glial cell membrane injury. 
increase in the vascular . e.g. generalized 

permeability. hypoxic/ischemic insult or with 
» Can be either localized exposure to some toxins. 
adjacent to inflammation or 
tumors or generaliz 

Gross: 
» The edematous brain is softer than 

normal and often appears to "overfill" 
the cranial vault. 

» In generalized edema the gyri are 
flattened as a result of compression of 
the expanding brain by the dura 
mater and inner surface of the skull, 
the intervening sulci are narrowed, 
and the ventricular cavities are compressed (Fig. 9.18). 

Fig.9.18: Cerebral edema 

Hypoxia- Ischemia- Infarction 

General concepts: 
¢ The brain may be deprived of oxygen by two general mechanisms: 

- Functional hypoxia, caused by a low partial pressure of oxygen (e.g., high altitude), 

impaired oxygen-carrying capacity (e.g., severe anemia, carbon monoxide poisoning), 

or inhibition of oxygen use by tissue (e.g., cyanide poisoning). 

- Ischemia due to tissue hypoperfusion, which can be caused by hypotension, vascular 

obstruction, or both. 
e Transient ischemic attack (TIA) is due to small platelet thrombi or atheroemboli and is 

characteristically reversible, with symptoms lasting less <24 hours. 

eo Stroke is the clinical term for acute-onset neurologic deficits resulting from hemorrhagic 

or obstructive vascular lesions. Stroke can be due to infarction (85% of all stroke cases) or 

hemorrhage (15% of all stroke cases). 

482



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE & 
DEMYLINATING DISEASES, 

TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

eo Multi-infarct Dementia: Cerebral atherosclerosis Presents as a slow, stepwise, decrease in 
mental function Important to distinguish from Alzheimer and other causes of dementia 

Patterns of Ischemia: Global (diffuse ischemic encephalopathy) or Focal: 
A- Global Cerebral ischemia 

Cause: Widespread ischemic-hypoxic injury can occur in the setting of severe systemic 
hypotension, usually when systolic pressures fall below 50 mm Hg, as in cardiac arrest, 
shock, and severe hypotension. 

B- Focal Cerebral Ischemia 

Cerebral arterial occlusion leads first to focal ischemia and then to infarction in the 
distribution of the compromised vessel. The size, location, and shape of the infarct and 
the extent of tissue damage that results may be modified by collateral blood flow. 

» Arterial occlusion: occlusion of the cerebral arteries by either thrombi or emboli is the 
most common cause of cerebral infarction. 

Thrombotic occlusion of the carotid or cerebral arteries is most frequently the result 
of atherosclerosis, and rarely, from arteritis of the cranial arteries. 
Embolic arterial occlusion is commonly derived from the heart, most often from mural 
thrombosis complicating myocardial infarction, from atrial fibrillation and 
endocarditis. 
Lacunar Infarcts are small infarcts in deep non-cortical white matter or the brainstem 
that result from the occlusion of penetrating branches of cerebral arteries. The term 
“lacune” refers to the crescent- or moon-shaped tiny stroke that results from 
occlusion of these vessels. Cerebral small vessel disease is most commonly related to 
aging and hypertension. 

» Venous occlusion: Venous infarction in the brain is an infrequent phenomenon due to 
good communications of the cerebral venous drainage. However, 

» in cancer, due to increased predisposition to thrombosis, superior sagittal thrombosis 
may occur leading to bilateral, parasagittal, multiple haemorrhagic infarcts. 

» Non-occlusive causes: Compression of the cerebral arteries from outside such as occurs 
during herniation may cause cerebral infarction. Mechanism of watershed (border zone) 
cerebral infarction in hypoxic encephalopathy has already been explained above. 

483

Intracranial Non-traumatic (Spontaneous) Hemorrhage 

Intraparenchymal hemorrhages are associated with (1) hypertension and other diseases 
leading to vascular wall injury e.g. cerebral amyloid angiopathy (2) structural lesions such 
as arteriovenous and cavernous malformations, and (3) tumors. 
Subarachnoid hemorrhages Spontaneous subarachnoid hemorrhage is usually caused by a 
structural vascular abnormality, commonly aneurysm but also occur with arteriovenous 
malformation (AVM). 

Note: Subdural or epidural hemorrhages usually are associated with trauma.

» Affecting the blood vessels around circle of Willis (Fig. 9.20). 
» Usually multiple saccular aneurysms and if they rupture, they cause subarachnoid

» Although referred to as congenital, they are not present at birth and develop over

» Associated conditions: Polycystic kidney disease (autosomal dominant).

e Congenital defects of the media at the arterial bifurcations. 
eo Focal destruction of the internal elastic membrane resulting from hemodynamic 

alterations. 
eo Abnormalities in specific collagen subsets.

» Due to subacute bacterial endocarditis or polyarteritis nodosa. 
» It occurs when the wall of an artery is weakened by mild infection. 
» Commonest site is branches of middle cerebral artery.

» |t occurs due to cerebral atherosclerosis.

» Sites: Internal carotid and basilar arteries. 
4. Charcot-Bouchard Microaneurysms: 

» Hypertension causes hyaline arteriolar sclerosis of the deep penetrating arteries and 
arterioles that supply the basal ganglia, the hemispheric white matter, and the brain 
stem. Affected arteriolar walls are weakened and are more vulnerable to rupture (slit 
hemorrhage). 

» In some instances, minute aneurysms form in vessels less than 300 um in diameter 
called Charcot-Bouchard microaneurysms. 

Effects of Cerebral Aneurysms: 
1. Increased intracranial tension. 
2. Rupture with subsequent hemorrhage. 
3. Hydrocephalus. 
4. Thrombosis and calcification.

TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

CNS Tumors 

- Tumors of the central nervous system (CNS) can be broadly grouped into two major 
categories: primary tumors and secondary (metastatic) tumors. Primary CNS tumors are 
the second commonest form of cancer in infants and children under the age of 15 years, 
exceeded in frequency only by leukemia. 

- The CNS tumors can also be grouped according to location and their corresponding 
incidence by age. In adults, approximately 70% of all brain tumors occur supratentorially 
(i.e., within the cerebral hemispheres or coverings); these include, in order of frequency, 
metastases followed by gliomas, meningiomas, and schwannomas. In children, 
approximately 70% of brain tumors occur infratentorially (i.e., within the cerebellum or 
brainstem) and are neuroepithelial in origin, the most common being pilocytic astrocytoma, 
medulloblastoma, and ependymoma in decreasing order of frequency. Spinal cord tumors 
constitute about 15% of all primary CNS tumors and include schwannomas, meningiomas, 
and gliomas (ependymoma, astrocytoma) in decreasing order of frequency. 

Traditionally, the classification of nervous system tumors has relied on the histological 
appearance of a particular neoplasm supplemented by immunohistochemical features; 
however, tumor classification and patient management are increasingly dependent on the 
evaluation and incorporation of molecular and genetic information. The classification 
widely used for CNS tumors is based on the World Health Organization (WHO) 
Classification of Tumors of the Central Nervous System. 

Gliomas include Diffuse astrocytic and oligodendroglial tumors (Diffuse gliomas), Other 
(localized) astrocytic tumors e.g. pilocytic astrocytoma, and ependymal tumors. 

The term neuroepithelial tumors include gliomas, choroid plexus tumors, neuronal and 
mixed neuronal glial tumors, embryonal tumors, and tumors of pineal region. 

The brain tumors are assigned into one of four grades (1 to 4) according to the WHO 
system of CNS classification. Yet, the anatomic site of the neoplasm can influence outcome 
independent of histologic classification due to local effects {e.g., a benign meningioma may 
cause cardiorespiratory arrest from compression of the medulla) or non-resectability {(e.g., 
brain stem gliomas). 

Metastatic spread of brain tumors to other regions of the body is extremely rare, but the 
brain is not comparably protected against spread of distant tumors mainly carcinomas. 

Radiologic examination of brain tumors is an indispensable part of the assessment with 
expert evaluation of the exact site, size, relation to anatomic structures and possible 
extensions, morphology as solid/ cystic, associated edema and mass effect, and 
enhancement pattern following radioactive material administration. 

488 



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 

DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

|- Diffuse Gliomas 
Diffuse gliomas are characterized by diffuse infiltration of normal brain tissue by tumor 
cells beyond the tumor core in distinction to other types of neuroepithelial neoplasms that 
are more circumscribed (e.g., ependymoma, pilocytic astrocytoma). Complete surgical 
resection of diffuse gliomas is consequently not possible in most circumstance. 
Diffuse gliomas are associated with a variety of acquired mutations, which cluster in several 
important pathways. Mutations that alter the enzymatic activity of two isoforms of the 
metabolic enzyme isocitrate dehydrogenase (IDH1 and IDH2) are common in diffuse 
glioma. As a result, immunostaining for the mutated form of IDH1 has become an 
important diagnostic and prognostic tool. 

oligodendroglial tumors | | Diffuse astrocytic tumors 

A. Diffuse Astrocytic Tumors 

- Diffuse astrocytic tumors account for about 80% of adult gliomas. 
- They are most frequent in the fourth through the sixth decades of life. 
- They are usually found in the cerebral hemispheres. 
- The most common presenting signs and symptoms are seizures, headaches, and 

focal neurologic deficits related to the anatomic site of involvement. 
They show a spectrum of histologic differentiation that correlates well with clinical 
course and outcome. On the basis of histologic features, they are stratified into three 
groups with increasingly grim prognosis as the grade increases: 
- Diffuse astrocytoma, WHO Grade 2. 
- Astrocytoma, WHO Grade 3. 
- and astrocytoma/glioblastoma 4. 

Prognosis: Diffuse astrocytomas can be static for several years, but at some point, they 
progress; the mean survival is more than 5 years. Eventually, patients suffer rapid 
clinical deterioration that is correlated with the appearance of anaplastic features and 
more rapid tumor growth with progression to anaplastic astrocytoma and astrocytoma 
grade 4. Other patients present with glioblastoma from the start (primary ' 
glioblastoma). Once the histologic features of glioblastoma appear, the prognosis is 
very poor; with treatment (resection, radiotherapy, and chemotherapy), the median 
survival is only 15 months. 

Gross: (Fig. 9.24) Diffuse astrocytomas are poorly defined, gray, infiltrative tumors that 
expand and distort the invaded brain without forming a discrete mass. Infiltration 
beyond the grossly evident margins is always present. The cut surface of the tumor is 
either firm or soft and gelatinous; cystic degeneration may be seen. 
In astrocytoma grade 4/glioblastoma, variation in the gross appearance of the tumor 
from region to region is characteristic. Some areas are firm and white, others are soft 
and yellow (the result of tissue necrosis), and still others show regions of cystic 
degeneration and hemorrhage. 

489



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 

DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

Fig. 9.23: Gross and radiology of diffuse astrocytic tumors: Left; Large diffuse astrocytoma 
occupying the left temporal lobe. Note the homogeneous surface and the enlargement of 
local anatomical structures. Middle; glioblastoma of the left frontal lobe with typical 
coloration: whitish-grey tumor tissue in the periphery, yellow areas of necrosis, and 
extensive hemorrhage. Note the extension through the corpus callosum into the right 
hemisphere. Right; Postcontrast MRI of glioblastoma showing irregular rim-enhancing mass 
in the frontal lobe. 

Microscopic: Diffuse astrocytomas are characterized by a mild to moderate increase 
in the number of glial cell nuclei, somewhat variable nuclear pleomorphism, and an 
intervening feltwork of fine, glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP)-positive astrocytic cell 
processes that give the background a fibrillary appearance. The tumor cells can be seen 
infiltrating normal tissue many centimeters from the main lesion. Astrocytomas grade 
3 show regions that are more densely cellular and have greater nuclear pleomorphism; 
mitotic figures are present. Astrocytoma/Glioblastoma grade 4 has a histologic 
appearance similar to that of astrocytoma grade 3, as well as either necrosis (often with 
pseudopalisading) or vascular proliferation. (Table 9.3, Fig. 9.24)

2 Diffuse astrocytoma No appreciable mitotic activity 
3 Astrocytoma Mitoses 

4 Astrocytoma/Glioblastoma | Necrosis AND/OR Microvascular proliferation 
usually associated with frequent mitoses

B. Oligodendrogliomas 
-— Oligodendrogliomas account for 5% to 15% of gliomas. 

Mostly in the fourth and fifth decades of life. 
Patients may have had several years of antecedent neurologic compiaints, often 
including seizures. 
Mostly in the cerebral hemispheres, mainly in the frontal or temporal lobes. 
Oligodendrogliomas have a better prognosis than that of diffuse astrocytic tumors 
of similar grade. 
The hallmark genetic finding in oligodendrogliomas is codeletion of chromosomes 
1p and 19q, Tumors. 

Microscopic: 
- Oligodendroglioma, WHQ grade 2: the tumor 

is composed of sheets of regular cells with 
rounded nuclei containing finely granular 
chromatin surrounded by a clear halo of 
cytoplasm (Fig. 9.26). The tumor typically 
contains a delicate network of anastomosing 
capillaries. Calcification is common in these 
tumors. Mitotic activity is low. 

- Anaplastic oligodendroglioma, WHO grade 3: 
is a more aggressive with higher cell density, Fig. 9.25: Oligodendroglioma:

II- Other (localized) astrocytic tumors e.g. pilocytic astrocytoma 
Pilocytic Astrocytoma, WHO Grade 1: 
- Pilocytic astrocytomas typically affecting children and young adults. 
- Most commonly located in the cerebellum, they also may involve the third ventricle, 

the optic pathways, spinal cord, and occasionally the cerebral hemispheres. 
- The dominant genetic alterations of pilocytic astrocytoma result in the activation of the 

mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathway and include activating alterations of 
BRAF and inactivation of neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1). Mutations in IDH1 and IDH2 
(common in diffuse astrocytomas) are not found in pilocytic tumors. These genetic 
distinctions support the division of these astrocytic neoplasms into distinct diagnostic 
categories. 

- Gross: A pilocytic astrocytoma often is cystic, with a mural nodule in the wall of the 
¢ cyst; if solid, it is usually well circumscribed (Fig. 9.26). 

- Microscopic: This astrocytic tumor of low to moderate cellularity often has a biphasic 
. pattern, with varying proportions of compacted bipolar cells with Rosenthal fibers and 

loose-textured multipolar cells with microcysts. Eosinophilic granular bodies are more 
often encountered in the loose regions. Necrosis and mitoses are rare (Fig. 9.27). 

Fig. 9.26: Pilocytic astrocytoma: Left: Large, partially — sloeylie astrocytoma of the 
cerebellum with a typical mural nodule. Right: A Typical biphasic pattern with alternating 

compact and loose architectural patterns. 

Fig. 9.27: Pilocytic astrocytoma: |L eft: Abundanrt p ram bodies (EGBs). Right: 
Rosenthal fibers. 

lil- Ependymal tumors e.g. Ependymoma 
Ependymoma: 
- Ependymomas is a circumscribed glioma that most often arise next to the ependyma 

lined ventricular system, including the central canal of the spinal cord. 
- Inthe first 2 decades of life, they typically occur near the fourth ventricle and constitute 
5% to 10% of the primary brain tumors in this age group. In adults, the spinal card is 
their most common location; tumors in this site are particularly frequent in the setting 
of neurofibromatosis type 2. 

492 



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 
DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

-Gross: Ependymomas are well-circumscribed tumors usually arising in or near the 
ventricular system. They are soft and spongy, occasionally with calcium deposits. 
Tumors arising in the fourth ventricle often flow through the foramina of Luschka and 
Magendie. 

- Microscopic: A circumscribed glioma composed of uniform small cells with round to 
oval nuclei with granular chromatin in a fibrillary matrix and characterized by 
perivascular anucleate zones (pseudo-rosettes) with ependymal rosettes also found in 
about one quarter of cases. Classic ependymoma generally has a low cell density and a 
low mitotic count. It very rarely invades adjacent CNS parenchyma to any significant 
extent (Fig. 9.28). Anaplastic ependymomas show increased cell density, high mitotic 
rates, necrosis, and less evident ependymal differentiation. Traditionally, classic 
ependymoma are graded histologically to WHO §rades 2 and 3. 

- However, no association between grade 
and biological behavior or survival has 
been definitively established in 
ependymomas. Given that specific 
genetic alterations and molecular groups 
have recently been proposed as | 
prognostic or predictive factors for these 
tumors, the practice of histologically i: 
grading ependymoma may soon become 
obsolete altogether.

V- Embryonal Neoplasms perivascular rosettes. 

Embryonal tumors of the central nervous 
system (CNS) manifest as highly cellular, poorly differentiated, and mitotically active 
neoplasms that are reminiscent of the developing embryonic nervous system. Such 
tumors constitute the most common group of malignant pediatric brain tumors. Often 
referred to as “small blue cell tumors,” they are biologically aggressive and have a 
tendency to disseminate along cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) pathways. This group includes the 
medulloblastoma, the atypical teratoid/ rhabdoid tumor (AT/RT), the embryonal tumor 
with multilayer rosettes, and a spectrum of other CNS embryonal tumors that were 
previously called “CNS primitive neuroectodermal tumors (PNETs).” All Embryonal 
neoplasms corresponds to WHO Grade 4. 

Medulloblastoma 
See chapter 3 

Tumors of the cranial and paraspinal nerves 

Schwannomas 
Schwannomas account for 8% of all intracranial tumors, 85% of cerebellopontine angle 
tumors, and ~ 30% of spinal nerve root tumors. Approximately 90% of cases are solitary 
and sporadic and 4% arise in the setting of NF2. Of the 5% of schwannomas that are 
multiple but not associated with NF2, some may be associated with schwannomatosis. 
The peak incidence is in the fourth to sixth decades of life. 
Clinically, peripherally situated schwannomas may present as incidental (asymptomatic) 
paraspinal tumors, as spinal nerve tumors with radicular pain and signs of nerve root / 

493 



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVEKDEMYLANTING DISEASES 

DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

spinal cord compression, or as eighth cranial nerve tumors with related symptoms 
(including hearing loss, tinnitus, and occasional vertigo). Motor symptoms are uncommon 
because schwannomas favor sensory nerve roots. Bilateral vestibular tumors are the 
hallmark of NF2. Pain is the most common presentation for schwannomas in patients with 
schwannomatosis. 
Morphology (Gross and Microscopic): Refer to locomotor module. 

Vi- Meningiomas 
Meningiomas are predominantly benign tumors that arise from arachnoid meningothelial 
cells. Most meningiomas correspond histologically to WHO grade 1 (benign). Certain 
histological subtypes or meningiomas, with specific combinations of morphological 
parameters, are associated with less favorable clinical outcomes and correspond 
histologically to WHO grades 2 (20-25%- Atypical meningiomas) and 3 (1-6%- Anaplastic 
meningiomas). 
They usually occur in adults and are often attached to the dura. Overall, females are at 
greater risk than males. 
lonizing radiation is the only established environmental risk factor for meningioma. An 
association between hormones and meningioma risk is suggested. 
Meningiomas commonly occur as sporadic solitary tumors in the general population and 
may be found at autopsy in asymptomatic individuals. They also occur in ~50% of 
individuals with the inherited disorder neurofibromatosis 2 (NF2), which involves 
inactivation of the NF2 gene on chromosome 22, encoding the merlin tumor suppressor. 
About 60% of sporadic solitary meningiomas occur due to merlin inactivation and usually 
display loss of one copy of chromosome 22. 
The overall prognosis is determined by the lesion size and location, surgical accessibility, 
and histologic grade. 
Gross: Most meningiomas are rubbery or firm, well-demarcated, sometimes lobulated, 
rounded masses that feature broad dural attachment (Fig. 9.29). Occasional meningiomas 
invade into the adjacent skull, where they may induce characteristic hyperostosis. 
Adjacent brain is often compressed but rarely frankly invaded. In certain sites, particularly 
along the sphenoid wing, meningiomas may grow as a flat, carpet-like mass, a pattern 
called en-plague meningioma. 
Microscopic: 
Meningiomas, WHO grade 1: Among the varied histologic variants are syncytial, named 
for whorled clusters of cells without visible cell membranes; fibroblastic, with elongated 
cells and abundant collagen deposition between them; transitional, which shares features 
of the syncytial and fibroblastic types; psammomatous, with numerous psammoma 
bodies (Fig. 9.30). 
Atypical meningiomas, WHO grade 2: A meningioma of intermediate grade between 
benign and malignant forms, with increased mitotic activity, brain invasion on histology, 
or at least three of the following features: increased cellularity, small cells with a high 
nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio, prominent nucleoli, sheeting (i.e. uninterrupted patternless 
or sheet-like growth), and foci of spontaneous necrosis. 
Anaplastic (malignant) meningiomas, WHO grade 3 exhibit overtly malignant cytology 
(resembling that of carcinoma, melanoma, or high-grade sarcoma) and/or markedly 
elevated mitotic activity (> 20 mitoses per 10 high-power). 

494



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 
DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

Fig. 9. 29: Meningioma: Gross of the left parasagittal region of the i lobe, 
Microscopic appearance with whirling and psammoma bodies 

Vil- Primary Central Nervous System Lymphoma 
- Primary CNS lymphoma, occurring mostly as diffuse large 8B cell lymphomas, accounts 

for 2% of extranodal lymphomas and 1% of intracranial tumors. 
- It is the most common CNS neoplasm in immunosuppressed persons, in whom the 

tumors are nearly always positive for the oncogenic Epstein-Barr virus. 
- In non-immunosuppressed populations, the age spectrum is relatively wide, with the 

incidence increasing after 60 years of age. 
- Primary brain lymphoma is an aggressive disease with relatively poor response to 

chemotherapy as compared with peripheral lymphomas. 

ViII- Germ Cell Tumors 

- Primary brain germ cell tumors occur along the midline, most commonly in the pineal 
and the suprasellar regions. 

- They account for 0.2% to 1% of brain tumors in people of European descent but as 
many as 10% of brain tumors in persons of Japanese ethnicity. 

- They are a tumor of the young, with 90% occurring during the first 2 decades of life. 
- Germ cell tumors in the pineal region show a strong male predominance. 
- The most common primary CNS germ cell tumor is germinoma, a tumor that closely 

resemble testicular seminoma. 

Metastatic Tumors 

- Metastatic lesions, mostly carcinomas, account for approximately one fourth to one half 
of intracranial tumors. 

- Approximately 80% of all brain metastases are located in the cerebral hemispheres 15% in 
the cerebellum, and 5% in the brain stem. The majority of metastases affecting the spinal 
cord expand from the vertebral body or paravertebral tissues into the epidural space. 

- The most common primary sites are lung, breast, skin (melanoma), kidney, and 
gastrointestinal tract—together these account for about 80% of cases. 

- Gross: metastases form sharply demarcated masses and elicit edema. 
- Microscopic: the boundary between tumor and brain parenchyma is sharp with 

surrounding reactive gliosis. 

495 



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 
DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

Familial Tumor Syndromes 

Several inherited syndromes caused by mutations in various tumor suppressor genes are 
associated with an increased risk of particular types of cancers. Examples of those syndromes 
with particular involvement of the CNS are Tuberous Sclerosis and von Hippel-Lindau Disease. 

Von Hippel-Lindau Disease: 
An autosomal dominant disorder caused by germline mutations of the VHL tumor suppressor 
gene, located on chromosome 3p. The von Hippel-Lindau disease tumor suppressor protein 
(VHL protein) plays a key role in cellular oxygen sensing.

Cognitive Dysfunction and Memory Impairment 

Memory 

Definition 

Memory refers to the processes that allows us to record, store, and later retrieve 

experiences and information. 

Anatomical basis of memory 

the hippocampus and related structures have been implicated as a site for the consolidation 

of memory. 

The hippocampal formation consists of (regions CA1, CA2, CA3), the dentate gyrus and the 

subiculum. 

Types of memory 

A-Sensory memory: 

- Hold large amount of information 

-Very brief {visual }2 second, auditory 3 sec) 

-Registered at the sense receptors {visual iconic store, auditory: echoic store) 

-Based on electrical changes 

B-Short Term memory (STM): 

- A memory store that temporarily holds a limited amount of information. 

-Limited capacity (7+ 2 bits of information) 

-Limited duration (15-20 seconds). 

- Limited storage. 

- Information could pass to the long-term memory by rehearsal or consolidation based on 

chemical changes. 

- How to assess? 

- Tell the patient three word and ask about them later during the examination.

-Unlimited capacity. 

-Very long duration. 

-Permanent but subjected to distortion or replacement.
