CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 
DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

Dementia: Types, Causes, and Clinical Considerations 

Introduction 

-Dementia is a broad term used to describe a group of neurodegenerative conditions 

characterized by a progressive decline in cognitive function, including memory, language, 

problem-solving, and other higher-order mental processes. Dementia is defined as a clinical 

syndrome characterized by a persistent and often progressive decline in cognitive function 

that interferes with a person's ability to perform everyday activities. This impairment goes 

beyond the normal age-related cognitive changes and can significantly impact an individual's 

quality of life, social interactions, and independence. 

-The global incidence of dementia is on the rise, largely due to the aging of the world’s 

population. According to the World Health Organization, the number of people living with 

dementia is projected to increase from an estimated 55 million in 2020 to 78 million by 2030 

and 139 million by 2050. 

-This surge in prevalence highlights the critical need for comprehensive understanding and 

effective management of this condition. 

- Types of Dementia: Dementia can be broadly classified into two main categories: 
reversible and irreversible. 

-Reversible dementias are those that can be caused by underlying conditions that can be 

treated, leading to the potential for cognitive function improvement or even restoration. 

Irreversible dementias, on the other hand, are progressive neurodegenerative disorders that 

result in permanent and often worsening cognitive impairment. 

Reversible Causes of Dementia include, but are not limited to, the following: 

eo Alcoholism 

e Drug/medication intoxication 

+ Vitamin deficiencies: B1, B6, B12 

* Endocrine disorders: Thyroid, parathyroid, adrenal hypo- or hyperfunction 

¢ Organ failure: Liver, kidney, pulmonary 

* Chronic infections: Neurosyphilis, PMLE, tuberculosis, fungal, protozoal 

e Chronic inflammation: Sarcoidosis, coeliac disease, Whipple's disease 

e Surgical conditions: Sub-dural hematoma, normal-pressure hydrocephalus (NPH), primary 

and metastatic brain tumors 

Psychiatric disorders: Depression, schizophrenia, conversion disorder. 



CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 

DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

Irreversible Causes of Dementia : is the most common form of dementia and is characterized 

by a progressive and permanent decline in cognitive function. 

-The four most prevalent types of irreversible dementia are: 1. **Alzheimer's Disease** 2. 

**Vascular Dementia** 3. **Frontotemporal Dementia** 4. **Lewy Body Dementia** Each 

of these subtypes of dementia has distinct underlying pathophysiology, clinical 

manifestations, and management approaches. 

-Alzheimer's Disease Pathophysiology Alzheimer's disease (AD): is the most common form of 

dementia, accounting for approximately 60-80% of all cases. The primary pathological 

hallmarks of AD include the accumulation of amyloid-beta (AB) peptides and the formation of 

neurofibrillary tangles composed of hyperphosphorylated tau protein. The deposition of AB 

peptides leads to the formation of senile plaques, which disrupt synaptic function and 

ultimately result in neuronal death and widespread brain atrophy. The hyperphosphorylation 

of tau protein, on the other hand, causes the microtubule-associated protein to form tangles 

within neurons, impairing their normal function and contributing to neurodegeneration. 

These pathological changes are believed to be driven by a complex interplay of genetic, 

environmental, and lifestyle factors, leading to a gradual and progressive decline in cognitive 

abilities. 

Clinical Presentation: The characteristic clinical presentation of Alzheimer's disease is a 

gradual and insidious onset of memory impairment, typically beginning with the inability to 

form new memories (anterograde amnesia). As the disease progresses, other cognitive 

domains, such as language, visuospatial skills, and executive function, become increasingly 

affected. Patients with Alzheimer's disease may also experience changes in personality, mood, 

and behavior, including apathy, depression, agitation, and delusions. The course of the disease 

is typically marked by a steady decline in cognitive and functional abilities, eventually leading 

to a complete dependence on caregivers for activities of daily living. 

Diagnostic Investigations: The diagnosis of Alzheimer's disease is primarily based on a 

comprehensive clinical evaluation, which includes a thorough medical history, cognitive and 

neuropsychological assessments, and the exclusion of other potential causes of dementia. 

Additionally, the following diagnostic investigations may be employed: 

1. **Neuroimaging™*: Structural imaging techniques, such as magnetic resonance imaging 

(MRI) or computed tomography (CT), can identify patterns of brain atrophy associated with 

Alzheimer's disease, particularly in the hippocampus and medial temporal lobe regions. 

Cerebrospinal Fluid Analysis, Genetic Testing**: In a small percentage of cases, Alzheimer's 

disease can be linked to specific genetic mutations, and genetic testing may be considered, 

particularly in individuals with a strong family history of the condition. 

Treatment and Management: The management of Alzheimer's disease involves a 

multifaceted approach that combines pharmacological and non-pharmacological 

interventions. 

500



| 
CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVE&DEMYLANTING DISEASES 

DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

The primary pharmacological treatments include: 

1. **Cholinesterase Inhibitors**: Drugs such as donepezil, rivastigmine, and galantamine, 

which work by inhibiting the breakdown of acetylcholine, a neurotransmitter important for 

cognitive function. 

2. ** NMDA Receptor Antagonists**: Memantine, which acts by modulating the activity of the 

N-methyi-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor, can be used alone or in combination with 

cholinesterase inhibitors. 

In addition to pharmacological interventions, non-pharmacological approaches, such as 

cognitive stimulation, physical activity, and caregiver support, play a crucial role in managing 

the symptoms of Alzheimer's disease and improving the quality of life for both patients and { 

their families.

ANTIPSYCHOTIC DRUGS 

e Antipsychotics are drugs used to control manifestations of psychosis’. 
Manifestations of Psychosis (mind is separated from reality) 

1. Hallucinations (perception without stimulus): e.g. auditory {most frequent), visual, 
tactile... 

2. Delusions (False fixed beliefs): e.g. paranoid (suspicious) persecution (people are 
conspiring against him), grandeur (believes he is a genius). 

3. Incoherent speech & abnormal behavior (aggression, agitation). 
Etiologoyf Psychosis 

1. Psychiatric causes: Schizophrenia, Bipolar disorder, Delusional disorder ...etc. 
2. Non-psychiatric causes: Dementia, epilepsy, Cushing Syndrome, Brain tumors or 

trauma, drugs e.g. alcohol, cannabis, cocaine, amphetamines, corticosteroids .....etc. 
Schizophrenia (the most classic Prevalence 1% worldwide) 
e |t is a chronic psychotic mental disorder characterized by disturbances in cognition, 

emotion, perception, thinking, and behavior (DSM-5). Characterized by: 
eo Positive symptoms: Hallucinations, delusions, disorganized speech & behavior and 

Hostility. 

oe Negative symptoms: (5A) Affect flattening (flat emotions), Alogia (decrease speech), 
Anhedonia, Amotivation & Asociality intellectual dysfunction. 

e Cognitive Symptoms: There is defect in attention, Working memory and Executive function. 

Dopaminergic and serotonergic theories of Schizophrenia’! (Fig. 10.1) 

- Schizophrenic symptoms may result from increased mesolimbic dopamine activity at Da- 
receptors (positive symptoms) and decreased prefrontal cortex “PFC” or mesocortical 
dopamine activity at Di-receptors (negative symptoms and cognitive impairment). 

- All antipsychotics (Typical & Atypical) block mesolimbic D,-receptors = improve positive 
symptoms. 

- Only Atypical antipsychotics block 5-HT2A receptor (presynaptic heterotrophic negative 
control on dopamine) - 1 dopamine at PFC or mesocortical Di-receptors = improve 
negative symptoms and cognitive impairment. 

0 Psychosis (psyche = mind/soul, and -osis = abnormal ") refers to an abnormal condition of the mind.

receptor function in PFC — positive and negative symptoms of schizophrenia.

DRUG ABUSE 

Drug abuse: Intentional and inappropriate use of a psychoactive drug (usually self- 
administration) resulting in physical, emotional, financial, social and intellectual harmful 
consequences seeking for euphoria or alter perception. 
Drug abuse results in: 

1. Physical dependence: “A drug is needed to function normally”. It indicates an altered 
physiological state due to repeated administration of a drug, the cessation of which 
results in a specific syndrome (Withdrawal Syndrome). 

2. Tolerance: { Drug response following its continuous use =» larger dose is required to 
produce the same initial effect =» loss of control of addict over amount of drug used. 

3. Drug addiction®': Compulsive repeated use of a substance, despite its negative, 
harmful or dangerous effects. There is craving and Loss of control over taking a 
substance. it was considered as a psychological dependence. 

4. Craving: Severe compulsion to take a drug repeatedly to re-experience a pleasant 
feeling, e.g. euphoria. Due to 4 of DA in reward system (NAc). 

Classifofi Drcugas tof iAbousne 
I. CNS Depressants: 

1. Sedative hypnotics: barbiturates - benzodiazepines — alcohol. 
2. Narcotics: heroin - morphine - pethidine - codeine. 

II. CNS Stimulants: caffeine - nicotine - cocaine — amphetamine - khat. 
Hl. Hallucinogens: LSD -Mescaline -Phencyclidine (PCP)-Ketamine- DMT. 
IV. Cannabinoids: Natural (Hashish “Hash”, Marijuana or Cannabis) and Synthetic Voodoo 

and Strox. 
V. Inhalants: induce euphoria and hallucinations. 

1) Solvents in glues & paints (strong dependence). 
2) Anesthetics; nitrous oxide & ether (moderate dependence). 

Short-acting agents have more Abuse occurs and more severe withdrawal 
syndrome than that following longer-acting agents. 

Reward (Pleasure) Pathway include: (plays a key role in addiction) 
1. Nucleus accumbens (NAc) = Pleasure center (Fig. 10.3) 
2. Ventral tegmental area (VTA). 
3. Prefrontal cortex (PFC). 

In either “NATURAL” e.g., food, water, sex, ... or “ARTIFICIAL” e.g., drugs rewarding stimulus, 
information travels from the VTA to the NAc and then up to the PFC. VTA contains dopamine 
(DA) neurons which release DA in the NAc (= Euphoria ) and in the PFC (Behavior). 

89Drug misuse: unintentional or inappropriate use of the prescribed over the counter drugs (or using drugs 

without a prescription). Misuse: taking a drug in a manner or at a dose that was not recommended by the 

physician. 

81 It is possible to be dependent on a drug without being addicted. Although, if one is addicted they are most 
likely also dependent. e.g. A terminal cancer patient being treated with morphine for pain will experience 
withdrawal if the drug is stopped, but they are not a compulsive user of the drug therefore they are not addicted.

Management of Withdrawal “or Addition” in substance Abuse®* 

|. Barbiturates (Strong Dependence) and BZDs (Moderate Dependence) 
Acute Effects: Euphoria - relieve anxiety and insomnia. 
Risks of Chronic Abuse: Memory loss. 
Withdrawal Syndrome (severe with barbiturates - mild with BZDs). 
e Insomnia - anxiety - tremors - delirium - hallucinations - convulsions. 

Management of Barbiturates & BZDs Abuse 
e Replace short-acting agent by a longer-acting one =» less severe withdrawal: 

0 Phenobarbital for pentobarbital. 
0 Diazepam for clonazepam, alprazolam, flunitrazepam. 

ll. Alcohol (Strong Dependence) 

Mechanism: acts on GABA-A receptor->4* GABA effect (cross tolerance with BZDs) and |, 
glutamate action (NMDA receptor antagonist) & release endogenous opioid in VTA (euphoric 
effect). 
Acute Effects®®: Euphoria - relaxation - increased self-confidence. 
Withdrawal Syndrome 
e Similar to barbiturates with delirium tremens: delirium - tremors - psychosis (visual 

hallucinations of crawling bugs). 
Risks of Chronic Abuse (Alcoholism) 

1. Withdrawn, homicidal or suicidal individual - work & family problems. 
2. Liver cirrhosis - peptic ulcer — cardiomyopathy. 
3. Dementia - peripheral neuropathy. 
4. Thiamine deficiency => Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome (ophthalmoplegia, ataxia, confusion, 

psychosis). 

Management of Alcohol Abuse 
1. Diazepam or chlordiazepoxide (longer acting sedatives). 

a. Replaces alcohol (gradual withdrawal of diazepam is smoother). 
b. Anticonvulsant (controls convulsions of withdrawal syndrome). 

2. Thiamine supplements. 
3. Psychotherapy (group therapy) together with drugs to avoid relapse following 

detoxification: 
a. Disulfiram (Antabuse): 

e Inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase involved in alcohol metabolism =» accumulation 
of acetaldehyde =» nausea & vomiting & flushing (disulfiram reaction). To avoid 
this reaction, patients give up drinking. 

b. Naltrexone: block opioid receptors block euphoric effect= Jd, relapse. 
¢. Acamprosate: weak NMDA antagonist, GABA A receptor activator =»c raving. 

83 Also, Haloperidol injection for Agitation and psychosis (especially in stimulants) and Anti-impulsivity Drugs: 
To reduce impulsive behavior in addicts as SSRIs e.g. sertraline, and antiepileptics e.g. valproate and 
carbamazepine. 

% Treatment of alcoho! Intoxication: (1) IV glucose to correct hypoglycemia (to avoid fatal fits) as alcohol induces 
pancreatitis destroying B-cells — release insulin. (2) Thiamine 100 mg IV or IM to prevent neuropathy.

Ill. Opioids (Very Strong Dependence) 
Acute Effects 

1. Eupharia (rush). 
2. Apathy: drowsiness & hypo activity. 

Riskosf Chronic Abuse 

1. Fatal overdose - homicide - suicide - accidents. 
2. 1 Risk of infections due to syringes (hepatitis, AIDS) & ¢ immunity. 

Withdrawal Syndrome (sympathetic overactivity) 

1. Craving for the drug - anxiety - insomnia - tremors. 
2. Piloerection — mydriasis - lacrimation — rhinorrhea. 
3. Tachycardia - hypertension — hyperthermia- hot & cold flushes. 
4. Abdominal cramps - vomiting — diarrhea. 

Management of Opioid Abuse 

1. Symptomatic treatment of withdrawal symptoms 
e Anxiolytics - antiemetics — antispasmodics. 

e Clonidine: inhibits sympathetic discharge. 
2. Detoxification: Methadone or buprenorphine 
e Replace heroin or morphine by methadone. Gradual withdrawal of 
methadone (longer acting) is less severe. 

2. Maintenance: Naltrexone 
e Given chronically after detoxification to block opioid receptors =» loss of 
euphoric effects of opioids = loss of desire to take the drug. 

IV. Nicotine (Very Strong Craving) 

Mechanism: stimulates asf: nicotinic Ach receptors in VTA - DA release in reward pathway 
— reward experienced upon smoking =» craving = dependence... 
Acute Effects: euphoria - { anxiety - T concentration -dappetite 
Risks of Chronic Abuse: cancer, lung diseases, ischemic heart diseases. 
Withdrawal Syndrome: Insomnia - anxiety — aggressiveness - 1 appetite. 

‘ Management of Nicotine Abuse 

1. Psychotherapy (group therapy). 
2. Nicotine replacement by: nicotine gum - transdermal patch - oral inhaler ~ nasal spray. 
3. Bupropion: antidepressant and PDA = craving & Blocks a4B2 nAch R-> | Relapse. 
4. Varenicline®® : partial agonist at CNS aaf3, nicotinic Ach receptors => partial stimulation 

while competitively inhibiting nicotine binding = blocks ability of nicotine to stimulate 
DA system in VTA-> { craving & withdrawal syndrome. 

85 Taken one week before quitting smoking & continued for 12 weeks; repeated if necessary. 
Taken twice daily after meals with a full glass of water to nausea. 
Not recommended for <18 year- old smokers. May induce insomnia, mood changes or suicidal ideas.

¢ 5. Philosophical & creative thinking. 

Withdrawal syndrome®®: weak and usually mild 
? Irritability, Anxiety, Depression, Sweating, Confusion, Tremor, Insomnia, Body weight

| 5. Gateway drug: opens the way to poly-drug abuse.

DEVE ENT OF HEAD AND NECK 

I. Pharvngeal arch 
(Figs. 10.4 to 10.8) 

- After formation of the head fold, the buccopharyngeal membrane is buried at the bottom 
of an ectodermal depression called stomodeum “primitive mouth” in between the 
forebrain cranially and the primitive heart caudally. 
In the 4th and 5th weeks, six elevated bars appear, on each side, in the region between the 
stomodeum and the primitive heart. They contribute in the development of the neck and 
face. 
They are called “Pharyngeal or Visceral arches” because they develop in the region which 
surrounds the primitive pharynx (the cranial part of the foregut).

Fig. 10.4: Stomodeum is surrounded by 5 mesenchymal prominences 

A) 24 days, B) slightly older embryo 

They are also called “branchial arches” because they resemble the gills (branchia) of fishes 
however, the term pharyngeal is better. 
Each arch has 3 elements: 
1. An ectodermal covering 
2. An endodermal lining 
3. A mesenchymal core which is derived from 2 sources: 

a. The paraxial mesoderm: which forms the arch musculature (a group of striated 
muscles which may migrate). 

b. Migrating neural crest cells: which form a skeletal element (an arch cartilage). 
Five grooves are seen internally and externally between the arches. 
» The internal grooves, lined by endoderm, are called “pharyngeal pouches” 
>» The external grooves, lined by ectoderm, are called “pharyngeal clefts”. 
The endoderm and ectoderm in the bottom of the grooves are in contact, with no 
intervening mesoderm. These thin membranes rupture in fishes but remain intact in man. 
The mesenchyme of each arch is invaded by: 
1. An aortic arch which connects the aortic sac with the corresponding dorsal aorta. 
2. A cranial nerve derived from the adjacent hind brain. 
The arches appear in a cranio-caudal sequence. The 5th arch disappears early, leaving no 
remnants. 
The 1% arch divides into 2 prominences which project on the side of the stomodeum: a 
ventral mandibular prominence and a dorsal maxillary prominence.

Anomalies of arches: 
* First arch syndromes (Fig. 10.8) develop due to insufficient migration of neural crest cells 

into the first pharyngeal arch: 
1. Treacher Collins Syndrome (autosomal dominant) = underdevelopment of the zygomatic 

bones + defects of lower eyelids + deformed external ears. 
2. Pierre Robin Syndrome = hypoplesia of mandible + cleft palate + eye & ear defects.

e Originally there were 5 clefts covered by ectoderm, but after disappearance of the 5% arch 
they become 4 only. (Fig. 10.9) 

¢ The 1% cleft persists & gives the external auditory meatus + the outer surface of the tympanic

e The mesoderm of the 2nd arch proliferates causing its surface ectoderm to cover the 2", 3 
& 4!" clefts & finally it fuses with the ectodermal covering of the 6th arch. A temporary cavity 
lined with ectoderm “the cervical sinus” is formed and buried beneath the surface 
ectoderm. It soon disappears.

Anomalies of clefts: (Fig. 10.10) 
1. Preauricular sinus: due to failure of fusion between some of the hillocks of the auricle 
2. Branchial cyst: due to failure of obliteration of the cervical sinus. The cyst may be found 
anywhere along the anterior border of sternomastoid, usually closteo the angle of mandible. 

3. Branchial fistula: 
a. External: due to failure of fusion between the 2" & 6" arches. 
b. Internal: due to rupture of the cervical sinus with the 2" pouch establishing 

communication with the interior of the pharynx.

Development of the Face 

The face develops from 5 swellings “prominences” which appear during the 4th week around the 
stomodeum (primitive mouth); due to migration of neural crest cells under the surface ectoderm, 
raising it. (Fig. 10.13) 
1. One frontonasal prominence above the stomodeum, ventral to the forebrain vesicle 
2. Two maxillary prominences on either side of the stomodeum; from the first pharyngeal arches. 
3. Two mandibular prominences under the stomodeum; from the first pharyngeal arches.

I. The Frontonasal Prominence 
- During the 5th week, two ectodermal thickenings called olfactory (or nasal) Placodes 

appear on each side of the frontonasal prominence. 
- Each placode becomes depressed at its center to form the olfactory pit (nasal sac). The 

margins of the placodes proliferate to form the medial and lateral nasal Prominences. 

Il. The Two Maxillary Prominences (Figs. 10.14 & 10.15) 
- Each maxillary prominence proliferates and grows medially. 
- The growing maxillary prominence pushes the medial nasal prominence towards the 

midline until both medial nasal prominences fuse together forming the intermaxillary 
segment at the tip of the frontonasal prominence. The intermaxillary segment gives 3 
derivatives: 
1. Labial component-» the philtrum of upper lip. 
2. Jaw component => the part of upper jaw that carries the 4 incisor teeth. 
3. Palatal component = 1% palate. 

- Each maxillary prominence is initially separated from the corresponding lateral nasal 
prominence by a naso-lacrimal groove. The ectoderm between them gives rise to a 
solid cellular rod, which becomes canalized to form the naso-lacrimal duct. [ts caudal 
end opens into the lateral nasal wall while its cranial end expands to form the lacrimal 
sac. Later, union between the maxillary prominence and the lateral nasal prominence 
establishes continuity of the surface between the nose and cheek.

(underdevelopment of the lower jaw).

Fig. 10.18: Formation of the neural tube - Dorsal view 

Histogenesis of the neural tube (Fig. 10.19) 

a At first, the wall of the neural tube is a single layer of columnar neuroepithelial cells 
between outer & inner limiting membranes. The cells proliferate & form a pseudo stratified 
neuroepithelium differentiated into three zones: 
1. Inner ependymal (germinal) zone = lining of central canal & ventricles. 
2. Intermediate mantle zone —= gray matter. 
3. Outer marginal zone devoid of cell bodies < white matter. 

QO The cells in the mantle zone are differentiated into two types: 
a. Neuroblasts (= young nerve cells) 
b. Gliablasts (or spongioblasts = young neuroglia cells). They differentiate into 2 types of 

glial cells: 
1. Astrocytes: found mainly in the grey matter & form the supporting connective 

tissue between neurons 
2. Oligodendroglia: found mainly in the white matter & are responsible for 

myelination of nerve fibers within the CNS. 
N.B: The 3" type of glia cells called microglia migrate into the developing neural tube with 
the invading blood vessels; they are mesodermal & are believed to be derived from 
monocytes. 

QO After the neuroepithelial cells stop producing migrating neuroblasts & gliablasts the 
remaining cells become ependymal cells.

Fig. 10.19: Histogenesis of cells of the CNS 

0 The elongating axons of neuroblasts invade the marginal layer which becomes white 
matter. The 1% axons to invade are the short intersegmental fibers. Other axons form tracts 
which reach specific destinations within the CNS. Some axons project outside the neural 
tube forming motor nerves (somatomotor to skeletal muscles & visceromotor or 
autonomic preganglionic). 

0 Myelination of nerve fibers 
- starts in the 4" month and is not completed till the 2" year after birth. 
- Myelination of motor fibers occurs earlier than sensory fibers. 
- Myelination in the CNS is by Oligodendroglia while outside the CNS is by Schwann 

cells (derived from the neural crest). 
11. 

Fate of the neural tube (Fig. 10.20) 
1. The lateral walls continue to increase in thickness, the tube becomes oval in outline and 

its canal becomes narrow, slit-like. The dorsal & ventral midline portions of the tube are 
devoid of mantle layer, i.e., ependymal & marginal layers only, forming a dorsal lamina 
(previously called roof plate) and a ventral lamina (previously called floor plate). These 
regions serve primarily as sites of crassing of commissural fibers between the right & left 
sides. 

EE —— " 
EE ISi e ili ET em. 



CHAPTER X: MISCELEANOQUS&EMBRIOLOGY 

2. Later, the canal becomes diamond-shaped in cross section due to appearance of a 
longitudinal sulcus limitans on each side. The part of mantle layer dorsal to it forms the 
dorsolateral lamina (previously called alar lamina) containing sensory neurons while the 
part ventral to it forms the ventrolateral lamina (previously called basal lamina) 
containing sensory neurons. The visceral (autonomic) neurons are located opposite the 
sulcus limitans.

Fig.10.20: Fate of neural tube - Formation of the spinal cord 

Development of the Spinal Cord (Fig. 10.20 & 10.21) 

Q (tis derived from the caudal part of the neural tube. 
Q The histogenesis of the neural tube is already described. The following changes occur in 

the wall of the neural tube: 
- The diamond-shaped lumen becomes circular = the central canal. 
- The ependymal layer = the ciliated columnar lining of the central canal. 
- The mantle layer = the grey matter. 
- The marginal layer is invaded by ingrowing axons = the white matter. 
- The ventrolateral (basal) lamina @ the ventral horn of grey matter (motor). Nerve cells 

accumulate in the regions which innervate the limb buds forming the cervical & lumbar 
enlargements of the cord. 

- The dorsolateral (alar) lamina =» the dorsal horn of grey matter (sensory). 
- Neurons opposite the sulcus limitans —- the lateral horn (autonomic) in the 

thoracolumbar region (sympathetic) & in sacral regions (parasympathetic). 
- The posteromedian septum is formed by occlusion of the dorsal part of the central 

canal while the anteromedian fissure is formed by bulging of the two basal laminae 
more than the floor plate.

Fig. 10.21: Formation of the spinal nerves 

QO The roots of spinal nerve develop as follows: 
- The ventral root is derived from elongating axons of neurons in the basal lamina 

(motor) & opposite the sulcus limitans (autonomic). 
- The dorsal (sensory) root & the DRG are derived from the neural crest. 

Q Positional changes of the cord (Fig. 10.22): 
- In the 3" month of intrauterine life, the spinal cord fills the vertebral canal completely 

therefore the spinal nerves pass horizontally from their segments of origin to the 
intervertebral foramina. 

- Later, the vertebral canal & dura elongate faster than the neural tube thus the spinal 
nerves pass obliquely from their origin to the foramina of exit which gradually acquire 
lower positions. 

- At birth, the spinal cord ends opposite L3 vertebra. 
- In adults, it ends opposite the upper border of L2 vertebra. Below this point, the track 

of spinal cord regression is represented by the filum terminale.

Congenital malformations of the spinal cord 
1. Rachischisis (Fig. 10.23) = failure of the neural tube to close. 

Fig. 10.23: Rachischisis 

2. Spina bifida: (Fig. 10.24) (1:1000 of population) = failure of the dorsal portions of the 
vertebrae to fuse together leaving a midline defect usually at L5-S1. There are 2 types: 
** Spina bifida occulta: asymptomatic; may be marked externally by a tuft of hair. 
*¢ Spina bifida cystica: much more serious. May be: 

- Meningiocele = the meninges covered by skin bulge through the defect. 
- Meningiomyelocele: the meninges, spinal cord, and nerves bulge; 75% of all Spina 

bifida cystica are of this type; usually associated with hydrocephalus. 
- Myeloschisis or Rachischisis = the neural tube is not closed, and nervous tissue is 
exposed to the surface. In the brain the condition is called anencephaly. Neural 
tube defects are detected by increased alpha fetoprotein during amniocentesis.

Il. Brain Flexures: (Fig. 10.26) 
As the result of unequal growth of the different parts of the brain 3 flexures are 
formed and the brain becomes bent in a zigzag fashion. 
1. The two earliest flexures are concave ventrally and are associated with the 

development of the head fold. These are 
a. The cephalic flexure between the forebrain and midbrain 
b. The cervical flexure at the junction of the hindbrain and spinal cord. 

2. A third flexure called the pontine flexure develops in the region of the future pons. its 
development is responsible for the formation of the 4" ventricle. It differs from the 
other two in that: 

Its convexity is forward 
It does not affect the head.

The Hind-Brain Vesicle (Rhombencephalon) (Fig. 10.27) 
QO At first, the hind-brain vesicle consists of two lateral walls, connected across the midline 

by floor- and roof-plates. Each lateral wall consists of an alar and a basal lamina, separated 
by the sulcus limitans. 

a The following changes occur: 
1. The cavity dilates forming the 4" ventricle and the lateral walls rotate around the axis 

of the floor plate like opening a book. In this way, the basal lamina becomes medial & 
the alar lamina becomes lateral. 

2. The remains of the sulcus limitans are seen in the adult brain on the floor of the 4% 
ventricle.

(J The Basal lamina = motor nuclei in 3 columns, from medial to lateral: 
1. General somatic efferent (GSE or somatomotor column) to muscles derived from

CHAPTER X: MISCELEANOUS&EMBRIOLOGY 

3. General visceral efferent (GVE or visceromotor parasympathetic column). 
( The Alar lamina => sensory nuclei in 3 columns, from medial to lateral: 
1. General visceral afferent (GVA) column: receives general sensations from viscera and 

represented by the solitary nucleus. 
2. Special visceral afferent (SVA) column: Represented by the solitary nucleus, this receives 

taste sensations 
3. General somatic afferent (GSA) column receives general sensations from somatic 

structures. 
4. Special somatic afferent (SSA): represented by the four vestibular nuclei (equilibrium) & the 

two cochlear nuclei (hearing). 

Development of the Cerebellum 
0 Develops as 2 dorsolateral outgrowths of the alar lamina of the metencephalon called 

rhombic lips which fuse across the midline forming the vermis (Fig. 10.28). 
0 Originally, the 3 basic layers (ependyma, mantle & marginal) are arranged in this order 

within cerebellum. Later, some cells from the mantle layer migrate to the surface => the 
cerebellar cortex, the remaining cells => the cerebellar nuclei (Fig. 10.29) 

Q At first, the surface is smooth. The earliest fissure to appear is the posterolateral fissure 
which separates the Flocculonodular lobe (Archicerebellum) from the corpus cerebelli. The 
second fissure to appear is the fissura prima which separates the anterior lobe 
(Paleocerebellum) from the posterior lobe (neocerebellum).

Telencephalon (Cerebral Hemispheres) 
1. The original occipital pole curves forwards to form the temporal pole and a new 

occipital pole is formed. A hidden depression called lateral cerebral fossa <the insula. 
(Fig. 10.32) 

2. Some neuroblasts of the mantle layer migrate to the surface ~-» the cerebral cortex. 
And the remaining cells > the corpus striatum. Axons descending from the cortical 
neurons (the internal capsule) penetrate the corpus striatum dividing it into caudate 
nucleus (dorsomedially) & lentiform nucleus (ventrolaterally). 

3. Due to growth of the cortex, the smooth surface of the hemisphere starts to show sulci 
& gyri beginning from the 3rd month. (Fig. 10.33) 

4. The neural cavity becomes the ventricles which are lined by ependyma. At certain sites, 
the ependyma of the roof of the ventricle and the pia mater are invaded by blood 
vessels to form the choroid plexus which forms the CSF. (Fig. 10.34) 

5. Three groups of commissural axons invade the lamina terminalis to reach the opposite 
hemisphere including; anterior commissure, hippocampal commissure & corpus 
callosum. Three other commissures develop outside the lamina terminalis including; 
the posterior commissure, habenular commissure & optic chiasma.

Congenital malformations of the brain (Fig. 10.35 & Fig. 10.36) 

. Skull defect through which the brain/meninges herniate; mostly seen in the occipital bone 
. Cranial Meningiocele = herniation of the meninges 
. Meningeoencephalocele = herniation of both meninges and brain. 
Meningohydroencephalocele = herniation of the meninges, brain and ventricles. 

. Microcephaly = small skull due to impaired growth of the brain. 

. Anencephaly = exposed, protruding brain due to failure of the anterior neuropore to close; 
in most cases, the hindbrain remains intact. It causes hydramnios & is incompatible with life. 

4. Hydrocephalus = enlargement of the ventricles due to overproduction or obstruction to the 
flow of CSF as in congenital stenosis of the aqueduct or the foramina of the 4th ventricle. 

5. Agenesis of the corpus callosum or cerebellum. 
6. Mental retardation: Teratogens known to affect the nervous system include radiation, 

alcohol, viruses, and toxoplasmosis.

The neural crest: (Fig. 10.37) 

%\/*  Its cells are derived from the edges of the neural folds and migrate to give rise to most of 
the peripheral nervous system. 

*%* Its cells differentiate into many types: 
1. Neuroblasts => all ganglia 

337 

WNO DOO RB 



CHAPTER X: MISCELEANOUS&EMBRIOLOGY 

Sensory ganglia = the dorsal root ganglia and the ganglia of cranial nerves V (semilunar), 
Vil {(Geniculate), IX (superior & inferior), and X (superior & inferior). The neuroblasts of the 
sensory ganglia send two processes: 
Central process: invades the neural tube, forming collectively the dorsal roots of spinal 
nerve or sensory roots of cranial nerves then terminate in the sensory nuclei of the alar 
lamina or ascend in the marginal layer forming tracts which terminate on higher centers. 

ii. Peripheral process: elongates peripherally to terminate on sensory receptors. The 
proximal parts of the two processes fuse together & the cells become pseudounipolar. 
Autonomic ganglia (sympathetic & parasympathetic). Their neuroblasts form the 
postganglionic neurons of the autonomic nervous system. 
Spongioblasts will give rise to the following: 
Schwann cells which migrate peripherally & wrap themselves around the axons forming 
the neurilemmal sheath which is responsible for myelination in the peripheral nervous 
system. 
Cells which migrate to surround the neural tube = the pia & arachnoid maters. 
N.B.: The dura mater is derived from the surrounding mesoderm. 

. Melanocytes: migrate to the dermis of skin. 

. Chromaffin cells will give the following: 
Adrenal medulla (N.B.; the adrenal cortex is mesodermal) 
Carotid body & paraganglia. 

. Skeletal elements = odontoblasts & cartilaginous elements of the branchial arches.

CHAPTER IX: NEURODEGENERATIVEEDEMYLANTING DISEASES 

DISEASES TRAUMA, VASCULAR DISORDER & TUMORS 

B Semantic memory represents general factual knowledge about the world and language, 

including memory for words and concepts. You know that Mount Everest is the world’s 

tallest peak. 

Non declarative: 

B® Procedural memory is reflected in skills and actions, like riding a bike. Done without 

reflection 

Bm Classically conditioned responses not consciously remembered 

Chemical basis of memory 

e Acetylcholine plays important role in encoding of new information and the formation of 

working memory 

e Dopamine plays an important role in consolidation of the working memory 

eo Norepinephrine is a neurotransmitter that is important for attentiveness, emotions, 

sleeping, dreaming, and learning. 

e GABA (gamma amino butyric acid) an inhibitory neurotransmitter. GABA acts like a 

brake to the excitatory neurotransmitters that lead to motor control, vision, and many 

other cortical functions. It also regulates anxiety. 

e Glutamate an excitatory relative of GABA. It is the most common neurotransmitter in 

the central nervous system - as much as half of all neurons in the brain - and is 

especially important regarding memory. 

Types of memory and its relation to neurotransmitter: 

B Working memory is our ability to absorb and retain new information and correlate it with 

older memories. If we are deficient in dopamine in our frontal lobe, our working memory 

will be overloaded, and we will dump older memories. 

B Immediate Memory: Immediate memory depends on acetylcholine in the parietal lobe. A 

deficiency here will affect our learning capacity and our basic alertness. It must transfer 

visual and auditory signals to long term memory within 30 seconds of receiving them. 

BW Verbal Memory: Verbal memory is associated with our temporal lobe. If we have 

insufficient GABA, we will have difficulty in producing and understanding sounds, words, 

sentences, and stories. 

BW Visual Memory: Visual memory is our ability to remember faces, colors, shapes, designs, 

surroundings, pictures, and symbols. It is a function of our occipital lobe and the 

neurotransmitter serotonin.
